Chapter 11 Neuropsychological Assessment and Clinical Rating Scales

In this chapter
  1. 11.1 What Neuropsychological Assessment Is: Questions, Domains, Approaches and Premorbid Ability: explain what neuropsychological assessment asks, which domains it tests, the battery and flexible approaches, and how premorbid ability and decline are estimated.
  2. 11.2 The Fixed Batteries: Halstead-Reitan and Luria-Nebraska: describe the tests of the Halstead-Reitan and Luria-Nebraska batteries, how they are scored, and what each is used for.
  3. 11.3 Indian Neuropsychological Tools: The NIMHANS Battery, the PGI Battery and the PGI Memory Scale: describe the main Indian batteries and memory scale, who built them, and what their tests measure.
  4. 11.4 Memory Tests: The Wechsler Memory Scale, Digit Span and the Learning Tests: explain how memory is tested, from digit span to the Wechsler Memory Scale and the verbal and visual learning tests.
  5. 11.5 Visuomotor and Visuospatial Tests: The Bender-Gestalt, the Benton Tests and Constructional Tasks: explain the Bender-Gestalt Test and the other visuomotor, visuospatial and body-schema tests, and what errors on them suggest.
  6. 11.6 Executive Function, Attention, Concept Formation and Language Tests: explain the Wisconsin Card Sorting Test, the Trail Making Test and the other tests of executive function, attention, abstract thinking and language.

  9. 11.9 Clinical Rating Scales II: Psychosis, Mania, Substance Use, Children, Disability and Forensic Use: explain the main scales for psychosis, mania, substance use, children, global functioning and disability, and psychopathy, and choose the right scale for a question.

Weightage. 40 of the 148 questions mapped in this book (27.0%) test this chapter, 11 of them from real past papers. 2 of its 9 sections are High yield.

In 1935 a young psychologist named Ward Campbell Halstead (1908 to 1968, the year most reference works give; the Library of Congress record has 1969) arrived at the University of Chicago as a National Research Fellow, fresh from a doctorate at Northwestern University. He set out to study people whose brains had been injured, many of them patients of the university's neurosurgeons, among them people who had lost part of a frontal lobe in an operation to remove a tumour. Halstead's laboratory for this work was one of the first of its kind in North America, and he began in an unusual way. Before he built a single test, he spent many hours watching and talking with brain-injured people as they went about the ordinary business of their lives, and he became convinced that the intelligence tests of the day missed much of what such people had lost: a person could lose a large part of the frontal lobes in surgery, he pointed out, without a large fall in IQ. Whatever these people had lost, the IQ test was not measuring it.

Halstead called the missing quality biological intelligence, the adaptive abilities that depend directly on the health of the brain, as distinct from the school-taught knowledge that intelligence tests sample so well. He built tasks to capture it, gave them to patients and to healthy people, and in 1947 published the results in Brain and Intelligence: A Quantitative Study of the Frontal Lobes, where a factor analysis of his tests yielded four factors that together made up biological intelligence. His most important student was Ralph M. Reitan (1922 to 2014), who met Halstead in 1945, while working as a psychologist with brain-injured soldiers at an army hospital in Illinois. Halstead encouraged him to apply to graduate school and took him into the laboratory as a research assistant; it was there that Reitan learned how to test, and he completed his doctorate at Chicago in 1950. In 1951 he moved to the Indiana University Medical Center in Indianapolis and founded a neuropsychology laboratory of his own. Its battery of tests took one to one and a half days to give, and by the beginning of 1959 the laboratory had tested about 2,000 patients. In 1955, in the Archives of Neurology and Psychiatry, Reitan reported the study that made Halstead's tests famous. He compared 50 patients with brain damage and 50 people with no evidence of it, matched in pairs for race ("colour", in the language of the day), sex, age and education, so that both groups averaged about 32 years of age and about 11.6 years of schooling. On Halstead's summary score, the Impairment Index, not one of the brain-damaged patients did better than his or her matched partner, and at the best cut-off the index misclassified only 4 per cent of the patients and 14 per cent of the controls. Not every test passed: two measures based on Halstead's flicker-fusion test did not separate the groups to a significant degree, and the flicker test was later dropped from the battery.

Reitan became best known for something more daring. At Halstead's suggestion he made a habit of interpreting test results blind: from the scores alone, without seeing the patient or knowing the medical findings, he wrote down whether the brain was damaged, on which side, roughly where, and what kind of process was likely to be at work. Only then did he compare his conclusions with what the neurologists, the neurosurgeons and the pathologists had found, and study his mistakes. A report his laboratory issued in January 1959 prints a series of such cases, each in the same order: the scores, Reitan's report signed with his initials, a comment on the reasoning, and finally the neurological and neurosurgical summary. For one 42-year-old man he wrote that the left hemisphere was working worse than the right and that the pattern fitted a fairly large tumour lying mainly in the left temporal lobe and reaching forward towards the frontal lobe; his strongest clue was the man's difficulty with language. The surgeons who operated found a fast-growing tumour, a glioblastoma, in the left frontal and temporal region. The report printed the misses too. For a 57-year-old man Reitan placed the trouble in the right temporal region but judged it slow or static, perhaps the after-effects of an old head injury; the man had a tumour of an unusually slow-growing kind, and the laboratory's comment admitted that it could not change its conclusion after the fact and must accept the case "as an important illustration of the limitations of our procedures".

Within twenty years of that report, the computerised tomography (CT) scanner, and later magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), could show a tumour or a stroke directly, and nobody now needs a psychologist to say on which side of the head a lesion lies. Yet neuropsychological testing did not disappear; it grew. A scan shows the brain's structure, but it cannot show what a person can still remember, plan, say or do, and those are the questions that patients, families, doctors and courts actually ask. Hold on to Halstead's laboratory and Reitan's blind reports, because they contain this chapter's central lesson: behaviour on carefully chosen tasks can tell you a surprising amount about the brain. The chapter asks which tasks, how they are scored, and against what standard. It then turns to the clinician's rating scales, which answer a different kind of question: not "where is the damage?" but "how ill is this person, and are they getting better?" Remember Halstead and Reitan; you will return to them at the end of the chapter.

11.1 What Neuropsychological Assessment Is: Questions, Domains, Approaches and Premorbid AbilityMedium yield · 4 Qs

Suppose a neurologist sends you a patient with a one-line note: "Kindly do a neuropsychological assessment." What exactly are you being asked to do? Not to give a test, because no single test answers anything useful about a brain. A neuropsychological assessment is an inquiry. It starts from a question, samples a set of abilities chosen to answer it, reads the resulting scores by rules that Halstead's successors worked out over decades, and judges them against a standard: either what healthy people of the same age and background achieve, or what this particular person could do before the illness began. This section takes those pieces in turn. It begins with what neuropsychology is and what an assessment is for, moves to the abilities that are tested and the history of how the field learned to test them, sets out the three ways of building an assessment and Reitan's four ways of reading one, and then turns to the hardest question of all: how much has this person lost, when nobody tested them before they fell ill? It ends with two questions that every report must face, whether the person was giving their best effort and whether "organic or functional?" is still the right thing to ask, and then walks one patient from the referral note to the final report.

Brain, behaviour and the referral question

Neuropsychology is the study of the relationship between the brain and behaviour. "Behaviour" here has its broadest meaning: remembering, speaking, perceiving, planning, moving and feeling all count, because all of them depend on the brain. Clinical neuropsychology applies this knowledge to people whose brains have been affected by injury or disease (Book 2, Section 10.9.9 describes the specialty and the settings it works in). Its central tool is the neuropsychological assessment: the systematic measurement of a person's cognitive abilities and behaviour with standardised tests, interpreted together with the history, the clinician's observations and the medical findings, in order to answer a question about how the brain is working and what that means for the person's life.

Why should a test of behaviour tell you anything about a brain? Because each ability depends on particular brain systems. Damage to a system lowers the abilities that depend on it and leaves the others alone, so the pattern of strengths and weaknesses carries information that a single score cannot. That was Halstead's and Reitan's wager, and the opening of this chapter shows how far it could be pushed. But a test measures behaviour, and behaviour depends on much more than the brain. A person who is tired, frightened, depressed, half-deaf, short-sighted without her glasses, tested in a language she does not use at home, or hoping to look more impaired than she is will also score badly. Every interpretation therefore weighs the brain against these other explanations, and much of this section is about how that is done.

Every assessment begins with a referral question: the question, usually asked by someone else, that the assessment exists to answer. A psychiatrist asks whether an older man's forgetfulness is early dementia or the effect of depression. A neurosurgeon asks which abilities a planned operation might put at risk. A rehabilitation team asks whether a woman who had a head injury in a road accident can go back to running her shop. A school asks why a bright boy with epilepsy cannot keep up in class; a court asks whether a man accused of a crime can follow his own trial. A good referral question names the decision that hangs on the answer. A vague one ("assess cognition") has to be turned into a useful one, usually by a conversation with the person who sent it, because the question decides which tests are given, how long the assessment takes and what the report must say. Figure 11.1 sets out the path from the question to the report; Section 11.1.10 walks one patient along it.

The questions that reach a neuropsychologist fall into a handful of families, and between them they define what the assessment is for:

  • Detecting and describing impairment. Is there a cognitive impairment at all, and if so, which abilities are affected, how badly, and which are spared? John Pinel and Steven Barnes, in their biopsychology textbook, point out that testing helps most where brain imaging, the electroencephalogram (EEG) and the neurological examination have proved equivocal, as they often are after a mild head injury or early in a degenerative disease.
  • Differential diagnosis. Which of several conditions best explains what is seen? The classic case is an older person with poor memory and low mood, who may have early dementia, a depression that is impairing concentration (the "pseudodementia" of Book 3, Section 10.11.6), or both. Others are delirium versus dementia (Book 3, Section 10.8.8), one kind of dementia versus another, and the lasting effects of alcohol.
  • Localisation. Which part of the brain is likely to be affected? This was the great purpose of Reitan's era, and the next paragraph explains why it has shrunk.
  • A baseline for measuring change. A first assessment becomes the yardstick for later ones: after surgery, during a course of electroconvulsive therapy, through the slow course of a dementia or a drug trial, or in recovery from a head injury. Because people improve on tests simply by having taken them before (Chapter 3, Section 3.2.3 explains this practice effect), a later score must rise or fall by more than practice and measurement error alone would produce; the reliable change index of Chapter 3, Section 3.7.6 is built for exactly this.
  • Planning rehabilitation and care. A clear map of what is lost and what remains tells the team what to retrain, what to work around with diaries and alarms, and what the family should expect (Book 2, Section 9.9 describes neuropsychological rehabilitation).
  • Capacity and fitness. Can this person consent to treatment, manage money, make a will, drive, return to a demanding job, or follow a trial? The tests do not answer these questions on their own, but they supply much of the evidence on which the people who must answer them rely.
  • Medico-legal and disability work. Claims for compensation after road accidents and injuries at work, and the certification of disability (Section 11.9 and Book 2, Section 9.9.8), depend on a documented, defensible account of what has been lost.

Localisation deserves its own word, because it is the purpose that changed most. In the 1950s a neurologist who suspected a tumour had only crude and often unpleasant ways of looking inside the head: X-rays of the skull, the EEG, angiography (X-rays after dye is injected into the arteries of the neck) and pneumoencephalography, in which air was introduced into the spaces of the brain so that they would show on an X-ray. Reitan's 1959 case reports mention both of the last two. Against that background, a battery of tests that could say "left hemisphere, probably temporal, probably a fast-growing lesion" was a genuine diagnostic tool. CT, introduced in the early 1970s, and MRI, which entered hospitals in the 1980s, took most of that job away. Neuropsychological assessment still contributes to localisation where structure and function part company, as when the scan is normal after a mild head injury or in the earliest stage of a degenerative disease, and in planning epilepsy surgery, where the team must know which abilities the tissue to be removed supports. But its main business today is description: not where the damage is, but what it does to the person.

Clinical link

Capacity is a legal judgement, not a test score. In India the Mental Healthcare Act, 2017 (Section 4) says that every person, including a person with mental illness, is deemed to have the capacity to make mental healthcare and treatment decisions if he or she has the ability to understand the information relevant to the decision, or to appreciate any reasonably foreseeable consequence of a decision or of not deciding, or to communicate the decision by speech, expression, gesture or any other means (the Act joins the three abilities with "or"). The information must be given in simple language the person understands, or in sign language or visual aids, and a decision that others perceive as inappropriate or wrong does not, by itself, mean that the person lacks capacity. A neuropsychologist's findings on comprehension, memory, reasoning and judgement are evidence for each of those abilities, so a good report says what the person can and cannot understand, retain and weigh, in words a doctor or magistrate can use.

Exam trap

Neuropsychological assessment measures how the brain is working through behaviour: its purposes are detection and description of impairment, differential diagnosis, localisation, a baseline for change, rehabilitation planning, capacity and medico-legal work. It is not personality assessment, vocational guidance, projective testing or the measurement of attachment, which is why "neuropsychological assessment" is the answer whenever a paper asks what the Halstead-Reitan battery is for (Section 11.2). And if an item asks for the main purpose today, prefer describing the pattern of deficits and its consequences over localisation, which imaging now does better.

What is tested: the domains of cognition

A neuropsychologist does not test "the brain" or "intelligence" in general. The abilities that brain damage can impair are grouped into cognitive domains: families of related abilities that can be lost independently of one another and can therefore be tested separately. A stroke can destroy speech and leave memory intact; a head injury can leave a man able to remember everything and unable to plan his day. The usual list runs from the most basic abilities to the most complex, and the order matters, because a person who fails an early item on the list cannot be fairly tested on the later ones (Book 2, Section 2.6.1 makes the same point about the bedside examination).

Orientation and level of consciousness come first. Is the person awake and alert, and does she know who she is, where she is and what the date is? Orientation is the person's awareness of time, place and person. A drowsy or disoriented patient will fail every later test for that reason alone, so these are checked before anything else (Book 2, Section 2.6.2 describes the levels of consciousness, and Section 11.7 the Glasgow Coma Scale that grades them). Attention and concentration come next: the ability to take in a certain amount at once (attention span), to keep the mind on a task over time (sustained attention, or vigilance), to pick out what matters and ignore the rest (selective attention) and to share attention between two tasks (divided attention). Repeating strings of digits, crossing out every target letter on a page and pressing a key whenever a target appears on a screen are typical tasks (Sections 11.4 and 11.6). Closely tied to attention is processing speed, the speed with which a person carries out simple mental work, such as copying symbols that match digits against the clock. Slowing is one of the commonest effects of almost any brain disorder, but it is also produced by ageing, depression and sedative drugs, so it is sensitive without being specific.

Memory is the domain most often complained of. The neuropsychologist separates learning new material from keeping it over a delay and from getting it back out, verbal material from visual, free recall from recognition, and new learning from memory for the remote past (Section 11.4, and the PGI Memory Scale in Section 11.3). Language covers naming objects, the fluency and grammar of speech, understanding what is said, repeating, reading and writing (Section 11.6 gives the tests; the aphasias themselves are in Book 3, Section 10.8.3). Visuospatial skill is the perception of shape, position, distance and direction, and constructional ability is the capacity to put parts together into a whole, as in copying a drawing or building a pattern from blocks (Section 11.5, and block design in Chapter 6). Executive functions are the higher-order control processes that plan, start, stop, switch, monitor and adjust behaviour towards a goal, together with abstract reasoning and the formation of concepts; they depend especially on the frontal lobes and their connections, and they are the abilities that most often decide whether a person can live independently (Section 11.6). Motor and sensory function covers the speed, strength and dexterity of each hand and the perception of touch on each side of the body, which matter less for their own sake than for what a difference between the two sides reveals (Section 11.1.5 below, and Sections 11.2 and 11.5).

Two further things are always estimated, though they are not domains of the same kind. One is general intellectual ability, now and before the illness, because every other score is judged against it (Chapter 6 teaches the intelligence tests, and Section 11.1.7 below the estimation of the earlier level). The other is the context: the person's mood, behaviour and effort. Depression and anxiety lower scores on attention, speed and memory, which is why neuropsychologists routinely add a mood inventory such as the Beck Depression Inventory to their tests (Section 11.8), and an assessment that ignored whether the person was trying would be worthless (Section 11.1.9). If you have read Book 3, you will recognise the same territory in a different arrangement: DSM-5-TR builds its neurocognitive disorders on six domains, complex attention, executive function, learning and memory, language, perceptual-motor function and social cognition (Book 3, Section 10.8.2). "Perceptual-motor" gathers the visuospatial and constructional abilities, and social cognition, the ability to recognise other people's emotions and infer their intentions, is the newest member of either list. Table 11.1 is the map of the whole chapter: each domain, what it means, the tests you will meet for it and where they are taught.

Table 11.1 The domains of a neuropsychological assessment and the tests taught in this chapter.
DomainWhat it meansTypical testsTaught in

Notice what every entry in the table has in common. Each is an ability: something a person can do well or badly, quickly or slowly, and that depends on the working of particular brain systems. That is the test for whether something can be a neuropsychological domain. An opinion fails it at once. What a person believes about politics, prefers in music or thinks of her employer has no right answer and no speed, and it is measured, when it is measured at all, by the attitude and opinion scales of Chapter 8. The same is true of a person's "reaction" in the everyday sense of how she feels about something. Neither is a domain, and no neuropsychological test is designed to assess either.

Exam trap

Asked which of language, memory, executive functioning and opinion is not assessed by neuropsychological tests (RML 2025), answer opinion: an attitude, not a brain-dependent ability. Asked which set of domains neuropsychological tests assess, choose language, memory and executive functioning; every other option in that RML 2025 item contains "opinion" or "reaction", neither of which is a domain. One caution for next year's paper: reaction time is measured, as an index of processing speed and attention, so do not reject an option that says "reaction time" or "processing speed" on the strength of this item.

From the localisers to the process approach: a short history

The idea that a careful study of what a patient can and cannot do reveals where the brain is damaged is older than psychology's tests. In 1861 the French physician Paul Broca (1824 to 1880) showed at autopsy that a patient who had lost almost all speech had damage in the left inferior frontal region, and in 1874 the German neurologist Carl Wernicke (1848 to 1905) described a different loss, of the understanding of speech, after damage further back in the left temporal lobe (Book 1, Section 8.9.6 tells both stories). These localisers established the principle on which all of neuropsychology rests, the localisation of function: particular abilities depend on particular parts of the brain. What they could not supply was a way of measuring those abilities in a living patient, reliably, against a standard.

A first challenge to simple localisation came from Kurt Goldstein (1878 to 1965), a German neurologist who studied soldiers with brain wounds after the First World War. Goldstein argued that brain damage changes the whole person's way of meeting the world, not just one isolated skill. His patients had lost what he called the abstract attitude, the ability to think about what is merely possible, to group things by a category, to shift from one point of view to another and to grasp what is common to different things, and were left with the concrete attitude, bound to the immediate, visible situation. To take an invented illustration of the kind of loss he described: such a patient may drink from a glass of water perfectly well when he is thirsty, yet be unable to pretend to drink from an empty glass when asked to, because the pretence requires him to step outside the concrete situation. The sorting tests he built with Martin Scheerer to assess this loss are taught in Section 11.6.

For several decades after the 1930s, the question that psychiatrists put to clinical psychologists was blunt: is this patient "organic" (suffering from a disease of the brain) or "functional" (suffering from a psychiatric illness with no visible brain disease)? The psychologist answered with a single test of organicity, the supposed general effect of brain damage, such as the copying test of Lauretta Bender (1938) or the design-memory test of Arthur Benton (1946), both taught in Sections 11.5 and 11.4. Pinel and Barnes call this the single-test approach, and they explain why it failed: no single test could be sensitive to all the varied effects that brain damage can have, because brain damage is not one thing. A stroke in the right parietal lobe and a tumour in the left frontal lobe produce entirely different pictures, and a test that catches one may miss the other.

The failure of single tests produced the batteries. Halstead and Reitan's battery (the opening of this chapter, and Section 11.2) gave every patient the same broad set of tests, scored against cut-offs, and by the 1960s this standardised-battery approach dominated North American neuropsychology. Pinel and Barnes add its limitation: such batteries separate neurological patients from healthy people well, but separate neurological patients from psychiatric patients much less well. In Moscow, meanwhile, Alexander Romanovich Luria (1902 to 1977) had developed a very different method, much of it from his work with soldiers wounded in the head during the Second World War (you met one of his patients, Zasetsky, in Book 2, Chapter 9). Luria saw every complex act, writing a word for example, as a functional system: a chain of links, each supported by a different region of the brain, all of which must work for the act to succeed. A lesion breaks one link, and the act then fails in a characteristic way that depends on which link is broken. So Luria did not count right answers against norms. He set the patient a task, watched exactly how it went wrong, changed the task to test his guess, and reasoned from the kind of failure to the broken link, a method called syndrome analysis. He also grouped the brain into three functional units: one for regulating arousal and waking (the brainstem and its connections), one for receiving, analysing and storing information (the rear of the cortex: occipital, temporal and parietal lobes), and one for programming, regulating and checking activity (the frontal lobes). His methods appear in Higher Cortical Functions in Man (English edition 1966) and The Working Brain (1973), and their later standardisation as the Luria-Nebraska battery is described in Section 11.2. Pinel and Barnes credit Luria and his Soviet colleagues with the customised, flexible approach that spread to American research centres from the 1960s and now predominates in both the laboratory and the clinic.

Two American women gave the modern field its textbook and its method. Muriel Deutsch Lezak (1927 to 2021) published Neuropsychological Assessment in 1976, a book that, in its later editions written with colleagues, is still the standard reference of the field; it taught a generation to treat the assessment as the testing of hypotheses about a particular patient rather than the administration of a fixed list. Edith Kaplan (1924 to 2009), working at the Boston Veterans Administration Hospital, developed the Boston process approach, which shares Luria's interest in how a patient fails but builds it into standardised, scorable tests (Section 11.1.4).

Exam trap

Match the person to the idea. Broca (1861) and Wernicke (1874): localisation of language. Goldstein: abstract versus concrete attitude, and sorting tests with Scheerer. Halstead: biological intelligence (1947). Reitan: validated and extended Halstead's battery, blind interpretation, the four methods of inference. Luria: functional systems, three functional units, qualitative syndrome analysis. Lezak: the textbook Neuropsychological Assessment (1976). Kaplan: the Boston process approach. A favourite confusion is to credit the process approach to Luria: Luria's method was qualitative and Soviet; the process approach is Kaplan's, American, and built into standardised tests.

Three ways to build an assessment: fixed, flexible and process

The history leaves three ways of building an assessment, and every neuropsychologist works with some mixture of them. They differ in a simple question: who decides which tests the patient receives, and what is recorded about the answers?

In the fixed battery approach the answer is the battery itself. Every patient receives the same comprehensive set of tests, in the same way, scored against norms or cut-offs, often with a summary index such as Halstead's Impairment Index. The Halstead-Reitan and Luria-Nebraska batteries are the two classic fixed batteries (Section 11.2). The strengths are real. Nothing is missed because the examiner did not think to look for it; every patient's record can be compared with every other's, and with the same patient's record a year later; the large bodies of data collected with the same tests make research possible; and a fixed procedure is easy to defend in court, because nobody can say that the examiner chose tests to suit a conclusion. The weaknesses are equally plain. A full fixed battery takes many hours (Reitan's own battery took a day or more), it tires and sometimes distresses patients, it includes tests that the referral question does not need, and it may lack the tests that the question does need.

In the flexible battery approach, also called the hypothesis-testing approach, the clinician decides. A core set of tests is given to every patient to survey the main domains, and further tests are then chosen to fit the referral question and the first results. If the core tests show a memory problem, the clinician adds tests that can tell whether the trouble lies in learning, in keeping or in retrieving; if they show slowness and poor switching, tests of executive function follow. This is what Pinel and Barnes call the customised-test-battery approach, and it is how most clinicians now work; the NIMHANS battery, for example, is usually used as a set of domain-based tests from which the clinician selects (Section 11.3). It is efficient and tailored, and it can use the newest tests built on modern theories of memory and attention. But it depends heavily on the clinician's skill, it makes patients harder to compare with one another because each has had a different set of tests, and it carries a quiet danger: a clinician who forms a hypothesis early may choose only the tests likely to confirm it.

The process approach of Edith Kaplan and her Boston colleagues adds a third question: not only whether the patient got the answer right, but how he or she got there. The examiner records the order in which a drawing was made, the kinds of errors, the strategies used, the time taken over each item and the self-corrections, and may add steps such as offering a multiple-choice version of an item that the patient failed to answer freely, to see whether the knowledge is there but cannot be retrieved. Kaplan's group built this thinking into standardised tests, among them the WAIS-R as a Neuropsychological Instrument (1991), the California Verbal Learning Test (Section 11.4) and the Delis-Kaplan Executive Function System (2001).

A worked example shows why "how" matters. Imagine two invented patients who each copy the same complex line drawing and each earn exactly the same score, say 20 out of a possible 36. The first drew it piece by piece, getting many small details right but losing the overall shape, so that the parts never quite fitted together; this fragmented, piecemeal style is common after damage to the right hemisphere. The second drew the outline correctly at once but left out many of the internal details; a simplified copy with an intact overall shape is more typical of damage to the left hemisphere. The score says the two patients are equal. The process says they have different problems, different likely lesions and different needs in rehabilitation. The price of such richness is that observations of process are harder to score reliably and to norm than a simple total, and they take longer. In practice the three approaches blend: most clinicians give a flexible selection of standardised tests and watch the process as they go. Table 11.2 compares them.

Table 11.2 The three approaches to neuropsychological assessment compared.
ApproachAssociated withWhat the patient receivesStrengthsWeaknesses
Fixed batteryHalstead and Reitan; Golden's Luria-NebraskaThe same full set of tests, scored against norms or cut-offsComprehensive; comparable across patients and over time; good for research and courtLong and tiring; not tailored; may omit what the question needs
Flexible (hypothesis-testing) batteryLuria's tradition; Lezak; most clinicians todayA core set, then tests chosen for the question and the first resultsEfficient; tailored; uses modern theory-based testsDepends on skill; harder to compare patients; risk of confirming the first guess
Process approachEdith Kaplan, BostonStandardised tests with strategy, errors and sequence recordedExplains why a score is low; separates problems that look alikeHarder to score reliably and to norm; takes longer
Exam trap

The fixed batteries are the Halstead-Reitan and the Luria-Nebraska; the process approach is Kaplan's Boston approach; the flexible or hypothesis-testing approach is a core set plus chosen extras. Do not be thrown by the irony that the Luria-Nebraska is a fixed, standardised battery although Luria's own method was flexible and qualitative: Charles Golden and his colleagues standardised Luria's tasks, as Section 11.2 explains. And remember the one-sentence summary of the process approach: two patients with the same score may have failed for different reasons.

Reading a profile: Reitan's four methods of inference

Once the scores are in, how does a clinician reason from them to the brain? Reitan's answer, refined over decades of blind interpretation, was that no single kind of evidence is enough. He taught four methods of inference, each asking a different question of the same record, and insisted that they be used together, because each catches mistakes that the others make.

1. Level of performance. The first question is simply whether each score is worse than it should be. The level of performance method compares a score with a cut-off or with norms for healthy people. It is the most obvious method and the bluntest. Reitan's own 1955 data show why. With a cut-off at 51 or more errors, Halstead's Category Test (Section 11.2) misclassified 16 per cent of the brain-damaged patients and 18 per cent of the controls, far worse than the Impairment Index, which pooled the levels of many tests. Low scores have many causes besides brain damage, among them little schooling, depression, old age and poor effort, and some people with brain damage still score within normal limits on any one test.

2. Pattern of performance. The second question is how the scores relate to one another. The pattern of performance method looks at the shape of the profile: which abilities are relatively low and which relatively high, compared with the person's own other scores. A pattern can point where a level cannot. Fatigue, low education or a general illness tends to lower everything a little; a lesion tends to lower some abilities much more than others. Reitan's laboratory listed many such patterns as working hypotheses; one was that an outstandingly low score on the block-design subtest of the Wechsler scale, against otherwise even scores, suggested damage in the right parietal region.

3. Pathognomonic signs. The third question is whether the record contains any error that healthy people almost never make. A pathognomonic sign is a finding so rarely seen without the condition that its presence alone is strong evidence for it. Reitan's chief example was dysphasia, a disturbance of language: his laboratory stated that dysphasic symptoms, when present, provide a valid basis for inferring brain damage, and in his blind reports a definite dysphasia took precedence over every other indicator in placing a lesion in the left hemisphere. Others are a constructional dyspraxia, the inability to copy even a simple shape, and sensory suppression, in which a person feels a touch on either hand alone but, when both hands are touched at the same moment, reports only one. The method has a built-in asymmetry that you must remember: the presence of a sign is strong evidence, but its absence proves very little, and Reitan's own case reports include a mislocalised lesion that he blamed on leaning too heavily on a sign that was missing.

4. Right-left comparisons. The fourth question uses a fact of anatomy: each cerebral hemisphere controls movement on, and receives touch from, the opposite side of the body. A right-left comparison sets the two sides of the body against each other on the same task: tapping speed, grip strength, the time to complete a form board blindfolded, the recognition of touches and of numbers traced on the fingertips. Because each person serves as his own control, the comparison works even when norms are doubtful. A common rule of thumb in Reitan's tradition is that the preferred hand taps about 10 per cent faster than the other; a much larger difference, or a reversal, points to the hemisphere opposite the weaker side.

Now push one invented record through all four methods, as Reitan might have done with nothing but the scores in front of him. Omesh is a 41-year-old right-handed draughtsman with 14 years of education, referred after two seizures. His invented results are these.

  • Level. Omesh makes 78 errors on the Category Test, well past the 1955 cut-off of 51 or more, and his Impairment Index is 0.7 (about seven of every ten core scores in the impaired range; Section 11.2 explains the index). Conclusion: something is wrong, but the level says nothing about where.
  • Pattern. His vocabulary and verbal reasoning scores are in the high average range, as his education would predict, but his scores for copying drawings and building block patterns are far below the rest. A general cause such as fatigue or low schooling would have pulled everything down; this pattern singles out visuospatial and constructional abilities, which points towards the right hemisphere, especially its rear part.
  • Pathognomonic signs. He names, repeats, reads and writes without error, so there is no dysphasia (which, remember, proves little). But his copy of a simple geometric shape is broken and lopsided, with sides that fail to meet, a constructional dyspraxia; and when both hands are touched at once he repeatedly reports only the right, although he feels single touches on the left hand perfectly well, a suppression on the left side.
  • Right-left comparison. He taps 50 times in 10 seconds with his right hand and 34 times with his left. By the 10 per cent rule the left hand should manage about 50 − (10 per cent of 50) = 50 − 5 = 45 taps, so it is 45 − 34 = 11 taps short, and 11 ÷ 45 = 0.244, about 24 per cent below expectation. On the form board he takes 7.5 minutes with his right hand and then 9.0 minutes with his left. Because the left hand works second, on the same board, it normally benefits from the practice and finishes faster; Omesh's left hand was 1.5 minutes slower instead. Figure 11.2 draws both comparisons.

Put the four methods together and they converge. The level says the brain is not working normally; the pattern, the signs and the right-left comparisons all point to the right hemisphere; and the constructional dyspraxia and the tactile suppression suggest that the rear part of it, around the parietal lobe, is most affected. In Reitan's laboratory such a report would now have been checked against the neurologists' findings; today the clinician turns to the MRI report, and then to the question that the scan cannot answer: what does a draughtsman whose visuospatial and constructional abilities have been damaged need in order to return to work? Remember too that these numbers are invented for teaching; a real interpretation uses norms corrected for age, education and sex (Section 11.2). Table 11.3 summarises the four methods.

Table 11.3 Reitan's four methods of inference, applied to Omesh (invented example).
MethodQuestion it asksOmesh's findingMain caution
Level of performanceIs each score worse than norms or cut-offs?78 Category Test errors; Impairment Index 0.7Many causes besides brain damage lower scores
Pattern of performanceHow do the scores relate to one another?Verbal high average; copying and block building far lowerHealthy people also show some scatter
Pathognomonic signsAre there errors healthy people almost never make?Constructional dyspraxia; suppression on the left; no dysphasiaPresence is strong evidence; absence proves little
Right-left comparisonDo the two sides of the body differ?Left hand slow at tapping and on the form boardPeripheral injury to one hand also produces a difference
Exam trap

Reitan's four methods are level of performance, pattern of performance, pathognomonic signs and right-left comparisons; none of them is a "projective sign" or a personality index. In right-left comparisons the brain is contralateral: a slow or insensitive left hand implicates the right hemisphere. And "pathognomonic" means a sign whose presence nearly settles the matter, which is why its absence settles nothing.

Compared with whom? Normative and individual standards

A score means nothing on its own. Thirty-four taps, 78 errors or a memory score of 100 become informative only when they are compared with something, and Lezak's textbook names the two standards that a neuropsychologist can use. The normative comparison standard compares the person with a large group of healthy people of the same age, and often of the same education and sex: the norms of Chapter 5. This is a nomothetic comparison, from the Greek for "law-giving", the study of what holds for people in general; the philosopher Wilhelm Windelband (1848 to 1915) coined the pair of terms in 1894, and Gordon Allport (1897 to 1967) brought them into psychology in 1937. The individual comparison standard compares the person's present performance with his or her own earlier level of ability, the premorbid level ("before the illness"). This is an idiographic comparison, the study of the individual case. A third, related idea you have already used: comparing a person's scores with one another, as the pattern method did for Omesh, is an ipsative comparison, from the Latin ipse, "self".

Which standard is right? It depends on the ability. Some abilities are shared by almost every healthy adult, whatever his or her intelligence or schooling: knowing the year, naming a pen, copying a simple shape, recognising a familiar face. For these the normative standard is enough, because failure is abnormal in anyone. Other abilities vary widely among healthy people, among them vocabulary, reasoning, arithmetic and the amount that can be learned from one hearing of a list. For these, an average score can mean very different things in different people.

Ujwala, an invented example, shows the difference. She is a 54-year-old chartered accountant in Pune, a partner in her firm, who has started to make mistakes at work and to forget what clients told her the week before. On a memory test her standard score is 100, exactly the average for women of her age (standard scores with a mean of 100 and a standard deviation of 15 are explained in Chapter 5). By the normative standard she is normal. But everything known about her past (Section 11.1.7 shows how it is estimated) places her premorbid level at about 117. By the individual standard she has lost about 17 points, more than one standard deviation, and for a person of her ability an average memory is a decline. Figure 11.3 draws the two verdicts side by side. The error can run the other way as well: a man with four years of schooling may score below the average of norms built on better-educated people without having lost anything at all, which is why Indian batteries are normed separately by education (Section 11.3). Whether a gap of 17 points is larger than measurement error alone would produce is a question for the standard error of the difference (Chapter 3, Section 3.7.6).

In practice a good report uses both standards and says which it is using. The normative standard protects against calling ordinary scores abnormal; the individual standard protects against missing a real loss in an able person. The individual standard has one obvious difficulty: it needs an estimate of a level that nobody measured. That difficulty is the subject of the next subsection.

Exam trap

An RML 2025 item asks what the "ideometric approach" in neuropsychological assessment primarily focuses on. The term is not standard: you will not find it in Lezak or the other major textbooks, and it is probably a garbling of idiographic. The item uses it for the individual comparison standard, so answer (a) evaluating the patient's premorbid functioning and current status. Option (b), quantitative analysis of standardised test scores, is psychometric scoring, which every approach uses and which is not a standard of comparison. Option (c), comparison with normative population data, is the normative (nomothetic) standard, the opposite of what is asked. Option (d), emotional and psychological well-being, is mood assessment (Section 11.8), not a way of judging cognitive scores.

Estimating premorbid ability: demographics, best performance, hold tests and reading

Very few people are given a neuropsychological assessment before they fall ill. Occasionally there is a record of the earlier level: school marks, board examination results, a university degree, a selection test taken for a job or for the armed forces, or an assessment done years before for some other reason. When such records exist they are gold, though they rarely measure the same abilities as today's tests. Most of the time the earlier level has to be estimated, and over the past eighty years neuropsychologists have devised four main families of methods. Each has a typical way of going wrong, so a careful clinician uses more than one and looks for agreement. Premorbid ability is the level of intellectual and cognitive ability a person had before the onset of the illness or injury, and estimating it is what makes the individual comparison standard possible.

Demographic estimates. The simplest method uses what is known about the person's life. Years of education and the kind of work a person did are strongly related to measured intelligence, so an estimate can be made from them, either informally ("a chartered accountant who qualified at the first attempt was very probably of high average ability or above") or with a regression equation. A widely used equation of this kind for American adults was published by Barona, Reynolds and Chastain in 1984 for the WAIS-R; it combines age, sex, race, education, occupation and region of residence. The great advantage of a demographic estimate is that illness cannot touch it: a stroke does not change how many years a person spent in school. Its weaknesses follow from its nature. Equations of this kind pull every estimate towards the middle of the range, so they underestimate the most able people and overestimate the least able; they describe what is typical of a group, not what was true of this person; and they are built on particular populations, so an equation made in the United States says little about a person educated in a Kannada-medium village school. For Ujwala, with a commerce degree and the chartered accountancy qualification (about 17 years of education in all) and a career as a partner in a firm, an informal demographic estimate would be about 115.

The best-performance method. Lezak described a second method: take the person's best current scores as the best guide to the earlier level. The reasoning is that brain damage can lower an ability but almost never raises one, so the highest scores in the record are the ones least affected. Ujwala's best score is on vocabulary, a scaled score of 15. Scaled scores have a mean of 10 and a standard deviation of 3 (Chapter 6), so 15 lies (15 − 10) ÷ 3 = 5 ÷ 3 ≈ 1.67 standard deviations above the mean, which on the familiar scale with a mean of 100 and a standard deviation of 15 is 100 + (1.67 × 15) ≈ 100 + 25 = 125. The method's weakness is that it overestimates. Healthy people are not equally good at everything: their scores scatter, and anyone's single best score is, by definition, the luckiest point in the record. A best-performance estimate of 125 is probably a little too high for Ujwala, and the method is most useful as an upper limit.

Hold and don't-hold tests. The third method belongs to David Wechsler (1896 to 1981), and it is the one examiners ask about by name. Wechsler noticed, while building the age norms of his Wechsler-Bellevue scale (Book 1, Section 9.3.2), that some abilities decline much more slowly with age than others. Knowledge of general information and of word meanings holds up well; the ability to copy symbols quickly against the clock, or to repeat long strings of digits, falls away much faster. He reasoned that the abilities that resist ageing might also be the ones that resist the "deteriorative process" of brain disease and prolonged mental illness, so that a person's scores on them would preserve his earlier level, and that a comparison of the two kinds of subtest would therefore show how much he had lost. Wechsler set out this differential-test-score method in the third edition of The Measurement of Adult Intelligence (1944). The subtests that resist decline he called hold tests; those that decline he called don't-hold tests. Taking care to balance verbal and performance subtests in each group, he listed Information, Comprehension, Object Assembly, Picture Completion and Vocabulary as hold tests, and Digit Span, Arithmetic, Digit Symbol, Block Design and Similarities as don't-hold tests. When he revised the method for the WAIS in The Measurement and Appraisal of Adult Intelligence (1958), the four hold tests were Vocabulary, Information, Object Assembly and Picture Completion, and the four don't-hold tests Digit Span, Similarities, Digit Symbol and Block Design: these are the lists to learn.

Remember: Wechsler's deterioration index

Deterioration index = (Hold − Don't Hold) ÷ Hold, usually multiplied by 100 to give a percentage loss.

Hold is the sum of the scaled scores on the hold tests (Vocabulary, Information, Object Assembly, Picture Completion) and Don't Hold the sum on the don't-hold tests (Digit Span, Similarities, Digit Symbol, Block Design). Wechsler also gave the ratio form, Don't Hold ÷ Hold × 100, which in 1944 he called the deterioration quotient; the two carry the same information, because the quotient is simply 100 minus the percentage loss. In the 1958 WAIS book he gave the name deterioration quotient to the percentage-loss formula itself, and most textbooks now use the two names for the same thing. In 1944 he suggested that a loss more than 10 per cent greater than the normal loss for the person's age indicates possible deterioration, and more than 20 per cent greater definite deterioration. For the WAIS, whose age-corrected scores already allow for normal ageing, he judged a loss of more than about 15 to 20 per cent significant.

Work it through for Ujwala, with invented scaled scores that are already corrected for her age (the WAIS version of the method works with age-corrected scores, so the normal decline with age has been allowed for before the comparison is made). Her hold tests: Vocabulary 15, Information 14, Picture Completion 13 and Object Assembly 12, so Hold = 15 + 14 + 13 + 12 = 54. Her don't-hold tests: Digit Span 10, Similarities 12, Digit Symbol 8 and Block Design 9, so Don't Hold = 10 + 12 + 8 + 9 = 39. Then:

Deterioration index = (54 − 39) ÷ 54 = 15 ÷ 54 = 0.2778, which multiplied by 100 is 27.8 per cent (rounded to one decimal place).

The quotient form gives 39 ÷ 54 × 100 = 72.2, the same result seen from the other side (100 − 27.8 = 72.2). In plain words: Ujwala's scores on the abilities that usually resist illness are about 28 per cent higher than her scores on the abilities that usually give way, a gap beyond Wechsler's 20 per cent mark for definite deterioration. Taken alone, the index would say that she has lost a good deal of her former efficiency.

Why, then, is the method now mostly of historical interest? Because both of its assumptions are shaky. The hold tests are not immune: vocabulary and general information do fall as a dementia advances, so the index underestimates loss in exactly the patients who have lost most. And the don't-hold tests fall for many reasons besides brain disease: anxiety, depression, slowness, fatigue, poor effort and little schooling all lower digit span, speeded coding and block design. Reitan tested the method against his own and found that Halstead's Impairment Index separated brain-damaged patients from controls far better than any Wechsler measure, the hold tests, the don't-hold tests and Wechsler's deterioration ratio included; and in 1984 Dano Leli and Susan Filskov, opening a study in the Journal of Clinical Psychology, summed up four decades of work: none of the deterioration formulas and ratios of that era, Wechsler's 1958 index included, had been shown empirically to be useful. What the index really measures is a fall in mental efficiency from any cause. That is not a defect for the purpose Wechsler intended: he wrote that the method would be most useful in organic brain cases but could also be applied in schizophrenia, and to appraise the effects of shock treatment, which is why the examiners' phrasing, the impact of brain or psychiatric illness on cognitive and intellectual ability, is a fair description of it.

Reading tests: the National Adult Reading Test. The fourth method is the most successful, and it comes from a clever observation about reading. English is full of irregular words, words whose pronunciation cannot be worked out from their spelling by the ordinary rules: "yacht" and "colonel" are the examples Book 1 uses (Section 8.9.6) to explain the dual-route model of reading, and a reader can only say them correctly if he already knows them as whole words. So the ability to read irregular words aloud is really a test of vocabulary, built up over a lifetime of reading and closely related to general intelligence. Better still, it is an old, overlearned skill that requires no new learning, no speed and almost no reasoning, so it survives the early stages of a dementia when memory and reasoning are already failing.

Key study: Nelson and O'Connell and the reading test that dementia spares (1978)

The question. The British psychologist Hazel E. Nelson had shown with her colleague McKenna in 1975 that current reading ability was useful in assessing dementia. Could a reading test be designed specifically to estimate the intelligence a patient had before the dementia began? The method. With her colleague O'Connell she built a new test of 50 words chosen to assess familiarity with words rather than the ability to decode them phonetically: each word was irregular enough that intelligent guesswork alone would not produce the right pronunciation. They gave it to patients with cortical atrophy (shrinkage of the brain's outer layer, as in dementia) and to a control group. The result. The test predicted intelligence more accurately than the best word-reading list then available, the Schonell Graded Word Reading Test, and the patients' reading of the words did not seem to be significantly affected by the dementing process. The conclusion. The reading score could provide an accurate estimate of premorbid intelligence in such patients (Cortex, 1978, where it was called the New Adult Reading Test). The sequel. Nelson's manual of 1982 renamed it the National Adult Reading Test (NART), and it became one of the most widely used premorbid estimates and the model for later reading tests.

The NART is scored by counting the words read wrongly, and the error count is turned into an estimate with a regression equation. Nelson's 1982 equation for the Full Scale IQ of the original WAIS is:

Predicted Full Scale IQ = 127.7 − (0.826 × number of errors)

Here 127.7 is the IQ predicted for a person who makes no errors, and 0.826 is the number of IQ points the estimate falls for each word read wrongly. Suppose, in an invented exercise, that Ujwala reads aloud a list of 50 irregular English words and makes 13 errors. Then 0.826 × 13 = 10.738, and 127.7 − 10.738 = 116.962, which rounds to an estimated premorbid IQ of 117. Like any regression estimate it is a band rather than a point: the published standard error of estimate for this equation is between 7 and 8 IQ points, so the honest statement is "about 117, give or take 7 or 8 points". (The arithmetic is real but the application is not: Nelson's equation was built on a sample of British adults tested with the original WAIS, and it would need fresh validation before it could be trusted for an Indian accountant.)

The reading method has clear limits. It assumes that the person is fluent in the language of the test; it is invalid in a person who is dyslexic, aphasic or cannot see the words; and once a dementia is moderate or severe, reading declines too, so the estimate falls with it. It also shares the tendency of all regression estimates to pull extreme people towards the middle. Successors include the revised NART of Nelson and Willison (1991), North American versions, and the Wechsler Test of Adult Reading (2001) and Test of Premorbid Functioning (2009), which were normed together with the Wechsler scales so that reading can predict them directly. Many clinicians now combine a reading score with demographic information in a single equation, getting the stability of the one and the individuality of the other.

For Ujwala the methods agree well: about 115 from her history, about 117 from reading, 125 as an upper limit from her best score, and a deterioration index pointing to a substantial fall in efficiency. A clinician would report her premorbid level as "high average, about 117", the figure used in Figure 11.3. Table 11.4 sets the methods side by side.

Table 11.4 Methods of estimating premorbid ability, applied to Ujwala (invented example).
MethodHow it worksUjwalaMain weakness
RecordsSchool, examination or earlier test resultsNone availableRarely exist; seldom measure the same abilities
Demographic estimateEducation and occupation, informally or by equationAbout 115Pulls estimates to the middle; equations built on other populations
Best performanceHighest current scores taken as the earlier level125 (vocabulary)Overestimates, because healthy scores scatter
Hold versus don't holdResistant subtests compared with sensitive onesLoss of 27.8 per centHold tests also fall; don't-hold tests fall for many reasons
Reading irregular words (NART)Error count converted to IQ by an equationAbout 117Needs fluent reading of the test language; fails in aphasia and later dementia
Indian context

Almost every premorbid method was built in Britain or the United States. An English irregular-word test assumes a fluency in English that many Indian adults do not have, and it cannot simply be translated: Indian scripts such as Kannada and Devanagari are written in aksharas that are read much more regularly than English spelling (Book 1, Section 8.9.6), so they offer few "irregular words" to test. Years of schooling also mean very different things in different schools, and American demographic equations do not transfer. Indian clinicians therefore lean on norms stratified by age and education (Section 11.3), on school and examination records where they exist, on the person's work history and on the accounts of family members.

Exam trap

"The impact of psychiatric illness on one's cognitive and intellectual abilities can be estimated on the basis of..." (IHBAS 2020): the answer is the deterioration quotient (also called the deterioration index or mental deterioration index), Wechsler's comparison of hold and don't-hold subtests. The distractors come from other fields. A mediation index is a statistic from mediation analysis, which asks whether a third variable carries the effect of a cause on an outcome (the Research Methodology book). The sense of coherence is Aaron Antonovsky's (1923 to 1994) idea, from his work on salutogenesis, of a person's sense that life is comprehensible, manageable and meaningful (Book 1, Section 11.10.3): a predictor of health, not a measure of cognitive loss. A theory of mind index would concern social cognition, the ability to infer other people's beliefs and intentions (the Developmental Psychology book), and estimates nothing about intellectual decline. Learn the two lists too: hold, Vocabulary, Information, Object Assembly, Picture Completion; don't hold, Digit Span, Similarities, Digit Symbol, Block Design.

Organic or functional? An old question reframed

For much of the twentieth century the commonest referral to a psychologist in a psychiatric hospital asked, in effect, "organic or functional?" Was the patient's trouble caused by a demonstrable disease of the brain, or was it a "functional" disorder such as schizophrenia, depression or a neurosis, in which no such disease could be found? Book 3, Section 10.8.1 tells the history of the two words and of their retirement from the diagnostic manuals. Here the point is why the question, put that way, could not be answered by tests.

There are three reasons. The first you have already met: brain damage is not one thing, so there is no single "organic" pattern for a test to detect, which is why the single tests of organicity failed. The second is that the functional disorders themselves impair performance. People with schizophrenia have real and often substantial cognitive deficits in attention, memory and executive function; depression slows thinking and weakens effortful memory; anxiety disrupts attention. A low score on a test sensitive to brain damage can therefore come from an illness that the old scheme called functional, which is exactly why, as Pinel and Barnes note, the batteries that separate neurological patients from healthy people so well separate them from psychiatric patients much less well. The third is that the premise behind the dichotomy is false. Schizophrenia and the mood disorders are disorders of the brain too, as twin studies, imaging and the drugs that treat them show, and a person can have both kinds of trouble at once: a depressed man can also be in the earliest stage of Alzheimer's disease, and a heavy drinker may also have had a head injury.

So the modern question is framed differently. Instead of a yes-or-no verdict, the neuropsychologist describes the profile of abilities, asks whether it matches a recognised pattern (a severe, isolated loss of new learning; a frontal pattern of poor planning and switching; a lateralised pattern like Omesh's), asks how much of it is explained by mood, effort, medication, education or the testing conditions, and states what it means for the person's daily life. "Is he organic?" becomes "Is his pattern of memory loss the kind seen in Alzheimer's disease, the kind seen in depression, or a mixture, and what should be done next?" That question can be answered, and Section 11.1.10 answers it for one patient.

Exam trap

If a paper asks what the early single tests such as the Bender-Gestalt (1938) and the Benton Visual Retention Test (1946) were used for, the answer is screening for organic brain damage ("organicity"), as distinct from functional psychiatric illness. If it asks why the single-test approach failed, the answer is that brain damage is not a unitary condition: different lesions produce different deficits, and psychiatric illnesses also lower test scores.

Is the person doing their best? Effort and performance validity

An ability test measures what a person can do only if he or she is trying to do it. Every score in a neuropsychological report rests on that assumption, and in some settings it cannot be taken for granted. Fatigue, pain, depression and plain lack of interest can all reduce effort; so can a wish to appear more impaired than one is, in order to win compensation after an accident, to obtain a disability benefit or to escape a criminal charge. The deliberate production or gross exaggeration of symptoms for an external reward is malingering, which DSM-5-TR treats as a condition that may be a focus of clinical attention rather than as a mental disorder (Book 3, Section 7.9.6). Neuropsychologists therefore build checks on effort into their assessments. A performance validity test checks whether a person's effort on ability tests was adequate; a symptom validity test checks whether the symptoms a person reports are exaggerated, as the validity scales of the MMPI-2 do for questionnaires (Chapter 9).

Performance validity tests work by one of two kinds of logic. The first is a test that looks difficult but is in fact easy, so easy that people with genuine brain injury usually pass it. The best known is the Test of Memory Malingering (TOMM) of Tom N. Tombaugh (1996). The person is shown 50 line drawings of everyday objects one at a time and then, for each, a pair of drawings, and must point to the one seen before; there are two such learning trials and an optional retention trial later. Despite its name it is not really a test of memory, because the task is far easier than it seems. Scoring below 45 out of 50 on the second trial, that is, making more than five errors, is the usual sign that effort was inadequate, a level that patients with real head injuries rarely fall below.

The second kind of logic is more powerful, and it rests on simple probability. In a test where every item offers two choices, a person who knows nothing and merely guesses will be right about half the time. On 50 two-choice items, pure guessing gives about 50 × 0.5 = 25 correct. A person who scores far below 25 is doing worse than chance, and the only way to do worse than chance is to know the right answer and choose the other one. How far below is far enough? For pure guessing on 50 items the standard deviation of the score is √(50 × 0.5 × 0.5) = √12.5 ≈ 3.54 (this formula for the spread of chance scores, the binomial distribution, is taught in the Research Methodology book). A score of 15 is 25 − 15 = 10 points below the chance level, and 10 ÷ 3.54 ≈ 2.83 standard deviations below it. The exact probability of guessing one's way to 15 or fewer is about 0.0033, roughly 3 chances in 1,000. A score that low is very hard to explain except as deliberate. This is below-chance performance, and on a forced-choice test (one in which the person must choose an answer on every item) it is the strongest evidence of feigning that testing can provide.

Exam trap

A score at chance means the person got nothing right beyond guessing, which can happen in genuine severe impairment such as advanced dementia; a score below chance means the person must have known the answers to avoid them. Failing a performance validity test shows that the other scores cannot be trusted; it does not by itself prove malingering, which is a clinical judgement that weighs the test results together with the incentive, the history and the consistency of the person's behaviour. And the TOMM, despite its name, measures effort, not memory.

From referral to report: one assessment, step by step

Everything in this section comes together in a single assessment. Follow Vipul, an invented patient, along the eight steps of Figure 11.1.

Step 1: the question. Vipul is a 67-year-old retired headmaster from Dharwad. His wife died ten months ago. His son brings him to a psychiatrist because for about eight months Vipul has been forgetting conversations, asking the same question twice in an evening and leaving the newspaper unread; he sleeps badly and has lost weight. The psychiatrist's note asks: "Early dementia or depression?" A short telephone call turns this into the real question. The psychiatrist needs to know whether to treat a depression first and wait, or to tell the family to plan for a dementia; the son, who now handles the household money, wants to know whether his father can still look after his own pension and bank account.

Step 2: history. The interview with Vipul, and separately with his son as informant, establishes that Vipul has about 16 years of education, taught English and mathematics for decades and ran a school for twenty years. There is no history of head injury, stroke or heavy drinking. The physician's blood tests for thyroid function and vitamin B12 are normal, and the MRI report describes changes usual for his age. One detail matters: a sleeping tablet of the benzodiazepine type was started three months ago, and drugs of this kind can slow thinking and weaken memory.

Step 3: choosing the tests. The question decides the selection. Orientation and a brief screen come first; then attention and processing speed; then memory, with a word-list test that separates free recall, recall after a delay and recognition, because that comparison is the most useful single clue in telling depression from Alzheimer's disease (Section 11.4 explains why); then naming and verbal fluency, copying and clock drawing, and two tests of executive function. A depression scale for older adults is added (Section 11.8), along with a performance validity check and a premorbid estimate from his education, his career and his reading.

Step 4: testing. Vipul is tested over two mornings of about ninety minutes each, wearing his reading glasses and his hearing aid, and in English and Kannada as he prefers. The examiner notes, as the process approach would want, that he answers slowly, says "I can't" quickly and often does better when gently encouraged to try again.

Step 5: scoring. Against age and education norms, his attention and speed scores are in the low average range and his verbal fluency is low. His free recall of the word list is poor, but when he is given the words to recognise he picks out nearly all of them, and he forgets little between the immediate and the delayed trials. Naming and copying are normal. His depression score is well into the depressed range, and he passes the performance validity check. His premorbid level is estimated as high average, so against his own past the falls in speed and free recall are larger than the norms alone suggest.

Step 6: interpretation. The pattern is one of slowness and of difficulty getting information out rather than of failing to store it: when recognition removes the need for effortful retrieval, his memory is close to normal. That pattern fits the cognitive effects of depression, perhaps added to by the sleeping tablet, better than the pattern of early Alzheimer's disease, in which newly learned material is typically lost over a delay and recognition does not rescue it (Section 11.4, and Book 3, Section 10.11.6 on pseudodementia). It is not proof, because depression in later life is sometimes the first sign of a dementia, and the report says so.

Step 7: report and feedback. The written report answers the two questions in plain words. It recommends treating the depression, reviewing the sleeping tablet with his doctor, and retesting in about six months. On the money question, it notes that Vipul understood and could explain his pension and bank arrangements, and suggests that he continue to manage them with his son's help for now. The neuropsychologist also explains the findings to Vipul and his son in person.

Step 8: review. Six months later, with his mood much improved, Vipul is retested. If his scores have risen by more than practice and measurement error would explain (the reliable change index of Chapter 3, Section 3.7.6), the depression account is supported; if they have stayed low or fallen despite a better mood, the team must think again about an early dementia. Either way, the first assessment has done its job as a baseline.

Clinical link

The feedback session is part of the assessment, not an afterthought. Patients and families come wanting to know "Is it dementia?", "Will he get worse?" and "What can we do?", and a report full of percentiles answers none of these. A good feedback session describes strengths before weaknesses, explains what the results mean for everyday life (for Vipul, that his memory is likely to improve as his mood lifts, and that lists and routines will help in the meantime), is honest about uncertainty, and checks that the family has understood. Interviewers for clinical psychology programmes often ask how you would give difficult results; this is the shape of a good answer.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. Neuropsychology is the study of the relationship between the brain and .
  2. The question that names the decision an assessment must inform is the question.
  3. Halstead called the brain-dependent adaptive abilities that IQ tests missed intelligence.
  4. The approach of Edith Kaplan that records how a patient reaches an answer is the Boston approach.
  5. Reitan's four methods of inference are level of performance, pattern of performance, signs and right-left comparisons.
  6. A right-handed man taps 60 times in 10 seconds with his right hand. By the 10 per cent rule, his left hand should tap about times.
  7. Comparing a patient's current scores with her own estimated earlier level uses the comparison standard.
  8. In the WAIS version of Wechsler's method, Vocabulary, Information, Object Assembly and Picture Completion are the tests.
  9. A patient's hold tests sum to 60 and his don't-hold tests to 48. His deterioration index is per cent.
  10. On a test of 50 two-choice items, pure guessing gives about correct, so a score of 12 is below-chance performance.

Answers: (1) behaviour; (2) referral; (3) biological; (4) process; (5) pathognomonic; (6) 54 (10 per cent of 60 is 6, and 60 − 6 = 54); (7) individual; (8) hold; (9) 20 ((60 − 48) ÷ 60 = 12 ÷ 60 = 0.20, that is, 20 per cent); (10) 25 (50 × 0.5).

11.2 The Fixed Batteries: Halstead-Reitan and Luria-NebraskaMedium yield · 5 Qs

Suppose you were asked to build, from nothing, a set of tasks that could tell a doctor whether a person's brain was damaged, without opening the skull and without a scanner. Which tasks would you choose? You would want some that are easy for healthy people and hard for injured ones. You would want to test many abilities, because damage in different places breaks different things. You would want each task given in exactly the same way to every person, so that scores could be compared, and you would want some tasks that can be done with the right hand and the left hand separately, because each side of the body is run by the opposite side of the brain. Two famous answers to this problem dominated the second half of the twentieth century. The first grew out of the laboratory of Ward Halstead in Chicago and the decades of work of his student Ralph Reitan, whose story opens this chapter. The second began with the bedside methods of the Russian neuropsychologist Alexander Luria and was turned into a standardised battery in Nebraska. Both are fixed batteries in the sense explained in Section 11.1: every patient receives the same tests, in the same order and the same way, and the scores are compared with the same norms. This section takes each battery apart test by test (what the person does, what the examiner scores, and what a poor score tends to mean), then shows how the scores are summarised, what each battery is good for, and why both drew fierce criticism.

From "biological intelligence" to a clinical battery

The opening of this chapter told how Ward C. Halstead set out to measure what he called biological intelligence, and how Ralph M. Reitan (1922 to 2014) tested his measures against patients and controls and learned to read a profile of scores almost like an X-ray. That story is not repeated here. What matters for this section is what the two men left behind. Halstead meant something different from the intelligence measured by Binet-type tests (Chapter 6). An IQ test asks how much a person knows and how well they reason compared with others of the same age; Halstead wanted to measure the brain's basic capacity to adapt, to grasp a new principle, to organise information from the senses and to act on it, a capacity he believed depended heavily on the frontal lobes. His book Brain and Intelligence: A Quantitative Study of the Frontal Lobes (1947) described more than two dozen procedures as possible "quantitative indicators" of this biological intelligence. The theory about the frontal lobes has not survived well; what survived is the observation that several of his tests are very sensitive to brain damage of many kinds.

Reitan's contribution was to turn a research collection into a clinical instrument. Working at Indiana University in Indianapolis, he kept the measures that best separated brain-damaged patients from controls, dropped those that did not, and added tests of his own: the Trail Making Test, an aphasia screening test, a sensory-perceptual examination, a test of grip strength and an examination of which hand, eye and foot the person prefers. The result is the Halstead-Reitan Neuropsychological Battery (often abbreviated HRNB or HRB). With Deborah Wolfson he set out its theory and interpretation in a manual first published in 1985 (second edition 1993). The adult battery is meant for people aged 15 and over. Two children's versions exist: the Halstead Neuropsychological Test Battery for Older Children, for ages 9 to 14, and the Reitan-Indiana Neuropsychological Test Battery for Children, for ages 5 to 8, with shorter or simpler forms of the adult tests. In routine practice an intelligence scale (the WAIS, Chapter 6) and a personality inventory (the MMPI, Chapter 9) are almost always given alongside the battery as allied procedures; they help the interpretation, but they are not part of the battery itself.

A test battery is simply a set of separate tests given together, each yielding its own score, chosen so that together they sample the abilities you care about. Figure 11.4 shows the Halstead-Reitan battery grouped by what each test mainly measures. Notice two things before you read on. First, the battery is heavy on doing things with the hands, against the clock: placing blocks, tapping, drawing lines between circles.
