Second, several tests are done with each hand separately, which is what allows the battery to compare the two sides of the body. Both features will matter when we come to its strengths and weaknesses.

Exam trap

Halstead built the tests and the idea of biological intelligence (1947); Reitan validated them, added Trail Making, the aphasia screening test and the sensory-perceptual examination, and turned the set into a clinical battery. The WAIS and the MMPI are often given with the battery, but an option that says the battery "is the WAIS plus the MMPI" is wrong: they are allied procedures, not its tests.

Halstead's core tests, one by one

The Category Test. Picture the original apparatus. The person sits in front of a small screen on which a picture is projected: a few geometric figures, lines or shapes. Below the screen is a panel with four keys numbered 1 to 4. The instruction is simply that each picture suggests one of the numbers, and the task is to press the right one. Nobody says what the rule is. If the answer is right a pleasant bell rings; if it is wrong a buzzer sounds. That is the only help the person ever gets. Within each subtest one principle holds throughout, and the person who works it out can answer every remaining item correctly. To see the idea, take an invented item (not one from the test): if a card showed three small circles and the hidden rule were "count the shapes", the answer would be 3, and a person who discovered the rule from a few bells and buzzers could then apply it to every new card. The adult test has 208 items in seven subtests; Halstead's original version was longer. The principle changes from one subtest to the next, so the person must also give up an old rule and look for a new one, and the seventh subtest mixes items from the earlier ones to see whether the principles have been remembered. The score is simply the number of errors. Because projectors and keys are awkward, booklet and computer versions are now common, and children's versions are shorter (168 items for older children and 80 for young children).

What does the Category Test measure? It is the battery's test of abstract reasoning and concept formation: noticing what several different pictures have in common, forming a hypothesis, testing it against feedback and changing it when it fails. It needs sustained attention and learning from experience too. It is often described as the single most sensitive test in the battery to brain damage in general, which also tells you its weakness: because so many kinds of damage raise the error count, a poor score says little about where the damage is. It is also long, about an hour for most adults and longer for a severely impaired patient. You will meet a close cousin in Section 11.6: the Wisconsin Card Sorting Test also asks the person to discover an unstated rule from "right" and "wrong" and to shift when the rule changes.

The Tactual Performance Test. This test borrowed a much older piece of equipment, the form board, a wooden board with cut-out spaces into which shaped blocks (a star, a cross, a triangle and so on) must be fitted. The French physician Edouard Seguin (1812 to 1880) had used such boards to train children with intellectual disability, and Henry Herbert Goddard (1866 to 1957) later used one as a timed test, which is why it is often called the Seguin-Goddard form board. Halstead's change was the blindfold. The person is blindfolded before ever seeing the board, and then places the ten blocks of the adult board (six for children under 15) into their spaces, first using only the preferred hand, then only the other hand, and finally both hands together; each trial is timed. Then the board and blocks are hidden, the blindfold is removed, and the person is asked, without having been warned in advance, to draw the board from memory, showing the shapes in their correct places.

Three scores come out of this one test, and all three count in the Impairment Index. Total time is the sum of the three trials. Memory is the number of shapes drawn correctly. Localisation is the number of shapes drawn in roughly the right position on the board. Because the person did not know they would be asked to draw the board, the memory and localisation scores measure incidental memory, what was learned without any intention to learn. The test as a whole draws on touch and the sense of limb position, fine movement, spatial problem solving and learning across trials. It also gives a right-left comparison. The second trial is normally quicker than the first, because the person has already explored the board, and the third is quicker still. If the trial with the non-preferred hand is no faster, or is slower, than the trial with the preferred hand, that hand's performance is relatively poor, and the hemisphere on the opposite side becomes a suspect.

The Seashore Rhythm Test. Carl Emil Seashore (1866 to 1949), a psychologist at the University of Iowa, built tests of musical talent early in the twentieth century, and Halstead took one of them for his battery. The person hears 30 pairs of short rhythmic patterns of beats, presented from a recording in three sets of ten, and marks each pair "same" or "different". The score is the number correct (often converted to a ranked score). The task sounds musical, but it is mainly a test of sustained auditory attention and concentration together with the perception of non-verbal sound patterns: the pairs come one after another at a steady pace, and a person whose attention drifts for a few seconds simply misses items. Poor scores occur with damage on either side of the brain, so the test is not a good pointer to one hemisphere.

The Speech-Sounds Perception Test. Here the person hears 60 spoken nonsense words, each built around an "ee" sound, and for each one marks, from a short row of printed spellings, the spelling that matches what was heard. The score is the number of errors. The test needs sustained attention, accurate hearing of speech sounds, and the ability to match a heard sound to letters on a page. That last requirement is worth noticing: a person who reads poorly, or who has had little schooling in the Roman alphabet, can fail it for reasons that have nothing to do with the brain. Section 11.3 returns to this problem.

The Finger Tapping Test (also called the Finger Oscillation Test). The person rests the hand flat and taps a small key, attached to a mechanical or electronic counter, with the index finger as fast as possible for ten seconds. Trials are repeated with each hand (until five consecutive trials fall within five taps of one another, usually five to ten trials per hand) and those five are averaged. This is a test of simple fine motor speed, and it is the battery's classic lateralising test, meaning a test that can suggest which side of the brain is affected. In right-handed people the preferred hand is normally about 10 per cent faster than the other. If the preferred hand is no faster than the other, or a hand is much slower than expected, the motor areas of the opposite hemisphere become a suspect, provided nothing is wrong with the hand itself.

The Trail Making Test, which Reitan took from the Army Individual Test Battery of 1944 and added to the battery, asks the person to draw a line connecting numbered circles in order (Part A) and then circles that alternate between numbers and letters (Part B), as quickly as possible. It is taught in full, with its scoring, in Section 11.6. Table 11.5 brings the tests of the battery together.

Table 11.5 The Halstead-Reitan battery: what the person does, what is scored, and what the test is most sensitive to.
TestWhat the person doesMain scoresMainly measures
Category TestPresses one of four keys for each picture; bell or buzzer after each answer; 208 items in 7 subtestsErrorsAbstraction, concept formation, learning from feedback; most sensitive to damage in general, poor at localising
Tactual Performance TestBlindfolded, fits blocks into a form board with the preferred hand, the other hand, then both; then draws the board from memoryTotal time; memory; localisationTactile and spatial problem solving, motor skill, incidental memory; right-left comparison
Seashore Rhythm TestJudges 30 pairs of rhythms "same" or "different"Number correctSustained auditory attention, non-verbal sound perception
Speech-Sounds Perception TestHears 60 nonsense words; marks the matching printed spellingErrorsAttention, speech-sound perception, matching sound to print
Finger Tapping TestTaps a key with the index finger for 10 seconds, each handMean taps per handFine motor speed; the classic lateralising test
Trail Making Test A and BConnects circles: numbers only (A), numbers and letters alternating (B)Time (and errors)Visual search, speed, set-shifting (Section 11.6)
Reitan-Indiana Aphasia Screening TestNames, reads, writes, spells, repeats, calculates, copies simple shapesSigns of specific deficitsAphasia, alexia, agraphia, constructional difficulty
Reitan-Klove Sensory-Perceptual ExaminationReports touches, sounds and sights on one or both sides; names touched fingers; reads numbers traced on the fingertipsErrors on each sideSensory loss, extinction on double stimulation, finger agnosia
Grip strengthSqueezes a hand dynamometer, each handKilograms per handMotor strength; right-left comparison
Lateral Dominance ExaminationShows which hand, eye and foot are preferred on simple tasksPreference patternHandedness, so that the right-left comparisons are read correctly
Exam trap

The "classic subtests" of the Halstead-Reitan are the Category Test, the Tactual Performance Test, the Seashore Rhythm Test, the Speech-Sounds Perception Test, the Finger Tapping Test and the Trail Making Test, with the aphasia and sensory-perceptual examinations. The BDI and the Hamilton Rating Scale for Depression (HAM-D) are depression rating scales (Section 11.8); the Bender-Gestalt is a single visuomotor copying test (Section 11.5), not this battery. The Rorschach is a projective personality test (Chapter 10) and is never part of the Halstead-Reitan, so in an "all EXCEPT" item it is the odd one out. Do not confuse the Tactual Performance Test (blindfolded form board, Halstead) with the Benton tests (Section 11.5) or with the Kohs Block Design test of Bhatia's battery (Chapter 6).

The tests Reitan added: language, the senses, strength and handedness

Halstead's tests are good at detecting that something is wrong, but they say little about language or about simple sensation, two areas where damage to one side of the brain leaves very clear marks. Reitan filled these gaps with short examinations that a neurologist would recognise.

The Reitan-Indiana Aphasia Screening Test is Reitan's modification of the Halstead-Wepman Aphasia Screening Test, which Halstead had published with Joseph Wepman in 1949. In a few minutes the person is asked to name common objects from drawings, to spell, read and write short words, to repeat words and phrases, to do simple arithmetic, to follow simple instructions and to copy a few simple geometric shapes. The test is not scored by adding up a total. Instead each failure is noted as a sign of a specific deficit: an inability to name an object is a sign of dysnomia, a gross spelling or writing failure a sign of dysgraphia, a badly distorted copy of a shape a sign of constructional difficulty, and so on. This is the "pathognomonic sign" method of inference described in Section 11.1: a few such errors are rare in healthy adults, so even one or two carry weight. The aphasias themselves are described in Book 1, Section 8.9.6 and Book 3, Section 10.8.3.

The Reitan-Klove Sensory-Perceptual Examination, developed by Reitan with the Norwegian neuropsychologist Hallgrim Klove, tests touch, hearing and vision on each side of the body. Its most important part is double simultaneous stimulation. The examiner touches the person's right hand, then the left, then both at once (and does the same with sounds near each ear and with small movements in each half of the visual field). Many patients with damage to one hemisphere, particularly the parietal lobe, report a single touch on either side correctly but, when both sides are touched together, report only the touch on the side opposite the healthy hemisphere. The stimulus on the other side is said to be extinguished, or suppressed. The examination also asks the person, with eyes closed, to say which finger has been touched (failure suggests finger agnosia), to identify numbers traced on the fingertips, and to recognise small shapes placed in the palm by touch alone.

Grip strength is measured with a hand dynamometer, a spring device with a dial that the person squeezes as hard as possible, in alternating trials with each hand. As with tapping, the preferred hand is normally somewhat stronger. The Lateral Dominance Examination records which hand the person uses for writing and other everyday actions, which eye they use to sight through a tube, and which foot they kick with. It may look trivial, but every right-left comparison in the battery depends on it: a left hand that is slower than the right means something quite different in a left-handed person.

Clinical link

The right-left comparisons are the most direct link between the battery and the anatomy of the brain, because the motor and sensory pathways cross. A clear pattern (the left hand slower at tapping, weaker in grip and clumsier on the Tactual Performance Test, with extinction of touches on the left) points towards the right hemisphere. But before you write "right hemisphere dysfunction" in a report, look at the hands and ask about them. An old fracture of the wrist, arthritis, a nerve injury or a plaster cast slows a hand without any help from the brain, and a good examiner rules these out first. This is one reason why Reitan insisted on combining several methods of inference (Section 11.1) rather than trusting a single score.

Exam trap

The aphasia screening test is the Reitan-Indiana (from the Halstead-Wepman test of 1949); the sensory examination is the Reitan-Klove. Extinction on double simultaneous stimulation is a sensory-perceptual finding, not a memory or attention test score. The test that "shows whether a person makes more errors on one side of the body" in a past item is Benton's Finger Localization Test (Section 11.5), but the same logic runs through the whole Halstead-Reitan: tapping, grip, the Tactual Performance Test and the sensory examination all compare the two sides.

Summarising the battery: the Halstead Impairment Index and its successors

A full Halstead-Reitan examination yields dozens of numbers. How do you turn them into an answer to the first question a referring doctor asks, "Is this brain impaired or not?" Halstead's answer was the simplest summary imaginable. For each of his key scores he set a cut-off, the point beyond which healthy people rarely fall; then he counted how many of the patient's scores lay beyond their cut-offs, and expressed the count as a proportion. This proportion is the Halstead Impairment Index. Halstead's original index was built from ten scores. Two of his measures, a critical flicker fusion test (the rate at which a flickering light appears to become steady, which gave two scores) and a test of time sense, added little and were dropped, which leaves the seven scores used today: the Category Test, the three Tactual Performance Test scores (total time, memory and localisation), the Seashore Rhythm Test, the Speech-Sounds Perception Test and the Finger Tapping Test (preferred hand).

Remember

Halstead Impairment Index = (number of the seven core scores in the impaired range) ÷ 7

The numerator is how many of the seven scores fall beyond their cut-offs; 7 is the number of scores counted. The index runs from 0.0 (no score impaired) to 1.0 (all seven impaired) and is reported to one decimal place. By the traditional rule, an index of 0.5 or more suggests brain damage, and some guides treat 0.4 as borderline. Some reference works add a severity grading (one gives 0.0 to 0.2 normal, 0.3 to 0.4 mild, 0.5 to 0.7 moderate and 0.8 to 1.0 severe impairment), but such gradings vary from source to source; the figure to remember is the 0.5 cut-off.

Let us push a case through the formula. Ehsan is a 48-year-old bank officer (an invented case) who was hurt in a road accident eight months ago. His CT scan showed bruising of the brain at the time, and he has now gone back to work but says he cannot follow new procedures, gets lost in his files and tires by midday. His employer's insurer and his own lawyer both want to know whether there is lasting impairment, and his doctor wants to plan his return to work. Table 11.6 shows his seven index scores. The cut-off for each score is printed in the battery's manual (for the Category Test, for example, Reitan's cut-off was about 50 errors); the last column simply records whether each of Ehsan's scores lies beyond it.

Table 11.6 Ehsan's seven Impairment Index scores (an invented case).
ScoreEhsan's resultBeyond the cut-off?
Category Test errors88 errorsYes (impaired)
Tactual Performance Test, total time26.4 minutes for the three trialsYes (impaired)
Tactual Performance Test, memory4 shapes drawnYes (impaired)
Tactual Performance Test, localisation1 shape correctly placedYes (impaired)
Seashore Rhythm Test27 of 30 correctNo
Speech-Sounds Perception Test5 errorsNo
Finger Tapping, preferred hand53 taps in 10 secondsNo

Step 1: count the impaired scores. The Category Test and all three Tactual Performance Test scores are beyond their cut-offs, so the count is 1 + 3 = 4. Step 2: divide by the number of scores counted, which is 7. So the index is 4 ÷ 7 = 0.5714. Step 3: round to one decimal place, as the index is always reported: 0.5714 becomes 0.6. Step 4: interpret. An index of 0.6 is above the traditional cut-off of 0.5, so by this rule Ehsan's performance is in the impaired range, and in the severity grading quoted above it would fall in the moderate band. Now look at the pattern, not just the level. His simple attention to sounds (Rhythm), his perception of speech sounds and his motor speed are intact; what has failed is the Category Test and the Tactual Performance Test, the two tests that demand working out a new problem with no instructions, learning as you go, and remembering what you never meant to learn. That pattern fits his complaint (he cannot follow new procedures) far better than a single number does, and it tells the rehabilitation team what to work on.

Suppose instead that three of the seven scores had been impaired. Then the index would be 3 ÷ 7 = 0.4286, which rounds to 0.4: below the cut-off of 0.5, but in the zone that some guides call borderline. Notice what this means for a person with only seven scores. Each impaired score moves the index by 1 ÷ 7, about 0.14, so a single test on a bad day can carry a patient across the cut-off. That is one reason why no experienced neuropsychologist interprets the index alone.

The index answers one question (how much?) and leaves others open. It cannot tell you where the damage is, what kind it is, when it happened or what caused it; two patients with the same index of 0.6 may have failed completely different tests. Later summary scores tried to do better. The Average Impairment Rating, from Elbert W. Russell, Charles Neuringer and Gerald Goldstein's book Assessment of Brain Damage: A Neuropsychological Key Approach (1970), rates each of a set of the battery's scores (including the Digit Symbol subtest of the Wechsler scale) on a short scale running from normal to severely impaired and averages the ratings, so it reflects how badly each test was failed, not just whether it was. Reitan's own later General Neuropsychological Deficit Scale rates 42 of the battery's variables for how far each departs from normal and adds the ratings; in Reitan's bands a total up to 25 is normal, 26 to 40 mild, 41 to 67 moderate and 68 or more severe impairment. For all three summary scores, a higher number means more impairment.

A deeper problem was that the original cut-offs were the same for everyone. A score that is ordinary for a 75-year-old with five years of schooling may be a clear sign of damage in a 25-year-old engineer, because performance on almost every test falls with age and rises with education. Robert K. Heaton, Igor Grant and Charles G. Matthews addressed this in Comprehensive Norms for an Extended Halstead-Reitan Battery (1991), which converts each raw score into a demographically corrected T score: a standard score with a mean of 50 and a standard deviation of 10, computed against healthy people of the same age, education and sex (Chapter 5 explains T scores). A revision in 2004 by Heaton and colleagues provided separate norms for African American and Caucasian adults. With demographically corrected scores, a T score below about 40 (one standard deviation below the mean of comparable people) is commonly read as impaired. Section 11.3.1 shows what goes wrong when such norms are borrowed for a population they were not built on.

Exam trap

The Halstead Impairment Index is a proportion from 0 to 1, not a T score, a percentage of items or an IQ. The traditional cut-off is 0.5 (about four of the seven scores impaired). The seven scores are the Category Test, the three Tactual Performance Test scores, Rhythm, Speech-Sounds and Tapping; Trail Making, the aphasia screening test and the sensory examination are interpreted separately and are not in the index. On the index, the Average Impairment Rating, the General Neuropsychological Deficit Scale and the Luria-Nebraska T scores, a high number is bad; on Heaton's T scores, as on IQ, a low number is bad.

What the Halstead-Reitan is for, and what it costs

Put plainly, the Halstead-Reitan battery is used for comprehensive neuropsychological assessment: to find out whether a person's brain is working normally, and if not, how severely and in what pattern it is impaired. In Reitan's hands it was also used to suggest whether damage was on the left, on the right or on both sides, whether it was focal or diffuse, and whether it was recent or long-standing. Before CT and MRI, that localising purpose was a large part of the battery's value; imaging now shows the lesion directly, and the battery's main uses have shifted to the questions a scan cannot answer (Section 11.1): how is the person actually functioning, which abilities are spared, has there been change since the last assessment, and what does this mean for work, driving, independent living or a legal claim. Its long, standardised and well-documented procedure has made it particularly popular in medico-legal and research settings, where an assessment may be scrutinised by the other side.

Its strengths follow directly from its design. Because every patient takes the same tests, scores can be compared across patients, across clinics and across decades, and an enormous research literature has accumulated on what different patterns mean. The battery samples many abilities, so it is unlikely to miss a major deficit in the areas it covers. Its right-left comparisons give it genuine lateralising power. And with the large demographically corrected norms of Heaton and colleagues, a raw score can be judged against people like the patient.

Its weaknesses are just as real. The first is time. A full battery with its allied procedures usually takes most of a day, commonly quoted at five to eight hours, which is exhausting for a patient who is already ill, expensive for the family or the health service, and impossible for someone who cannot sit for long. The second is content. The battery is heavily loaded with motor and speed demands (placing blocks, tapping, drawing lines against the clock), so slow, elderly or physically disabled people can look impaired for reasons that have little to do with cognition; and apart from the incidental memory scores of the Tactual Performance Test it contains no proper test of memory, which is why clinicians add the Wechsler Memory Scale or a word-list learning test (Section 11.4). The third is the logic of a fixed battery itself: it gives every patient tests they may not need, and it may still miss the one specific deficit that matters for this patient. The fourth concerns what it can discriminate. As the biopsychologist John Pinel notes, standardised batteries of this kind separate neurological patients from healthy people well, but they are much less good at separating neurological patients from psychiatric ones, whose attention, motivation and speed are often affected too. These criticisms are why most clinicians today prefer the flexible and process approaches described in Section 11.1, using individual Halstead-Reitan tests (Trail Making, Finger Tapping, the Category Test) inside a battery chosen for the referral question.

Exam trap

If an item asks what the Halstead-Reitan battery is used for, the answer is neuropsychological assessment: a broad range of cognitive, sensory and motor functions sampled to detect and localise brain dysfunction and estimate its severity. Each wrong option names a different kind of test taught elsewhere: personality assessment measures enduring traits and styles (inventories such as the MMPI and 16PF, Chapter 9); vocational testing means aptitude and interest measures used to guide careers, such as the Differential Aptitude Tests or the Strong inventory (Chapter 8); projective testing, of adults or of children, uses ambiguous stimuli such as inkblots or pictures (the Rorschach, the TAT, the Children's Apperception Test, Chapter 10); attachment style is assessed by observation and interview methods such as the Strange Situation and the Adult Attachment Interview, not by a neuropsychological battery; and a self-report depression screen is a questionnaire such as the BDI (Section 11.8).

Luria's method turned into a battery: the Luria-Nebraska

The second fixed battery has a stranger history, because it was built from the work of a man who did not believe in fixed batteries. Alexander Romanovich Luria (1902 to 1977), the Russian neuropsychologist whose theory of functional systems is outlined in Section 11.1, examined patients at the bedside with a large repertoire of small tasks: tapping a rhythm back to the examiner, copying a sequence of hand positions, repeating words and sentences, reading, writing, doing sums, recalling short stories, solving problems. He did not add up scores or compare them with norms. He watched exactly how each patient failed, chose the next task to test his hypothesis about which link in a functional system was broken, and built up a picture of the syndrome. It was a brilliant method in the hands of someone with Luria's experience, and almost impossible to teach to anyone else from his books.

The first step towards a battery was taken by the Danish psychologist Anne-Lise Christensen, who set down Luria's bedside methods in English in Luria's Neuropsychological Investigation (1975), published as a text with a manual and a set of test cards. Christensen's version kept Luria's spirit: the examiner was still expected to choose tasks and to interpret qualitatively. The second step was taken at the University of Nebraska by Charles J. Golden, Thomas A. Hammeke and Arnold D. Purisch. They took the items in Christensen's compilation, fixed the way each was to be given, laid down how each was to be scored, grouped the items into scales and collected norms. Their first validity study appeared in the Journal of Consulting and Clinical Psychology in 1978, and the published test and manual followed in 1980. The result is the Luria-Nebraska Neuropsychological Battery (LNNB).

The original form, Form I, has 269 items grouped into eleven clinical scales, listed in Table 11.7. Each item is scored 0, 1 or 2: 0 for normal performance, 1 for borderline and 2 for clearly abnormal. Some items are scored for both speed and accuracy. The item scores on a scale are added to give the raw scale score, and the raw score is converted into a T score (mean 50, standard deviation 10) from the manual's tables. Here the T score runs the opposite way from most tests you know: a higher T score means more impairment, because the raw score counts errors. The whole battery takes about two and a half hours, often quoted as two to three, which was one of its main selling points against the Halstead-Reitan.

Table 11.7 The eleven clinical scales of the Luria-Nebraska Neuropsychological Battery, Form I (abbreviations as used in Figure 11.5).
ScaleAbbreviationWhat its items sample
C1 MotorMotSimple and complex hand movements, copying positions and sequences, drawing, oral movements
C2 RhythmRhyJudging and reproducing tones and rhythmic patterns
C3 TactileTacTouch on each hand, position sense, recognising shapes and figures by touch
C4 VisualVisRecognising objects and pictures, visual-spatial judgement
C5 Receptive SpeechRecHearing speech sounds, understanding words, sentences and grammar
C6 Expressive SpeechExpRepeating, naming, producing speech
C7 WritingWriCopying and writing letters, words and phrases
C8 ReadingReaReading letters, words and sentences
C9 ArithmeticAriReading and writing numbers, simple calculation
C10 MemoryMemLearning and recalling words, pictures and short material
C11 Intellectual ProcessesIntUnderstanding pictures and stories, reasoning, problem solving

How do you decide whether a T score is "high"? Not with a single fixed line, because older and less-educated people make more errors on many of these items even when their brains are healthy. The manual instead provides a critical level, a T score computed from the person's age and years of education; older people and people with less schooling are allowed a higher T score before a scale is counted as abnormal. The manual's decision rules then count how many clinical scales lie above the critical level: the more of them there are, the stronger the evidence of brain dysfunction (rules of thumb of two, or three, scales are both used). Beyond the clinical scales the battery provides summary scales, including a Pathognomonic scale made of items that people without brain damage seldom fail, Left Hemisphere and Right Hemisphere scales built from the motor and tactile items done with the right and with the left side of the body, and scales for the overall elevation of the profile and the level of impairment; and localisation scales intended to point to the frontal, sensorimotor, parietal-occipital and temporal regions of each hemisphere. The figure of "fourteen scales" that you will meet in some notes counts the eleven clinical scales plus the Pathognomonic, Left Hemisphere and Right Hemisphere scales.

Work through an invented profile. Juhi is a 40-year-old schoolteacher with twelve years of schooling and a two-year teacher's diploma who had a stroke four months ago and has been referred by a rehabilitation unit. Take one scale first. Suppose that on the Expressive Speech scale she scores 2 on five items, 1 on four items and 0 on all the rest. Her raw score is (5 × 2) + (4 × 1) + 0 = 10 + 4 = 14. The examiner looks up 14 in the manual's table for that scale and reads off a T score; in our invented example it is 71. Doing the same for all eleven scales gives the profile in Figure 11.5. For Juhi's age and education, suppose the critical level works out at a T score of 60 (an invented value for the example). Now count: Receptive Speech (66), Expressive Speech (71), Writing (68), Reading (64) and Memory (67) are above 60, so five scales lie above the critical level. That is well past any of the usual rules, so the profile indicates brain dysfunction. The pattern is the interesting part: the elevated scales are the language scales and a memory scale that uses words, while motor, tactile and visual scales sit close to the normal mean of 50. In Luria's terms that points to the left hemisphere, which fits a left-sided stroke. Hold on to that interpretation for a moment, though, because the battery's critics would ask a pointed question about it.

Two later versions complete the family. Form II (1985) has 279 items; the extra items form a twelfth clinical scale, Intermediate Memory, which asks about material presented earlier in the session. And a children's revision, the Luria-Nebraska Neuropsychological Battery: Children's Revision (Golden, 1987), adapted the battery for children aged 8 to 12. In India, the Luria-Nebraska was the model for the Hindi battery developed at the All India Institute of Medical Sciences, described in Section 11.3.6.

Exam trap

Alexander Luria did not write the Luria-Nebraska. The chain is Luria (the bedside methods) to Christensen (compiled them, 1975) to Golden, Hammeke and Purisch (standardised and scored them at Nebraska, 1978; manual 1980). Form I has 269 items in 11 clinical scales; Form II has 279 and adds Intermediate Memory. Items are scored 0, 1 or 2, and a high T score means impairment. "Rhythm" appears in both batteries (the Seashore Rhythm Test of the Halstead-Reitan and the C2 Rhythm scale of the Luria-Nebraska), so read the option carefully. And although Luria's own method was flexible and qualitative, the Luria-Nebraska is a fixed battery: an answer explanation that calls it "the flexible battery" is wrong.

The Luria-Nebraska controversy, and the two batteries compared

Few tests have been attacked as fiercely, or as soon, as the Luria-Nebraska. The pointed question about Juhi's profile is the heart of the attack. Her five elevated scales have names that suggest five different abilities, but every one of them depends on understanding and producing language: the instructions for the memory items are spoken, the writing and reading items need letters, and even many "intellectual" items are given in words. A single problem, an aphasia from a left-hemisphere stroke, could raise all five together. If so, the profile is not five findings but one, and a clinician who read the scale names literally ("memory impaired, reading impaired, writing impaired") would be misled.

Key study: the Luria-Nebraska controversy (1980 to 1983)

In 1980 the neuropsychologist Kenneth M. Adams published a critique in the Journal of Consulting and Clinical Psychology with the blunt title "In search of Luria's battery: A false start". He argued that the battery had been put into clinical use before its development and validation could support it, and that the early validation studies were not strong enough to justify the claims made for it. Golden replied in the same issue. In 1981 Paul A. Spiers reviewed the dispute under the title "Have they come to praise Luria or to bury him? The Luria-Nebraska battery controversy", questioning whether a scored, fixed set of items could capture a method whose whole point was flexible, qualitative observation. In 1983 Dean Delis and Edith Kaplan, the founder of the process approach (Section 11.1), added "Hazards of a standardized neuropsychological test with low content validity": the scales' names, they argued, imply functions that the items do not measure purely, because the same item can be failed for many different reasons. The defenders replied that the battery was never meant to replace Luria's method, only to give clinicians a standardised, reasonably brief examination with norms, and that validity evidence was accumulating. The debate taught the whole field a lesson that applies to every battery in this chapter: a scale's name is a hypothesis about what it measures, not a guarantee.

So how do the two batteries compare in practice? When Gerald Goldstein and Carolyn Shelly compared them in 1984, as Barlow and Durand summarise the study, the Halstead-Reitan and the Luria-Nebraska were about equally good at detecting brain damage, each classifying roughly 80 per cent of cases correctly. That figure is a good reminder of what "fixed battery" accuracy means: impressively better than chance, but still wrong in about one case in five, which is why neither battery is used on its own to make a diagnosis. Table 11.8 sets the two side by side.

Table 11.8 The Halstead-Reitan and the Luria-Nebraska compared.
FeatureHalstead-Reitan BatteryLuria-Nebraska Battery
OriginHalstead's tests of biological intelligence (1947), selected and extended by ReitanLuria's bedside methods, compiled by Christensen (1975), standardised by Golden, Hammeke and Purisch (1978; manual 1980)
ContentAbout ten separate tests, several with many items (the Category Test alone has 208)269 items (Form I) or 279 (Form II) in 11 (or 12) clinical scales
AgesAdults 15 and over; versions for 9 to 14 and 5 to 8Adults; children's revision for 8 to 12
TimeMost of a day: about 5 to 8 hours with allied testsAbout 2 to 3 hours
ScoringErrors, times and counts per test; cut-offs or demographically corrected T scoresEach item 0, 1 or 2; scale totals converted to T scores (high = impaired)
Summary scoreImpairment Index (0.5 or more suggests damage); Average Impairment Rating; General Neuropsychological Deficit ScaleNumber of clinical scales above the critical level set by age and education; summary and localisation scales
StrengthsHuge research base, large demographically corrected norms, strong right-left comparisonsShorter, cheaper, portable; samples language, reading, writing and arithmetic widely
WeaknessesLong, tiring, motor and speed loaded, little memory testing, fixedHeavily verbal, so language deficits spread across scales; low content validity; strays from Luria's qualitative method
Main criticsAdvocates of flexible and process approaches (Section 11.1)Adams (1980), Spiers (1981), Delis and Kaplan (1983)
Exam trap

Both batteries are fixed; what differs is their origin. The Halstead-Reitan is empirical and American (tests kept because they separated patients from controls), long, and summarised by the Impairment Index; the Luria-Nebraska is Luria's theory-driven methods given American norms, shorter, and summarised by T scores against a critical level. If an option says the Luria-Nebraska "takes longer than the Halstead-Reitan" or "was standardised by Luria", it is wrong. And neither battery is a personality, projective or vocational test.

11.3 Indian Neuropsychological Tools: The NIMHANS Battery, the PGI Battery and the PGI Memory ScaleLower yield · not asked yet

Imagine giving the Speech-Sounds Perception Test of Section 11.2.2 to a 60-year-old farmer from a village in Mandya district who left school after two years, speaks only Kannada and has never held a test booklet. He will make a great many errors. Is his brain damaged? You cannot tell, because the test asks him to match spoken English-style nonsense words to Roman spellings, something his brain was never trained to do. The same problem appears, less dramatically, in almost every test built in America or Europe: in the words chosen for a memory list, in the pictures chosen for a naming test, in the alphabet used in Trail Making Part B, and above all in the norms, the scores of healthy people against which a patient's score is judged. This section asks why Indian clinicians built their own tools and what they built. It describes the three you are most likely to meet in an Indian clinic or an Indian question paper (the NIMHANS Neuropsychology Battery from Bengaluru, and the PGI Battery of Brain Dysfunction and the PGI Memory Scale from Chandigarh) and then the newer Indian batteries that sit beside them.

Why India needed its own tools

A raw score on a test means nothing until it is compared with the scores of a normative sample, a group of healthy people tested in the same way (Chapter 5 explains norms and standard scores in full). Norms built in one population can be used in another only if the two are alike in the things that affect scores on that test. For neuropsychological tests those things are age, education, literacy, language and familiarity with being tested, and on every one of them India differs from the populations on which the classic tests were normed, and differs enormously within itself.

Education and literacy come first. Healthy people with more schooling do better on almost every cognitive test, and the effect is largest exactly where Indian samples are most varied: among people with little or no schooling. As early as 1980 Dwarka Pershad and S. K. Verma of the Postgraduate Institute of Medical Education and Research (PGIMER) in Chandigarh reported, in the Indian Journal of Psychiatry, tentative education-wise norms for several tests built or standardised in their department, including the verbal section of an adult intelligence scale, the PGI Memory Scale and proverb and similarity tests, for adults aged 16 to 50. The mean scores of the different educational groups differed markedly, and they concluded that norms must be reported separately for different levels of education. Even years of schooling are an imperfect guide in India, as a 2017 review of cognitive testing in Indian adults by A. P. Porrselvi and V. Shankar pointed out: schools and curricula vary widely, and many older people who completed school have since lost the reading, writing, drawing and arithmetic skills they learned there because their lives have not needed them, a state the authors call functional illiteracy.

Language is the second difficulty. A verbal test translated into another language is, strictly speaking, a new test: the words in a memory list differ in how common, how long and how familiar they are, and a reading or spelling task depends on the script. Tests that use the Roman alphabet, such as Trail Making Part B with its sequence 1-A-2-B, cannot be given fairly to a person who does not read it; the Colour Trails Test (Louis D'Elia and colleagues, 1996), which replaces the letters with two colours, was designed for exactly this problem and is the version used in the NIMHANS battery (Section 11.6 teaches both). Familiarity with testing is the third. The same 2017 review noted that many Indian adults have little experience of psychological tests and treat them like a school examination, which brings test anxiety, and that a social pressure of "scoring the highest" makes some people reluctant to attempt tasks such as drawing or recall unless they are sure of doing well. Content is the fourth: a picture of an object that is common in Iowa may be unknown in Assam.

How much difference does the norm group make? A striking answer comes from a study outside India. In 2021 a team led by A. Morlett Paredes gave the Halstead Category Test (Section 11.2.2) to 252 healthy native Spanish speakers living in the United States-Mexico border region. When their scores were judged against published, demographically adjusted norms for English-speaking non-Hispanic White Americans, 48 per cent of these healthy people were classified as impaired; against the norms for Black Americans, 27 per cent were. Now do the arithmetic. The impairment rule used was a T score below 40, one standard deviation below the mean, and by definition only about 16 per cent of a healthy group falls below that line. So 48 per cent is 48 ÷ 16 = 3 times what the norms promise: borrowed norms made healthy people look brain-damaged three times as often as they should. That is the risk an Indian clinician runs by scoring an Indian patient against American tables.

Indian clinicians responded in two ways. One was to take widely used Western tests, adapt their content and instructions, and collect fresh norms on Indian samples broken down by age, sex and education; this is the approach of the NIMHANS batteries. The other was to assemble a battery from tests that had already been built or adapted in India, and to derive a single rating of overall dysfunction from them; this is the approach of the PGI Battery of Brain Dysfunction. Both are described below, and Book 2, Section 2.6.1 shows where they fit in clinical work: the bedside examination of cognition screens, and these batteries measure.

Exam trap

The reason for Indian norms is not that Indians "score lower"; it is that education, literacy, language and test familiarity change scores in healthy people, so a borrowed cut-off misclassifies healthy people as impaired (a false positive). Education is the variable the Indian test-builders stressed most: Pershad and Verma's 1980 paper and every Indian battery report norms by education level.

The NIMHANS Neuropsychology Battery for adults

The National Institute of Mental Health and Neuro Sciences (NIMHANS) in Bengaluru has the longest tradition of neuropsychological testing in India. Its first battery was described by C. R. Mukundan and V. N. Murthy in a paper presented at NIMHANS in 1979 on lateralising and localising cerebral lesions with a battery of neuropsychological tests. It was a "lobe function" battery built on Luria's principles of the localisation and lateralisation of higher mental functions (Section 11.1), with about 20 tests, several of them adapted Western tests and one an indigenous delayed-response test of working memory. A review of Indian cognitive testing in 2017 called it the earliest neuropsychological test battery developed in India.

The battery in use today is the NIMHANS Neuropsychology Battery, whose manual, NIMHANS Neuropsychology Battery-2004 Manual, was written by Shobini L. Rao, D. K. Subbakrishna and K. Gopukumar and published by NIMHANS in 2004. (You will see it called both the "Neuropsychology" and the "Neuropsychological" Battery; they are the same.) It consists of 19 widely used Western tests that were adapted and standardised for Indian adults, with norms for ages 16 to 65. Unlike the Halstead-Reitan, it has no single summary index. Each test is tied to a particular cognitive function and, where the research allows, to a brain region: tests of planning and set-shifting to the prefrontal cortex, verbal learning tests traditionally to the left temporal lobe, the complex figure to right-hemisphere visuospatial memory, and so on. The clinician chooses the tests that the referral question calls for, so in practice it is used as a flexible, domain-based battery (Section 11.1), not a fixed one.

Table 11.9 groups the tests you will most often see reported from the battery by the domain each assesses. Most are taught in their own sections of this chapter; here you need to know what each is for and where it sits. Two are taught nowhere else in this chapter. The Digit Vigilance Test is a page densely packed with rows of digits on which the person crosses out two target digits as fast as possible; the score is the time taken together with the omissions (targets missed) and commissions (other digits crossed out), and it measures sustained attention. The Triads Test measures divided attention: the person must respond to words that the examiner reads out in groups of three while, at the same time, identifying digits that the examiner traces on the person's hand, so that attention has to be split between two streams of information at once.

Table 11.9 Tests commonly reported from the NIMHANS Neuropsychology Battery (2004), grouped by domain.
DomainTestWhat the person does (in general terms)Function measured
SpeedFinger Tapping TestTaps with the index finger, each handMotor speed
SpeedDigit Symbol Substitution TestWrites the symbol paired with each digit, using a keyMental (processing) speed
AttentionDigit Vigilance TestCrosses out target digits on a dense page of digitsSustained attention
AttentionColour Trails Test, Parts 1 and 2Joins numbered circles in order; then alternates between two coloursFocused attention; shifting
AttentionTriads TestResponds to spoken word triads while identifying digits traced on the handDivided attention
ExecutiveControlled Oral Word AssociationSays words beginning with given letters, a minute eachPhonemic fluency
ExecutiveAnimal Names TestNames as many animals as possible in a minuteCategory fluency
ExecutiveDesign Fluency TestDraws as many different novel designs as possibleNon-verbal fluency
ExecutiveVerbal N-back; Spatial SpanSays whether each item matches the one 1 or 2 items back; repeats a sequence of tapped positionsWorking memory (verbal; visuospatial)
ExecutiveTower of LondonRearranges pieces to match a goal in the fewest movesPlanning
ExecutiveWisconsin Card Sorting TestSorts cards by a rule that is never stated and that changesConcept formation, set-shifting
ExecutiveStroop TestNames the ink colour of printed colour wordsResponse inhibition
ComprehensionToken TestCarries out spoken commands with coloured shapesVerbal comprehension
MemoryAuditory Verbal Learning TestLearns a word list over trials; recalls after interference and delay; recognisesVerbal learning and memory
MemoryComplex Figure TestCopies a complex line drawing, then draws it from memory, at once and after a delayVisuoconstruction, visual memory

The Colour Trails Test, the Wisconsin Card Sorting Test, the Stroop, the Tower of London, the fluency tests and the Token Test are taught in Section 11.6; the Auditory Verbal Learning Test and the Complex Figure Test in Section 11.4.

The battery's norms are what make it Indian, so it is worth seeing how they were built. As the battery's users describe them, healthy volunteers were tested in every combination of three levels of education (illiterate; school, 1st to 10th standard; and college, 11th standard and above), three age bands (young adults 16 to 30, middle-aged adults 31 to 50, and older adults 51 to 65) and both sexes, with 30 volunteers in each combination. That gives 3 × 3 × 2 = 18 groups, and 18 × 30 = 540 people in all. For every score of every test, the manual gives percentile norms within each of the 18 groups: the percentage of comparable healthy people who scored at or below a given level. The usual cut-off is the 15th percentile, close to one standard deviation below the mean (which strictly corresponds to about the 16th percentile): a score that fewer than 15 per cent of comparable healthy people would obtain is read as a deficit in that function.

Follow one score through the tables. Juhi, the schoolteacher of Section 11.2.6 (an invented case), is 40, female and has twelve years of schooling plus a diploma, so she belongs in the group of women aged 31 to 50 with education of 11th standard and above. On the Digit Vigilance Test she takes 690 seconds. For a timed test, a longer time is a worse score, so the cut-off is a time: suppose (invented figures, not the manual's) that the 15th-percentile time for her group is 600 seconds. Step 1: compare. Her 690 seconds is slower than 600 seconds. Step 2: translate. Slower than the 15th-percentile time means that fewer than 15 per cent of comparable healthy women were as slow, so her score falls below the 15th percentile. Step 3: conclude. Juhi has a deficit in sustained attention, which fits her complaint that she loses her place when marking a pile of answer scripts. Now suppose an older woman of 58 with eight years of schooling also took 690 seconds. She belongs in a different group, and if (again invented) that group's 15th-percentile time were 840 seconds, her identical 690 seconds would be faster than the cut-off and would count as normal. The same raw score is a deficit for one person and normal for another, which is the whole point of stratified norms.

Published studies show how widely the battery is used: to measure outcome in cognitive retraining after head injury and in schizophrenia (Book 2, Section 9.9 describes cognitive retraining), to profile patients with epilepsy, bipolar disorder and obsessive-compulsive disorder, and to follow patients after brain surgery. In such studies raw scores are typically converted to percentiles from the battery's age-, sex- and education-matched tables, and a score below the 15th percentile is classed as impaired in that domain.

Clinical link

In awake brain surgery, a tumour near the language or motor areas is removed while the patient is conscious, so that the surgeons can check from moment to moment that the patient can still speak, understand and move. NIMHANS clinicians have described how a clinical neuropsychologist prepares such a patient: a detailed assessment a few days before surgery, typically with tests from the NIMHANS Neuropsychology Battery normed for Indian age, education and sex, sets a baseline; the same functions are then checked during the operation, with the aim of preserving the patient's existing level of functioning. Here the battery is doing exactly what Section 11.1 said modern neuropsychology is for: not finding the lesion, which the scan has already found, but measuring what the patient can do and protecting it.

Exam trap

NIMHANS battery = Bengaluru; Rao, Subbakrishna and Gopukumar; 2004 manual; 19 tests; adults 16 to 65; percentile norms by age, sex and education; 15th percentile cut-off; domain-based, with no single index. It uses the Colour Trails Test, not the alphabet-based Trail Making Part B. It was built on Luria's principles of localisation and lateralisation, but it is not a translation of the Luria-Nebraska (the Hindi version of the Luria-Nebraska is the AIIMS battery, Section 11.3.6). Do not confuse it with the NIMHANS battery for specific learning disability used in disability certification (Book 2, Section 9.9.8), which is a different instrument.

The NIMHANS batteries for children and for older adults

Children are not small adults, neuropsychologically speaking. Their abilities are still developing, at different rates for different functions, so a child's score must be compared with children of the same age, and a test that suits a 40-year-old may be meaningless at seven. The NIMHANS Neuropsychological Battery for Children was developed by Bhoomika R. Kar, Shobini L. Rao, B. A. Chandramouli and K. Thennarasu, with a manual dated 2004, and has norms for ages 5 to 15. It has been validated on children with brain injury. In a study published in 2011 the same authors used the battery to chart the growth of neuropsychological functions in 400 healthy children from three schools in Bangalore city, at eleven age levels from 5 to 15. The tests reported in that study show how the adult domains were adapted for children: the Finger Tapping Test for motor speed; a Colour Cancellation Test for visual scanning and sustained attention and the Colour Trails Test for focused attention; the Design Fluency Test, the Porteus Maze Test for planning and foresight, and verbal and visual N-back tasks for working memory, as tests of executive function; the Motor-Free Visual Perception Test, a picture completion test and a block design test for visuoperceptual, visuoconceptual and visuoconstructive ability; the Token Test for verbal comprehension; and Rey's Auditory Verbal Learning Test and a Memory for Designs Test for verbal and visual learning and memory. The Indian Psychiatric Society's 2025 guidelines on autism also list the Wisconsin Card Sorting Test and a phonemic fluency test among the children's battery's tests. Chapter 7 describes developmental assessment more broadly.

At the other end of life, the NIMHANS Neuropsychological Battery for Elderly (NNB-E) was developed for Indian older adults aged 55 to 80, a group in whom the main question is often whether memory complaints mark the beginning of dementia. In a preliminary report of its clinical validity in the Indian Journal of Psychiatry in 2013, Ravikesh Tripathi and his colleagues found that patients with Alzheimer's disease performed significantly worse than healthy older people on every test, memory and non-memory alike, but that tests of episodic and semantic memory were particularly good at telling the two groups apart. Scores on the battery's subtests rose with scores on the Hindi Mental State Examination and fell as ratings on the Clinical Dementia Rating and the Everyday Abilities Scale for India rose, which is the pattern you would expect if all of them measure the same decline. Section 11.7 teaches the Hindi Mental State Examination and the Clinical Dementia Rating.

Exam trap

Three NIMHANS neuropsychological batteries, three age ranges: adults 16 to 65 (Rao and colleagues, 2004), children 5 to 15 (Kar and colleagues, 2004) and elderly 55 to 80 (NNB-E; validity reported by Tripathi and colleagues, 2013). The children's battery is associated with Bhoomika Kar; the adult battery with Shobini Rao.

The PGI Battery of Brain Dysfunction

In Chandigarh, the psychiatry department of PGIMER faced a practical question in the 1970s. Patients were referred to its psychology section with a single request: is there brain pathology here or not? This was the old "organic versus functional" question that Section 11.1 describes, and no single test answered it well. The psychologists already had several tests built or adapted in their own department, including a memory scale, verbal and performance tests of intelligence and tests of perception and visuomotor copying. Could they be combined into one judgement?

Key study: screening for organic brain dysfunction at PGIMER (1984)

Dwarka Pershad, S. K. Verma, S. Malhotra and S. Prabhakar reported in the Indian Journal of Psychiatry in 1984 on 180 adult patients, aged 20 to 50, who had been referred to the psychology section between 1976 and 1980 for differential diagnosis of brain pathology, and in whom at least two of three cognitive functions (intelligence, memory and perceptuo-motor ability) had been tested and a clinical diagnosis had later been reached from follow-up and other evidence. They rescored nineteen psychological test variables to reduce the effects of age and education. For each variable they drew the frequency distributions separately for patients with and without brain damage, and plotted them as cumulative curves (ogives) to find the score that separated the two groups best. Each score was then converted into a rating, with 0 for average performance and higher ratings for lower scores, and the ratings were combined. The combined rating identified about two thirds of the organic patients correctly among these difficult-to-diagnose cases. Two thirds may sound modest, but these were the cases that clinicians had found hard; the study turned a collection of separate tests into a single, reproducible rating, and that rating is the core of the PGI battery.

The battery that grew from this work is the PGI Battery of Brain Dysfunction (PGI-BBD), whose handbook by Pershad and Verma was published by the National Psychological Corporation, Agra, in 1990. It is administered in Hindi, and it combines five tests:

  1. The PGI Memory Scale (Section 11.3.5), which contributes ten scores, one for each of its subtests.
  2. A short form of C. M. Bhatia's Battery of Performance Tests of Intelligence (Bhatia's short scale), consisting of the Kohs Block Design test and Alexander's Pass-Along test, which contributes two scores. Bhatia's full battery, built for Indians who cannot read, is described in Chapter 6.
  3. The Verbal Adult Intelligence Scale (VAIS), a Hindi verbal intelligence scale whose four subtests carry Wechsler names (Information, Digit Span, Arithmetic and Comprehension); the VAIS contributes five scores.
  4. The Nahor-Benson Test of perceptual acuity, eight cards of which five each carry a design to be copied and three carry instructions to draw an object; it is read as a test of visual acuity and depth perception, and it contributes one score.
  5. The Bender Visual Motor Gestalt Test, nine designs to be copied (Section 11.5), which contributes one score.

Add the contributions: 10 + 2 + 5 + 1 + 1 = 19, the same nineteen variables as the 1984 study. Each raw score is looked up in the handbook's tables, which allow for age and education, and converted into a dysfunction rating from 0 to 3; the 19 ratings are then added to give a total. Since each of the 19 ratings can be at most 3, the highest possible total is 19 × 3 = 57. Studies using the battery report its bands as follows.

Remember

PGI-BBD total dysfunction rating = sum of the 19 ratings (each 0 to 3), so the total runs from 0 to 19 × 3 = 57.

Bands: 0 to 19 no impairment; 20 to 38 slight impairment; 39 to 57 severe impairment. Higher means more dysfunction. The norms are for adults, usually given as 20 to 59 years (one Indian guideline gives 20 to 50), and the full battery takes about 90 to 120 minutes.

Return to Ehsan from Section 11.2.4 and suppose that, instead of the Halstead-Reitan, he had been assessed in Hindi with the PGI-BBD. His invented ratings (Figure 11.6) are: on the ten memory scores, 0, 0, 1, 1, 2, 0, 1, 2, 2 and 1; on the two performance scores, 3 and 3; on the five verbal scores, 0, 1, 1, 0 and 1; on the Nahor-Benson, 2; and on the Bender, 3. Step 1: add within each test. Memory: 0 + 0 + 1 + 1 + 2 + 0 + 1 + 2 + 2 + 1 = 10. Performance: 3 + 3 = 6. Verbal: 0 + 1 + 1 + 0 + 1 = 3. Nahor-Benson: 2. Bender: 3. Step 2: add the five subtotals: 10 + 6 + 3 + 2 + 3 = 24. Step 3: place the total in its band. 24 lies between 20 and 38, so the battery rates Ehsan as having slight impairment. Step 4: look at where the ratings came from, just as you did with his Halstead-Reitan profile. His verbal intelligence scores are almost untouched (3 out of a possible 15), while the block design and pass-along tests, which demand working out a new spatial problem against the clock, earned the maximum rating of 3 each, and the two copying tests are also affected. The two batteries, built on different continents from different tests, tell the same story about Ehsan: old verbal knowledge preserved, new problem-solving and visuospatial work impaired.

What is the PGI-BBD good for? It answers the screening question it was built for, "is there brain dysfunction, and roughly how much?", in a language and with tests suited to Hindi-speaking North Indian adults, and in about two hours rather than a day. It remains in use in Indian hospitals and in Indian research on alcohol and opioid dependence, depression and the cognitive after-effects of COVID-19, and it is listed among the tools for assessing cognition after head injury (see the Indian context box below). Its limits follow from the same design. A single total says little about which functions are affected (although, as with Ehsan, the pattern of ratings helps); the battery contains no real test of executive function, which the NIMHANS battery covers well; and its tests and norms are decades old.

Exam trap

PGI-BBD = PGIMER, Chandigarh (not NIMHANS, Bengaluru); Pershad and Verma; handbook 1990. Its five tests are the PGI Memory Scale, Bhatia's short scale (Kohs Block Design and Alexander's Pass-Along), the Verbal Adult Intelligence Scale, the Nahor-Benson Test and the Bender-Gestalt: 19 scores, total rating 0 to 57. The perceptual test is Nahor-Benson, not Benton (Arthur Benton's tests are in Sections 11.4 and 11.5). In a "which is NOT part of the PGI-BBD" item, the Wisconsin Card Sorting Test, the Rey Auditory Verbal Learning Test, the Tower of London and the Halstead Category Test are the outsiders. Note that it contains the Bender, and that the memory scale is part of the battery as well as a test in its own right.

The PGI Memory Scale

Memory complaints are among the commonest reasons for referral to a clinical psychologist, and in the 1970s Indian clinicians had no memory test built for their patients. The Wechsler Memory Scale (Section 11.4) existed, but its stories, word pairs and designs had been built and normed in the United States. Dwarka Pershad and N. N. Wig of PGIMER described "a battery of simple tests of memory for use in India" in the journal Neurology India in 1976, published a normative study on elderly people in 1977 and reported its reliability and validity in the Indian Journal of Psychiatry in 1978. The PGI Memory Scale (PGIMS) is usually dated to 1977. It is given in Hindi, it uses simple material that people with limited education can manage, and its norms are for adults aged 20 to 45, broken down by age and education.

The scale has ten subtests, set out in Table 11.10 with the maximum score of each as commonly reported (record forms in circulation differ slightly, so check the maxima on the form you are using). The table describes each subtest in general terms; the actual questions and lists are in the test materials and are not reproduced here.

Table 11.10 The ten subtests of the PGI Memory Scale (Pershad and Wig).
No.SubtestWhat it samples (in general terms)Maximum
1Remote memoryQuestions about events of the person's distant past6
2Recent memoryQuestions about recent events and the present day5
3Mental balanceOverlearned sequences under control: counting backwards, serial subtraction9
4Attention and concentrationDigit span forward and backward15
5Delayed recallRecall of a list of words after a delay10
6Immediate recallRepeating sentences of increasing length at once12
7Retention for similar pairsLearning pairs of words that belong together in meaning5
8Retention for dissimilar pairsLearning pairs of unrelated words15
9Visual retentionDrawing simple designs from memory after a brief exposure13
10RecognitionPicking out objects or pictures shown earlier from a larger set10

Read the list as a map of memory, using the distinctions taught in Section 11.4 and in Book 1, Sections 7.3.4 and 7.3.6. Subtests 3 and 4 test attention, span and mental control rather than storage: mental balance is the old clinical name for running an overlearned sequence under deliberate control, and has nothing to do with emotional balance. Subtest 6 tests immediate verbal memory. Subtests 5, 7, 8 and 9 test what clinicians most often need to know about, the learning and retention of new material, verbal and visual. Subtest 10 tests recognition, which is easier than free recall and can show that material was stored even when it cannot be recalled. Subtests 1 and 2 test memory for the distant and the recent past. The pair of paired-associate subtests is particularly neat. A pair of words that already belong together can be remembered by leaning on what the person already knows; a pair of unrelated words must be bound together from scratch. A person whose new learning is impaired often manages the related pairs reasonably and fails the unrelated ones badly.

How good is the scale? Later Indian reviews, summarising the original reports, give test-retest reliabilities of 0.69 to 0.85 for the individual subtests, and Pershad and Wig's 1978 paper reported evidence of the scale's validity as well as its reliability (Chapter 3 explains test-retest reliability; Chapter 4, validity). One limitation shows up in research: healthy people, and many patients, score at or near the maximum on the easy subtests (remote memory, recent memory and mental balance), a ceiling effect that makes those subtests insensitive to mild impairment. One Indian study of schizophrenia dropped those three subtests from its analysis for exactly this reason.

Each subtest score is judged against the norms for the person's age and education group. A clear way to see how far a score lies from the norm is to turn it into a z score: the distance of the score from the norm group's mean, measured in standard deviations (Chapter 5 teaches standard scores in full).

Remember

z = (X − M) ÷ SD

X is the person's score on the subtest, M the mean of the norm group, and SD the standard deviation of the norm group. A z of 0 is exactly average; −1 is one standard deviation below average. Conventions vary, but a score more than about 1.5 to 2 standard deviations below the mean is commonly read as a deficit.

Bela is a 42-year-old nurse in Chandigarh with 14 years of education (an invented case). Three months ago she had a viral infection of the brain, encephalitis, from which she has physically recovered, but her colleagues say she asks the same questions again and again and forgets what she was told at the morning handover. Table 11.11 shows her PGI Memory Scale scores against invented norms for her group (the real norm tables are in the handbook). Work two rows by hand. Delayed recall: X = 3, M = 8.6, SD = 1.6, so z = (3 − 8.6) ÷ 1.6 = −5.6 ÷ 1.6 = −3.50. Remote memory: X = 6, M = 5.8, SD = 0.5, so z = (6 − 5.8) ÷ 0.5 = 0.2 ÷ 0.5 = +0.40. The other rows are worked the same way and rounded to two decimal places.

Table 11.11 Bela's PGI Memory Scale profile against invented norms (an invented case; the means and standard deviations are not the handbook's).
SubtestBela's scoreNorm meanNorm SDz
Remote memory65.80.5+0.40
Recent memory34.60.6−2.67
Mental balance77.51.4−0.36
Attention and concentration1010.51.8−0.28
Delayed recall38.61.6−3.50
Immediate recall1010.41.3−0.31
Similar pairs44.70.5−1.40
Dissimilar pairs511.52.2−2.95
Visual retention711.41.6−2.75
Recognition89.40.8−1.75

Now read the profile. Bela's total is 6 + 3 + 7 + 10 + 3 + 10 + 4 + 5 + 7 + 8 = 63, but, as with every total in this chapter, the pattern matters more than the sum. Her attention span, her mental control, her immediate repetition of sentences and her memory for the distant past are all within about half a standard deviation of the mean: she can take information in and hold it for a few seconds, and her old memories are intact. What has failed is everything that requires new material to be stored and kept: delayed recall (z = −3.50), learning unrelated word pairs (−2.95), retaining designs (−2.75) and memory for recent events (−2.67). Related pairs (−1.40) are much better than unrelated pairs, because existing knowledge carries her part of the way. Both verbal and visual new learning are affected, which suggests damage on both sides of the brain rather than one. And her recognition (−1.75) is clearly better than her free recall (−3.50), a sign that some of what she cannot recall has been stored and could be prompted. Taken together, this is the profile of an anterograde amnesia, a failure to lay down new memories with old ones spared, which fits an encephalitis that has damaged the memory structures of the temporal lobes (Book 3, Section 10.8.3 describes the amnestic disorders; Section 11.4 explains how recall and recognition are used to separate problems of storage from problems of retrieval). The profile also tells the team what will help: written handover notes and a fixed routine will work far better for Bela than asking her to try harder to remember.

Exam trap

PGI Memory Scale = Pershad and Wig (not Pershad and Verma), PGIMER, 1977; 10 subtests. Learn them as five pairs: remote and recent memory; mental balance and attention-concentration; delayed and immediate recall; retention for similar and for dissimilar pairs; visual retention and recognition. "Mental balance" is mental control (counting backwards, serial tasks), not an emotional measure. The scale is part of the PGI-BBD as well as a test on its own. It is not the Wechsler Memory Scale and has no "memory quotient" index structure like the WMS-IV (Section 11.4).

Other Indian tools, and choosing among them

Two further batteries complete the picture. The AIIMS Comprehensive Neuropsychological Battery in Hindi (adult form) was developed at the All India Institute of Medical Sciences, New Delhi, by S. Gupta, S. K. Khandelwal, P. N. Tandon and their colleagues, and published in the Journal of Personality and Clinical Studies in 2000. It is the Indian descendant of the Luria-Nebraska (Section 11.2.6): 160 items divided into ten basic scales (motor, tactile, visual, receptive speech, expressive speech, reading, writing, arithmetic, memory and intellectual processes) and four secondary scales (left hemisphere, right hemisphere, pathognomonic and a total score), with norms for ages 15 to 80. If you compare its basic scales with Table 11.7, you will see that it keeps ten of the Luria-Nebraska's eleven clinical scales and has no separate rhythm scale. The newest addition is the ICMR Neurocognitive Tool Box (ICMR-NCTB), developed by a consortium under the Indian Council of Medical Research to standardise the diagnosis of dementia across India's linguistic and educational diversity; its design was published in 2020 and its validation in 2021, in five languages (Hindi, Bengali, Telugu, Kannada and Malayalam), with tests of attention and executive function, episodic memory, language and visuospatial function.

Several other Indian adaptations are taught where their topic belongs. Mary Ganguli's Hindi Mental State Examination (1995), an adaptation of the Mini-Mental State Examination for illiterate rural older people, is in Section 11.7. Savita Malhotra's Childhood Psychopathology Measurement Schedule, an Indian rating scale for children's behaviour problems from PGIMER, is in Section 11.9. Bhatia's Battery of Performance Tests of Intelligence, which supplies two tests to the PGI-BBD, is in Chapter 6. Table 11.12 brings the Indian neuropsychological tools together.

Table 11.12 The Indian neuropsychological tools.
ToolAuthors and yearInstitutionWhat it containsAges
NIMHANS Neuropsychology Battery (adult)Shobini L. Rao, D. K. Subbakrishna, K. Gopukumar, 2004 (first battery: Mukundan and Murthy, 1979)NIMHANS, Bengaluru19 adapted tests of speed, attention, executive function, comprehension, learning and memory; percentile norms by age, sex and education16 to 65
NIMHANS Neuropsychological Battery for ChildrenBhoomika R. Kar, Shobini L. Rao, B. A. Chandramouli, K. Thennarasu, 2004NIMHANS, BengaluruChild versions of motor, attention, executive, visuoperceptual, comprehension and memory tests5 to 15

AIIMS Comprehensive Neuropsychological Battery in Hindi (adult form)S. Gupta, S. K. Khandelwal, P. N. Tandon and colleagues, 2000AIIMS, New Delhi160 items in 10 basic and 4 secondary scales, modelled on the Luria-Nebraska15 to 80
ICMR Neurocognitive Tool Box (ICMR-NCTB)ICMR-NCTB consortium; design 2020, validation 2021Indian Council of Medical Research (multicentre)Attention and executive, episodic memory, language and visuospatial tests in 5 languages, for dementia diagnosisOlder adults

Which should a clinician choose? The answer follows from the question, just as Section 11.1 said. If a Hindi-speaking adult is referred with the old question, "is there any brain dysfunction, and how much?", and a single rating is wanted quickly, the PGI-BBD was built for that. If the question is "which functions are impaired, and which are spared?", as it is when planning rehabilitation, measuring change after cognitive retraining or preparing for brain surgery, the domain-by-domain profile of the NIMHANS battery, with its age-, sex- and education-matched percentiles, is the better tool; the NIMHANS children's battery answers the same question for a child of 5 to 15. If the complaint is memory, the PGI Memory Scale on its own takes far less time than either battery. If an older person may be developing dementia, the NNB-E or the ICMR-NCTB, which were built for that question, are better than a general battery. And whichever battery is used, the clinician still asks of every score the question this section began with: compared with whom?

Indian context

These tools are not museum pieces. The Indian Psychiatric Society's clinical practice guidelines published in the Indian Journal of Psychiatry in 2025 list the PGI-BBD, the PGI Memory Scale, the NIMHANS Neuropsychological Battery, the AIIMS battery and the ICMR-NCTB among the tools for assessing cognitive impairment after traumatic brain injury, and the guidelines on alcohol use disorder list the NIMHANS, AIIMS, ICMR and PGI batteries alongside the Luria-Nebraska and the Halstead-Reitan. The same alcohol guidelines describe a brief cognitive screen for alcohol use disorder built from three tests of the NIMHANS battery (finger tapping, the N-back and the Auditory Verbal Learning Test). The tools were built where they are still most used: the NIMHANS batteries in Bengaluru, the PGI tests in Chandigarh and the AIIMS battery in New Delhi.

Exam trap

Indian syllabi (the RML and IHBAS syllabi among them) name these tools, and the questions usually test the pairing of tool, author and institution: NIMHANS battery, Rao, Bengaluru; PGI-BBD, Pershad and Verma, Chandigarh; PGI Memory Scale, Pershad and Wig, Chandigarh; AIIMS battery, Hindi, based on the Luria-Nebraska, New Delhi. Two other traps: the Hindi Mental State Examination is a screening test (Section 11.7), not a battery; and an option that calls the NIMHANS battery "a Hindi adaptation of the Luria-Nebraska" has confused it with the AIIMS battery.

Scan Your Brain
