11.4 Memory Tests: The Wechsler Memory Scale, Digit Span and the Learning TestsLower yield · 3 Qs

"Doctor, she keeps forgetting." Few complaints bring more people to a clinic, and few are vaguer. The daughter who says it may mean that her mother repeats the same question five times in an hour, or loses her spectacles, or cannot recall what she cooked yesterday, or has started to confuse the names of her grandchildren, or cannot follow a television serial any more. Each of these is a failure of something people call memory, but they are not failures of the same thing. A clinician who simply writes "memory poor" in the notes has said almost nothing. Is the trouble in taking information in, in keeping it, or in getting it back out? Is it worse for words or for pictures? Does it affect what was learned this morning, or what was learned forty years ago as well? Is it really memory at all, or is the mother simply not attending, or too depressed to try?

A memory test is a way of answering these questions under controlled conditions. It puts known material into the person at a known moment and asks for it back at chosen moments afterwards, so that the examiner knows exactly what was offered, when, and how much came back. Everything else in this section is a variation on that plan: a few digits asked for at once, a list of words offered five times, a story asked for after half an hour, a drawing copied and then drawn again from memory. The theory of memory itself, the stores, the working memory model and the kinds of forgetting, is taught in Book 1, Chapter 7, and the disorders of memory, the amnesias and dementias, in Book 3, Sections 2.7 and 10.8.3. This section is about the tests: what each one asks, how it is scored, and how a pattern of scores is read.

What a memory examination asks

Start with the three processes that every account of memory recognises (Book 1, Section 7.1.1). Getting information in is encoding, keeping it over time is storage, and getting it back out when it is needed is retrieval. A person can fail at any of the three, and the remedy, the diagnosis and the advice to the family differ for each. The art of memory testing is to arrange the moments of a test so that each process shows itself separately.

Clinicians and cognitive psychologists use slightly different words for the same territory, and you need both vocabularies. At the bedside (Book 2, Section 2.6.5) memory is divided by how long ago the material was learned. The first, immediate memory, is the reproduction of material within seconds of hearing or seeing it; recent memory covers the last few minutes, hours and days; remote memory covers months and years. Cognitive psychology divides memory by the stores and systems involved: short-term memory, working memory and long-term memory, which divides in turn into episodic, semantic and procedural memory (Book 1, Sections 7.2 and 7.5). The two vocabularies do not map neatly onto each other. When a neuropsychologist reads a list of fifteen words and asks for them back at once, she calls the result "immediate recall", yet fifteen words are far more than short-term memory can hold, so much of what comes back has already been carried into long-term memory. "Immediate" in a test manual means "asked for straight away", not "held in the short-term store".

With those words in hand, the moments of a typical memory examination fall into place, and Figure 11.7 sets them out. The examiner first presents the material: a list of words, a short story, a set of word pairs or a design. She then asks for it back at once. If the person cannot reproduce even a short sequence immediately, the problem is one of attention or registration, and there is no point in asking whether the material was stored, because it never got in. If the material is offered several times, the examiner can watch learning happen: a healthy person recalls more on each trial, and the rise across trials is the learning curve. Next comes something to interfere: a second list, or other tasks. Then, after a delay of about twenty to thirty minutes filled with other tests so that the person cannot rehearse, comes delayed recall, the best single measure of whether new material has been retained. Last comes a recognition trial, in which the person is shown the studied items mixed with new ones and asked which were on the list. Recognition is easier than recall because the item itself acts as a cue (Book 1, Section 7.1.3), and the contrast between the two is one of the most useful comparisons in the whole of neuropsychology: if a person cannot recall the words but can recognise them, they were stored and the problem lies in retrieval; if he cannot even recognise them, they were never stored.

Two more distinctions complete the map. The first is between kinds of material. Verbal memory (words, stories, word pairs) and visual memory (designs, faces, locations) can fail separately, and the classic finding behind this is that people who had the left temporal lobe removed for epilepsy lost more verbal memory while those who lost the right temporal lobe lost more memory for designs and faces. A deficit confined to one kind of material is a material-specific memory deficit, and it is the reason every good battery tests both. The second is between new learning and old memories. Failure to lay down new memories after an illness or injury is anterograde amnesia; loss of memories laid down before it is retrograde amnesia (Book 1, Section 7.10.1). Almost every test in this section measures new learning, which is what most illnesses damage first; remote memory needs its own tests (Section 11.4.7). Table 11.13 brings the kinds of memory together with the tasks that test them.

Table 11.13 The kinds of memory a clinician tests, and how.
What is testedWhat the person doesTypical tasksTheory in Book 1
Immediate spanRepeats a short sequence straight backDigits forward, Corsi blocksSection 7.2.3
Working memoryHolds a sequence and rearranges itDigits backward, digit sequencing, Spatial AdditionSections 7.3.4 and 7.3.6
LearningImproves over repeated presentationsRey AVLT, CVLT, word pairsSection 7.1
Delayed recallRecalls after a filled delay of 20 to 30 minutesStory, list and design recallSection 7.2.5
RecognitionPicks studied items out from new onesYes or no lists, multiple choiceSection 7.1.3

Exam trap

"Immediate memory" in a test manual means recall asked for straight after presentation, not the short-term store of cognitive psychology: fifteen words recalled at once are mostly long-term memory already. "Recent memory" at the bedside (minutes to days) is long-term memory for recent events. Recognition is easier than recall; good recognition with poor recall points to a retrieval problem, poor recognition to a storage problem.

Digit span forward and backward

The oldest memory test in the clinic is also the simplest. The examiner says a string of digits and the person says them back. Joseph Jacobs measured span in this way in 1887 (Book 1, Section 7.2.3), Alfred Binet put the repetition of digits into his intelligence scale, and David Wechsler made it a subtest of his adult and school-age intelligence scales and of his original memory scale. A test that has lasted well over a century must be doing something useful. What exactly is it measuring?

First, the procedure, because the details matter. In digit span forward the examiner reads the digits at a steady rate of about one per second, in an even voice, without grouping them the way people group a telephone number, and the person repeats them in the same order. The strings start short and grow by one digit at a time, and there are two different strings at each length. Testing stops when the person fails both strings of the same length. In digit span backward the person hears a new set of strings and must say each one in reverse: hearing 7, 2, 9 he must say 9, 2, 7. The fourth edition of the Wechsler Adult Intelligence Scale (2008) added a third task, digit sequencing, in which the person repeats the digits in ascending order, so that hearing 7, 2, 9 he says 2, 7, 9. Two numbers come out of each task. The longest span is the length of the longest string the person repeated correctly on at least one of its two trials. The points are simply the number of trials passed. Wechsler's scales add the points across the tasks into a raw score and convert it into a scaled score against age norms (Chapter 5 explains scaled scores).

Work through an invented record to see how the counting goes. Neel is a 24-year-old postgraduate student who volunteered as a healthy comparison subject in a hospital study. His performance is set out in Table 11.14.

Table 11.14 Neel's digit span (an invented record). Two strings are given at each length; one point for each string repeated correctly.
Digits in the stringForward: trials 1 and 2Forward pointsBackward: trials 1 and 2Backward points
2Not given (forward starts at 3)−Pass, pass2
3Pass, pass2Pass, pass2
4Pass, pass2Pass, fail1
5Pass, pass2Fail, pass1
6Pass, fail1Fail, fail (stop)0
7Fail, pass1−−
8Fail, fail (stop)0−−

Read the table one task at a time.

  • Forward, longest span: the longest length at which Neel passed at least one trial is 7 (he failed the first string of seven digits but passed the second). His forward span is therefore 7.
  • Forward, points: 2 + 2 + 2 + 1 + 1 + 0 = 8.
  • Backward, longest span: he passed at least one trial at length 5 (the second one) but neither at length 6, so his backward span is 5.
  • Backward, points: 2 + 2 + 1 + 1 + 0 = 6.
  • Total points for the two tasks: 8 + 6 = 14.
  • Forward span minus backward span: 7 − 5 = 2.

How do these numbers compare with other adults? Most adults of average education repeat six or seven digits forward and four or five backward, backward span is usually one or two digits shorter than forward span, and a forward span below five is usually abnormal (Book 2, Section 2.6.4, gives the same bedside figures). Neel's 7 forward and 5 backward, two apart, are ordinary healthy values. Note that span depends on schooling, on hearing and on language: digit names that take longer to say give a shorter span (Book 1, Section 7.3.2), so a person tested in a language other than his own, or compared with norms collected in another language, can look worse than he is.

Now to what the two tasks measure, which is what the papers ask. Repeating digits in the order heard requires the person to attend to the string, register it and hold it for a few seconds: it measures immediate attention span, the capacity of the short-term store that George Miller put at about seven items (Book 1, Section 7.2.3). Repeating them backwards requires all of that and more. The person must hold the string while he works on it, reading it off from the end, keeping his place and suppressing the urge to say it the way it came in. Holding and manipulating information at the same time is exactly what Alan Baddeley and Graham Hitch meant by working memory (Book 1, Sections 7.3.4 and 7.3.6), and backward span is the classic clinical test of it. Digit sequencing is a second working memory task of the same kind, and Letter-Number Sequencing in the Wechsler scales (hear "7, C, 2, A", say "2, 7, A, C") is a third. There is a visual counterpart too. In the Corsi block-tapping test, devised by Philip Corsi at McGill University in 1972, the examiner taps a sequence on blocks scattered on a board and the person taps the same sequence back, which gives a visuospatial span.

What do span scores tell a clinician? Three things, mainly. A short forward span tells you that attention and registration are impaired, as they are in delirium, in severe anxiety, in acute intoxication and in many people who are simply exhausted or unwilling, and it warns you that every later memory test will be hard to interpret, because material that never got in cannot be stored. A normal forward span alongside very poor delayed recall is the signature of the classic amnesic syndrome: the patient H.M. could repeat digits normally yet could not learn a new list (Book 1, Section 7.2.6). A disproportionately poor backward span points to a weakness of working memory and executive control, common after frontal and diffuse brain injury, in attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder and in schizophrenia. What span does not tell you is anything about long-term retention, about memory for events or about skills. An impossibly low span in a person who chats fluently is itself informative, because it can be a sign of poor effort; Section 11.1 explains how effort is checked.

Clinical link

Span is fragile in exactly the people a psychiatric clinician sees most. An anxious student at a viva, a man three days into alcohol withdrawal and a woman with a high fever can all show a forward span of four. Before concluding that memory is impaired, check that the person can hear you, is not delirious (Book 3, Section 10.8.8), understands the language of testing and is trying. Repeat the test later in the session if anxiety seems to be the cause: anxious span improves with reassurance; the span of a damaged brain does not.

Exam trap

Backward digit span measures working memory (a real 2025 paper asked exactly this), because the digits must be held and manipulated at once. Forward span measures immediate attention span (short-term memory). The wrong options are all real memory systems tested in other ways: long-term memory is tested by delayed recall after a filled delay of 20 to 30 minutes (Section 11.4.4); sensory memory, the brief iconic or echoic trace lasting a fraction of a second to a few seconds, is studied with laboratory methods such as Sperling's partial report (Book 1, Section 7.2.2), not with spoken digits; procedural memory, memory for skills, is tested by learning a motor task such as mirror drawing, which H.M. learned without remembering the practice (Book 1, Sections 7.5.1 and 7.10.2). Amnesic patients can have a normal digit span, which is exactly why span is not a test of long-term memory.

The Wechsler Memory Scale

Digit span tells you whether material gets in. It cannot tell you whether a story heard ten minutes ago is still there, or whether a design is remembered as well as a sentence. For that a clinician needs a battery of memory tests built on the same plan and standardised on the same people, and the most widely used one is the work of David Wechsler (1896 to 1981). Wechsler, the chief psychologist at Bellevue Hospital in New York, had published the Wechsler-Bellevue Intelligence Scale in 1939 (Book 1, Section 9.3.2; Chapter 6). In 1945 he published "A standardized memory scale for clinical use" in the Journal of Psychology: a short memory examination that any trained psychologist could give in the same way, so that the memory of one patient could be compared with that of others and with the same patient's intelligence.

The original Wechsler Memory Scale (WMS) had seven subtests, which you should know by type rather than by item. Personal and Current Information asked simple questions about the person's own life and about well-known public facts. Orientation asked about time and place. Mental Control asked for overlearned sequences under pressure, such as counting backwards or running through the alphabet. Logical Memory read two short stories aloud and asked the person to retell each at once, scoring the number of ideas recalled. Memory Span was digits forward and backward. Visual Reproduction showed simple geometric designs for a few seconds and asked the person to draw them from memory. Associate Learning read pairs of words, some easy because the words belonged together and some hard because they did not, and then gave the first word of each pair and asked for the second, over three trials. The raw points were added, an age correction was added to the total, and a table converted the result into a memory quotient (MQ) on the same scale as the Wechsler-Bellevue IQ. The point of that design was comparison: if a patient's MQ fell far below his IQ, his memory was worse than his general ability would predict. An alternate form, Form II, followed in 1946 (by C. P. Stone, J. Girdner and R. Albrecht) so that a patient could be retested without simply remembering the stories; why alternate forms help is explained in Chapter 3, Section 3.3.

The 1945 scale was used for four decades, and its weaknesses taught the field a great deal. All of its recall was immediate, so it could not detect the rapid forgetting over half an hour that is the hallmark of the amnesic syndrome. Its single MQ mixed true memory with orientation, attention and general knowledge, so two patients with the same MQ could have quite different problems. It was almost entirely verbal, which made it blind to many right-hemisphere disorders, and its norms came from a small sample. Clinicians patched it first: in 1975 Elbert Russell added a recall of the stories and designs after a delay of about thirty minutes. Then the publisher rebuilt it, four times over, and Table 11.15 traces the editions.

Table 11.15 The Wechsler Memory Scale from 1945 to the WMS-5.
EditionYearWhat changedSummary scores
WMS (Form I)1945Seven brief subtests, all recall immediateOne memory quotient (MQ)
Form II1946Alternate form for retesting (Stone, Girdner and Albrecht)MQ
Russell's revision1975Stories and designs recalled again after about 30 minutesImmediate and delayed scores
WMS-R1987Delayed recall built in; more visual subtestsGeneral Memory, Attention/Concentration, Verbal Memory, Visual Memory, Delayed Recall
WMS-III1997Auditory and visual, immediate and delayed separated; recognition and working memory addedUsually counted as eight primary indices, including Working Memory and General Memory
WMS-IV2009Adult (16 to 69) and Older Adult (65 to 90) batteries; co-normed with the WAIS-IVFive indices: Auditory, Visual, Visual Working, Immediate and Delayed Memory
WMS-52025Same seven subtests with new content; co-normed with the WAIS-5; ages 16 to 90The same five primary indices, plus ancillary indices

The fourth edition, the WMS-IV of 2009, is the one the papers ask about, and its successor the WMS-5 (2025) kept its plan, so learn it well. It was standardised together with the Wechsler Adult Intelligence Scale, Fourth Edition, on about 1,400 adults in the United States, which means that a person's memory scores and intelligence scores come from the same norms and can be compared directly. There are two batteries. The Adult battery, for ages 16 to 69, has six core subtests and an optional screen. The Older Adult battery, for ages 65 to 90, leaves out two of the visual subtests to keep testing short. (For people aged 65 to 69 the examiner may use either.) Most subtests come in two parts: part I is the immediate recall, and part II is the recall of the same material after a delay of 20 to 30 minutes, usually followed by a recognition trial. Table 11.16 describes the subtests in general terms; the actual stories, pairs and designs are protected test material.

Table 11.16 The WMS-IV Adult battery: what each subtest asks and which index it feeds.
SubtestWhat the person doesPart I (immediate) feedsPart II (delayed) feeds
Logical MemoryRetells short stories read aloudAuditory and Immediate MemoryAuditory and Delayed Memory
Verbal Paired AssociatesLearns word pairs over trials; given the first word, says the secondAuditory and Immediate MemoryAuditory and Delayed Memory
DesignsRemembers which designs appeared and where on a gridVisual and Immediate MemoryVisual and Delayed Memory
Visual ReproductionDraws designs from memoryVisual and Immediate MemoryVisual and Delayed Memory
Spatial AdditionCombines two grids of dots in the mind and shows the resultVisual Working MemoryNo delayed part
Symbol SpanRecalls a series of abstract symbols in the order shownVisual Working MemoryNo delayed part
Brief Cognitive Status ExamQuick tasks such as orientation, mental control and clock drawingOptional screen; not in an index−

Read the table as a grid and the five indices fall out of it. The Auditory Memory index combines the two verbal subtests, Logical Memory and Verbal Paired Associates, parts I and II. The Visual Memory index combines Designs and Visual Reproduction, parts I and II. The Visual Working Memory index combines Spatial Addition and Symbol Span. The Immediate Memory index takes the four part I scores, and the Delayed Memory index the four part II scores. So the same subtest scores are cut two ways, by kind of material (auditory against visual) and by time (immediate against delayed), and the two cuts answer the two questions the clinician most wants answered: is memory worse for words or for designs, and is the trouble in learning or in keeping? Notice what is missing. The WMS-III had a Working Memory index built on an auditory task (letter-number sequencing) and a visual one (spatial span); the WMS-IV dropped the auditory working memory task, because the WAIS-IV already measures auditory working memory with digit span and arithmetic, and kept only visual working memory, now measured by two new subtests. And because the Older Adult battery omits Designs and Spatial Addition, it reports only four indices, with no Visual Working Memory index at all.

Each subtest gives a scaled score with a mean of 10 and a standard deviation of 3, and each index an index score with a mean of 100 and a standard deviation of 15, the same metric as an IQ (Chapter 5 teaches standard scores in full). To read an index score, turn it into a distance from the mean in standard deviation units, a z score.

Remember

How far an index score lies from the average:

z = (index score − 100) / 15

where 100 is the mean of the index scores in the norm group of the person's age, 15 is their standard deviation, and z is the number of standard deviations the person stands above (positive) or below (negative) that mean.

Urvi is an invented 71-year-old retired schoolteacher whose family noticed over the past year that she asks the same questions again and again. She is given the Older Adult battery. Her General Ability Index from the WAIS-IV, which reflects reasoning rather than memory, is 102. Her WMS-IV indices are Auditory Memory 78, Visual Memory 74, Immediate Memory 82 and Delayed Memory 70.

  • Delayed Memory: z = (70 − 100) / 15 = −30 / 15 = −2.00. On the normal curve about 2 people in 100 score this low or lower (the exact table value is 2.3 percent), so the score is at about the 2nd percentile.
  • Immediate Memory: z = (82 − 100) / 15 = −18 / 15 = −1.20, about the 12th percentile (table value 11.5 percent, rounded to the nearest whole percentile).
  • The gap between general ability and delayed memory: 102 − 70 = 32 points, a little more than two standard deviations (32 / 15 = 2.13).

What does this mean in plain words? Urvi reasons like an average woman of her age, but she keeps new material far worse than her reasoning predicts, and she loses more over the delay than she fails to take in at first (her delayed score is 12 points below her immediate score). A memory deficit out of proportion to general ability, worst after a delay, is the pattern of a disorder of storage such as early Alzheimer's disease, although no single test makes that diagnosis (Book 3, Section 10.9.6). Whether a gap between two scores is larger than measurement error alone would produce is a question for the standard error of the difference (Chapter 3, Section 3.7.6); the WMS-IV manual supplies tables for exactly these comparisons, which is one reason the co-norming with the WAIS-IV matters.

Exam trap

The WMS-IV (and the WMS-5) indices are Auditory Memory, Visual Memory, Visual Working Memory, Immediate Memory and Delayed Memory. The lures each come from somewhere real. Verbal IQ and Performance IQ were the summary scores of the old Wechsler intelligence scales up to the WAIS-III; the WAIS-IV replaced them with four indices (Chapter 6). Crystallised and fluid intelligence are Raymond Cattell's and John Horn's broad abilities, a theory of intelligence (Chapter 6). "Attention, concentration and vigilance only" borrows the WMS-R's Attention/Concentration index; vigilance is measured by continuous performance tests (Section 11.6). Mechanical Reasoning is a subtest of the Differential Aptitude Tests of George Bennett, Harold Seashore and Alexander Wesman (1947), an aptitude battery (Chapter 8). An index called plain "Working Memory" belonged to the WMS-III. Only the 1945 scale gave a memory quotient.

Learning a list: the Rey Auditory Verbal Learning Test

Batteries like the WMS give a broad survey. When the question is specifically how a person learns, and where learning breaks down, many clinicians turn to a list of words read again and again. The idea goes back to Geneva. Édouard Claparède (1873 to 1940) used a list of fifteen words, read once, as a memory test. His student André Rey (1906 to 1965) turned it into a learning test by reading the list five times and asking for recall after each reading, and he and the workers who adapted his test added an interfering list, a delayed recall and a recognition trial. Rey's version is usually cited from his book of 1958 (some sources give 1964, so expect either date); an English version appeared in 1959, and Muriel Lezak's textbook made it standard in the English-speaking world. Today it is called the Rey Auditory Verbal Learning Test (Rey AVLT or RAVLT), and a version of it is part of the NIMHANS battery, with Indian norms (Section 11.3).

The procedure follows the moments of Figure 11.7 almost exactly. The examiner reads List A, fifteen common nouns that have nothing to do with one another, at about one word per second, and asks the person to say as many as he can, in any order. This is Trial I. The whole list is read again, and the person again recalls all he can, including the words he gave before: Trial II. And so on to Trial V. The examiner writes down every word in the order given, and notes repetitions and intrusions (words that were never on the list). Next comes List B, fifteen new words read once and recalled once. Then, without hearing List A again, the person is asked for List A: Trial VI. After a delay of 20 to 30 minutes, filled with tests that are not verbal so that he cannot rehearse, he is asked for List A once more: Trial VII. Last comes a recognition trial, in which the List A words are mixed with others and the person picks out the ones he heard (the formats differ: a printed list of words, or a short story that contains them). Healthy young adults usually recall about six or seven words on Trial I and about twelve or thirteen by Trial V; older adults recall fewer, and people with more schooling more, which is why the norms are given by age and education.

The power of the test lies in the comparisons it allows, and each comparison has a simple formula. Learn them in words first.

Remember

The main Rey AVLT indices (I to VII are the words recalled on each trial, B the words recalled from List B):

Total learning = I + II + III + IV + V (out of 75)

Learning over trials = V − I

Proactive interference ratio = B / I

Retention after interference = (VI / V) × 100

Retention after delay = (VII / V) × 100

Corrected recognition = hits − false positives

where a hit is a List A word correctly recognised and a false positive (false alarm) is a word wrongly claimed to have been on List A. Total learning says how much was learned overall; V − I says how much the person gained by repetition; B / I compares a fresh list heard once (B) with the first hearing of List A (I), so a ratio well below 1 means the first list is getting in the way of the new one; the two retention percentages say how much of what had been learned by Trial V survived the interfering list and the delay.

Figure 11.8 plots three invented records, and Table 11.17 works the indices for each. Neel is the healthy 24-year-old you met in Section 11.4.2. Mridul is a 58-year-old bank manager admitted with a severe depressive episode, who complains that his memory "has gone". Urvi is the 71-year-old you met in Section 11.4.3.

Table 11.17 The Rey AVLT indices worked for the three invented records in Figure 11.8.
IndexNeel (24, healthy)Mridul (58, depressed)Urvi (71, forgetful)
Trials I to V7, 10, 12, 13, 144, 6, 7, 8, 93, 5, 5, 6, 6
Total learning563425
Learning over trials (V − I)753
List B; ratio B / I7; 1.004; 1.002; 0.67
Trial VI; (VI / V) × 10013; 93%7; 78%2; 33%
Trial VII; (VII / V) × 10013; 93%7; 78%0; 0%
Recognition: hits − false positives15 − 0 = 1514 − 1 = 138 − 5 = 3

Push one record through every step, so that none of the arithmetic is mysterious. Take Urvi.

  • Total learning: 3 + 5 + 5 + 6 + 6 = 25 words out of a possible 75.
  • Learning over trials: V − I = 6 − 3 = 3. Five repetitions bought her only three extra words.
  • Proactive interference ratio: B / I = 2 / 3 = 0.667, which rounds to 0.67. A fresh list heard once gave her fewer words than the first hearing of List A, so the earlier list is crowding out the new one.
  • Retention after interference: VI / V = 2 / 6 = 0.333; multiplied by 100, 33 percent. Hearing List B wiped out two thirds of what she had built up by Trial V.
  • Retention after delay: VII / V = 0 / 6 = 0; 0 percent. After half an hour nothing is left.
  • Corrected recognition: she picked out 8 of the 15 List A words (hits) but also claimed 5 words that were not on the list (false positives): 8 − 5 = 3. Even with the words in front of her she can barely tell old from new.

Now do Neel's two retention figures yourself and check them against the table: VI / V = 13 / 14 = 0.929, which is 93 percent when rounded to the nearest whole percent; VII / V gives the same 93 percent.

Set the three records side by side and the test starts to talk. Neel's curve is the healthy shape: a good first trial, a steep rise, a small dip after the interfering list and almost no loss over the delay. Urvi shows the pattern of a storage deficit. She learns little however often the list is read, she loses what she has learned when another list intervenes and over a delay, and recognition does not rescue her, because the words were never laid down in a lasting form; her many false positives show that she cannot even tell which words are familiar. Rapid forgetting with poor recognition is the mark of damage to the medial temporal lobe memory system, and it is typical of Alzheimer's disease and of the amnesic syndromes. Mridul is different. His learning is slow and his free recall poor, but what he learned by Trial V he keeps: there is no further loss between Trial VI and the delayed Trial VII (7 and 7), and when the words are put in front of him he recognises 14 of the 15. The words were stored; he has trouble getting them out. This is a retrieval deficit, the pattern seen when the frontal and subcortical systems that organise effortful search are not working well: in depression, in Parkinson's and Huntington's diseases, in subcortical vascular disease and, to a milder degree, in normal ageing.

A few further details complete the reading. A very low Trial I with a normal rise afterwards suggests a problem of attention or initial encoding rather than of storage. Many intrusions and repetitions suggest poor monitoring of one's own recall, common after frontal damage and in Korsakoff's syndrome. And the serial position of the words recalled matters: healthy people recall words from the start (primacy) and the end (recency) of the list best, while patients with amnesia keep the recency effect, which depends on the short-term store, and lose the primacy effect, which depends on transfer to long-term memory (Book 1, Section 7.2.6).

Clinical link

Mridul's complaint is one of the commonest referrals in a psychiatric hospital: is this depression or the beginning of dementia? Depressed patients often complain bitterly of poor memory, try little, answer "I don't know", and recall poorly; but they usually recognise well and do not forget over a delay, and they get better as the depression lifts. When depression produces a picture that looks like dementia it is called pseudodementia (Book 3, Section 10.11.6). The recall versus recognition contrast of a list-learning test is one of the most useful pieces of evidence for telling the two apart, but it is evidence, not a verdict: depression and early dementia can coexist, and depression in late life is sometimes the first sign of a dementing illness, so a retest after treatment is wise.

Exam trap

Rey AVLT = 15 unrelated words, 5 trials, then List B, then recall of List A (VI), then delayed recall after 20 to 30 minutes (VII), then recognition. Keep the two interferences straight with the rule from Book 1, Section 7.8.2: proactive means old disrupts new, so poor recall of List B because of List A is proactive interference; retroactive means new disrupts old, so the drop from Trial V to Trial VI caused by List B is retroactive interference. Poor recall with good recognition is a retrieval deficit; poor recall with poor recognition is a storage deficit. Rey is also the Rey of the complex figure (Section 11.4.6).

The California Verbal Learning Test and how people organise what they learn

Rey's words were chosen to have nothing in common, so the only way to learn them is by rote. Real memory rarely works like that. When you go to the market with a mental list, you probably group the items without trying: the vegetables together, the spices together, the things for the puja together. Groups make a list easier to learn and easier to search, and whether a person spontaneously finds the groups says a good deal about the brain systems that organise memory. That insight, and Edith Kaplan's broader conviction that the way a person fails matters as much as the score (the process approach, Section 11.1), lie behind the California Verbal Learning Test (CVLT) of Dean C. Delis, Joel Kramer, Edith Kaplan and Beth Ober (1987).

The CVLT is built like the Rey test but with one crucial change: its list is a shopping list. Sixteen words are read, four from each of four categories, mixed so that no two words from the same category come one after another. As in the Rey test there are five learning trials, then an interfering second list, then recall of the first list straight away (short-delay free recall) and with the category names given as cues (short-delay cued recall), then free and cued recall again after about twenty minutes, and finally recognition. The second edition (CVLT-II, 2000) and the third (CVLT-3, 2017) kept the plan, and a children's version (CVLT-C, 1994) uses a shorter list. Because the categories are known, the examiner can score not only how many words come back but how they come back.

The central process score is clustering. In semantic clustering the person recalls words from the same category together, which shows that he has noticed the structure of the list and is using it, an active strategy that depends on the frontal lobes. In serial clustering he recalls the words in the order he heard them, a passive strategy that leans on the sequence of input. A simplified way to see the difference is to count, in a recall, the adjacent pairs of words that come from the same category, and divide by the number of adjacent pairs (one fewer than the number of words recalled). Suppose two invented people each recall eight words, and we write F for fruit, T for tool, C for clothing and S for spice. The first recalls F, F, F, T, T, C, C, S. Of his seven adjacent pairs, four join two words of the same category (his three fruits make two such pairs, his two tools one and his two clothes one), so his index is 4 / 7 = 0.571, or 57 percent to the nearest whole percent. The second recalls the words in the order heard, F, T, C, S, F, T, C, S; none of her seven adjacent pairs is from the same category, so her index is 0 / 7 = 0. Both recalled eight words, but only the first used the structure of the list. (The published test uses a more refined formula that also corrects for the clustering expected by chance.)

The cued recall trials add a second process comparison. If a person recalls few words freely but many more when given the category names, the words were stored and the cues have helped him search for them, a retrieval pattern like Mridul's. If cues add little, the words were not stored. With these scores the CVLT became a tool for distinguishing patterns of memory disorder: patients with Alzheimer's disease, for example, forget rapidly and make many intrusions, while patients with Huntington's disease retrieve poorly but recognise and benefit from cues much better.

Exam trap

Rey AVLT: 15 unrelated words, so no strategy but rote. CVLT (Delis, Kramer, Kaplan and Ober, 1987): 16 words in 4 categories (a shopping list), so it can measure semantic clustering, cued recall and intrusions. Edith Kaplan's name on the CVLT links it to the process approach.

Visual memory: the Rey-Osterrieth Complex Figure and the Benton Visual Retention Test

Words are easy to rehearse silently, so a person with a weak memory system can sometimes prop up his verbal scores by talking to himself. Designs that cannot easily be named are harder to prop up, which is why visual memory tests use abstract figures. Two are classics.

The first again comes from André Rey. In 1941, in a paper on the psychological examination of people with traumatic brain injury, he introduced a complicated abstract line drawing: a large rectangle crossed by diagonals and midlines, with many smaller details inside it and attached to its sides. The drawing is meaningless, so it cannot be remembered as a picture of something, and it is complicated, so it cannot be copied well without a plan. In 1944 his colleague Paul-Alexandre Osterrieth standardised its administration and scoring, and the test has carried both names ever since: the Rey-Osterrieth Complex Figure (ROCF, or simply "the Rey figure"). The figure itself is protected test material; the description above is all you need.

Key study: Osterrieth standardises the Rey figure (1944)

Osterrieth asked a developmental question: how do people of different ages go about copying a figure too complex to take in at a glance? He gave the copy task to about 230 children and 60 adults and watched not only how accurate each copy was but in what order it was drawn. He sorted the copying procedures into a small number of types, from building the whole drawing on the big central rectangle and fitting the details to it, through drawing the outline first or assembling the details one beside another, down to a mere scribble. Young children mostly pieced details together; with age the approach became organised around the main structure, and most adults began with the large rectangle. He also divided the figure into 18 scoring units. The conclusion still guides practice: a copy shows how a person perceives and plans, so it must be judged before the recall is read as a memory score. Later workers added qualitative scoring systems for exactly this planning, because the 18-unit score alone says nothing about how the copy was made.

Administration has three stages. The person first copies the figure with it in full view, and the examiner times the copy and records the order in which the parts are drawn (in the process approach, by handing over pencils of different colours at intervals, so that the sequence can be read later). Then, without warning, the figure is taken away and the person draws it from memory, a few minutes later (immediate recall) and again after about 30 minutes (delayed recall). John and Kelly Meyers's version (1995) adds a recognition trial. Each drawing is scored on Osterrieth's 18 units: a unit earns 2 points if it is correct and correctly placed, 1 point if it is correct but misplaced or distorted but well placed, half a point if it is distorted and misplaced but still recognisable, and nothing if it is missing or unrecognisable. The maximum is 18 × 2 = 36. Because the copy and the recall are scored on the same scale, a simple percentage tells you how much of what was drawn was remembered.

Remember

Complex figure score and retention:

Score = (units scoring 2 × 2) + (units scoring 1 × 1) + (units scoring 0.5 × 0.5), out of 36

Percentage retained = (delayed recall score / copy score) × 100

where the copy score shows what the person was able to perceive and construct with the figure in view, and the delayed recall score what remained after about 30 minutes.

Return to Urvi. On the copy, 13 units earn 2 points, 3 units earn 1 point, 2 units earn half a point and none earns nothing (13 + 3 + 2 + 0 = 18 units, so every unit is accounted for). Her copy score is (13 × 2) + (3 × 1) + (2 × 0.5) = 26 + 3 + 1 = 30 out of 36, a reasonable copy for her age. Her delayed recall earns 8 points. Percentage retained = (8 / 30) × 100 = 0.2667 × 100 = 26.67, which rounds to 27 percent. Because her copy is adequate, her poor recall cannot be blamed on poor seeing or poor drawing: it is a genuine failure of visual memory, which fits her low WMS-IV Visual Memory index and her verbal storage pattern on the Rey AVLT. Contrast two other invented patients. A man with damage to the right parietal lobe produces a fragmented copy that loses the overall shape and leaves out details on the left; his recall will be poor too, but it cannot be read as a memory failure, because the figure was never properly perceived. A woman with frontal damage copies the details accurately but piece by piece, with no overall plan, and then recalls very little, because a disorganised copy is a poorly encoded one. The same recall score of 8 means three different things in the three patients, and only the copy tells you which.

The second classic is the work of Arthur Lester Benton (1909 to 2006), whose other tests you will meet in Section 11.5. During the Second World War Benton served as a psychologist in the United States Navy, and at the San Diego Naval Hospital he examined servicemen with penetrating brain wounds alongside the neurologist Morris Bender (a different Bender from the one in Section 11.5). Out of that work came a short test of memory for designs, described in 1945 and published as the Benton Visual Retention Test (BVRT) in 1946. Its fifth edition, revised by Abigail Benton Sivan, appeared in the early 1990s.

The BVRT is a set of cards, each showing one or more simple geometric figures; most designs have two large figures and one small figure off to one side. There are three equivalent forms, called C, D and E, of ten designs each, so that a patient can be retested before and after treatment without simply remembering the first set (Chapter 3, Section 3.3). There are also four ways of giving it, labelled Administrations A to D. In Administration A, the standard one, each card is shown for 10 seconds and then removed, and the person draws it from memory at once. In B the exposure is only 5 seconds. In C the person copies the design with the card in view, so there is no memory demand at all. In D the card is shown for 10 seconds and the person waits 15 seconds before drawing. Two scores are kept: the number of designs reproduced correctly (0 to 10) and an error score, with each error classed as an omission, a distortion, a perseveration (repeating a figure from an earlier design), a rotation, a misplacement or an error of size. The manual gives the expected number correct by age and estimated intelligence, so a score well below expectation suggests impairment.

Two features make the BVRT more useful than it looks. The small peripheral figures are sensitive to unilateral neglect: a patient who consistently leaves out the small figure when it is on the left is ignoring the left side of space (Section 11.5.7). And comparing Administration C with Administration A separates two quite different failures. A person who copies well but draws poorly from memory has a visual memory problem; a person who cannot even copy has a problem of perception or construction, and his memory cannot be judged from this test at all. That is the same logic as the Rey figure's copy, and it is the single most important rule for reading any visual memory test: check the copy before you read the recall.

Exam trap

Benton (1946) = visual retention: ten designs drawn from memory, Forms C, D and E, Administrations A to D (C is the copy condition). Bender (1938) = visual-motor copying of nine designs (Section 11.5). The letters are a favourite trick: the forms are C, D, E; the administrations are A, B, C, D. Rey-Osterrieth: Rey devised the figure (1941), Osterrieth standardised it (1944); 18 units × 2 points = 36; copy, then immediate and delayed recall.

Everyday and remote memory, and choosing a memory test

A woman can score poorly on a word list and still run her kitchen; a man can score in the average range on every subtest of the WMS-IV and still forget to take his tablets, miss his appointments and get lost on the way back from the market. Laboratory-style tests sample memory under ideal, quiet, one-to-one conditions, and they say little about the memory demands of ordinary life, especially prospective memory, remembering to do something in the future (Book 1, Section 7.5.5). Barbara A. Wilson, a British neuropsychologist who worked for decades in brain injury rehabilitation, built the Rivermead Behavioural Memory Test (1985) with Janet Cockburn and Alan Baddeley to close that gap. Its tasks imitate everyday demands: remembering the name of a person shown in a photograph, remembering where a belonging of the person's was hidden and asking for it back at the end, remembering to ask about an appointment when an alarm sounds, remembering a short route around the room and a message to deliver on the way, and recognising pictures and faces. Because it measures the memory problems people actually report, it is used mainly to plan and evaluate rehabilitation (Book 2, Section 9.9).

Remote memory needs different tests again, because the examiner did not present the material and must check the answers against someone who knows. The Autobiographical Memory Interview of Michael Kopelman, Barbara Wilson and Alan Baddeley (1989) asks about personal facts (the names of schools and teachers, addresses) and about specific remembered incidents from childhood, early adult life and recent years, and checks them with relatives. Tests of public events and famous faces ask about well-known happenings and people of different decades, and they must be built locally: a question about an Indian election or a Bollywood star of the 1970s means something in Delhi that it would not mean in Denver. In many amnesic patients such tests show a temporal gradient, with the oldest memories best preserved (Book 1, Section 7.10.1).

Two more tools complete the kit. Elizabeth Warrington's Recognition Memory Test (1984) has a words half and a faces half, each a forced choice between a studied item and a new one, so it compares verbal and visual memory directly with a recognition format. And in India the PGI Memory Scale of Dwarka Pershad and N. N. Wig (1977), with its ten subtests and Indian norms, is often the first memory test given to an adult; Section 11.3 teaches it. Effort tests are built to look like memory tests. The Test of Memory Malingering is explained in Section 11.1. Rey's own 15-item test works the same way: fifteen simple characters, arranged in five rows of three that follow obvious patterns, are shown for ten seconds and then drawn from memory. The patterns make the task far easier than it looks, so most people with genuine memory loss reproduce most of the items, and a score below about nine has traditionally raised a question about effort rather than memory.

How does a clinician choose among all these? By the referral question, as always. "Is there a memory problem at all, and how bad?" calls for a broad standardised battery such as the WMS-IV or, in India, the PGI Memory Scale. "Is this a storage or a retrieval problem?" calls for a list-learning test with delayed recall and recognition, the Rey AVLT or the CVLT. "Is it worse for words or for pictures?" calls for matched verbal and visual tests, the auditory and visual indices of the WMS-IV, or a word list against the Rey figure. "Can he manage at home, and is rehabilitation helping?" calls for the Rivermead. "Is he trying?" calls for an effort test. Table 11.18 summarises the tests of this section.

Table 11.18 The main memory tests.
TestAuthor and yearMaterialWhat it measuresTypical use
Digit span forward and backwardJacobs 1887; Wechsler intelligence scalesSpoken digitsAttention span (forward); working memory (backward)Every examination; bedside
Corsi block-tapping testCorsi 1972Blocks tapped in sequenceVisuospatial spanVisual counterpart of digit span

Exam trap

Match the person to the memory test: Wechsler (1945) = Wechsler Memory Scale and the memory quotient; Rey = the Auditory Verbal Learning Test and the complex figure; Osterrieth (1944) = standardised the complex figure; Benton (1946) = Visual Retention Test; Delis, Kramer, Kaplan and Ober (1987) = California Verbal Learning Test; Wilson, Cockburn and Baddeley (1985) = Rivermead Behavioural Memory Test; Pershad and Wig (1977) = PGI Memory Scale. A test of everyday memory is the Rivermead, not the WMS.

11.5 Visuomotor and Visuospatial Tests: The Bender-Gestalt, the Benton Tests and Constructional TasksMedium yield · 6 Qs

Ask a healthy adult to copy a simple drawing, a few dots and lines, and the request seems almost insulting. Yet copying is one of the most demanding things you ask of a brain in a clinic. The eyes must register the lines; the visual system must turn them into a shape and see how its parts relate; something must judge where each part sits, at what angle and how far from the next; something else must plan where on the page to begin; and the hand must be steered, stroke by stroke, under the constant guidance of the eyes. A failure at any of these links spoils the copy, which is why copying tasks were among the first tests of brain damage and why, for half a century, the most popular of them, the Bender-Gestalt, was one of the most widely used psychological tests in the world. This section teaches that test in full, then the shorter, sharper tests of Arthur Benton that isolate single links of the chain, and finally the constructional and neglect tasks that complete a clinician's visual kit.

What visuomotor, visuospatial and constructional tests ask

Four words recur in this area and the papers use them loosely, so fix them now. The first, visuoperception, is the recognition and discrimination of what is seen: telling one shape, one face or one object from another. The second, visuospatial ability, is the judgement of where things are: their position, direction, orientation and distance, and their relations to one another and to the viewer, including the ability to attend to both halves of space. The third, visuoconstruction (constructional ability), is putting parts together to make a whole, by drawing or by assembling blocks or sticks; its loss after brain damage is constructional apraxia, the difficulty in copying a cube or a clock face described in Book 3, Section 10.8.3. The fourth, visual-motor integration, is the coordination of what is seen with fine movements of the hand, the hand-eye coordination that copying, handwriting and threading a needle all need. The first two are about seeing; the last two are about doing what one sees.

Why separate them? Because the same bad copy can come from very different places, and the treatment and the prognosis differ. Damage to the back of the right hemisphere, especially the parietal lobe, tends to wreck the overall shape: the drawing is fragmented, parts are scattered or misplaced, the spatial relations between them are lost, and the left side is often left out altogether. Damage to the left hemisphere tends to leave the overall outline recognisable but simplified, with details missing and the drawing slow and laboured. Damage to the frontal lobes spoils planning: the person starts in the wrong place, runs out of room, repeats elements he has already drawn. Diffuse disorders such as dementia and delirium degrade every element. And a person with poor eyesight, a tremor, an injured hand or very little schooling will copy badly with an entirely healthy brain. So a careful examiner never stops at "the copy is poor". She asks which link failed, and she has tests designed to answer that: matching tasks that need perception but no drawing (Benton's Judgment of Line Orientation and Facial Recognition tests, Section 11.5.6), simple motor tasks that need drawing but little perception, and the copy conditions of memory tests that need drawing but no memory (Administration C of the Benton Visual Retention Test, Section 11.4.6).

Education deserves a special word in India. A woman who has never been to school and has rarely held a pencil may copy a diamond as a lopsided circle and draw overlapping shapes side by side, and an examiner who reads that as brain damage will be badly wrong. Copying tests are more sensitive to schooling than many people realise, so the norms must match the person's education, and matching tasks, which ask only that the person point, are fairer to people who cannot write. Chapter 5 explains why norms collected on one population can mislead when applied to another, and Section 11.3 describes Indian batteries normed by education.

Exam trap

Visuoperceptual = what is it? (shapes, faces). Visuospatial = where is it? (position, angle, the two sides of space). Visuoconstructional = can he put it together? (drawing, blocks); its loss is constructional apraxia. Visual-motor integration = can the hand do what the eye sees? The Bender is a test of visual-motor integration; Benton's Judgment of Line Orientation is a pure visuospatial test with no drawing at all.

The Bender Visual Motor Gestalt Test: Lauretta Bender's nine designs

There is a fine irony in the life of the woman who built the most famous test of visual-motor maturity. Lauretta Bender (1897 to 1987), born in Butte, Montana, had to repeat the first grade at school and was thought to be intellectually slow, largely because she reversed her letters. She proved an able student after all, took degrees at the University of Chicago and a medical degree at the State University of Iowa in 1926, and trained in neuropathology under Samuel Orton, the pioneer of research on reading disability. In 1930 she joined the psychiatric staff of Bellevue Hospital in New York, and from 1934 she headed its children's psychiatric division. She described herself as a psychiatrist and neurologist, not a psychologist, and much of her career was spent with severely disturbed children.

Bender's test grew out of Gestalt psychology. In 1923 Max Wertheimer (1880 to 1943) had published his study of the laws by which the visual system groups elements into wholes, proximity, similarity, continuity and the rest, illustrated with simple patterns of dots, lines and curves (Book 1, Sections 2.3.4 and 4.2.3). Bender took several of Wertheimer's patterns, modified some, put each on a card and asked her patients to copy them. She first tried them with adult psychiatric patients, including mute patients with schizophrenia, and then with children. Her reasoning was that copying a design is not a mechanical tracing of lines but a reconstruction of a perceived whole, a "gestalt", by the integrated action of perception and movement, and that this integrative capacity matures with age. Young children reproduce designs as primitive loops and circles; with age they come to handle the relation of figure to background, the boundaries of forms and vertical lines, and then crossing lines, slanting lines and angles, a later level of maturation that usually appears at about six to eight years. Her monograph, A Visual Motor Gestalt Test and Its Clinical Use (1938), was published by the American Orthopsychiatric Association, and presented the test as a way of evaluating the maturation of visual-motor gestalt function in children of about 4 to 11 years. She believed that immature or "primitive" copies in older children could signal childhood schizophrenia, brain damage or learning difficulties.

The Bender Visual Motor Gestalt Test (BVMGT, the "Bender-Gestalt" or simply "the Bender") consists of nine cards, an introductory design labelled Figure A and eight more labelled Figures 1 to 8. The designs are built from dots, small circles, lines, curves and angles, some joined and some overlapping; they are protected test material and are not shown in this book. Administration is simple. The person sits at a table with a pencil and blank paper. The cards are shown one at a time in a fixed order, starting with Figure A, and the person is asked to copy each design as well as he can; the card stays in view while he copies. There is no time limit, and rulers and other aids are not allowed. The whole test usually takes about five to ten minutes. The examiner watches as carefully as she scores. Does the person count the dots or work by eye? Does he turn the card or the paper to make a design easier? Does he plan the page or crowd the designs into one corner? Does he notice his errors and correct them, or rush on? Is he frustrated, impulsive, painstaking?

Over the years examiners added variations that Bender's original did not have. In a recall phase, after all the copies are done, the cards are put away and the person is asked to draw as many designs as he can from memory, which adds a measure of visual memory. In a brief-exposure version each card is shown for only a few seconds and then drawn from memory. The designs can be projected on a screen for group testing in schools. Max Hutt added phases for projective use, in which the person elaborates the designs and says what they remind him of (Chapter 10, Section 10.9.4). And in 1966 Arthur Canter introduced the Background Interference Procedure: the person copies the designs once on plain paper and once on paper printed with a mesh of intersecting wavy lines. The idea was that a damaged brain finds it harder than a healthy one to pick out a figure from a distracting background, so the copies of brain-damaged patients should deteriorate more on the busy paper. Some studies found that it did improve detection of brain damage over the standard procedure; others found it of limited use as a screen. In India the Bender is part of the PGI Battery of Brain Dysfunction (Section 11.3) and remains one of the commonest tests in clinical psychology departments.

Exam trap

Bender = Lauretta Bender, 1938, a child psychiatrist at Bellevue (a woman, and not a psychologist). Nine cards: Figure A and Figures 1 to 8. The designs were adapted from Max Wertheimer's 1923 Gestalt grouping patterns; the test is named after Gestalt perception, not Gestalt therapy. The original test is copying only: the recall phase, the background interference procedure (Canter, 1966) and Hutt's projective phases came later.

Scoring the Bender: Pascal and Suttell, Hutt, Lacks and Koppitz

Bender herself judged copies clinically, by comparing them with the designs typical of children of each age. That made her test depend on the experience of the examiner, and for the next forty years psychologists built scoring systems that would let two examiners reach the same score from the same drawings (scorer reliability is the subject of Chapter 3, Section 3.6). Four of them are worth knowing, and Table 11.19 sets them out with the later systems.

For adults, Gerald R. Pascal and Barbara J. Suttell published The Bender-Gestalt Test: Quantification and Validity for Adults (1951). They listed, design by design, the many ways a copy can deviate from the model, weighted each deviation, added them up and converted the total into a standard score, with higher scores meaning more deviant copies; their conversion tables were for adults. Max L. Hutt, who trained army psychologists during the Second World War and circulated a guide to the Bender in 1945, published The Clinical Use of the Revised Bender-Gestalt Test with Gerald Briskin in 1960. Hutt and Briskin picked out a set of errors they regarded as the essential signs of brain damage, and they also read copies projectively, a use taught in Chapter 10, Section 10.9.4. In 1984 Patricia Lacks turned the Hutt-Briskin signs into a brief screening system for adults, Bender Gestalt Screening for Brain Dysfunction: twelve kinds of error, each scored present or absent, with five or more errors suggesting brain dysfunction. Studies of the Lacks system found it useful in some populations and a much poorer basis for screening in others.

The system that came to dominate, especially in schools, was built for children by Elizabeth Munsterberg Koppitz, an American school psychologist. In The Bender Gestalt Test for Young Children (1963, often cited by the 1964 date of the book) she published the Developmental Bender Scoring System, normed on children of about 5 to 10, and in a second volume (1975) she reviewed a decade of research and extended the norms to age 11. The system has 30 scoring items, each a specific error on a specific design, scored 1 if present and 0 if absent, so the total error score runs from 0 to 30 and a higher score means a less mature copy. Every item belongs to one of four types of error, and these four are what the papers ask about.

  • A distortion of shape: the shape is misformed. Angles are lost or added, a figure is badly out of proportion, or one kind of element is replaced by another (dots drawn as small circles, for example).
  • A rotation: the design, or part of it, is turned through 45 degrees or more from its proper orientation; turning the card and copying it in the turned position counts too.
  • An integration difficulty: the parts are not put together into the whole. Parts that should meet are left apart, overlap when they should only touch, or a design that should be closed is left open or falls apart into pieces.
  • A perseveration: a pattern is continued beyond what the model shows (many more dots, columns or curves than the design has). Some other scoring systems also count a feature of one design carried over into the next as perseveration.

Figure 11.9 shows the four types on invented shapes.

Table 11.19 The main ways of scoring the Bender.
SystemWho and whenFor whomHow it scores
Bender's ownLauretta Bender, 1938Children about 4 to 11Clinical comparison with the copies typical of each age
Pascal-SuttellGerald Pascal and Barbara Suttell, 1951AdultsWeighted deviations on each design, converted to a standard score; higher = more deviant
Hutt-BriskinMax Hutt (guide 1945) and Gerald Briskin, 1960Adolescents and adultsEssential signs of brain damage; also projective scales (Chapter 10, Section 10.9.4)
LacksPatricia Lacks, 1984Adults12 Hutt-Briskin errors; 5 or more suggests brain dysfunction
Developmental Bender Scoring SystemElizabeth Koppitz, 1963 (Volume II, 1975)Children about 5 to 10, later 1130 items of distortion, rotation, integration and perseveration; total errors against age norms
Global Scoring SystemGary Brannigan and Scott Decker, 2003Bender-Gestalt II, ages 4 to 85 and overEach drawing rated 0 to 4; totals turned into standard scores
Koppitz-2Cecil Reynolds, 2007Ages 5 to 85 and overKoppitz-style errors with new norms

Koppitz scores are read against age norms, and since the error score falls as children grow, a child's score can be turned both into a distance from the average for her age and into an age equivalent. Here is a worked example, in which the child and every number in the norm table are invented for teaching; Koppitz's published tables are different.

Chanda is a Class 2 pupil aged 7 years 2 months, referred by her teacher because her handwriting is untidy and she is slow to copy from the blackboard, although she answers well in class. On the Bender she makes 3 distortion errors, 2 rotation errors, 3 integration errors and 1 perseveration error, a total of 3 + 2 + 3 + 1 = 9 errors. Table 11.20 gives an invented norm table.

Table 11.20 An invented norm table for a Koppitz-style error score, for teaching only (not Koppitz's real values).
Age groupMean errorsStandard deviation
5 years134
6 years104
7 years63
8 years43
9 years32
10 years22
Remember

For an error score, where more errors means a worse performance:

z = (child's errors − mean errors for her age) / standard deviation for her age

Age equivalent = lower age + [(mean at lower age − child's errors) / (mean at lower age − mean at upper age)] × (upper age − lower age)

where the lower and upper ages are the two age groups whose mean error scores bracket the child's score. A positive z here means more errors than average, so it is a poor result, the opposite of the usual reading of a positive z.

  • Distance from her age group: z = (9 − 6) / 3 = 3 / 3 = +1.00. Chanda makes one standard deviation more errors than the average 7-year-old. On the normal curve about 84 percent of children score at or below +1.00, so she makes more errors than roughly 84 percent of children her age; put the other way, her performance is at about the 16th percentile.
  • Age equivalent: her 9 errors fall between the 6-year mean (10) and the 7-year mean (6). Age equivalent = 6 + [(10 − 9) / (10 − 6)] × (7 − 6) = 6 + (1 / 4) × 1 = 6 + 0.25 = 6.25 years, which is about 6 years 3 months (0.25 of a year is 3 months).

In plain words, Chanda copies like a child about a year younger than she is. That is a finding, not a diagnosis. Koppitz herself stressed that a high error score shows immaturity of visual-motor integration, which can come from many sources: a problem of vision, of fine motor control, of attention, of general intellectual development, of schooling, or simply a slower rate of development that will catch up. The next steps for Chanda are an eye test, a test that separates visual perception from motor coordination (the Beery tests of Section 11.5.7), observation of her attention in class, and, if questions remain, an intelligence test (Chapter 6). Koppitz also listed a separate set of "emotional indicators" in children's Bender copies, such as heavy reworking of lines or a confused order on the page; like her emotional indicators in human figure drawings they are taught in Chapter 10, Section 10.9.4, and they are viewed sceptically. Koppitz died in the early 1980s, and her system was later revised by Cecil Reynolds as the Koppitz-2 (2007), with new norms on about 3,600 people aged 5 to 85 and over and separate scoring for ages 5 to 7 and 8 upwards.

Exam trap

Koppitz (1963) = developmental scoring of the Bender for children, 30 items, four error types: distortion, rotation, integration, perseveration; a higher score means more errors and a less mature copy. Pascal and Suttell (1951) = adults. Hutt = the Bender read projectively (and, with Briskin, signs of brain damage). Lacks (1984) = a 12-error adult screen, 5 or more errors suggesting brain dysfunction. Koppitz also built the 1968 emotional indicators for human figure drawings (Chapter 10).

The Bender-Gestalt II

By the 1990s the Bender was showing its age. Its designs were too easy for adults, so a healthy adult made almost no errors and the test could detect only fairly marked problems (a ceiling effect); they were too hard for very young children; its norms were old; and its users scored it in half a dozen incompatible ways. A revision was prepared by Gary G. Brannigan and Scott L. Decker, who published the Bender-Gestalt II (Bender Visual-Motor Gestalt Test, Second Edition) in 2003.

The Bender-Gestalt II keeps Bender's nine designs and adds seven new ones, sixteen in all. Four of the new designs are easier and are used only with young children, from 4 years to 7 years 11 months; three are harder and are used only from 8 years upwards. So a young child copies 13 designs (the original 9 plus the 4 easy ones) and an older child or adult 12 (the 9 plus the 3 hard ones). The test was normed on about 4,000 people in the United States, aged from 4 to 85 years and over. It has two phases. In the Copy phase the person copies each design with the card in view, as in the original. In the Recall phase, which follows without warning, he draws as many of the designs as he can from memory, which gives a measure of visual-motor memory. Two short supplemental tests help locate the cause of a poor copy: a Motor test, which needs drawing but very little perception, and a Perception test, which needs perception but no drawing, the person simply pointing to the design that matches. A poor copy with a normal Perception test points to the motor side of the chain; a poor copy with a poor Perception test points to the perceptual side. The copy and recall take about five to ten minutes, the supplemental tests about five more.

Scoring uses Brannigan's Global Scoring System. Instead of hunting for specific errors, the scorer rates each drawing as a whole on a five-point scale, from 0 (a scribble, or no resemblance to the design) to 4 (a nearly perfect copy). The ratings are added, and the totals for Copy and for Recall are converted, by age, into standard scores (on the familiar scale with a mean of 100 and a standard deviation of 15) and percentile ranks. The arithmetic of the raw score is easy. Because each design is rated 0 to 4, the maximum raw Copy score is 13 × 4 = 52 for a young child and 12 × 4 = 48 for anyone aged 8 or more. Suppose an invented 45-year-old man is tested after a head injury and his twelve copies are rated 4, 4, 3, 4, 3, 2, 4, 3, 3, 2, 3 and 2. His raw Copy score is 4 + 4 + 3 + 4 + 3 + 2 + 4 + 3 + 3 + 2 + 3 + 2 = 37 out of 48, which is 37 / 48 = 0.771, or 77 percent of the maximum. Whether 37 is low for a man of his age is not something the percentage can tell you; only the norm table in the manual, which turns raw scores into standard scores for each age group, can say how unusual it is. The authors chose the global system for its reliability and validity in their studies and because it is quick and easy to use.

Exam trap

Original Bender (1938) = 9 cards, copy only. Bender-Gestalt II (Brannigan and Decker, 2003) = 16 designs (13 given below age 8, 12 from age 8), ages 4 to 85 and over, a Copy phase and a Recall phase, supplemental Motor and Perception tests, and the Global Scoring System (each drawing 0 to 4). If a question says the Bender-Gestalt II uses 9 figures, it has confused it with the original.

What the Bender measures, and what it does not

Take stock of what the evidence of seventy years says. The Bender is above all a test of visual-motor integration: the ability to perceive a design and reproduce it by hand. In children it is a measure of the development of that ability, which is why school psychologists used it for decades to judge readiness for school and to investigate children who struggle with handwriting and copying. In adults it is a brief screen for organic brain dysfunction, the old term for damage to or disease of the brain itself: brain-damaged patients as a group make more errors than healthy people, and a copy full of rotations, fragmentations and perseverations should prompt a fuller neuropsychological examination. That is the answer every paper wants: the Bender assesses visual-motor integration and screens for neurological (organic) impairment and for developmental immaturity.

The limits are just as important, and they explain why expert opinion turned against using it alone. First, it does not localise. As Barlow and Durand's abnormal psychology textbook puts it, the Bender can raise a suspicion of brain dysfunction when a child's errors exceed the number expected for her age, but it cannot tell you the nature or the location of the problem. Second, it is not specific. Errors arise from brain damage, but also from intellectual disability, from a low developmental level (a mental age below the age at which children normally copy the designs well), from learning disorders, from poor vision or motor control, from lack of schooling, from inattention and from emotional disturbance; a high error score says that something is wrong with copying, not what. Third, healthy adults make so few errors that only marked impairment shows. Fourth, the test was used for decades to answer the question "organic or functional?" with a single score, as if brain damage were one thing that any sensitive test would detect. Section 11.1 explains why that question has been abandoned: brain damage is many different things, and a single test of one function will miss most of them. Bender's early hopes that the test would identify schizophrenia did not survive research either, and the projective reading of copies for personality has little support (Chapter 10, Section 10.9.4). From the 1960s to the early 1990s the Bender was among the five tests most used by practitioners; a later Delphi poll of experts on discredited psychological tests placed its use as a test of neuropsychological impairment among the most discredited. The fair conclusion is that the Bender is a quick, pleasant, non-verbal screen of one function, useful as one part of a battery and as an ice-breaker with a child, but never a diagnosis on its own.

The papers test this from the other side, with a list of things the Bender might be thought to measure. Table 11.21 goes through every such option that has appeared, says why it is wrong and names the test that does measure it.

Table 11.21 What the Bender does not measure, and what does.
Option offeredWhy the Bender is not the answerWhat does measure it
Verbal intelligenceCopying needs no wordsVerbal subtests of the Wechsler scales (Chapter 6)
Unconscious sexual conflictsIt is not a projective test in its own rightProjective techniques (Chapter 10)
Vocational interestsIt measures a skill, not preferencesInterest inventories (Chapter 8)

Personality "via projective interpretation only"The word "only" is wrong: the main use is neuropsychological; Hutt's reading is secondaryHutt's adaptation (Chapter 10, Section 10.9.4)
