Exam trap

Five practice papers asked what the Bender is "primarily used to assess" or "to screen for". Every keyed answer is a version of one phrase: visual-motor integration and possible neurological (organic) impairment, with developmental (maturational) status in children. Reject verbal intelligence, sexual conflicts, vocational interests, personality disorders or structure, schizophrenia, ADHD, depression, reading, auditory or verbal working memory, verbal abstraction and premorbid intelligence. If two options both mention visual-motor function, prefer the one that adds neurological or developmental screening, and reject the one that says "projective only".

Clinical link

A teacher's referral like Chanda's is common in child guidance clinics. The Bender, or the Beery tests of Section 11.5.7, can show that a child's copying is immature, and the supplemental motor and perception tasks can show which half of the skill is weak, which matters for an occupational therapist planning help with handwriting. But a slow, untidy copier may also be a child with a specific learning disorder, with attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder, with poor eyesight or with a borderline intellectual level (Book 3, Chapter 3 describes these conditions), and the copying test cannot choose among them. It opens the enquiry; it does not close it.

The Benton tests: finger localization, line orientation, faces and right-left orientation

The Bender asks a person to do one complicated thing and then tries to work out what went wrong. Arthur Benton, whom you met as the author of the Visual Retention Test (Section 11.4.6), preferred the opposite strategy: many short tests, each aimed as narrowly as possible at a single function, each standardised with norms corrected for age and education, so that a failure could be read precisely. After the war Benton taught at the University of Louisville from 1946 and then at the University of Iowa from 1948, where he became professor of psychology and neurology in 1958 and built a laboratory that trained a generation of neuropsychologists. In 1983, with Kerry deS. Hamsher, Nils R. Varney and Otfried Spreen, he published Contributions to Neuropsychological Assessment: A Clinical Manual, which gathered about a dozen of these tests in one place. Four of them belong in this section.

The first is the one the papers ask about. Your sense of your own body includes a map of your fingers, so precise that you can tell, with your eyes shut, which finger has been touched. Loss of that ability is finger agnosia, a disorder of the body schema (Book 3, Section 2.10.3) and one of the four signs of Gerstmann's syndrome after damage to the dominant parietal lobe (Book 3, Section 10.8.3). Benton studied it with Bertram Cohen in 1955, alongside right-left discrimination, in normal and brain-injured adults, and the Finger Localization Test grew from that work. It has three parts. In the first, the person's hand rests in full view; the examiner touches the tip of one finger with the point of a pencil, and the person names the finger or points to it on an outline drawing of a hand (pointing lets people who cannot name fingers, such as some patients with aphasia, take the test). In the second, the hand is hidden behind a screen and single fingers are touched. In the third, the hand is hidden and two fingers are touched at once, and the person must identify both. Each part is given to each hand, ten trials per hand per part, sixty trials in all, and the score is the number correct for each hand separately.

Why score the hands separately? Because touch from each hand is analysed mainly in the parietal lobe of the opposite hemisphere. If a person makes consistently more errors on one hand than on the other, the most likely explanation is damage to the hemisphere opposite that hand. Errors on both hands equally have a different meaning: they are seen after damage to the left (dominant) parietal lobe, often together with aphasia and the other Gerstmann signs, and after more widespread brain disease. One precondition must be checked first. The test assumes that the person can feel each touch. If sensation itself is lost on one hand, the failure is sensory, not a failure of finger localisation, so the examiner first confirms that every touch is felt.

Remember

Scoring a side difference:

Percentage correct for a hand = (correct trials on that hand / trials given to that hand) × 100

Asymmetry = correct on the better hand − correct on the worse hand

where each hand receives 30 of the 60 trials. A large asymmetry points to the hemisphere opposite the worse hand; equal errors on both hands point to dominant-hemisphere or diffuse damage.

Here is an invented record. A 60-year-old man is tested two weeks after a stroke. He feels every touch on both hands. On his right hand he gets 29 of 30 trials correct; on his left hand, 19 of 30.

  • Right hand: (29 / 30) × 100 = 0.9667 × 100 = 96.67, about 97 percent.
  • Left hand: (19 / 30) × 100 = 0.6333 × 100 = 63.33, about 63 percent.
  • Asymmetry: 29 − 19 = 10 trials, a gap of about 33 percentage points (96.67 − 63.33 = 33.33).
  • Errors: 30 − 29 = 1 on the right hand; 30 − 19 = 11 on the left.

He makes consistently more errors on the left hand, with touch sensation intact, so the test points to damage in the right hemisphere, probably the parietal lobe. On its own that is a hypothesis; Section 11.5.7 shows how a second test, line bisection, can support it, which is exactly Reitan's method of right-left comparison described in Section 11.1.

Exam trap

"Which test can show that an examinee consistently makes more errors on one side of the body than the other?" The answer is Benton's Finger Localization Test, because it scores each hand separately and touch from each hand goes to the opposite hemisphere. The distractors are all attention tests, and attention is not tested side by side: the Continuous Performance Test (H. Enger Rosvold and colleagues, 1956) measures sustained attention through omissions and commissions over a long run of stimuli; a sustained attention test is the general name for such vigilance tasks (Book 1, Section 5.4.6); the Test of Everyday Attention (Ian Robertson and colleagues, 1994) measures sustained, selective and switching attention through everyday tasks. Section 11.6 teaches all three. The sensory-perceptual examination of the Halstead-Reitan battery (Section 11.2) uses the same right-left logic.

The second Benton test isolates visuospatial judgement more purely than any copying task can. In the Judgment of Line Orientation test (Benton, Varney and Hamsher, 1978) the person sees two short lines set at different angles and, below them, a fan of eleven numbered lines arranged in a semicircle, 18 degrees apart. He says which two lines of the fan have the same orientations as the pair above. After five practice items come 30 test items, and an item counts as correct only if both lines are identified, so scores run from 0 to 30. Two forms, H and V, contain the same items in different orders. Because the person only has to say or point to numbers, the test needs no drawing, very little movement and very little language, so a poor score cannot be blamed on a clumsy hand. It was developed to detect dysfunction of the right hemisphere, and patients with damage at the back of the right hemisphere do worst on it.

The third, the Facial Recognition Test (Benton and Van Allen, 1968), asks the person to match photographs of unfamiliar faces. In the first items he picks, from six photographs, the one identical to a target face; in later items he must pick three photographs of the target person taken from different angles or under different lighting. The long form (22 items) is scored out of 54, and a 13-item short form, scored out of 27, can be converted to the long-form scale. It is most impaired after damage to the back of the right hemisphere. Note what it does not test: recognition of familiar people. Some people who cannot recognise their own relatives by face (prosopagnosia) pass it by matching features slowly and carefully, so a normal score does not rule out a problem with face recognition in daily life.

The fourth, Right-Left Orientation, gives a series of commands. Some concern the person's own body ("show me your left hand", "touch your right ear with your left hand"); others concern the examiner, sitting opposite ("point to my left eye"), which requires the person to reverse left and right in imagination. Errors on his own body suggest left-hemisphere damage, especially with aphasia, and are part of Gerstmann's syndrome; errors only when the examiner's body must be mentally reversed suggest a spatial difficulty. The other tests of Benton's manual include Temporal Orientation, Visual Form Discrimination (matching complex designs that differ in small details, some of them at the edges, so neglect shows up), Three-Dimensional Block Construction, Tactile Form Perception, Pantomime Recognition, Serial Digit Learning, Phoneme Discrimination and Motor Impersistence; his verbal fluency test, the Controlled Oral Word Association test, is taught in Section 11.6.

Constructional tasks, tests of neglect and the Beery VMI

Drawing is only one way of constructing. In block design tasks the person arranges coloured cubes to copy a pattern, the method Samuel Kohs introduced and David Wechsler took into his scales as the Block Design subtest (Chapter 6); Kohs's blocks are also part of Bhatia's battery of performance tests in India (Chapter 6). How a person fails at block design is as telling as how a person fails at copying. Patients with right-hemisphere damage tend to break the overall square, laying blocks in a line or a jumble; patients with left-hemisphere damage tend to keep the square outline but get the details of the pattern inside it wrong. Benton's Three-Dimensional Block Construction asks for models built in depth. Stick construction, copying a pattern of matchsticks laid on the table, needs no pencil at all, which makes it useful for people who have rarely written. Drawing to command and to copy (a cube, a house, a flower) completes the set, and the most informative drawing of all, the clock, is taught with the bedside screens in Section 11.7.

Among the disorders these tasks reveal, unilateral neglect is the most striking: a failure to notice, attend to or respond to things on the side of space opposite a brain lesion, even though the eyes and the limbs on that side work. It is commoner and more severe after damage to the right parietal lobe, so it is usually the left side that is neglected. At the bedside such a patient eats only the right half of his plate, shaves only the right side of his face and bumps into door frames on his left (Book 3, Section 2.10.3, describes it with the disorders of body experience). Several simple tests measure it. In line bisection the person marks the middle of horizontal lines printed across a page (a version standardised by Thomas Schenkenberg and colleagues in 1980). A patient with left neglect treats the line as if it began further to the right than it does, so his mark falls to the right of the true centre.

Remember

Line bisection deviation:

Percentage deviation = [(position of the mark − true midpoint) / (half the length of the line)] × 100

where positions are measured from the left end of the line, a positive value means the mark is to the right of centre and a negative value to the left, and half the length of the line is the largest possible error on one side.

The man with the left-hand finger localisation errors bisects a line 200 millimetres long. The true midpoint is 100 millimetres from the left end; his mark is 136 millimetres from the left end. The deviation is 136 − 100 = +36 millimetres, to the right. Half the length of the line is 200 / 2 = 100 millimetres. Percentage deviation = (36 / 100) × 100 = 36 percent to the right. Healthy people usually place the mark within a few millimetres of the centre, often slightly to the left of it, so his large rightward error is evidence of left neglect, and it fits the right-hemisphere hypothesis raised by his finger localisation scores. Two tests of different functions pointing to the same hemisphere are far more convincing than either alone.

In cancellation tests the person crosses out every target on a page: every short line scattered across a sheet, in Martin Albert's line-crossing test (1973), or every star among words and letters, or every bell among other small pictures. A patient with left neglect leaves the targets on the left uncrossed. Drawings show neglect too: asked to draw a flower or a clock, the patient crowds all the petals or numbers onto the right half. Because paper tests and daily life do not always agree, Barbara Wilson, Janet Cockburn and Peter Halligan built the Behavioural Inattention Test (1987), which adds everyday tasks such as reading a menu, telling the time, sorting coins and dialling a telephone to the paper-and-pencil ones. The small peripheral figures of the Benton Visual Retention Test (Section 11.4.6) catch neglect as well.

Two more tests complete the kit. The Hooper Visual Organization Test (1958) shows thirty line drawings of common objects that have been cut into pieces and scattered, and the person says what each would be if the pieces were put together, a test of visual synthesis that needs no drawing. And for children, the main alternative to the Bender is the Beery VMI, the Developmental Test of Visual-Motor Integration of Keith E. Beery and Norman A. Buktenica, first published in 1967 and in its sixth edition (with Natasha Beery) in 2010, for ages 2 to 100 in its full form. The child copies geometric forms of increasing difficulty printed in a booklet, and two supplemental tests, Visual Perception and Motor Coordination, separate the two halves of the skill, exactly the question raised by Chanda's Bender. It is widely used by school psychologists and occupational therapists. Table 11.22 gathers the visuospatial, constructional and neglect tests of this section.

Table 11.22 Visuospatial, constructional and neglect tests beyond the Bender.
TestAuthor and yearWhat the person doesWhat it measures
Finger LocalizationBenton (with Cohen, 1955; manual 1983)Identifies fingers touched, hand seen or hiddenFinger gnosis; differences between the hands

Exam trap

Sort the names that sound alike. Lauretta Bender (1938): copying nine designs, visual-motor integration. Morris Bender: the neurologist who worked with Benton in the war; the Bender-Gestalt is not his. Arthur Benton: the Visual Retention Test (1946), Finger Localization, Judgment of Line Orientation (1978), Facial Recognition (1968). Keith Beery: the VMI for children (1967). Brannigan and Decker: the Bender-Gestalt II (2003). Left neglect follows right-hemisphere damage; a bisection mark to the right of centre means left neglect.

Scan Your Brain
