11.6 Executive Function, Attention, Concept Formation and Language TestsHigh yield · 8 Qs

Imagine a man whose intelligence quotient is in the superior range, whose memory scores are normal, who reads, writes, calculates and copies drawings without a mistake, and who nevertheless can no longer hold down a job or run his own affairs. The tests of Sections 11.2 to 11.5 would find little wrong with him, because they ask what a person can do when the examiner sets the task, times it and says when to stop. His problem lies somewhere else: in the controls that choose a goal, plan the steps, start the work, notice when it is going wrong and change course. Such patients are real (you will meet one of them in Section 11.6.8), and they are the reason neuropsychologists built a separate family of tests for the brain's "management".

This section teaches that family and three neighbours that are examined alongside it. The executive tests come first: the Wisconsin Card Sorting Test, the most widely used of them, then the Trail Making Test, the Stroop test, the tower tests and the fluency tests, and finally the newer tests that try to capture executive failure in everyday life. Then come the tests of attention, which executive tests depend on; the tests of concept formation, which clinical psychologists have used since the 1940s to examine disordered thinking in schizophrenia; and the language tests used to describe an aphasia. For each test you will learn what the person does, what is scored, what the score means and what it does not mean.

What executive functions are, and why they are hard to test

The executive functions are the higher-order control processes that let a person set a goal, organise behaviour towards it, start and keep it going, inhibit actions that do not serve it, monitor the result and change strategy when the situation changes. The word "executive" is borrowed from management: memory, language, perception and movement are the departments of a company, and the executive functions are the managers who decide what the departments should do, in what order and when to stop. A person can have every department intact and still fail, if the management is gone.

The clinical definition most quoted in neuropsychology comes from Muriel Lezak, whose textbook is introduced in Section 11.1. In a 1982 paper titled "The problem of assessing executive functions", in the International Journal of Psychology, and in later editions of her textbook, she divided executive functioning into four components. Volition (called goal formulation in the 1982 paper) is the capacity for intentional behaviour: forming a goal and the motivation to act on it. Planning is identifying and organising the steps needed to reach the goal. Purposive action is starting, maintaining, switching and stopping the sequence of actions the plan requires. Effective performance is monitoring the result and correcting it. Lezak's point was that each component can fail on its own: an apathetic patient has no volition, a disorganised one cannot plan, a perseverating one cannot switch, and a careless one cannot check.

Experimental psychology has broken the executive into parts in a different way. Book 1, Section 7.3.4 describes the central executive of the working memory model and the work of Akira Miyake and colleagues (2000), who found three related but separable executive functions in healthy adults: inhibition of unwanted responses, shifting between tasks or mental sets, and updating the contents of working memory. Clinicians add three more that matter at the bedside. Planning is working out a sequence of moves before making them. Fluency, in this sense, is generating many responses from memory under a rule and against the clock. Abstraction, or concept formation, is finding the general principle behind particular examples. Table 11.23 sets out these components with the tests that examine them; the rest of the section teaches the tests one by one.

Table 11.23 The executive functions and the tests that examine them.
ComponentWhat it isClassic testsTaught in
Set-shifting (cognitive flexibility)Letting go of one rule or strategy and adopting another when feedback says it is wrongWisconsin Card Sorting Test; Trail Making Part B; Colour Trails 211.6.2 to 11.6.5
InhibitionHolding back a strong, automatic responseStroop Colour-Word Test; go/no-go tasks11.6.6
PlanningWorking out a sequence of steps before actingTower of London; Tower of Hanoi11.6.6
Working memory (updating)Holding and manipulating information over secondsDigit span backward; N-back11.4; 11.3
Fluency (generation)Producing many responses under a rule, against timeLetter and category fluency; design fluency11.6.7
Abstraction and concept formationFinding the principle behind examplesCategory Test; WCST; Object Sorting Test; proverbs; similarities11.2; 11.6.10
Real-life organisation and decisionRunning several open-ended tasks; weighing risk and rewardMultiple Errands Test; BADS; Iowa Gambling Task11.6.8

Where in the brain do these functions live? The classic answer is the frontal lobes, and in particular the prefrontal cortex, the part of the frontal lobe in front of the motor and premotor areas. Three broad regions are usually distinguished. The dorsolateral prefrontal cortex, on the upper outer surface, is linked with organising, shifting, working memory and abstract reasoning. The orbitofrontal and ventromedial cortex, on the underside and the midline, is linked with social conduct, emotional control and decisions about reward and risk. The medial frontal cortex with the anterior cingulate is linked with starting behaviour and monitoring errors. Damage to each produces a recognisable frontal syndrome (disorganised, disinhibited or apathetic), which Book 3, Section 10.11.10 describes. But "executive" names a function and "frontal" names a place, and the two are not the same thing. The prefrontal cortex works through loops with the basal ganglia, the thalamus, the parietal lobes and even the cerebellum: in 1998 Jeremy Schmahmann and Janet Sherman described the cerebellar cognitive affective syndrome, in which disease confined to the cerebellum impaired planning, set-shifting, verbal fluency and abstract reasoning. Executive deficits are also found in schizophrenia, attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD), obsessive-compulsive disorder, autism, depression and the dementias. A poor executive score therefore says "the management is failing", not "the damage is in the frontal lobe".

Why are these functions so hard to test? There are four reasons, and you will meet each of them again. The first is the paradox Lezak pointed out: a testing session is the most structured hour of a patient's week. The examiner chooses the task, explains it, says when to begin, keeps the patient on track and says when to stop, so the examiner is supplying exactly the planning and control that the test is meant to measure. A patient may do well in the clinic and fall apart at home. The second is the task impurity problem, Miyake's term for the fact that every executive task also needs non-executive abilities: the Trail Making Test needs vision and hand speed, the Stroop needs reading and colour vision, fluency needs vocabulary. A low score can come from any of them, which is why so many executive scores are differences or ratios that try to remove the basic ability (you will compute three of them below). The third is novelty: an executive test works best the first time, when the problem is new; on a second attempt the patient may remember the solution, and the task has become a routine one that no longer needs the executive. That is one reason retest reliabilities for these tests are modest (Chapter 3, Section 3.8 lists the other things that lower reliability). The fourth is ecological validity, the extent to which a test score predicts how a person functions in everyday life; Section 11.6.8 shows that it can be surprisingly low.

Exam trap

Match each executive test to its main component: the WCST measures set-shifting and cognitive flexibility, the Stroop measures response inhibition, the Tower of London measures planning, verbal fluency measures generation, Trail Making Part B measures switching, and backward digit span and the N-back measure working memory. A question asking for "the test of planning" wants a tower test, not the WCST. And "executive" is not a synonym for "frontal": damage elsewhere, and many psychiatric disorders, can lower executive scores.

The Wisconsin Card Sorting Test: from a learning experiment to a frontal-lobe test

The most widely used test of executive function began life as an experiment on learning in healthy students. Its roots go back to the German neurologists of the 1920s. Kurt Goldstein (1878 to 1965) and his colleague Adhémar Gelb, studying soldiers with brain injuries in Frankfurt after the First World War, noticed that many could sort objects by one property, colour for example, but could not switch to sorting the same objects by another property when asked. One of their circle, Egon Weigl, described a simple colour-form sorting test for this purpose in 1927: a few cut-out shapes in several colours, which the patient sorts first one way and then the other. Section 11.6.10 returns to Goldstein's ideas.

Two decades later, at the University of Wisconsin, the experimental psychologist David A. Grant and his colleague Esta A. Berg turned Weigl's idea into a card-sorting task that could be scored objectively. Their 1948 paper in the Journal of Experimental Psychology has a title that tells you what they were interested in: "A behavioral analysis of degree of reinforcement and ease of shifting to new responses in a Weigl-type card-sorting problem". In the same year Berg published "A simple objective technique for measuring flexibility in thinking" in the Journal of General Psychology. Their question was about learning, not about brains: when a response has been rewarded with "right" many times, how easily can a person abandon it once it starts to be called "wrong"? The neuropsychologist Bryan Kolb, in a tribute to Brenda Milner published in 2022, notes that Grant designed the task as a human counterpart of the reversal problems that Harry Harlow, also at Wisconsin, was giving to monkeys. It was Milner who took the task to Montreal and made it a test of the frontal lobes, in the study told in Section 11.6.4. The test was standardised for clinical use by Robert K. Heaton, whose manual appeared in 1981 and was revised and expanded in 1993 (by Heaton, Gordon Chelune, Jack Talley, Gary Kay and Glenn Curtiss), with norms from about six and a half to 89 years, corrected for age and education. A short form using a single deck, the WCST-64, followed in 2000, and computer versions are now common.

The materials are simple. In front of the person the examiner lays four stimulus cards (key cards). Each card shows one, two, three or four copies of a single symbol, and the four key cards differ from each other in three ways at once: in the colour of the symbols, in their form (shape) and in their number. The person is then handed a pack of 128 response cards, made up of two decks of 64, each showing a combination of one colour, one form and one number. The instruction is to place each response card below whichever key card the person thinks it goes with. The examiner says nothing about how to match the cards: after each card the only feedback is "right" or "wrong". The examiner has a rule in mind, and in Heaton's procedure the first rule is colour. Once the person has matched ten cards in a row correctly, the rule changes without warning, to form, and after the next ten correct in a row to number, and then the cycle repeats (colour, form, number). The test ends when the person has completed six categories or has used all 128 cards. Figure 11.10 shows the logic with invented cards.

Why is such a simple task so revealing? Because it makes three separate demands, and a person can fail any of them. First, the person must form a concept from feedback alone: nobody says "sort by colour", so the rule has to be inferred from a run of "rights" and "wrongs". That is abstraction. Second, once found, the rule must be kept in mind and applied card after card, even when a card would also fit another key card. That is maintenance of set. Third, when the "wrongs" begin, the person must give up the rule that has just been rewarded ten times, and search for another. That is set-shifting, and it is the demand the test is famous for. The two great ways of failing are opposites. A person who cannot let go keeps sorting by the old rule despite being told "wrong" again and again. A person who cannot hold on finds the rule, gets several cards right, and then drifts away from it for no reason. The scores in the next subsection are designed to tell these failures apart.

Set-shifting, also called cognitive flexibility, is the ability to disengage from one mental set (a rule, a strategy or a way of seeing a problem) and engage another when the situation demands it. In daily life it is what lets you take another route when the road is closed, or change your explanation to a pupil who has not understood the first one. Its failure is what clinicians see as rigidity, and on the WCST as perseveration.

Exam trap

The WCST was devised by David Grant and Esta Berg in 1948, not by Robert Heaton (who wrote the clinical manual, 1981 and 1993) or Brenda Milner (who showed its link with frontal lesions, 1963). It uses four stimulus cards and 128 response cards; the rule changes without warning after ten consecutive correct sorts, in the order colour, form, number; and the test ends after six categories or 128 cards. Some Indian notes give 60 or 64 cards: the standard test has 128, in two decks of 64, and the one-deck WCST-64 is a short form. The task descends from Weigl's colour-form sorting test, which is why Grant and Berg called it a "Weigl-type" problem.

Scoring the WCST: perseveration and the other scores

The central idea in WCST scoring is perseveration: the continued repetition of a response, or a way of responding, after it has stopped being appropriate. On the card test it means going on sorting by a rule after the feedback has started to say "wrong". Heaton's manual makes the idea exact through the perseverated-to principle, the rule that the person is clinging to. In the simplest case, and in the worked example below, that is the rule that used to be right: after ten correct sorts by colour, the rule becomes form, and colour becomes the perseverated-to principle. A perseverative response is any response that matches the perseverated-to principle. Most such responses are wrong, but not all, because some response cards match a key card on two dimensions at once: a card that matches by both colour and form is "right" under the form rule, and yet it is still consistent with the old colour rule. So the manual distinguishes the perseverative response (which may be right or wrong) from the perseverative error, a perseverative response that is also wrong. The perseverative error count is the score most clinicians look at first.

Remember

Perseverative error = a response that is wrong under the current rule and matches the rule the person is clinging to (usually the previous correct one).

Percentage of perseverative errors = (perseverative errors ÷ trials administered) × 100

Here "trials administered" is the number of cards the person sorted (at most 128). Converting a count into a percentage allows a person who sorted 128 cards to be compared with one who finished in 80.

The other scores describe the rest of the performance. Table 11.24 lists them. Two need a word of explanation. Failure to maintain set is counted when a person makes an error after a run of five or more consecutive correct responses but before completing the category: the person had found the rule and then lost it. That is the opposite of perseveration, and points towards distractibility or a working memory lapse rather than rigidity. Conceptual level responses are correct responses that occur in runs of three or more in a row; the reasoning is that a run of three correct sorts is unlikely by chance and shows that the person has grasped the concept. The raw scores are converted, using the manual's tables, into standard scores (mean 100, standard deviation 15), T scores and percentiles corrected for age and years of education, so that a 70-year-old with eight years of schooling is compared with similar people and not with university students (Chapter 5 explains standard scores and norms).

Table 11.24 The main scores of the Wisconsin Card Sorting Test and what each means.
ScoreHow it is countedWhat a poor score suggests
Categories completedNumber of runs of ten consecutive correct sorts (0 to 6)General failure to form and shift concepts
Trials to complete the first categoryCards used up to and including the first run of ten correct (10 at best)Slow initial concept formation
Perseverative responsesResponses matching the perseverated-to principle, right or wrongRigidity; difficulty disengaging from a rule
Perseverative errorsPerseverative responses that are also wrong; also given as a percentage of trialsThe core sign of impaired set-shifting
Non-perseverative errorsErrors that do not match the perseverated-to principleRandom or inefficient hypothesis testing, inattention
Total errorsAll wrong responsesOverall inefficiency (not specific)
Conceptual level responsesCorrect responses in runs of three or moreLittle insight into the sorting principles
Failure to maintain setAn error after five or more correct in a row, before the category is completeLosing a correct rule: distractibility, not rigidity
Learning to learnChange in efficiency from earlier to later categoriesNo benefit from experience across the test

Scoring is easiest to understand by doing it. Pratik, a 47-year-old accountant, has been referred three months after surgery to remove a tumour from the left frontal lobe. His wife says he "gets stuck": he repeats the same suggestion in family discussions long after it has been turned down. Table 11.25 is an invented excerpt of twenty trials from his test, using the invented key cards of Figure 11.10 (card 1 one orange moon, card 2 two purple hearts, card 3 three green diamonds, card 4 four blue squares). When the excerpt begins, the rule is colour and Pratik has already sorted seven cards in a row correctly.

Table 11.25 Worked example: an invented twenty-trial excerpt of Pratik's WCST, scored. The rule is colour for trials 1 to 3 and form from trial 4.
TrialCard (invented)Placed underMatches onFeedback and score
13 orange heartsCard 1ColourRight (8th in a row)
22 blue diamondsCard 4ColourRight (9th)
31 purple squareCard 2ColourRight (10th): category 1 complete; rule silently becomes form
44 green moonsCard 3ColourWrong: perseverative error
52 orange diamondsCard 1ColourWrong: perseverative error
61 blue heartCard 4ColourWrong: perseverative error
73 purple moonsCard 2ColourWrong: perseverative error
82 green squaresCard 2NumberWrong: non-perseverative error
94 orange heartsCard 1ColourWrong: perseverative error
103 blue moonsCard 4ColourWrong: perseverative error
114 green diamondsCard 3Colour and formRight; a perseverative response, but not an error
122 orange squaresCard 1ColourWrong: perseverative error
133 blue heartsCard 2FormRight (1st in a row)
141 purple diamondCard 3FormRight (2nd)
154 orange moonsCard 1Colour and formRight (3rd); a perseverative response
162 green moonsCard 1FormRight (4th)
173 orange squaresCard 4FormRight (5th)
184 purple heartsCard 2Colour and formRight (6th); a perseverative response
193 green heartsCard 2FormRight (7th)
201 blue moonCard 4ColourWrong: perseverative error and failure to maintain set

Walk through it. Trials 1 to 3 complete Pratik's run of ten correct colour sorts, so he has completed one category, and the examiner silently switches to form. On trial 4 the card shows four green moons. Under the new rule it belongs with card 1 (the moon), but Pratik puts it under card 3 because both are green. He is told "wrong". He is told "wrong" again on trials 5, 6 and 7, every time for a colour sort. On trial 8 he tries something new: he puts two green squares under card 2 because both show two symbols. That is a sensible hypothesis, but it is wrong, and because it does not match the colour rule it is a non-perseverative error. Then, strikingly, he goes back to colour on trials 9 and 10, although colour has now been called wrong six times. On trial 11 he puts four green diamonds under card 3; that matches by colour (his old rule) and by form (the new one), so it is "right", but it is still a perseverative response, and it does not tell the examiner which rule he was using. Trial 12 shows that he was still using colour. Only from trial 13 does he sort by form, seven times in a row, until trial 20, when he slips back to colour once more.

Now tally the scores for the excerpt. The wrong responses are trials 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 12 and 20: 9 errors, so 11 responses were right. Of the nine errors, all but trial 8 match colour, so there are 8 perseverative errors and 1 non-perseverative error. Perseverative responses are the 8 perseverative errors plus the three right answers that also matched colour (trials 11, 15 and 18): 11 in all. One category was completed. The error on trial 20 came after seven consecutive correct responses, before the category was complete, so it also counts as 1 failure to maintain set. Conceptual level responses are the right answers in runs of three or more: trials 1 to 3 (three, part of a longer run) and trials 13 to 19 (seven), a total of 10 within the excerpt; trial 11 stands alone and does not count. Finally, the percentage of perseverative errors is (8 ÷ 20) × 100 = 0.40 × 100 = 40 per cent of the trials in the excerpt.

What does this mean in plain words? Pratik could form a concept (he completed colour), but when the rule changed he needed nine more cards, eight of them "wrong", before he settled on form, and seven of those eight errors were repetitions of the rule he had been told was wrong. A healthy adult usually needs only a few cards after a shift: one or two errors that "test" the old rule, then a quick search among the other two. Pratik's pattern is the classic perseverative one, and it matches his wife's description of him at home. His one failure to maintain set suggests that he also loses a rule he has found, so both rigidity and lapses of attention may be contributing. The manual's full rules are more detailed than this excerpt (for example, how to decide which principle a person is perseverating to after several errors of a new kind, and how to treat cards that match two rules), but the core logic is exactly this.

Exam trap

Perseverative errors are the signature score of the WCST and the one most tied to frontal dysfunction. Do not confuse them with failure to maintain set, which is losing a rule already found (a sign of distractibility), or with total errors, which mix every kind of failure. Categories completed run from 0 to 6, and a category is ten consecutive correct sorts. A response can be perseverative and still right, which is why "perseverative responses" and "perseverative errors" are separate scores.

The WCST and the brain: Milner's patients and the limits of a "frontal" test

Why does every textbook call the WCST a frontal-lobe test? The answer is one study, carried out in Montreal at a time when there were no brain scans, by a psychologist whose name you met in Book 1, Chapter 7, as the woman who studied H.M. Brenda Milner (born 1918) worked with the patients of the neurosurgeon Wilder Penfield (1891 to 1976) at the Montreal Neurological Institute. Penfield treated severe epilepsy by cutting out the area of cortex where the seizures began, and drew a map of each excision during the operation. That gave Milner something no other researcher of the time had: patients with known, localised removals of brain tissue who could be tested before and after surgery.

Key study: Milner's card-sorting patients (1963)

Question. In the 1940s and 1950s some researchers had concluded that the human prefrontal cortex did little for thinking, since patients with frontal damage often had normal intelligence test scores. Milner, aware that monkeys with frontal lesions could not cope when a learned rule was reversed, asked whether people with frontal removals would show a comparable difficulty in shifting.

Method. She gave the card-sorting task, obtained from Grant, to patients before and after surgery for epilepsy, comparing those whose excisions were in the dorsolateral frontal cortex with those whose excisions were orbitofrontal, temporal or in the posterior cortex. The procedure was the one you now know: four key cards, 128 response cards, "right" or "wrong", a silent change after ten correct, and an end at six categories or 128 cards.

Results. In the figures Milner reported in 1964 (as tabulated by Kolb in 2022), the dorsolateral frontal group completed on average 3.3 categories before surgery and only 1.4 after it, and their errors rose from 54.9 to 73.2. The orbitofrontal and temporal group completed 5.3 categories before and 4.9 after, with about 25 to 28 errors; the posterior group completed 4.5 and 4.6. The dorsolateral patients' errors were above all perseverative. Milner also noticed a curious dissociation: some patients could say that the old rule was no longer right, and yet went on sorting by it.

Conclusion. Dorsolateral prefrontal removals impair the ability to shift from one sorting principle to another, and the frontal lobe is not one functional unit, since orbitofrontal removals did not produce the deficit. The paper (Archives of Neurology, 1963, "Effects of different brain lesions on card sorting: the role of the frontal lobes") made the WCST the standard "frontal" test.

Criticism. All the patients had long-standing epilepsy, so their brains were not otherwise normal, and the excisions were known from the surgeon's drawings rather than from scans. Later studies of patients with strokes and tumours, described below, found that the WCST is sensitive to frontal damage but far from specific to it.

Milner's finding has been tested many times since, with a mixed result that every clinician should understand. In 1991 Steven W. Anderson, Hanna Damasio, Robert Jones and Daniel Tranel, at the University of Iowa, gave the WCST to 91 people with stable focal brain lesions whose locations were known from computed tomography and magnetic resonance imaging. They found no significant difference between patients with frontal and non-frontal damage; some people with extensive frontal damage did well, some with damage outside the frontal lobes failed, and the best cut-off score classified only 62 per cent of the patients correctly as frontal or non-frontal. In 2003 Gregory J. Demakis pooled the published studies in a meta-analysis and reached a more favourable view: patients with frontal damage did perform worse than those with posterior damage on most WCST scores (non-perseverative errors were the exception), the difference was largest for dorsolateral damage, and left and right frontal damage did not differ. In 2006 J. A. Alvarez and E. Emory reviewed the WCST, letter fluency and the Stroop together and concluded that the evidence does not support a one-to-one relationship between executive functions and the frontal lobes.

Put these results together and you get the modern view. The WCST is a good test of a function, set-shifting, that depends heavily on the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex and its connections, so frontal damage often lowers the score. But a low score does not localise the damage: any condition that disrupts the frontal networks, or the attention, working memory and motivation the test also needs, can produce one. In schizophrenia, for example, perseverative errors are common, and the classic imaging study of Daniel Weinberger and colleagues (1986), told in Book 3, Section 4.7.7, showed that the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex of medication-free patients failed to increase its blood flow during this very test. The test also picks out executive weakness in autism (Book 3, Section 3.6.7, describes Sally Ozonoff and colleagues' 1991 study), ADHD, obsessive-compulsive disorder, Parkinson's disease, alcohol-related brain damage and depression.

Two modifications are worth knowing. In 1976 the British psychologist Hazel Nelson (whose National Adult Reading Test appears in Section 11.1) published a Modified Card Sorting Test in Cortex. She found the full WCST too difficult and distressing for many older hospital patients, and noted that cards matching a key card on two dimensions made some responses ambiguous. Her version removed every response card that shares more than one attribute with any key card, leaving 48 cards, and the simpler test still separated patients with frontal lesions from those with lesions elsewhere in a group of 53. A different line of work produced the California Card Sorting Test of Dean Delis and colleagues (1992), in which the person must generate and explain sorting principles, and which became the Sorting Test of the Delis-Kaplan Executive Function System (2001).

Clinical link

Before giving the WCST, check colour vision: a person who is colour-blind cannot discover the colour rule and will look "perseverative" for the wrong reason. Watch the person's reaction too. Many patients find the endless "wrongs" upsetting, and the test can strain rapport, so it is often placed late in a session. Report the result as a description of behaviour ("after the rule changed he went on sorting by colour for eight further cards despite feedback") rather than as a diagnosis ("frontal damage"), because the score is sensitive to frontal dysfunction but not specific to it.

Exam trap

The WCST "mainly" or "most commonly" assesses executive function, set-shifting, cognitive flexibility and abstract reasoning. It is not a test of memory, short-term auditory memory or verbal memory (those are the tests of Section 11.4), language, reading fluency or reading comprehension (achievement and language tests), mood (rating scales, Section 11.8), visual acuity (an eye chart, not a psychological test) or manual dexterity (pegboards and Finger Tapping, Section 11.2). The region it is "most sensitive" to is the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex, not the hippocampus (memory), the primary motor cortex (movement, tested by finger tapping) or the cerebellum (coordination), although damage outside the frontal lobes can also lower the score. As "one of the most widely used tests of executive functioning", the answer is the WCST: the "Minnesota Number Ordering Test" and the "Manhattan Card Sorting Test" do not exist, so "none of the above" is wrong too. Beware real look-alikes: the Minnesota Rate of Manipulation Test is a real test of manual dexterity, and the Modified Card Sorting Test (Nelson, 1976) and California Card Sorting Test (Delis, 1992) are real card-sorting tests.

The Trail Making Test

If the WCST is the most famous executive test, the Trail Making Test is probably the most often given. It is quick (a few minutes), needs only a pencil and two printed sheets, is free of copyright in its original form, and is sensitive to almost anything that disturbs the brain. It was not invented by a neuropsychologist. It began as one of the tests of the Army Individual Test Battery, a set of individually given tests assembled by the United States Army during the Second World War, whose manual is dated 1944. Ralph Reitan, whose battery is the subject of Section 11.2, recognised its value, published a short paper in 1955 on "The relation of the Trail Making Test to organic brain damage" in the Journal of Consulting Psychology, and made it one of the Halstead-Reitan tests. Most of what is known about it comes from that tradition.

The test has two parts, each preceded by a short practice sample. In Part A the sheet shows 25 circles numbered 1 to 25, scattered over the page, and the person draws a continuous line joining them in numerical order as quickly as possible without lifting the pencil. In Part B there are again 25 circles, but they contain the numbers 1 to 13 and the letters A to L, and the person must alternate between the two series: 1 to A, A to 2, 2 to B, B to 3, and so on to the end at 13. (Thirteen numbers and twelve letters make 25.) If the person makes an error, the examiner points it out at once and the person returns to the last correct circle and carries on, while the stopwatch keeps running. So errors are not usually scored separately; they cost time, and the score for each part is simply the time to completion in seconds, with the number and kind of errors noted. Figure 11.11 shows the two layouts with a few invented circles.

What does each part measure? Part A needs visual scanning (finding the next circle among distractors), sustained attention, motor speed and the overlearned sequence of numbers, so it is a test of attention and processing speed, the rate at which simple mental operations are carried out. Part B needs all of that plus the executive demand of holding two sequences in working memory, switching between them at every step and resisting the pull of the overlearned sequence (after 3, the natural next circle is 4, not C). That is why Part B is classed as an executive, set-shifting task and Part A as an attention and speed task.

But a slow Part B could simply reflect slowness of everything. A person with a weak hand, poor eyesight or a generally slowed brain will be slow on both parts. To separate the switching cost from basic speed, clinicians compute two derived scores. The first is the difference score: how many extra seconds Part B took compared with Part A.

Remember

Difference score = B − A     Ratio score = B / A

Here B is the time in seconds to complete Part B and A is the time to complete Part A. The difference is the extra time the switching demand costs; the ratio says how many times longer Part B took, which removes the person's general speed more completely.

Push two invented cases through these formulas. Lina, a 34-year-old software tester with a degree, had a road accident six months ago with a moderate head injury, and complains that she "cannot juggle tasks" at work. She completes Part A in 38 seconds and Part B in 121 seconds. Her difference score is 121 − 38 = 83 seconds. Her ratio is 121 ÷ 38 = 3.184..., which rounds to 3.2. Samir, a 70-year-old retired schoolteacher seen for memory complaints, completes Part A in 60 seconds and Part B in 132 seconds. His difference score is 132 − 60 = 72 seconds, and his ratio is 132 ÷ 60 = 2.2. Now suppose, for illustration only (these are invented norms, not the published tables), that healthy adults of Lina's age and education take about 25 seconds for Part A and 55 for Part B, a difference of 30 seconds and a ratio of 55 ÷ 25 = 2.2.

Read the numbers. Lina is mildly slow on Part A (38 against 25 seconds) but very slow on Part B (121 against 55), and her ratio of 3.2 is well above the 2.2 of her peers: she is slower than she should be even after her basic speed is taken into account, which points to a specific difficulty with switching, consistent with her complaint. Samir's difference score (72 seconds) looks almost as bad as Lina's (83), yet his ratio (2.2) is exactly the healthy value: he is slow at everything, as many people of 70 are, but switching costs him no more than it costs anyone. The difference score alone would have made the two look alike; the ratio tells them apart. Many clinicians treat a B/A ratio of about 3 or more as a sign of a disproportionate switching problem; this is a rule of thumb, not a universal standard, and the published norms (for example Tom Tombaugh's 2004 norms, stratified by age and education) should always be used for the times themselves. Because both parts slow down sharply with age and with fewer years of schooling, comparing an elderly villager with university students would be meaningless.

The test is used across the lifespan. A shorter children's version forms part of the Halstead-Reitan battery for older children (Section 11.2), and the test is widely used in ADHD. In a child with ADHD the Trail Making Test helps to document the inattention: the child loses the place, skips circles, makes sequencing errors and takes longer, especially on Part B, where attention must switch back and forth between two sequences. What it does not measure is the child's social skills, which are judged from observation and informant reports, or over-activity, which is a behaviour seen in the classroom and recorded on rating scales such as the Conners scales (Section 11.9). Impulsivity may show itself in hasty errors, but the test has no score for it; impulsive responding is better captured by the commission errors of a continuous performance test (Section 11.6.9) and by rating scales.

The Trail Making Test has one obvious weakness in India: Part B assumes that the person knows the Roman alphabet as fluently as the numbers, which is not true of many people schooled in Hindi, Kannada, Tamil or another script, or of people who did not go to school. The Colour Trails Test solves the problem. It grew out of a World Health Organization cross-cultural study of people with HIV infection (Mario Maj, Louis D'Elia, Paul Satz and colleagues, 1993), and its manual was published by D'Elia, Satz, Craig Uchiyama and Travella White in 1996. Part 1 is like Trails A: join the circles numbered 1 to 25 in order, the circles being printed in colour. In Part 2 every number from 1 to 25 appears twice, once in a pink circle and once in a yellow one, and the person must join the numbers in order while alternating the colours (pink 1, then yellow 2, then pink 3, and so on), ignoring the distractor circle of the right number in the wrong colour. The alternation of colours replaces the alternation of letters, so nothing has to be read. The pink and yellow were chosen to be distinguishable by people with colour blindness. The Colour Trails Test is the version used in the NIMHANS Neuropsychological Battery (Section 11.3).

Exam trap

The Trail Making Test came from the Army Individual Test Battery (1944); Reitan adopted and validated it, but did not invent it. Part A (numbers only) measures visual search, attention and speed; Part B (numbers and letters alternating, 1 to 13 and A to L) adds set-shifting. The score is time in seconds, so errors show up as extra time. In ADHD it "helps to assess inattention": not social skills (not tested), not over-activity (observed and rated) and not impulsivity as such (shown best by commission errors on a continuous performance test). The Colour Trails Test (D'Elia and colleagues, 1996) is the language-free version, used in the NIMHANS battery.

Inhibition and planning: the Stroop test and the tower tests

In 1935 John Ridley Stroop showed that naming the ink colour of a colour word printed in a conflicting colour (the word RED printed in green ink) is slow and error-prone, because reading is automatic and the word intrudes. Book 1, Section 5.4.5 tells the experiments and the explanations. Here the question is how that effect became a clinical test. The answer is that it isolates a single executive operation unusually cleanly: response inhibition, the ability to hold back a strong, automatic response in order to give a weaker, deliberate one. A person who cannot suppress the word reads it out ("red") instead of naming the ink ("green"), or names the ink only after a long struggle.

Several clinical versions exist, and they share a three-part structure. The best known is the Stroop Colour-Word Test published in 1978 by Charles J. Golden (the same Golden who standardised the Luria-Nebraska battery, Section 11.2), revised in 2002 with Shawna Freshwater. It has three pages, each with 100 items in five columns, and the person works down the columns as fast as possible for 45 seconds per page. On the Word page (W) the person reads colour names printed in black ink. On the Colour page (C) the person names the ink colour of rows of meaningless Xs. On the Colour-Word page (CW) the colour names are printed in conflicting ink colours, and the person names the ink, not the word. The raw scores are the numbers of items completed in 45 seconds on each page. A shorter version, the Victoria Stroop (Regard, 1981), uses three small cards of 24 items, and on all three the person names the ink colours: of coloured dots, of common non-colour words (such as "when" and "over") and of conflicting colour words, so it has no word-reading page; the Delis-Kaplan Executive Function System (2001) adds a fourth condition in which the person must switch between naming the ink and reading the word.

How should the CW score be judged? A person who is slow at reading and slow at naming colours will be slow on the conflict page anyway, so the raw CW score mixes inhibition with basic speed. Golden's solution was to predict how many CW items the person ought to manage if the conflict page cost only the combined time of reading and naming, and then to compare the prediction with what the person actually did. The reasoning is simple once it is written out. If a person reads W words in 45 seconds, each word takes 45 ÷ W seconds; if the person names C colours in 45 seconds, each colour takes 45 ÷ C seconds. If a colour-word item took only the time of one reading plus one naming, it would take 45/W + 45/C seconds, and the number of such items in 45 seconds would be 45 ÷ (45/W + 45/C). A few lines of algebra reduce this to the formula below.

Remember

Predicted CW = (W × C) ÷ (W + C)

Interference score = CW − Predicted CW

W, C and CW are the numbers of items completed in 45 seconds on the Word, Colour and Colour-Word pages. A positive interference score means the person did better on the conflict page than basic speed predicts (good inhibition); a negative score means worse (more interference than expected).

Return to Lina. On the Word page she reads 98 words, on the Colour page she names 70 colours, and on the Colour-Word page she manages 22 items. Her predicted CW is (98 × 70) ÷ (98 + 70) = 6,860 ÷ 168 = 40.83. Her interference score is 22 − 40.83 = −18.83, which rounds to −18.8. Compare an invented healthy colleague of the same age: Word 105, Colour 78, Colour-Word 46. The colleague's predicted CW is (105 × 78) ÷ (105 + 78) = 8,190 ÷ 183 = 44.75, and the interference score is 46 − 44.75 = +1.25, about +1.2. The colleague did slightly better than predicted; Lina did nearly 19 items worse than her own reading and naming speeds predict. In plain words, Lina's difficulty is not that she is slow (her reading and naming are close to normal) but that she cannot stop the words getting in the way. In practice the interference score is converted to a T score from the manual's norms.

Other versions score time instead of items. In the version used in Indian studies with the NIMHANS battery, as described by B. R. Sahithya and colleagues in 2021, the person first reads a sheet of colour words and then names the ink colours of the same sheet, each timed, and the "Stroop effect" is the colour-naming time minus the word-reading time. If, for instance (invented figures), Samir takes 54 seconds to read the words and 96 seconds to name their ink colours, his Stroop effect is 96 − 54 = 42 seconds. The logic is the same: how much does the conflict cost, over and above basic speed?

Two cautions apply, and both matter in India. The Stroop effect depends on automatic reading, so a person who reads slowly, reads a different script, or cannot read at all suffers little interference from the words. A small effect in such a person is not evidence of good inhibition; it is evidence that the test does not apply. And the test needs normal colour vision. Imaging studies link Stroop interference with the anterior cingulate cortex and the lateral prefrontal cortex, the regions that detect conflict and resolve it, and interference is increased in many conditions, including frontal injury, ADHD, schizophrenia and dementia, so here again the test describes a function rather than locating a lesion.

Planning is tested with tower puzzles. The Tower of London was designed by the British neuropsychologist Tim Shallice (born 1940) and described in 1982 in a paper titled "Specific impairments of planning", in the Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London. Shallice wanted a task heavily loaded on planning, borrowing ideas from artificial intelligence research on problem solving. The apparatus has three upright pegs of unequal length: the tallest holds three balls, the middle one two and the shortest one. There are three balls of different colours (red, green and blue). The examiner shows a goal arrangement, and the person must move the balls from a fixed starting arrangement to the goal in a stated minimum number of moves, moving one ball at a time and never placing more balls on a peg than it can hold. Problems range from two moves to five. Shallice found that a group of patients with left anterior (frontal) lesions had a specific deficit on the task. The scores are the number of problems solved in the minimum number of moves, the total moves, the time taken before the first move (planning time), the time spent executing, and rule violations. A commercial version, the TOL-DX, and a version with Indian norms in the NIMHANS battery (Section 11.3) are in clinical use. Figure 11.12 shows an invented three-move problem.

Look at what makes the problem hard. Green is already on the tall peg, where it will end up, so the impulse is to leave it there. But blue has to go underneath it, so green must first move away, to the short peg, and come back. The move that leads to the goal looks like a move away from it. This goal-subgoal conflict is what the harder problems exploit, and it is exactly where patients with planning deficits fail: they start moving at once, without working the sequence out in advance, and wander into longer solutions or break the rules.

The Tower of Hanoi is older, and was a puzzle long before it was a test. The French mathematician Édouard Lucas (1842 to 1891) marketed it in 1883. It has three pegs of equal height and a set of discs of different sizes, stacked on the first peg with the largest at the bottom. The task is to move the whole stack to another peg, moving one disc at a time, taking only the top disc of a stack, and never placing a larger disc on a smaller one. In the Tower of London the balls are all the same size and the pegs differ; in the Tower of Hanoi the pegs are the same and the discs differ. The Tower of Hanoi also has an elegant rule for the minimum number of moves. To move a stack of n discs you must first move the top n − 1 discs out of the way, then move the largest disc once, then move the n − 1 discs back on top of it. Following that reasoning through gives:

Remember

Minimum moves for the Tower of Hanoi = 2n − 1

Here n is the number of discs. Each extra disc doubles the work and adds one move.

Work it through. With 3 discs, 23 = 2 × 2 × 2 = 8, and 8 − 1 = 7 moves. Check it by the reasoning: moving the top two discs takes 3 moves (22 − 1), moving the largest takes 1, and moving the two back takes 3 more, so 3 + 1 + 3 = 7. With 4 discs the minimum is 24 − 1 = 16 − 1 = 15, and with 5 discs it is 32 − 1 = 31. Clinical versions usually use three to five discs; the score is the number of moves (compared with the minimum), the time and the rule violations. Because the solution has a regular recursive structure, people who discover it can solve later problems almost automatically, which is one reason the Tower of London, with its many different problems, is preferred as a test of planning.

Exam trap

The Stroop test measures response inhibition (suppressing the automatic word), not set-shifting (the WCST) or planning (the towers). The clinical version most cited is Golden's (1978): three pages of 100 items, 45 seconds each, with interference = CW − (W × C)/(W + C). The Tower of London is Tim Shallice's (1982) test of planning, with three balls on pegs of unequal length; the Tower of Hanoi is Édouard Lucas's 1883 puzzle with discs of different sizes on equal pegs. The minimum number of moves for three discs is 23 − 1 = 7 (not 6, as some notes say); the "five moves" often quoted for the Tower of London is the length of the hardest problems in Shallice's original set, not a rule.

Verbal and design fluency

Ask a healthy adult to say, in one minute, as many words as possible that begin with a certain letter, and you will hear a burst of words, then a slowing, then a strategic search ("words for food... words for the body..."). Ask a patient with left frontal damage and you may hear five words and a silence. Verbal fluency tests measure this capacity to generate words from memory according to a rule and against the clock. They need a good vocabulary, but above all they need an executive search: a strategy, the ability to switch from an exhausted cluster to a new one, the monitoring of what has already been said and the inhibition of words that break the rules.

The idea has a long history. Word fluency was one of the primary mental abilities that Louis Leon Thurstone identified in his factor-analytic work of 1938 (Book 1, Section 9.4.4 describes his theory of intelligence), and a written word fluency test from that tradition, the Chicago Word Fluency Test, was the one Brenda Milner gave to her Montreal patients. Her results, reported in 1964, form one of the neatest double dissociations in neuropsychology. Asked to write down as many words as they could beginning with a given letter, patients with left frontal excisions produced a mean of 35.4 words, against 56.8 for patients with right frontal excisions and 57.6 for those with left temporal excisions. Yet on a test of verbal recall the left frontal patients were normal (18.9) and the left temporal patients impaired (12.4). Knowing words and remembering them is a temporal-lobe matter; finding them on demand is a frontal one.

Clinical fluency tests come in two forms. In letter fluency (also called phonemic fluency) the person has 60 seconds for each of three letters. The best-known version is the Controlled Oral Word Association test (COWA), developed by Arthur Benton and Kerry Hamsher as part of their Multilingual Aphasia Examination from 1976 onwards, which uses the letter sets C, F and L or P, R and W. An older set, F, A and S, comes from the earlier aphasia examination of Otfried Spreen and Benton (the Neurosensory Center Comprehensive Examination for Aphasia), and it is still so widely used that letter fluency is often called "the FAS test". The rules exclude proper nouns (names of people and places), numbers, and the same word with a different ending (if "play" counts, "plays" and "playing" do not). In category fluency (semantic fluency) the person names members of a category, most often animals, in 60 seconds. The score in each case is the number of correct words, with repetitions (perseverations) and rule breaks counted separately.

Scoring is straightforward, but it must follow the rules. Here is an invented one-minute record from Samir for the letter P (a letter chosen for this example; the real test letters differ): "pen, pencil, paper, pens, Pune, plate, pot, pen, pumpkin, parrot, pink, Prakash, potato, push, pushing". Go through it word by word. Pen, pencil, paper, plate, pot, pumpkin, parrot, pink, potato and push are correct: 10 words. "Pens" and "pushing" are the same words as "pen" and "push" with different endings: 2 rule breaks. "Pune" and "Prakash" are proper nouns: 2 more rule breaks. The second "pen" is a repetition: 1 perseveration. His score for P is 10. Suppose (invented figures) that across three letters his total is 31, and that in one minute of animals he names only 9, whereas healthy people of his age and schooling in this invented example average about 36 letter words in total and about 17 animals. Samir's letter fluency is modestly low; his category fluency is much lower. Compare Pratik, whose invented totals are 14 letter words and 15 animals: his letter fluency is far worse than his category fluency.

The difference between the two patterns is the point of giving both tests. Letter fluency asks for a search that has no natural organisation in memory (words are not stored by their first letter), so it depends heavily on a self-generated strategy and is especially sensitive to frontal, and particularly left frontal, damage, as Milner found. Category fluency can follow the natural organisation of meaning (farm animals, wild animals, birds), so it depends more on the integrity of the semantic store in the temporal lobes. A person whose category fluency is disproportionately poor, like Samir, shows the pattern typical of Alzheimer's disease, in which semantic memory degrades early (Book 3, Section 10.9.6 describes the diagnosis); a person whose letter fluency is disproportionately poor, like Pratik, shows the pattern of executive retrieval failure. Neither pattern is a diagnosis by itself, and meta-analyses show that frontal damage often lowers both kinds, but the comparison is one of the most useful quick contrasts a clinician has. Process scores add detail: Angela Troyer and her colleagues (1997) proposed counting clusters (runs of related words, such as several farm animals) and switches (moves to a new cluster), since frontal patients tend to switch less and patients with temporal damage tend to produce smaller clusters.

Fluency tests travel across languages more easily than most, but not without care. Letters differ in how many words begin with them, so Indian versions use letters common in the language of testing, and norms must be specific to that language and level of education; animal naming is the easiest version to adapt. The NIMHANS battery includes both letter fluency and animal-name fluency with Indian norms (Section 11.3).

Non-verbal fluency exists too. In 1977 Marilyn Jones-Gotman and Brenda Milner asked patients to invent as many different abstract drawings (doodles that do not represent anything) as they could in five minutes. This design fluency was poor after right frontal excisions, the mirror image of the left frontal deficit in verbal fluency: the patients drew few designs, broke the rule by drawing recognisable objects such as stick figures, and repeated similar designs again and again.

Exam trap

Letter (phonemic) fluency is the more "frontal" form and category (semantic) fluency the more "temporal" form; a disproportionate fall in category fluency is typical of early Alzheimer's disease. The COWA belongs to Arthur Benton and Kerry Hamsher's Multilingual Aphasia Examination (letters C, F, L or P, R, W), and the older FAS set to Spreen and Benton's aphasia examination: both come from Benton's group, not the Boston group. Proper nouns, numbers and variants of the same word do not count. Design fluency (Jones-Gotman and Milner, 1977) is the right-frontal, non-verbal counterpart.

When the patient passes the tests but fails at life: ecological validity

Return to the man in the opening paragraph of this section. He is real. In 1985 Paul Eslinger and Antonio Damasio described him in the journal Neurology as patient EVR. At the age of 35, a man with a record of prudent and responsible behaviour at work and at home, he had surgery to remove a tumour, and the operation left a lesion of the orbital and lower medial frontal cortex on both sides. Over the following eight years he could no longer meet his personal and professional responsibilities. Yet his measured intelligence was superior, his neurological and neuropsychological examinations were otherwise normal, and he sorted the Wisconsin cards without difficulty; because the tests found nothing, some of the professionals who saw him concluded that he was malingering. EVR was not malingering. His damage was in the ventromedial frontal cortex, which the WCST, a test of dorsolateral function, hardly taps, and the problems that ruined his life were in deciding, choosing and conducting himself in open-ended situations, which a structured test session never asks for.

In 1991 Tim Shallice and Paul Burgess described three more patients of this kind, men with traumatic injuries involving the prefrontal cortex who scored well on tests of perception, language and intelligence; two of them also did well on other tests of frontal function. Shallice and Burgess gave them two new tasks that required carrying out several simple but open-ended tasks over 15 to 30 minutes. In the Six Element Test the person has a set period in which to make a start on six different simple tasks while obeying a rule about their order. In the Multiple Errands Test the person goes into a real shopping street to buy several items and find out several pieces of information while obeying rules (for example, not entering a shop except to buy something). All three patients had severe difficulties: they spent too long on single tasks, broke rules and failed to return to tasks they had planned. Shallice and Burgess argued that the problem lay in reactivating, after a delay, an intention that nothing in the surroundings was signalling: exactly what daily life demands and a test with an examiner prompting each step does not.

These cases launched a search for tests with better ecological validity (Section 11.6.1). The best known product is the Behavioural Assessment of the Dysexecutive Syndrome (BADS), published in 1996 by Barbara Wilson, Nick Alderman, Paul Burgess, Hazel Emslie and Jonathan Evans. Its six subtests imitate real problems: shifting between two rules with a pack of playing cards, working out how to get a cork out of a tube with a few tools, drawing the route you would take to search a field for lost keys, estimating how long everyday events take, planning a route round a zoo map under rules, and a modified version of the Six Element Test. It comes with the Dysexecutive Questionnaire (DEX), filled in by the patient and by someone who knows the patient well, about everyday problems such as impulsivity, planning and distractibility. In a 1998 study Burgess and colleagues gave ten executive measures from six tests to 92 neurological patients and 216 controls, and found that every test predicted at least some of the everyday problems reported by relatives or carers, although the everyday problems fell into three groups (inhibition, intentionality and executive memory) that different tests predicted differently. A children's version, the BADS-C, followed, and for children the Behavior Rating Inventory of Executive Function (BRIEF; Gerard Gioia and colleagues, 2000) collects parents' and teachers' ratings of executive behaviour at home and at school.

EVR's kind of damage needed a different test. In 1994 Antoine Bechara, Antonio Damasio, Hanna Damasio and Steven Anderson published, in the journal Cognition, a card game that has become known as the Iowa Gambling Task. The player chooses cards one at a time from four decks, winning money on every card and sometimes losing money. Two decks give large wins but even larger occasional losses, so they lose money in the long run; the other two give small wins and small losses and make money in the long run. Healthy players gradually learn to prefer the safe decks. Patients with ventromedial prefrontal damage, though of normal intelligence, went on choosing the risky decks: in the authors' words they were oblivious to the future consequences of their actions and seemed to be guided by immediate prospects only. The task gave a laboratory measure of the decision-making failure that had wrecked EVR's life, and it is now used to study decision-making in addiction, psychopathy and other conditions. The theory Damasio built on it, the somatic marker hypothesis, belongs to the Physiological Psychology book.

At the bedside there are short executive screens. The Frontal Assessment Battery (FAB) of Bruno Dubois, Andrea Slachevsky, Irene Litvan and Bernard Pillon (2000) takes about ten minutes and has six subtests, each scored 0 to 3 (so a total of 18), covering conceptualisation (similarities), mental flexibility (letter fluency), motor programming (a series of hand movements), sensitivity to interference (conflicting instructions), inhibitory control (a go/no-go task) and environmental autonomy (whether the patient grasps the examiner's hands without being asked). Its scores correlated 0.77 with the number of categories achieved on the WCST and 0.68 with perseverative errors. The broader screens of Section 11.7, such as the Montreal Cognitive Assessment, include a few executive items too. At the other extreme, the Delis-Kaplan Executive Function System (D-KEFS; Dean Delis, Edith Kaplan and Joel Kramer, 2001) is a full battery of nine executive tests, including versions of trail making, fluency, the Stroop, card sorting, a tower and proverbs, with common norms from 8 to 89 years.

Clinical link

Because executive problems can hide in the structure of the testing room, a good executive assessment always adds information from outside it: an informant interview or a questionnaire such as the DEX or BRIEF, observation of how the patient organises the session itself (arriving late, losing papers, needing every instruction repeated), and, in rehabilitation, observation of a real task such as cooking a meal or using public transport. A normal WCST score never rules out executive dysfunction, and the report should say so when the history suggests it. Book 2, Section 9.9 describes how such findings are turned into neuropsychological rehabilitation.

Exam trap

EVR (Eslinger and Damasio, 1985) is the classic patient with normal or superior test scores, including the WCST, and devastated real-life decision-making after ventromedial (orbital and medial) frontal damage. The Iowa Gambling Task (Bechara and colleagues, 1994) taps ventromedial and orbitofrontal decision-making; the WCST taps dorsolateral set-shifting. The Multiple Errands Test and the Six Element Test come from Shallice and Burgess (1991); the BADS (Wilson and colleagues, 1996) is a battery designed for ecological validity; the FAB (Dubois and colleagues, 2000) is a ten-minute bedside executive screen scored out of 18.

Tests of attention

Every test in this chapter depends on attention: a patient who cannot attend cannot learn a word list, copy a figure or sort cards. Attention is therefore usually examined early, and impairments of it are interpreted before anything else (Book 2, Section 2.6.1 explains why the mental status examination tests cognition in this fixed order, and Section 2.6.4 describes the bedside tests, such as serial sevens). But attention is not one thing. A widely used clinical model, developed by McKay Moore Sohlberg and Catherine Mateer for their Attention Process Training programme (1987), distinguishes five levels. Focused attention is responding at all to a particular stimulus. Sustained attention is keeping attention on a task over time; when the events to be detected are rare it is called vigilance (Book 1, Section 5.4.6 tells the story of Mackworth's clock). Selective attention is attending to one stimulus or task while ignoring distractors. Alternating attention is switching attention back and forth between tasks with different demands. Divided attention is attending to two tasks at the same moment. Table 11.26 places the tests of this section against these kinds, together with processing speed, which underlies them all.

The classic laboratory test of sustained attention is the Continuous Performance Test (CPT). It was introduced in 1956 by H. Enger Rosvold, Allan Mirsky, I. Sarason, E. D. Bransome and L. H. Beck in a paper titled "A continuous performance test of brain damage", in the Journal of Consulting Psychology. (Mirsky is the same researcher who studied the Genain quadruplets in Book 3, Chapter 4.) Letters appeared one at a time, about one a second, for several minutes. In the simpler X version the person pressed a key whenever the letter X appeared; in the harder AX version the person pressed only for an X that came straight after an A, so that an X after any other letter, or an A followed by any other letter, required no response. Patients with brain damage detected fewer targets than controls. Modern computerised CPTs, such as the Conners Continuous Performance Test and the Test of Variables of Attention (TOVA), follow the same design and are widely used in the assessment of ADHD, while versions of the AX task are used in schizophrenia research.

The CPT yields two kinds of error, and the distinction between them matters. An omission error is a missed target: the person failed to respond when the target appeared, which is read as a lapse of attention. A commission error is a response to a non-target: the person pressed when they should not have, which is read as impulsivity or poor inhibition. Reaction time and, especially, the variability of reaction time across the test are also scored, because a person whose attention keeps drifting responds sometimes quickly and sometimes very slowly.

Here is a worked example with invented numbers. Karan, a 10-year-old boy referred by his school for inattention and restlessness, takes a CPT in which 400 letters are shown, 40 of them targets and 360 non-targets. He responds to 26 of the 40 targets, so he makes 40 − 26 = 14 omission errors, an omission rate of 14 ÷ 40 = 0.35, or 35 per cent. He also presses for 29 of the 360 non-targets, a commission rate of 29 ÷ 360 = 0.0806, about 8.1 per cent. An invented boy of the same age without ADHD misses 3 targets (3 ÷ 40 = 7.5 per cent) and presses for 6 non-targets (6 ÷ 360 = about 1.7 per cent). Karan's record therefore shows both many omissions (inattention) and many commissions (impulsivity), the pattern of the combined presentation of ADHD; a child who was inattentive but not impulsive would show high omissions with near-normal commissions. Because the CPT is a detection task, these figures can also be expressed with the signal detection index d′ (Book 1, Section 3.5): Karan's hit rate of 0.65 and false-alarm rate of 0.081 give d′ = 0.385 − (−1.401) = 1.79, against 3.57 for his peer (hit rate 0.925, false-alarm rate 0.017), so he discriminates targets from non-targets much less well. The test supports the diagnosis; it never makes it, because ADHD is diagnosed from the history across settings (Book 3, Chapter 3), and many anxious, tired or bored children also make CPT errors.

Several other attention tests are worth knowing. The Sustained Attention to Response Task (SART) of Ian Robertson, Tom Manly, Jackie Andrade, Bart Baddeley and Jenny Yiend (1997) reverses the CPT: digits appear one after another and the person presses for every digit except a rare target (one in nine), which must be withheld. Because pressing becomes automatic, sustained attention is needed to stop in time, and the false presses predicted everyday attentional slips in patients with traumatic brain injury; SART performance correlated −0.58 with the Glasgow Coma Scale scores of the injured group (Section 11.7 teaches the scale). In the Paced Auditory Serial Addition Test (PASAT) of Dorothy Gronwall (with H. Sampson, 1974; Gronwall's 1977 paper on recovery from concussion made it famous), the person hears a string of digits and must add each digit to the one just before it and say the sum aloud, at a fixed pace; it is a demanding test of divided attention, working memory and speed, widely used after concussion and in multiple sclerosis, though many people find it stressful. Paper-and-pencil cancellation tests, in which the person crosses out target letters or digits on a page, measure selective and sustained attention; the Digit Vigilance Test of the NIMHANS battery, in which the person cancels two target digits scattered among others, is one (Section 11.3), and Section 11.5 shows how cancellation tests reveal visual neglect. "Sustained attention test" is therefore a description of what these tasks do rather than the name of a single test.

The CPT and its relatives are laboratory tasks. In 1994 Ian Robertson, Tony Ward, Valerie Ridgeway and Ian Nimmo-Smith published the Test of Everyday Attention (TEA), built from tasks modelled on everyday activities. It has eight subtests: searching a coloured map for symbols of a given kind (80 symbols, two minutes), counting tones presented as the floors passed by a lift, with and without distracting tones and with occasional reversals of direction, searching a telephone directory for symbols, doing the same while counting tones, and listening to a long recording of lottery numbers for particular target numbers. It has three parallel forms and norms from 18 to 80 years. When the subtests were given to 154 healthy people, they separated into factors for sustained attention, selective attention, attentional switching and auditory-verbal working memory, which matches three of the kinds of attention in the clinical model above (sustained, selective and alternating) and adds working memory; selected subtests also distinguished patients with closed head injury, and patients with progressive supranuclear palsy, from matched controls, and patients with minimal Alzheimer's disease from those with mild Alzheimer's disease. A children's version, the TEA-Ch, followed.

Table 11.26 Kinds of attention and the tests that examine them.
KindWhat it meansTestsTypical failure
Processing speedRate of simple mental operationsTrail Making A; Colour Trails 1; digit symbol testsSlow times on every timed task
Sustained (vigilance)Keeping attention on a task over timeCPT; SART; TEA lottery and lift counting; digit vigilanceOmissions that rise as the test goes on
SelectiveAttending to one input, ignoring distractorsStroop; cancellation tests; TEA map and telephone searchSlowing or errors when distractors are present
AlternatingSwitching back and forth between tasksTrail Making B; Colour Trails 2; TEA lift counting with reversalLosing track of which task or sequence is current
DividedDoing two tasks at oncePASAT; TEA telephone search while countingOne or both tasks collapse under the double load
Exam trap

"Which test can show that an examinee consistently makes more errors on one side of the body?" The answer is Benton's Finger Localization Test (Section 11.5), because a lateralised pattern of errors in naming touched fingers points to the opposite hemisphere. The Continuous Performance Test (sustained attention: omissions and commissions), "sustained attention tests" in general, and the Test of Everyday Attention (sustained, selective, switching and divided attention through everyday tasks) all measure attention, and none of them compares the two sides of the body. Remember also: omission errors mean inattention, commission errors mean impulsivity; the CPT is Rosvold and colleagues' (1956) and the TEA is Robertson and colleagues' (1994).

Concept formation and the assessment of thought disorder

Long before "executive function" was a phrase, clinicians were testing the ability to form concepts, and for two different reasons: to detect brain damage, and to examine the disordered thinking of schizophrenia. Both traditions start with Kurt Goldstein, whose work with brain-injured soldiers is introduced in Book 2, Section 2.6.7. Goldstein argued that the basic effect of brain damage is the loss of the abstract attitude: the capacity to step back from the immediate situation, to grasp what things have in common, to keep several aspects of a situation in mind and shift between them, and to think about what is merely possible. What remains is the concrete attitude, in which thinking is tied to the immediate, the literal and the physical. A patient with only a concrete attitude may sort wool skeins by one shade but be unable to sort them by any principle when asked to "do it another way", or may use a key perfectly to open a door and yet be unable to show how one would use it when no door is present.

In 1941 Goldstein and the psychologist Martin Scheerer (1900 to 1961) published a monograph, "Abstract and concrete behavior: an experimental study with special tests", in Psychological Monographs. It set out a group of tests, later sold as a kit: a cube test (copying designs with coloured blocks), the colour-form sorting test that Weigl had devised, a stick test (copying and recalling patterns made with sticks), a colour sorting test with skeins of wool, and the Object Sorting Test. In the Object Sorting Test the person is given a collection of everyday objects (pieces of cutlery, tools, toys, smoking materials, objects of food and so on) and is asked to sort them into groups that belong together, and to explain why. The interest lies not in whether the person can make groups at all, but in the principle behind the groups and in the way the person explains it.

The test entered clinical psychology through David Rapaport (1911 to 1960), who with Merton Gill and Roy Schafer at the Menninger Clinic in Kansas published Diagnostic Psychological Testing (1945 to 1946), the book that defined the American test battery for a generation (Chapter 10, Section 10.5 tells how Rapaport also shaped Rorschach scoring). Rapaport's version has two parts. In the active part the examiner hands the person an object and asks the person to put with it all the other objects that belong with it, and then to say why. In the passive part the examiner makes the groups and asks the person what the objects in each group have in common. The responses are judged on two dimensions. The first is the level of the concept: abstract or conceptual (the objects are all "cutlery" or "tools"), functional (they are all "used for eating"), or concrete (they are all "made of metal" or "all on the table"). The second is the adequacy of the concept's boundaries: a concept may be too loose, taking in objects that do not belong; too narrow, leaving out objects that plainly do; or fabulated, built on a personal story rather than on the objects' properties.

Why should this reveal thought disorder? Because the disorganised thinking of schizophrenia is, in part, a disorder of concepts. In 1938 the American psychologist Norman Cameron described the thinking of schizophrenic patients as overinclusion: an inability to keep within the boundaries of a concept, so that the patient includes things that are only distantly related, or related by personal association. In Britain in 1962 R. W. Payne built an Object Classification Test and, with D. Friedlander, a short battery of simple tests to measure overinclusive thinking. In the United States in the late 1930s Eugenia Hanfmann and Jacob Kasanin adapted the coloured blocks with which Lev Vygotsky (1896 to 1934) had studied concept formation in children, wooden blocks of different colours, shapes, heights and sizes with nonsense words under them that mark the hidden categories, and reported that patients with schizophrenia formed new concepts poorly even when their former intellectual level had been high. In Australia in 1954 S. H. Lovibond published a study of "the object sorting test and conceptual thinking in schizophrenia", with a scoring method of his own. The Object Sorting Test, then, is the classic direct test of disordered conceptual thinking: it puts the person's concepts on the table, literally, for the examiner to see.

Here is an invented set of responses to show how scoring works. The examiner hands over a spoon and asks the person to add everything that belongs with it. Response 1: "a fork and a knife: they are cutlery". That is an adequate, conceptual sort. Response 2: "a plate and a cup: you use them when you eat". That is adequate but functional. Response 3: "a key and a screwdriver: they are all shiny metal". That is a concrete sort, built on a surface property. Response 4: "the pencil: both are long and thin". That concept is trivial and inconsequential, the kind of response one Indian scoring scheme calls impoverished. Response 5: "the candle and the matchbox: the spoon is for the holy water, the fire burns the bad thoughts, and that is why they came to my house". That sort is driven by personal significance and symbolism rather than by any property of the objects: a peculiar, fabulated response, the kind most strongly associated with thought disorder. Response 6: "everything on the table, because they are all things in the room" is overinclusive: the boundary of the concept has dissolved. A single odd response means little; a record full of responses like 5 and 6, given by a person who sorts ordinary objects competently in other respects, is strong evidence of disordered thinking.

Indian context

The Object Sorting Test is still part of diagnostic psychological testing in Indian clinical psychology training settings. An Indian adaptation was used by B. R. Sahithya, Shweta Rai and Rishikesh Behere in a study published in the Indian Journal of Psychological Medicine in 2021. It has 30 objects, an active phase of ten trials and a passive phase of sixteen, and responses are classified, using Rapaport's criteria, as adequate, fused (several concepts merged into one response, which the authors note is seen mostly in bipolar disorder), impoverished or peculiar; inter-rater reliabilities of 0.78 for the qualitative and 0.90 for the quantitative scoring are reported for it. In 30 patients with schizophrenia and 30 matched healthy people, the patients scored worse on the OST and on executive tests from the NIMHANS battery, and their impoverished responses were associated with poor planning, set-shifting and perseveration. Classical thought disorder and executive dysfunction, the authors concluded, are closely related.

Two quicker tests of abstraction are given at the bedside. Proverb interpretation asks what a familiar proverb means, and similarities ask what two things have in common; Book 2, Section 2.6.7 describes both, with Indian examples and the rule that a concrete answer to an unfamiliar proverb, or from a person with little schooling, is not by itself a sign of illness. A standardised version of the first, the Proverbs Test of Donald R. Gorham, appeared in Psychological Reports in 1956, with forms in which the person writes an interpretation and forms in which the person chooses the best interpretation from several, scored for abstraction; Gorham used it in the same year to separate patients with schizophrenia from healthy people. A standardised version of the second is the Similarities subtest of the Wechsler intelligence scales (Chapter 6). Halstead's Category Test, taught in Section 11.2, is a non-verbal test of abstraction and concept formation, and the WCST of this section is another. Table 11.27 gathers the tests of this subsection.

Table 11.27 Tests of abstraction, concept formation and thought disorder.
TestWho and whenWhat the person doesWhat it reveals

Colour-form sortingWeigl 1927Sorts shapes by colour, then by formAbility to shift the principle of sorting
Concept formation blocksVygotsky; Hanfmann and Kasanin (late 1930s)Discovers the hidden categories of coloured blocksConcept formation in schizophrenia
