Exam trap

"Disorder in thinking can be effectively assessed by" the Object Sorting Test, and that is the keyed answer. The Rorschach can show signs of disordered thinking only indirectly, through its special scores and the Perceptual-Thinking Index (Chapter 10, Section 10.5), and it is primarily a test of personality; the Thematic Apperception Test taps needs, press and themes in stories (Chapter 10, Section 10.6); the Bender-Gestalt measures visual-motor integration (Section 11.5). The Object Sorting Test examines concept formation directly, which is why it is the test of choice for "thought disorder" in the classical sense. Goldstein's terms are the "abstract attitude" and the "concrete attitude"; "overinclusion" is Norman Cameron's (1938), measured by Payne.

Language tests

The last family of tests in this section describes language. Its main use is in aphasia, the loss or disturbance of language after brain damage, most often a left-hemisphere stroke; the aphasia syndromes themselves (Broca's, Wernicke's, conduction, global, anomic and the transcortical aphasias) are described in Book 3, Section 10.8.3, with a pointer in Book 1, Section 8.9.6. A language examination samples six things: spontaneous speech (is it fluent, grammatical, informative?), comprehension of spoken language, repetition, naming, reading and writing. The pattern across these, especially fluency, comprehension and repetition, identifies the syndrome. The Reitan-Indiana Aphasia Screening Test of the Halstead-Reitan battery (Section 11.2) samples them briefly; the tests below examine them properly.

The Boston Naming Test (BNT) of Edith Kaplan, Harold Goodglass and Sandra Weintraub, published in 1983 after an experimental version in 1978, is the standard test of confrontation naming. The person is shown 60 line drawings, graded from very common objects to rare ones, and names each. If the person has plainly misperceived the picture, the examiner gives a stimulus cue (a hint about what kind of thing it is); if the person still cannot name it, the examiner gives a phonemic cue, the first sound of the word. Difficulty in finding words, anomia, is present in almost every aphasia and early in Alzheimer's disease, and the cues help to locate the problem: a person who names the object as soon as the first sound is given has the word stored but cannot retrieve it, while a person whom no cue helps may have lost the word's meaning. A second edition appeared in 2001. Some of the pictures show objects that are unfamiliar in India, so a failure on them may reflect culture rather than aphasia; local norms and adapted versions are needed.

Comprehension is tested with the Token Test, published in the journal Brain in 1962 by the Italian neurologists Ennio De Renzi and Luigi Vignolo, under the title "The Token Test: a sensitive test to detect receptive disturbances in aphasics". Twenty plastic tokens, in two shapes (circles and squares), two sizes and five colours, are laid out in front of the person, who carries out spoken commands that grow longer and more complex: from "touch a yellow circle" to commands of the kind "touch the small blue square and the large white circle" and, at the hardest level, commands with grammatical relations such as "before touching the green circle, pick up the red square" (these examples are invented in the style of the test). Because the vocabulary is tiny and concrete, failure cannot be blamed on not knowing the words; it reflects difficulty in processing the length and grammar of spoken language, so the test detects mild comprehension problems that conversation hides. A Short Token Test (De Renzi and Faglioni, 1978) and a version in Benton and Hamsher's Multilingual Aphasia Examination are widely used.

For a full description of an aphasia there are two comprehensive batteries. The Boston Diagnostic Aphasia Examination (BDAE) of Harold Goodglass and Edith Kaplan (1972; second edition 1983; third edition 2000, with Barbara Barresi) came from the aphasia research unit of the Boston Veterans Administration hospital, the same group whose interest in how patients fail grew into the Boston process approach of Section 11.1. It examines conversational and expository speech, auditory comprehension, oral expression, reading and writing, rates the overall severity of the aphasia on a scale from 0 to 5, and describes the profile that identifies the syndrome. The Western Aphasia Battery (WAB) of Andrew Kertesz, at the University of Western Ontario in Canada (1982; revised edition, WAB-R, 2006), covers similar ground and is valued for producing a single summary number, the aphasia quotient (AQ), from four parts of the test.

Remember

Aphasia quotient (AQ) = (spontaneous speech + auditory comprehension + repetition + naming) × 2

Spontaneous speech is scored out of 20 (10 for information content and 10 for fluency); auditory comprehension, repetition and naming are each converted to a score out of 10 (by dividing raw totals of 200, 100 and 100 by 20, 10 and 10). The four add up to at most 50, and doubling gives a maximum of 100. An AQ below 93.8 is conventionally taken to indicate aphasia.

Push an invented patient through it: a 58-year-old bank clerk, two weeks after a left-hemisphere stroke, speaking in short, effortful phrases. He scores 6 for information content and 4 for fluency, so spontaneous speech = 6 + 4 = 10 out of 20. His auditory comprehension raw total is 170 out of 200, and 170 ÷ 20 = 8.5. His repetition raw total is 30 out of 100, and 30 ÷ 10 = 3.0. His naming raw total is 45 out of 100, and 45 ÷ 10 = 4.5. The sum is 10 + 8.5 + 3.0 + 4.5 = 26.0, and AQ = 26.0 × 2 = 52.0. That is far below 93.8, so he is aphasic, and moderately severely so. The profile matters as much as the number: low fluency, comprehension relatively well preserved, and poor repetition and naming is the pattern of a non-fluent aphasia of Broca's type, which the WAB's classification rules use exactly these four scores to identify. The cut-off of 93.8 comes from the original standardisation by Kertesz and Poole (1974) and is a convention: some people above it still have subtle word-finding problems.

India adds a complication that the Boston and Western batteries were not built for: many patients speak two or more languages, often with different scripts, and a stroke can affect them differently. Language tests must therefore be given in the person's first language and, where it matters, in the others, with adaptations and norms for each language rather than translations of English items. Table 11.28 summarises the language tests of this subsection.

Exam trap

The Boston Naming Test tests naming (expressive word retrieval); the Token Test tests comprehension (receptive language). Do not confuse the Token Test with the token economy of behaviour therapy. The BNT and the BDAE come from the Boston group (Kaplan and Goodglass); the COWA comes from Benton; the Token Test from De Renzi and Vignolo (1962); the WAB from Kertesz (1982), and its aphasia quotient doubles a total out of 50.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  4. Brenda Milner (1963) found that card-sorting was impaired after excisions of the prefrontal cortex, but not after orbitofrontal or temporal excisions.

11.7 Bedside Screening and Staging: The MMSE, MoCA, Glasgow Coma Scale and Dementia RatingsLower yield · 3 Qs

The batteries of Sections 11.2 to 11.6 need hours, a quiet room and a person who can sit at a table and cooperate. Many of the people a psychologist is asked to see have none of these. A retired man of seventy-five on a medical ward has been "a bit muddled since admission". A young woman brought in after a road accident is drowsy and answers questions with single words. A daughter says that her mother has been forgetting things for two years and wants to know how bad it is and what lies ahead. Each of these referrals asks a narrower question than a full neuropsychological assessment does. Is there any cognitive impairment here at all, enough to justify the full work-up? How deeply is consciousness impaired, and is it getting better or worse? How far has a dementia progressed? Does its pattern suggest strokes or a degenerative disease? For questions like these, clinicians use short, standard, scored instruments that can be given at the bedside in a few minutes or rated from an interview with the family.

You have already met some of them in use. Book 2 showed the Mini-Mental State Examination as part of the mental status examination (Book 2, Section 2.6.10, whose Table 2.18 lists its tasks) and the Glasgow Coma Scale as the standard record of the level of consciousness (Book 2, Section 2.6.2). Book 3 met the Montreal Cognitive Assessment in the diagnosis of Alzheimer's disease (Book 3, Section 10.9.6) and the Hachinski Ischaemic Score in vascular dementia (Book 3, Section 10.10.2). This section does not retell those pages. It treats the same instruments as what this book is about, measuring instruments, and asks of each one: what exactly does its score count, how is the score turned into a decision, against what norms, how wrong can it be, and when should it not be trusted at all? It begins with the logic that every screening test shares, because without that logic none of the cut-offs that follow make sense.

What a screen can and cannot do

A screening test is a brief test given to many people in order to pick out the few who need a fuller examination. It is deliberately cheap: a few minutes, no special equipment, an examiner who need not be a specialist. The price of that cheapness is that its result is not a description of the person but a decision with two values. A positive screen means "look further"; a negative screen means "no need for now". The decision is made by a cut-off score, the point on the scale that separates the two outcomes. The Mini-Mental State Examination's usual cut-off is written 23/24. The slash marks the gap between the two scores that the line falls between: 23 or below is a positive screen, 24 or above a negative one. So "a cut-off of 23/24" and "scores of 23 or less suggest impairment" say exactly the same thing, a point that examiners like to test by writing it one way in the question and the other in the options.

Because no screen is perfect, each decision can be right or wrong, and there are four possible outcomes. A person who has the condition and screens positive is a true positive; a person who has it and screens negative, and so is wrongly reassured, is a false negative. A person who does not have it and screens negative is a true negative; a person who does not have it but screens positive, and so is sent for assessment and frightened for nothing, is a false positive. To know which is which, everyone screened must also be examined by the best available method of deciding who truly has the condition, which researchers call the reference standard or "gold standard": for dementia, a full clinical and neuropsychological assessment by experts.

Two numbers summarise how well a screen sorts people. Sensitivity is the proportion of people who truly have the condition whom the screen catches: of the ill, how many did we find? Specificity is the proportion of people who truly do not have the condition whom the screen correctly passes: of the well, how many did we leave alone? A family, though, asks a different question. "My mother screened positive. How likely is it that she really has dementia?" That is the positive predictive value, the proportion of positive screens that are true cases. Its partner, the negative predictive value, is the proportion of negative screens that are truly free of the condition. Chapter 4 teaches these indices in full, together with the receiver operating characteristic curve that compares every possible cut-off; here you need just enough to read the screening studies in this section.

Remember

Sensitivity = TP ÷ (TP + FN)
Specificity = TN ÷ (TN + FP)

Positive predictive value = TP ÷ (TP + FP)
Negative predictive value = TN ÷ (TN + FN)

where TP is the number of true positives (ill, screened positive), FN the false negatives (ill, screened negative), TN the true negatives (well, screened negative) and FP the false positives (well, screened positive). Sensitivity and specificity look down the columns of Table 11.29 (by true condition); the predictive values look across its rows (by screen result).

Push some numbers through it. Imagine (the figures are invented) a free memory camp at a community hall in Mysuru that screens 200 people aged 60 and over with a 30-point screen and a 23/24 cut-off, after which every one of them is assessed in full. Suppose that 40 of the 200 truly have dementia. The proportion of a group that has the condition is called the base rate (or prevalence), here 40 ÷ 200 = 0.20, or 20 percent. Table 11.29 shows how the screen sorted them.

Table 11.29 An invented memory camp: 200 people screened with a 23/24 cut-off and then assessed in full.
Screen resultDementia presentDementia absentTotal
Positive (23 or less)34 (true positives)24 (false positives)58
Negative (24 or more)6 (false negatives)136 (true negatives)142
Total40160200

Work down the columns first. Of the 40 people with dementia, the screen caught 34, so the sensitivity is 34 ÷ 40 = 0.85, or 85 percent. Of the 160 people without dementia, it correctly passed 136, so the specificity is 136 ÷ 160 = 0.85, also 85 percent. Now work across the rows. Of the 58 people who screened positive, 34 truly had dementia, so the positive predictive value is 34 ÷ 58 = 0.586, about 59 percent. Of the 142 who screened negative, 136 were truly free of dementia, so the negative predictive value is 136 ÷ 142 = 0.958, about 96 percent. In plain words: a negative result at this camp was very reassuring, but a positive result was right only a little more than half the time.

Now take the same screen to a general practice where only 5 percent of the older patients have dementia. Picture 400 patients, of whom 20 (5 percent) have dementia. Sensitivity and specificity belong to the test and the cut-off, so they stay at 85 percent. The screen therefore catches 0.85 × 20 = 17 of the 20 and misses 3. Of the 380 people without dementia it correctly passes 0.85 × 380 = 323 and wrongly flags 380 − 323 = 57. The positive predictive value is now 17 ÷ (17 + 57) = 17 ÷ 74 = 0.23, about 23 percent: more than three out of every four positive screens are false alarms. The negative predictive value rises to 323 ÷ (323 + 3) = 323 ÷ 326 = 0.99. Nothing about the test changed; only the base rate did. This is the first reason why a screen never diagnoses. Where the condition is uncommon, most positives are false, and every one of them must be checked by a full assessment.

The second reason is that a screen measures performance today, not the cause of that performance. A low score can come from dementia, but also from delirium, depression, deafness, poor eyesight, little or no schooling, a sleepless night on a noisy ward, or simply being tested in a language that is not one's own. The screen cannot tell these apart; the clinician must. The third reason is that the cut-off is a choice, not a fact of nature. Figure 11.13 shows why. The scores of healthy older people and of people with dementia form two overlapping distributions, and wherever you draw the line, some healthy people fall on the wrong side (false positives) and some ill people fall on the other wrong side (false negatives). Move the line up, to 25/26 say, and you catch more of the ill (sensitivity rises) but flag more of the well (specificity falls); move it down and the reverse happens. Which way to move it depends on the cost of each mistake. When the next step is a harmless and available full assessment, a sensitive cut-off is sensible. When a positive screen will alarm a family and the full assessment is a three-hour journey away, specificity matters more. Several of the instruments in this section publish two cut-offs for exactly this reason.

Exam trap

Sensitivity is the proportion of true cases a screen detects; specificity is the proportion of non-cases it correctly passes. Do not swap them. Sensitivity and specificity do not change with the base rate, but the positive predictive value does: the rarer the condition, the larger the share of positives that are false. A cut-off written "23/24" means 23 or below is positive. A positive screen means "assess further", never "this person has dementia".

The Mini-Mental State Examination as a measuring instrument

In 1975 the psychiatrists Marshal F. Folstein and Paul R. McHugh and their colleague Susan E. Folstein published, in the Journal of Psychiatric Research, a paper with a plain title: "'Mini-mental state': a practical method for grading the cognitive state of patients for the clinician". The full cognitive examination of the time took a skilled examiner half an hour or more and produced a description, not a number. They wanted the opposite: eleven questions that took five to ten minutes, gave a score, and could be repeated at every visit. The test was "mini", they explained, because it covered only the cognitive side of the mental state and left out mood, abnormal experiences and the form of thought. They tried it on 206 patients with dementia, depression (including depressed patients whose thinking was impaired), mania, schizophrenia and personality disorders, and on 63 healthy older people, whose scores ran from 24 to 30. It separated the patients with cognitive disturbance from those without, and it gave nearly the same score when the same examiner gave it again (a test-retest correlation close to 0.9). That is the Mini-Mental State Examination (MMSE), still the most widely used cognitive screen in the world. Book 2, Section 2.6.10 lists its tasks; here the question is what its score is made of.

Its 30 points are spread across six kinds of task, described here in general terms (the test is copyrighted, and its items must not be reproduced): orientation to time and to place (10 points), registering three words (3), attention and calculation (5), recalling the three words a few minutes later (3), and a group of language and praxis tasks, naming, repeating, following a staged command, reading, writing and copying a figure (9). Look at that list as a test builder would, and three facts stand out. A third of the points are for orientation, which is preserved until a dementia is fairly well established. Only 3 points test the learning and recall of new material, the very ability that fails first in Alzheimer's disease. And no task at all tests the executive functions of Section 11.6 (planning, switching set, inhibiting a habitual response), which fail first in frontal and vascular disease. The MMSE is therefore a measure of "global" cognition that leans heavily on orientation and language, and its weaknesses follow directly from its contents.

The first weakness is a ceiling effect: when a test is too easy for the people taking it, many score at or near the maximum, and differences among them, including the difference between a healthy person and one in the early stages of decline, cannot be seen. A retired professor in the first year of Alzheimer's disease may still score 29, because the MMSE never asks him anything hard enough to fail. The opposite problem appears at the other end. A floor effect occurs when a test is too hard, so that many people score at or near the minimum: in severe dementia MMSE scores bunch near zero and the test can no longer show further decline, which is why the staging scales of Section 11.7.8 take over there. (Chapter 3, Section 3.8 shows what floors and ceilings do to reliability coefficients; Figure 11.13 shows the healthy scores bunched just below the ceiling of 30.)

The second weakness is that the score depends on things other than the brain's health. In 1993 Rosa M. Crum and her colleagues, including Folstein himself, published the MMSE scores of 18,056 adults sampled from the general population of five American communities in the Epidemiologic Catchment Area surveys. The median score fell with age, from 29 among people aged 18 to 24 to 25 among those aged 80 and over. It fell even more steeply with schooling: the median was 29 for people with at least nine years of school, 26 for those with five to eight years and 22 for those with four years or fewer. Read that last figure slowly. It means that at least half the people in the community with little or no schooling score below the usual cut-off of 23/24, although most of them have no dementia at all. Crum's group concluded that the MMSE should be used to identify current cognitive difficulty and not to make diagnoses, and that a person's score should be compared with norms for his or her own age and education (Chapter 5 explains why norms must match the person).

Here is what that means in a clinic. Veena, aged 66, never went to school. For forty years she has run her household and a vegetable stall, and she keeps all her accounts in her head; her son, who brought her for a check-up after a neighbour's dementia frightened the family, reports no change at all in how she manages. On the MMSE she scores 22. She is fully oriented, registers and recalls all three words, and manages the naming, repetition and spoken command, but she loses the eight points that assume schooling: the five of the school-style serial subtraction task, reading, writing a sentence and copying a figure (30 − 8 = 22). A score of 22 is a positive screen by the 23/24 rule, but it is exactly the median for people with four years of schooling or fewer in Crum's study, and her history shows no decline from her own earlier level (the individual comparison standard of Section 11.1). The low score reflects the test's assumptions, not her brain. Rescreening her with a version built for people without schooling, the subject of Section 11.7.3, is the right next step, not a referral for dementia.

Mood matters too. A depressed older person may answer slowly, give up easily and score in the impaired range, and the score rises as the depression lifts; the Folsteins' original sample included exactly such patients, and Book 3, Section 10.11.6 describes this "pseudodementia". Hearing, eyesight, the language of testing and an acute illness (Section 11.7.7) can lower a score in the same way. Clinics and drug trials sometimes divide MMSE totals into rough severity bands (scores in the low twenties suggesting mild, the teens moderate and single figures severe dementia), but the bands differ from source to source and none of them is part of the test.

The MMSE is most useful for following a person over time, and that is where measurement error matters most. How much does the score fall in Alzheimer's disease? In 2000 a meta-analysis by Han and colleagues pooled 37 studies of 3,492 patients followed for about two years and found an average fall of 3.3 points a year (95 percent confidence interval 2.9 to 3.7). But averages describe groups. For one person the question is whether a change is larger than the score's own error, and Chapter 3, Section 3.7 gave the tool: the standard error of the difference between two scores. Suppose (the reference figures are illustrative) that in people like Utkarsh, a 72-year-old retired civil engineer whom you will meet properly in Section 11.7.4, MMSE scores have a standard deviation of 3 points and a test-retest reliability of 0.89.

Remember

SEM = SD × √(1 − rxx)
SEdiff = √2 × SEM
Critical change (95 percent) = 1.96 × SEdiff

where SEM is the standard error of measurement of one score, SD the standard deviation of the scores in the reference group, rxx the test-retest reliability, and SEdiff the standard error of the difference between two scores on the same test (Chapter 3, Section 3.7).

Work it through. First the SEM: 1 − 0.89 = 0.11; the square root of 0.11 is 0.332; and 3 × 0.332 = 0.995, about 1.0 point. Then the standard error of the difference: 1.414 × 0.995 = 1.41 points. Then the critical change: 1.96 × 1.41 = 2.76 points. Because MMSE scores are whole numbers, a change has to be 3 points or more before it is unlikely (at the 0.05 level) to be measurement error alone. If Utkarsh scores 28 this year and 27 next year, the one-point drop says nothing; if he falls from 28 to 24, the four-point drop is very probably real. Repeated testing adds a second problem, the practice effect: people improve slightly simply because they have done the test before, which can hide a small real decline. For this reason the second edition, the MMSE-2 of 2010, comes in two alternate forms (Blue and Red), and in three lengths: a 16-point brief version, a 30-point standard version comparable with the original, and a 90-point expanded version with a story-memory task and a timed symbol-digit coding task added to lift the ceiling.

The MMSE's history also changed how screening tests are shared. For twenty-five years it was photocopied freely in hospitals around the world. In 2000 the authors transferred their rights to a company, and from 2001 the American publisher Psychological Assessment Resources (PAR) held exclusive rights to it, so official forms had to be bought and free copies disappeared; in 2011 Newman and Feldman described the episode in the New England Journal of Medicine as a warning about copyright on bedside tools. One lasting effect was a move towards screens that were free for clinical use, such as the MoCA and the ACE-III (whose developers removed the MMSE items that the earlier versions of the ACE had contained).

Exam trap: what the MMSE is for

The MMSE (Folstein, Folstein and McHugh, 1975; 30 points; cut-off 23/24) is used to screen for and track global cognitive impairment, especially dementia. It does not diagnose personality disorders, which are assessed by structured clinical interviews and personality inventories (Book 2, Section 2.1.4; Chapter 9). It does not measure the severity of depression, which is rated with scales such as the Beck Depression Inventory and the Hamilton scale (Section 11.8), even though depression can lower an MMSE score. It does not yield an intelligence quotient, which needs a full intelligence test such as the Wechsler scales (Chapter 6): an MMSE of 30 says only that the person passed easy cognitive tasks. Nor is the MMSE the same as the MSE, the clinician's whole description of the mental state (Book 2, Section 2.6). The MMSE has no clock drawing; its only drawing task is copying a figure.

Screening people without schooling: the Hindi Mental State Examination

Veena's case is not a rare exception in India. A large share of the country's older people, especially women and especially in villages, never went to school, and a screen whose items assume reading, writing and school arithmetic will label many of them impaired. In the early 1990s an American and Indian research team set out to count dementia in exactly such a population. The Indo-US Cross-National Dementia Epidemiology Study, in which the psychiatrist and epidemiologist Mary Ganguli of the University of Pittsburgh was a leading investigator, worked in Ballabgarh, a rural area of Haryana near Delhi, whose Hindi-speaking elders mostly had little or no education; its screening survey later covered more than 5,000 people aged 55 and over. The MMSE could not simply be translated. As Ganguli and her colleagues reported in 1995, they had to build a new version, the Hindi Mental State Examination (HMSE), in which items that depended on literacy or school knowledge were replaced by tasks within the everyday experience of rural elders. For example, asking the year, a calendar idea many of them never used, gave way to asking the time of day, and abstract serial subtraction gave way to a spoken word problem about handling money, which asks for the same subtractions in a concrete, familiar form.

Why not simply translate? Because translation fixes the language but not the content, and it is the content that carries the bias. A small study by Tiwari, Tripathi and Kumar (published in 2009) showed this neatly. They gave 40 urban people aged 60 and over, 20 who could not read and 20 who could, first a plain Hindi translation of the MMSE and a month later the HMSE. On the translation every one of the 20 illiterate people fell below the cut-off; on the HMSE only four did. Among the literate people the two tests flagged three and one. The translated MMSE, in other words, was measuring schooling as much as cognition, which is exactly what Chapter 4 calls test bias.

There are three general ways to make a screen fair to people without schooling, and you will meet all three in this section. The first is to change the items, as the HMSE did, and as a later version of the MoCA built for people with less than five years of schooling, the MoCA-Basic (Julayanont, Nasreddine and colleagues, 2015, first validated in Thailand), also did. The second is to keep the items but change the norms or cut-offs by education, as Crum's tables allow for the MMSE and as the Indian ACE-III studies of Section 11.7.5 do with separate cut-offs for people with up to ten years of schooling and those with more. The third is a crude bonus, such as the MoCA's extra point for twelve or fewer years of education. None of them removes the problem completely; each is an attempt to make the comparison fair, that is, to compare a person with people like her.

Indian context: before you trust a screen

When an Indian clinic reports a screening score, ask three questions. Was the test adapted (items changed and checked for difficulty) or merely translated? In which language was it given, and was that the person's own? And against people with how much schooling was the cut-off set? The HMSE (Hindi, for rural and largely illiterate elders, 1995), the Malayalam adaptation of the ACE (2004) and the ACE-III validated in seven Indian languages (2020) were each built to answer these questions. A score from a test that fails them should be read as "unclear", not "impaired".

Exam trap

The HMSE is Mary Ganguli and colleagues' 1995 Hindi adaptation of the MMSE for rural, largely illiterate elders at Ballabgarh, Haryana, in the Indo-US Cross-National Dementia Epidemiology Study. It is an adaptation, not a translation: items that depended on literacy or schooling were replaced or recast in everyday terms (the time of day for the year, a spoken money problem for abstract serial subtraction). Do not confuse it with Indian neuropsychological batteries such as the NIMHANS or PGI batteries (Section 11.3), which are long, multi-test assessments, not five-minute screens.

The Montreal Cognitive Assessment and mild cognitive impairment

By the early 2000s the MMSE's ceiling had become a practical problem. Memory clinics were seeing more and more people in the grey zone between normal ageing and dementia: people who, with someone who knew them well, noticed a real decline, whose memory test scores were clearly below what was expected for their age and education, but who still managed their daily lives. In 1999 Ronald Petersen's group at the Mayo Clinic had given this state its standard definition as mild cognitive impairment (MCI), and Book 3, Section 10.9.8 tells its story: people with MCI convert to dementia at a much higher yearly rate than healthy older people, though not all of them do. The MMSE was nearly blind to them. In Montreal the neurologist Ziad S. Nasreddine and his colleagues set out to build a screen that a busy family doctor could give in ten minutes and that would catch MCI. They published the Montreal Cognitive Assessment (MoCA) in the Journal of the American Geriatrics Society in 2005.

Like the MMSE, the MoCA is scored out of 30 on a single page, but the points are spread differently (described here in general terms; the items are copyrighted). Five points go to visuospatial and executive tasks: a short alternating trail that joins numbers and letters in order (a miniature of Part B of the Trail Making Test, Section 11.6), copying a three-dimensional figure, and drawing a clock (3 of the 5 points). Three points go to naming pictures, six to attention (repeating strings of digits forwards and backwards, a vigilance task and serial subtraction), three to language (repeating sentences and a timed letter-fluency task), two to abstraction (saying what pairs of things have in common), five to the delayed recall of a list of five words, and six to orientation. Check the arithmetic: 5 + 3 + 6 + 3 + 2 + 5 + 6 = 30. The word list is read twice for learning, but those learning trials earn no points; only free recall after a delay of about five minutes counts. One point is added for people with 12 or fewer years of formal education (the total cannot exceed 30), and a final score of 26 or more is regarded as normal.

Compare the two tests' contents and you can predict the result. The MoCA's memory task is harder (five words instead of three, and no credit for recall with a cue), it adds the executive tasks the MMSE lacks, and it gives far fewer points to easy orientation questions. Healthy people therefore score lower and spread out more, the ceiling disappears, and small losses become visible. Nasreddine's validation study tested 94 people with MCI, 93 with mild Alzheimer's disease (all scoring 17 or more on the MMSE) and 90 healthy older controls. With a cut-off of 26 (applied in this study to both tests), the MoCA picked out 90 percent of the people with MCI, against 18 percent for the MMSE, and 100 percent of the people with mild Alzheimer's disease, against 78 percent. The specificities ran the other way: the MMSE passed 100 percent of the healthy controls and the MoCA 87 percent. Apply Section 11.7.1. The MoCA bought its much higher sensitivity at the price of some specificity: about one healthy person in eight was flagged.

That price turned out to be higher in some groups than in Montreal. Several validation studies reported that the cut-off of 26 produced an inflated rate of false positives, particularly among older people and people with less education. In 2018 Carson, Leach and Murphy pooled nine such studies in a meta-analysis and found that a cut-off of 23 lowered the false positive rate and gave the best overall accuracy, and they recommended it. Many clinics still use 26, and some use lower figures; what you must remember for an examination is the published rule (26 and above normal, one point added for 12 or fewer years of education), and what you must remember for practice is that any cut-off is a choice with a cost.

Here is the MoCA at work. Utkarsh, the retired civil engineer, has 17 years of education. Over the last year he has missed appointments, lost the thread of conversations and handed the household accounts, which he always kept, to his wife. On the MMSE he scores 28: a clear negative screen. On the MoCA (the profile is invented) he scores 3 of 5 on the visuospatial and executive tasks (he lost his way on the alternating trail and set the clock hands wrongly), 3 of 3 on naming, 5 of 6 on attention, 2 of 3 on language (too few words on the fluency task), 2 of 2 on abstraction, only 1 of 5 on delayed recall, and 6 of 6 on orientation. Add them in order: 3 + 3 = 6; 6 + 5 = 11; 11 + 2 = 13; 13 + 2 = 15; 15 + 1 = 16; 16 + 6 = 22. With more than 12 years of education he earns no extra point, so his final score is 22, a positive screen by the cut-off of 26 and even by Carson's lower cut-off of 23. Notice where the points went: delayed recall, the trail, the clock and fluency, that is, memory and executive function, while orientation and naming were perfect. That pattern, and the 28 on the MMSE, is exactly what a ceiling effect hides. The screen does not tell anyone that Utkarsh has MCI or early dementia, still less which disease; it tells his doctor to send him for the memory tests of Section 11.4, the executive tests of Section 11.6 and an informant history.

Two practical facts complete the picture. The MoCA spread partly because it was free for clinical use, but since 1 September 2019 its publisher has required official training and certification of the people who give and score it; the publisher's pages say that from December 2025 this became optional for clinicians who use only the total score for screening or referral, while those who interpret individual tasks or domains still need it. Such rules change, so check them before using the test. And the MoCA now exists in alternate forms for retesting, in a version for people who are blind, in the MoCA-Basic for people with little schooling (Section 11.7.3), and in translations into many languages, including several Indian ones.

Exam trap: MoCA versus MMSE, option by option

What makes the MoCA (Nasreddine and colleagues, 2005) different from the MMSE? It is more sensitive to mild cognitive impairment, it adds executive and visuospatial tasks (a short trail, a cube copy, clock drawing, fluency, abstraction) and a harder five-word delayed recall, and it is scored out of 30 with 26 and above normal; so an option giving "25 or below" as the screening cut-off is saying the same thing as "below 26". It is not a test of language only without memory: delayed recall carries 5 of its 30 points. It is not reserved for neurologists: it was designed for first-line doctors and is widely given by psychologists and nurses (subject to the publisher's training rules). An option that calls the MoCA "freely" available is describing its early years: since 1 September 2019 its use has been tied to the publisher's training and certification, which became optional in December 2025 only for clinicians who use the total score alone. It is not much shorter than the MMSE: both take about ten minutes, and the MoCA's advantage is sensitivity, not brevity. Remember the education rule: add one point for 12 or fewer years of schooling.

Addenbrooke's Cognitive Examination, the clock and the Mini-Cog

A 30-point screen can say "impaired" or "not impaired", but it has too few points in each area to say much about the pattern. At Addenbrooke's Hospital in Cambridge, England, a team that included the Indian neurologist Pavagada S. Mathuranath as first author and the neurologist John R. Hodges wanted a bedside test that could do both: detect mild dementia and help to tell Alzheimer's disease from frontotemporal dementia, whose early signs are in behaviour, fluency and language rather than memory (Book 3, Section 10.10.3). In 2000 they published Addenbrooke's Cognitive Examination (ACE) in Neurology: a 100-point bedside battery covering six areas (orientation, attention, memory, verbal fluency, language and visuospatial ability). They tested 139 new memory-clinic patients (115 of whom had dementia) and 127 healthy controls matched for age and education, and offered two cut-offs, 88 and 83, to be chosen according to the setting: at 88 the ACE caught 93 percent of the people with dementia; at 83 its sensitivity fell to 82 percent, but it raised fewer false alarms, and its predictive value was better than the MMSE's across a wide range of base rates. They also proposed a ratio of the fluency and language scores to the orientation and memory scores (the "VLOM" ratio), because the two diseases damage these pairs in opposite proportions: in frontotemporal dementia fluency and language suffer more, in Alzheimer's disease orientation and memory do.

The test went through two revisions. The ACE-R (Eneida Mioshi and colleagues, 2006) kept the MMSE inside it, as the original ACE had done, so that an MMSE score could be read off at the same time. The ACE-III (Sharpley Hsieh and colleagues, 2013) replaced those embedded items to avoid a copyright problem, and reports five domain scores that add to 100: attention 18, memory 26, verbal fluency 14, language 26 and visuospatial ability 16 (check: 18 + 26 + 14 + 26 + 16 = 100). Its usual cut-offs are 88 (very few cases missed) and 82 (very few false alarms), with scores between them treated as uncertain; a 30-point Mini-ACE followed for quick use. Because each domain has room for many points, the ACE-III gives a profile, not just a total.

Take an invented profile: attention 16 of 18, memory 12 of 26, fluency 9 of 14, language 24 of 26, visuospatial 15 of 16. The total is 16 + 12 + 9 + 24 + 15 = 76, below even the specific cut-off of 82, so impairment is very likely. Now turn each domain into a percentage of its maximum, so that domains of different sizes can be compared: attention 16 ÷ 18 = 89 percent; memory 12 ÷ 26 = 46 percent; fluency 9 ÷ 14 = 64 percent; language 24 ÷ 26 = 92 percent; visuospatial 15 ÷ 16 = 94 percent (all rounded to whole percentages). Memory stands far below everything else, a pattern that fits an amnestic, Alzheimer-type picture; had fluency and language been the lowest while memory held up, the pattern would have pointed towards frontotemporal dementia. Even so, a profile on a screen is a hypothesis for the full assessment to test, not a diagnosis.

The ACE has a strong Indian thread. In 2004 Mathuranath, Hodges and colleagues published a Malayalam adaptation (the m-ACE) for a community in south India. Most items were kept; for people with nine or more years of schooling the scores and the relative difficulty of the items matched the British sample, and for people with eight years or fewer the scores were lower but the items kept the same order of difficulty. In 2020 a multicentre Indian team (Mekala and colleagues, with Suvarna Alladi as senior author) adapted the ACE-III into seven Indian languages (Hindi, Telugu, Kannada, Malayalam, Urdu, Tamil and Indian English) and validated it on 757 controls, 242 people with dementia and 204 with MCI in five cities. Its sensitivity and specificity for dementia ranged from 0.90 to 1. Education, but not language, had an independent effect on scores, so the team set separate cut-offs for people with up to ten years of schooling and for those with more: the second of the three routes to fairness described in Section 11.7.3. Table 11.30 sets the brief screens of this section side by side.

Table 11.30 The brief cognitive screens compared.
Screen (authors, year)Points and timeWhat it coversUsual cut-offMain limits
MMSE (Folstein, Folstein and McHugh, 1975)30; 5 to 10 minutesOrientation, registration, attention, recall, language, one copy23 or below suggests impairment (23/24)Education-bound; ceiling; no executive tasks; copyrighted
HMSE (Ganguli and colleagues, 1995)Adapted from the MMSELiteracy-free substitutes for school-based itemsStudies differHindi only; built for rural elders
MoCA (Nasreddine and colleagues, 2005)30; about 10 minutesAdds trail, cube, clock, fluency, abstraction, five-word recall26 or above normal; +1 for 12 or fewer years of educationFalse positives in older, less educated people
ACE-III (Hsieh and colleagues, 2013)100; about 15 minutesFive domain scores: attention, memory, fluency, language, visuospatial88 and 82Longer; education effects (Indian cut-offs by schooling)
Mini-Cog (Borson and colleagues, 2000)About 3 minutesThree-word recall plus a clock drawingCombined rule for recall and clockCoarse; a brief first filter only

The single task that appears in more screens than any other is the clock drawing test. The person is asked to draw a clock face, put in all the numbers and set the hands to a given time; a setting such as ten past eleven is popular because the word "ten" pulls a careless or stimulus-bound patient's minute hand towards the 10 instead of the 2. One drawing demands a surprising amount: understanding the instruction, remembering what a clock looks like, planning the spacing of twelve numbers around a circle, placing them without neglecting half of space, translating "ten past" into a hand pointing at the 2, and inhibiting the pull of the visible "10". Because so many abilities are needed, clock drawing is sensitive to many kinds of brain disease, though for the same reason a poor clock does not say which ability failed; the way it fails is often more informative than the score, as Figure 11.14 shows. Many scoring systems exist, from a few points to ten or more. Reviewing the literature from 1983 to 1998, the Canadian psychiatrist Kenneth I. Shulman found in 2000 that across studies the clock's mean sensitivity and specificity were both about 85 percent and its correlations with the MMSE generally above 0.5, despite the variety of scoring systems. The clock is also affected by schooling, since a person who has never used numbers or an analogue clock may draw poorly for reasons that have nothing to do with the brain.

The clock is also half of the shortest screen in common use. In 2000 the psychiatrist Soo Borson and her colleagues in Seattle combined three-word recall with a clock drawing to make the Mini-Cog, which they meant as a "cognitive vital sign" that a doctor could take as routinely as blood pressure. They tried it on 129 people with probable dementia and 120 without, in a community sample in which 124 people did not speak English. The Mini-Cog took about three minutes against seven for the MMSE, detected 99 percent of the people with dementia and classified 96 percent of all participants correctly, and, unlike the MMSE, its accuracy was not affected by language or education in that sample. Its brevity is also its limit: with only two tasks it can serve only as a first filter.

Exam trap

Clock drawing is part of the MoCA and the Mini-Cog, not the MMSE. Addenbrooke's Cognitive Examination is scored out of 100 (ACE-III: five domains, attention 18, memory 26, fluency 14, language 26, visuospatial 16; cut-offs 88 and 82); the first author of the original ACE (2000) is P. S. Mathuranath; the ACE-III (2013) removed the embedded MMSE items. A screen that gives two cut-offs is offering a choice between sensitivity (the higher cut-off) and specificity (the lower one).

The Glasgow Coma Scale

Until the 1970s a patient's level of consciousness after a head injury was recorded in words such as "semicomatose", "obtunded" or "stuporous", and different doctors meant different things by them. A nurse watching through the night could not tell from the notes whether the patient was better or worse than when the surgeon had last seen him, and two hospitals could not compare their patients at all. In Glasgow the neurosurgeons Graham Teasdale (born 1940) and Bryan Jennett (1926 to 2008), the first full-time professor of neurosurgery in Scotland, who in 1972 had also coined the term "persistent vegetative state" with the American neurologist Fred Plum, set out to replace these words with observations that anyone at the bedside could make and repeat. In 1974 they published "Assessment of coma and impaired consciousness: a practical scale" in The Lancet. The Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS) that grew from it records three things the patient does: opens the eyes, speaks and moves. Book 2, Section 2.6.2 places it among the levels of consciousness; this subsection is about how it is scored and read.

Each of the three responses has its own ordered set of levels, and the examiner records the best response seen, first to speech and then, if needed, to a standard physical stimulus such as firm pressure on a fingertip. Eye opening has four levels, verbal response five and motor response six, as Table 11.31 shows (in the current wording of the Glasgow group: "to sound" rather than "to speech", "to pressure" rather than "to pain"). The scale grew in stages. The 1974 paper described the three responses side by side; in 1976 the motor scale was given its sixth level by separating normal flexion (withdrawal of the limb from the stimulus) from abnormal flexion, the stereotyped bending of the arms called decorticate posturing; and adding the three into a single total from 3 to 15 became common later in the 1970s, although Teasdale had not originally intended the responses to be summed.

Table 11.31 The Glasgow Coma Scale: the three responses and their levels.
ScoreEye opening (E)Verbal response (V)Motor response (M)
6(no level)(no level)Obeys commands
5(no level)Oriented: knows who, where and whenLocalises: moves a hand to the stimulus
4SpontaneousConfused conversationNormal flexion (withdraws)
3To sound (speech)Words: inappropriate, no conversationAbnormal flexion (decorticate)
2To pressure (pain)Sounds: moans, no wordsExtension (decerebrate)
1NoneNoneNone
Remember

GCS total = E + V + M, recorded with its parts, for example "GCS 10 (E2 V3 M5)". Eye opening runs 1 to 4, verbal 1 to 5 and motor 1 to 6, so the minimum is 3 (1 + 1 + 1, no response of any kind) and the maximum 15 (4 + 5 + 6, fully alert and oriented). Usual severity bands for head injury: 13 to 15 mild, 9 to 12 moderate, 3 to 8 severe; 8 or less is generally taken to mean coma.

Three measurement points follow from the way the scale is built. First, the levels within each response are ordered but not equally spaced, so the total is a convenient summary rather than a true measurement: a patient who moves from E2 to E3 has not improved by "the same amount" as one who moves from M2 to M3. Second, the same total can describe very different patients. A total of 10 could be E2 V3 M5, a drowsy patient who mutters a few words and pushes the examiner's hand away, or E4 V1 M5, a patient whose eyes are open and who localises well but says nothing, perhaps because a left-hemisphere injury has left him unable to produce language. Third, some responses cannot be tested at all. A patient with a tube in the windpipe cannot speak, and one whose eyelids are swollen shut cannot open them. Scoring such a response as 1 would wrongly suggest the deepest level of impairment, so it is recorded as "not testable" (NT), and the total is then meaningless. For all these reasons the Glasgow group, in a review marking the scale's fortieth year (Teasdale and colleagues, 2014), recommended that an individual patient be described by the three components, and that the total be used mainly to describe groups of patients.

Follow one patient. Bhushan, aged 23, came off his motorcycle on a highway without a helmet. Forty minutes later in the emergency department his eyes open only when the doctor presses firmly on his fingernail (E2); he says single words, sometimes swearing, but cannot hold a conversation (V3); and when pressure is applied above his eye he reaches up and pushes the hand away (localises, M5). His GCS is 2 + 3 + 5 = 10, recorded "GCS 10 (E2 V3 M5)", which falls in the moderate band of 9 to 12. Six hours later he opens his eyes when spoken to (E3), talks in sentences but thinks he is at his college (confused, V4) and squeezes the nurse's hand when asked (obeys commands, M6): GCS 3 + 4 + 6 = 13. The next morning his eyes are open spontaneously (E4), but he is still confused (V4) and obeys commands (M6): GCS 14. A rising score is the reassuring direction; a falling one, especially a fall in the motor response, is treated as an emergency because it can mean bleeding inside the skull.

Bhushan's 14 is also a reminder that the GCS stops where neuropsychology begins. He is awake but not yet laying down new memories reliably: each morning he asks again why he is in hospital. This state of confusion and disturbed new learning after a head injury is post-traumatic amnesia (PTA), and its duration is a second index of how severe the injury was. It is tracked by short daily orientation and memory checks, such as the Galveston Orientation and Amnesia Test of Harvey S. Levin and his colleagues (1979). DSM-5-TR's table for grading traumatic brain injury puts the three indices side by side: a mild injury has a GCS of 13 to 15 (measured about 30 minutes after the event), loss of consciousness of 30 minutes or less and PTA of a day or less; a moderate injury has a GCS of 9 to 12, loss of consciousness of more than 30 minutes but less than 24 hours and PTA of more than a day but less than seven days; a severe injury has a GCS of 3 to 8, loss of consciousness of 24 hours or more and PTA of seven days or more. If Bhushan's PTA lasts four days, both his early GCS and his PTA place him in the moderate band. A full neuropsychological assessment of his attention, memory and executive functions (Sections 11.4 and 11.6) should wait until the PTA has ended, because testing during PTA measures his confusion, not the lasting effects of the injury.

Two refinements complete the scale. Because the GCS leaves out the pupils' reaction to light, a key sign of brainstem function, Brennan, Murray and Teasdale proposed in 2018 the GCS-Pupils score (GCS-P): count how many pupils fail to react to light (0, 1 or 2) and subtract that number from the GCS total, giving a range of 1 to 15. A patient with a GCS of 6 and neither pupil reacting has a GCS-P of 6 − 2 = 4. And because infants cannot obey commands or converse, paediatric versions of the scale adapt the verbal and motor levels to what a young child can do.

Exam trap: what the GCS measures

The Glasgow Coma Scale (Teasdale and Jennett, 1974) rates the level of consciousness from eye opening (1 to 4), verbal response (1 to 5) and motor response (1 to 6). It does not measure attention (Digit Span forwards, the Continuous Performance Test and cancellation tasks do that, Sections 11.4 and 11.6), memory (the Wechsler Memory Scale and the learning tests of Section 11.4) or intelligence (the Wechsler intelligence scales, Chapter 6). The "oriented" level of the verbal response is a sign of clear consciousness, not a memory test. The lowest score is 3, not 0, the highest is 15, and 8 or below means severe injury or coma. Motor 3 is abnormal flexion (decorticate) and motor 2 is extension (decerebrate).

Clinical link: reading the head-injury file

A psychologist asked to assess a person months after a head injury is rarely given the injury itself, only the file. The first GCS, the duration of loss of consciousness and the duration of PTA tell you how hard to look and what to expect: a person with a GCS of 14 and an hour of PTA who now reports severe, worsening memory loss presents a different puzzle from one with a GCS of 5 and three weeks of PTA. Note too that DSM-5-TR grades the neurocognitive disorder by its present effect on daily life, not by the severity of the original injury, so a "mild" injury can still leave a disorder that matters to the person.

Screening for delirium: the Confusion Assessment Method

Between full alertness and coma lies the state that most often confuses the people who order cognitive screens: delirium, an acute, fluctuating disturbance of attention and awareness caused by a medical illness, a drug or its withdrawal (Book 3, Sections 10.8.4 and 10.8.8 teach it, and Book 2, Section 2.6.2 describes the clouding of consciousness at its core). Delirium is common on medical and surgical wards, dangerous, and easily missed, because the patient may be lucid at the moment the doctor arrives. In 1990 the geriatrician Sharon K. Inouye and her colleagues at Yale published the Confusion Assessment Method (CAM), designed so that clinicians without psychiatric training could detect delirium in under five minutes. The CAM rests on nine features drawn from the criteria of the time (DSM-III-R), but its diagnostic algorithm uses only four: (1) acute onset and fluctuating course, (2) inattention, (3) disorganised thinking and (4) an altered level of consciousness. Delirium is indicated when features 1 and 2 are both present, together with either 3 or 4. In its validation on 56 patients aged 65 to 98 at two hospitals, checked against psychiatrists' diagnoses, the CAM's sensitivity was 94 to 100 percent and its specificity 90 to 95 percent, and two raters agreed closely (kappa 0.81 to 1.0; Chapter 3, Section 3.6 explains kappa). Later versions adapted it for patients on ventilators in intensive care.

Abha, aged 79, was admitted with pneumonia. Her daughter says she was "her usual self" until three days ago; the night nurse reports that she is calm in the mornings but frightened and pulling at her drip at night (feature 1, acute onset and fluctuation, present). When asked to count backwards from 20 she loses her place twice and keeps looking at the door (feature 2, inattention, present). Her talk drifts from her grandson to the hospital food to a wedding forty years ago (feature 3, disorganised thinking, present), and she dozes off mid-sentence (feature 4, altered consciousness, present). Features 1 and 2 are present, and so is at least one of 3 and 4, so the CAM is positive: delirium is likely and its cause must be found and treated. One consequence matters for this section. An MMSE or MoCA given to Abha today would measure her delirium, not any underlying dementia; if her family's account suggests a longer decline, the dementia screens and the staging of Section 11.7.8 must wait until the delirium has cleared.

Exam trap

The CAM (Inouye and colleagues, 1990) diagnoses delirium only when acute onset with a fluctuating course and inattention are both present, plus either disorganised thinking or an altered level of consciousness. Inattention is the core feature: a patient who is attentive is very unlikely to be delirious. Never use a cognitive screen taken during delirium to diagnose or stage dementia.

Staging dementia: the Clinical Dementia Rating, the Global Deterioration Scale and FAST

Screening asks whether there is impairment. Once a dementia has been diagnosed, the questions change. How far has it progressed? Is it moving faster than expected? What help will the family need next year? Is the person eligible for a drug trial that admits only mild cases? These are questions of staging: placing a person on an ordered series of stages that describe the typical course of an illness. Staging scales differ from screens in three ways. They are usually rated by a clinician from an interview with the patient and someone who knows the patient well, not taken by the patient like a test. They weigh everyday functioning (managing money, travelling alone, dressing, eating) as heavily as test performance, because in moderate and severe dementia function is what matters and cognitive tests have reached their floor. And their scores are ordinal stages, not counts of correct answers. Figure 11.15 places the main screens and staging scales along the course of a degenerative dementia.

The most widely used staging scale in research is the Clinical Dementia Rating (CDR), published in the British Journal of Psychiatry in 1982 by Charles P. Hughes, Leonard Berg and their colleagues at Washington University in St Louis for a long-term study of mild senile dementia of the Alzheimer type, and given its current scoring rules by John C. Morris in 1993. The clinician conducts a semi-structured interview with the patient and an informant and then rates six areas, which the CDR calls "boxes": memory; orientation; judgement and problem solving; community affairs (work, shopping, social groups); home and hobbies; and personal care. Each box is rated on the same five-point scale: 0 (no impairment), 0.5 (questionable or very mild), 1 (mild), 2 (moderate) and 3 (severe); personal care has no separate 0.5 level. Two summary scores are then derived. The global CDR (0, 0.5, 1, 2 or 3) comes from a published algorithm that treats memory as the primary box. Its core rule is simple: if at least three of the five other boxes have the same rating as memory, the global CDR equals the memory rating; when the other boxes lean clearly to one side of memory, the global score follows the majority of them, and further rules (applied in practice with the official online calculator) settle the unusual patterns. The second summary, the CDR Sum of Boxes, simply adds the six ratings, so it runs from 0 to 18 and moves in half-point steps.

Why have two summaries? Because the global score has only five values and can stay at the same stage for years while the person declines within it. In 2008 O'Bryant and colleagues of the Texas Alzheimer's Research Consortium studied 1,577 people (110 controls, 202 with mild cognitive impairment and 1,265 with probable Alzheimer's disease) and found that Sum of Boxes ranges matched the global stages well: 0.5 to 4.0 for a global CDR of 0.5, 4.5 to 9.0 for a global 1, 9.5 to 15.5 for a global 2, and 16.0 to 18.0 for a global 3. The Sum of Boxes is therefore the finer measure for tracking change, and many drug trials use it as their main outcome.

Return to Abha. Six weeks after her pneumonia and delirium have cleared, the psychologist interviews her and her daughter. For two years Abha has repeated the same questions, forgotten recent family events and twice got lost in a market she has used all her life (memory 1, orientation 1). She can no longer sort out a disputed electricity bill or judge whom to trust with money (judgement and problem solving 1). She has stopped going alone to the temple committee she ran for decades and cannot shop alone (community affairs 1). She still cooks simple meals but has given up her embroidery and the more complicated dishes (home and hobbies 0.5). She dresses, bathes and eats without help (personal care 0). The global CDR: memory is 1, and three of the other boxes (orientation, judgement and community affairs) are also 1, so the global CDR equals the memory rating: CDR 1, mild dementia. The Sum of Boxes: 1 + 1 + 1 + 1 + 0.5 + 0 = 4.5, which falls in O'Bryant's range of 4.5 to 9.0 for a global CDR of 1. If next year her home and hobbies box falls to 1 and her personal care to 0.5, her Sum of Boxes will be 1 + 1 + 1 + 1 + 1 + 0.5 = 5.5: still global CDR 1, but a measurable decline that the global score alone would hide.

A second staging scale was built from close observation of how Alzheimer's disease unfolds. In 1982 the New York University psychiatrist Barry Reisberg and his colleagues Steven Ferris, Mony de Leon and Thomas Crook published the Global Deterioration Scale (GDS) for primary degenerative dementia in the American Journal of Psychiatry. Unlike the CDR, it has no boxes. It describes seven stages, each with a full clinical picture, and the clinician chooses the single stage that best fits the person (Table 11.32). Its stages run from no decline at all, through subjective complaints and the first objective deficits, to the point at which dementia can be diagnosed (stage 4) and on to the loss of speech and walking. Reisberg gave each stage a clinical "phase" name as well, and older papers use them. In 1988 he added a companion, Functional Assessment Staging (FAST), which stages the same course purely by the loss of everyday abilities. FAST has seven stages matched to the GDS, but its last two are divided into substages, five in stage 6 (6a to 6e) and six in stage 7 (7a to 7f), sixteen stages in all. The substages mark, in a typical order, the loss of the ability to dress, to bathe, to use the toilet, then continence, and finally speech, walking, sitting up and holding up the head. FAST is especially useful in severe dementia, where every cognitive test sits at its floor but the family and the care team still need to know where the person is and what comes next. Abha, with mild dementia and difficulty only with complex tasks such as money and shopping, would be at about GDS stage 4.

Table 11.32 The seven stages of Reisberg's Global Deterioration Scale (1982), in brief.
StageReisberg's labelClinical phaseWhat is typically seen
1No cognitive declineNormalNo complaints; nothing found at interview
2Very mild cognitive declineForgetfulnessComplains of misplacing things and forgetting names; no objective deficit
3Mild cognitive declineEarly confusionalFirst clear deficits, seen in demanding work and social situations
4Moderate cognitive declineLate confusionalClear deficits at interview; cannot manage complex tasks such as finances; dementia diagnosable from here
5Moderately severe cognitive declineEarly dementiaNeeds help choosing clothes; still eats and uses the toilet unaided
6Severe cognitive declineMiddle dementiaMay forget the spouse's name; needs help with dressing, bathing and toileting
7Very severe cognitive declineLate dementiaSpeech lost; needs help to eat and walk

Notice the trap built into the GDS's own labels: Reisberg's "moderate cognitive decline" (stage 4) corresponds to mild dementia, and his "severe cognitive decline" (stage 6) to moderately severe dementia, because his labels grade the cognitive decline, not the dementia. Read a question carefully before deciding which scale and which word it means.

Exam trap

There are two "GDS" scales. The Global Deterioration Scale (Reisberg, 1982) stages dementia in seven stages; the Geriatric Depression Scale (Section 11.8) is a self-report depression questionnaire for older people. Read the expansion. The CDR (Hughes and colleagues, 1982; scoring rules Morris, 1993) rates six boxes at 0, 0.5, 1, 2 or 3; memory is the primary box; a global CDR of 0.5 means questionable or very mild impairment (often the MCI range) and 1 means mild dementia; the Sum of Boxes runs from 0 to 18. Staging scales are clinician ratings from an interview with an informant, not tests the patient takes. FAST (Reisberg, 1988) stages by daily function and subdivides stages 6 and 7.

The Hachinski Ischaemic Score

Book 3, Section 10.10.2 tells how the neurologist Vladimir Hachinski and his colleagues argued in 1974 that "hardening of the arteries" was the wrong explanation for vascular dementia and that the real cause was the accumulated damage of many strokes, which they called multi-infarct dementia, and it lists the bedside features that point to a vascular cause. Here the question is how those features were turned into a score. In 1975, in a paper on cerebral blood flow in dementia in the Archives of Neurology, Hachinski's group published the Hachinski Ischaemic Score (often spelt "Ischemic", as in the original). It is not a test of cognition at all: it is a weighted checklist of 13 features of the history and examination, each present or absent. The five features most characteristic of strokes count 2 points each and the other eight count 1 point each (Table 11.33), so the maximum is 5 × 2 + 8 × 1 = 10 + 8 = 18. A total of 7 or more suggests a vascular (multi-infarct) dementia, a total of 4 or less a primary degenerative dementia such as Alzheimer's disease, and 5 or 6 leaves the question open.

Table 11.33 The 13 features of the Hachinski Ischaemic Score (1975) and their weights.
FeaturePointsFeaturePoints
Abrupt onset2Stepwise deterioration1
Fluctuating course2Nocturnal confusion1
History of strokes2Relative preservation of personality1
Focal neurological symptoms2Depression1
Focal neurological signs2Somatic (bodily) complaints1
Emotional incontinence1
History of high blood pressure1
Evidence of atherosclerosis elsewhere1
Five features at 2 points10Eight features at 1 point8

Score two patients. The cloth merchant in Book 3, Section 10.10.2 had an abrupt onset linked to "a turn" (2), a stepwise course (1), good days and bad days (fluctuating course, 2), relatively preserved personality and insight (1), sudden weeping and laughing (emotional incontinence, 1), long-standing high blood pressure (1), a history of strokes (2), focal symptoms in the weak right hand (2) and focal signs in his brisk right-sided reflexes and extensor plantar response (2). His total is 2 + 1 + 2 + 1 + 1 + 1 + 2 + 2 + 2 = 14, far above 7. Abha's dementia, by contrast, began insidiously and has declined smoothly, with no strokes, no focal symptoms or signs and no emotional incontinence; her personality has changed (she has become withdrawn and suspicious), so that feature scores nothing. She has treated high blood pressure (1) and low mood (1). Her total is 2, well below 4, which points towards a degenerative cause. One detail of scoring matters: the fluctuation and night-time confusion Abha showed in hospital belonged to her delirium, not to the course of her dementia, and must not be counted.

How good is the score? In 1997 Moroney and colleagues, Hachinski among them, pooled data from six centres on 312 patients whose diagnoses had been confirmed at autopsy: 191 with Alzheimer's disease, 80 with multi-infarct dementia and 41 with "mixed" dementia (Alzheimer changes plus cerebrovascular disease). The mean scores were 3.1 for Alzheimer's disease, 10.5 for multi-infarct dementia and 7.7 for mixed dementia, and the original cut-offs (4 or less, 7 or more) separated the pure Alzheimer cases from the pure multi-infarct cases with a sensitivity of 89.0 percent and a specificity of 89.3 percent. But the score could not pick out mixed dementia: separating multi-infarct from mixed cases, its specificity was only 17.2 percent, and separating Alzheimer from mixed cases only 29.4 percent. Since mixed dementia is common in the very old, and since modern brain imaging shows strokes directly, the Hachinski score today is a quick bedside guide and a research tool, not a diagnosis. A shortened eight-item version by Rosen and colleagues (1980), checked against autopsy findings, is also in use.

Exam trap

A high Hachinski score points to vascular dementia; a low score to Alzheimer-type (degenerative) dementia. The cut-offs are 7 or more and 4 or less; the scale has 13 items, the maximum is 18, and it dates from 1975, the same year as the MMSE. It is a checklist of history and signs, not a cognitive test, and it is weak at identifying mixed dementia.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. The proportion of people with a condition whom a screen correctly flags is its ; the proportion of people without the condition whom it correctly passes is its .
  2. A screen with 85 percent sensitivity and 85 percent specificity is used where 5 percent of 400 people have dementia. It gives 17 true positives and 57 false positives, so its positive predictive value is about percent.
  3. The MMSE of Folstein, Folstein and (1975) is scored out of 30; its usual cut-off of 23/24 means that a score of or below is a positive screen.
  4. When a test is so easy that healthy and mildly impaired people all score near the maximum, it shows a effect.
  5. The Hindi Mental State Examination was built by Mary and colleagues (1995) for rural, largely illiterate elders at in Haryana.
  6. A woman with ten years of schooling scores 25 on the MoCA. Her final score is , which is in the range.
  7. The ACE-III is scored out of across five domains, and the first author of the original ACE (2000) was P. S. .
