  9. On the Confusion Assessment Method, delirium requires acute onset with a fluctuating course and , plus either disorganised thinking or an altered level of consciousness.
  10. A CDR interview gives memory 2, orientation 2, judgement 2, community affairs 2, home and hobbies 2 and personal care 1. The global CDR is and the Sum of Boxes is .

Answers: (1) sensitivity; specificity, (2) 23, because 17 ÷ (17 + 57) = 17 ÷ 74 = 0.23, (3) McHugh; 23, (4) ceiling, (5) Ganguli; Ballabgarh, (6) 26, because one point is added for 12 or fewer years of education (25 + 1 = 26); normal, since 26 and above is normal, (7) 100; Mathuranath, (8) 8, because E2 + V3 + M3 = 2 + 3 + 3 = 8; severe (3 to 8), (9) inattention, (10) 2, because all five other boxes but one match the memory rating of 2; 11, because 2 + 2 + 2 + 2 + 2 + 1 = 11.

11.8 Clinical Rating Scales I: Principles, Depression, Anxiety and OCDHigh yield · 9 Qs

Picture two women who have each been told by a psychiatrist that they have a depressive episode. The first is still going to work, though everything takes effort and nothing gives her pleasure. The second has not left her bed for a week, has stopped eating and thinks her family would be better off without her. The diagnosis is the same word for both. What the clinician also needs is a number: how ill is each of them today, and in a month's time, is she better or worse than she was? The neuropsychological tests of Sections 11.1 to 11.7 ask what a person's brain can still do. The instruments of this section and the next ask a different question: how much of a disorder's symptoms does this person have, and how is that changing?

The modern clinical rating scale grew out of a practical problem of the 1950s. The first antipsychotic and antidepressant drugs had arrived, and psychiatrists had to show, in numbers that a sceptic could check, that patients given a drug improved more than patients given a placebo. A doctor's impression that a ward "seemed calmer" would not do. Within three years the three founding scales of this section were published: Max Hamilton's anxiety scale in 1959, his depression scale in 1960 and Aaron Beck's depression inventory in 1961. Most of the scales you will meet here, and in Section 11.9, were built in the three decades that followed, and nearly every drug trial, psychotherapy trial and audit of a clinic's results since then has been judged by them. This section first explains how a rating scale works and what makes one good or bad, and then teaches the main scales for depression, anxiety and obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD), with their scoring.

What a rating scale is, and what it is for

A rating scale is an instrument on which a rater (the patient, a clinician or someone who knows the patient well) assigns a number to the presence or severity of each of a set of symptoms or behaviours, choosing from defined response options, and then combines those numbers into a score. Three features are packed into that definition. There is a fixed list of items, each covering one symptom or one aspect of a symptom. There are defined response options, so that "moderate" means the same thing every time. And there is a rule for turning the item ratings into a total, usually by adding them up.

How does this differ from a test? The word "test" in the strict sense (Chapter 1 sets out the definitions) belongs to tasks with right and wrong answers that ask a person to do his best: a vocabulary item, a block design, a digit span. Lee Joseph Cronbach (1916 to 2001), in his Essentials of Psychological Testing (1949), divided instruments into measures of maximum performance, which ask "how well can you do this?", and measures of typical performance, which ask "what are you usually like?". Neuropsychological and intelligence tests are maximum-performance measures. A rating scale belongs to the second family: there is no right answer to "how much have you been sleeping?", only a true one. That is why a rating scale has no items that are "passed" or "failed", and why it depends so heavily on the honesty, insight and memory of whoever does the rating.

A rating scale also differs from a diagnostic interview. A structured diagnostic interview such as the Structured Clinical Interview for DSM (SCID) or the Mini International Neuropsychiatric Interview (MINI) asks, in effect, a yes or no question: does this person meet the criteria for disorder X? (Book 2, Section 2.1.4 describes these schedules.) A rating scale asks "how much?". The difference matters because a high score on a depression scale does not by itself diagnose a depressive disorder. A grieving widow, a patient with hypothyroidism and a student in the week of his examinations can all score high on a depression inventory for different reasons. The diagnosis comes from a clinical interview, a history and the diagnostic criteria (Book 3); the scale tells you how severe the picture is and, when it is repeated, whether it is changing.

Rating scales are used for four purposes, and a good clinician always knows which one he has in mind. A screening instrument is short and is given to many people, most of whom are well, to pick out those who may have a disorder and need a full assessment: a positive screen means "assess further", never "diagnosis made". A severity measure grades how ill an already identified patient is, which guides decisions such as whether to add medication or refer for specialist care. An outcome measure is the same severity measure given repeatedly, before, during and after treatment, so that change can be seen. And in research, scales define who enters a trial (for example, "a score of at least so much") and whether the treatment worked. Some scales serve several purposes; others were built for one and work badly at another. Max Hamilton said plainly that his depression scale was meant for patients already diagnosed with depression, to measure how ill they were; it was never meant to find cases in the community.

Exam trap

A rating scale measures how much; a diagnostic interview decides whether. When a question asks for "a measure of the severity of depression" the answer is a rating scale (Beck Depression Inventory, Hamilton scale); when it asks for "a structured instrument for making a DSM diagnosis" the answer is an interview schedule (SCID, MINI, Book 2, Section 2.1.4). A high Beck Depression Inventory score never diagnoses major depressive disorder by itself, and a screening tool's positive result means only "assess further".

Who does the rating: self-report, clinician-rated and informant-rated scales

The first thing to ask of any scale, and the thing examiners ask most often, is who fills it in. In a self-report scale (also called a self-rating scale or inventory) the patient reads each item and marks the answer that fits her; the clinician only adds up the score. The Beck Depression Inventory, the Beck Anxiety Inventory, the Zung Self-Rating Depression Scale, the PHQ-9, the GAD-7 and the State-Trait Anxiety Inventory all work this way. In a clinician-rated scale (also called an observer-rated scale) a trained interviewer talks to the patient, watches her, often reads the case notes and talks to the family, and then decides the rating for each item himself. The Hamilton Rating Scale for Depression, the Hamilton Anxiety Rating Scale, the Montgomery-Åsberg Depression Rating Scale and the Yale-Brown Obsessive Compulsive Scale are clinician-rated, and so are most of the psychosis and mania scales of Section 11.9. In an informant-rated scale a third person who sees the patient in daily life (a parent, a teacher, a spouse, a nurse) does the rating: the parent and teacher forms for children described in Section 11.9 are the standard examples.

Why have both kinds? Because each sees what the other misses. Only the patient knows how guilty she feels, whether she has thought about death, or how much the thoughts in her head distress her, and a self-report asks her directly, without an interviewer whose tone or expectations might colour the answer. Self-reports are also cheap: they take five to ten minutes, need no trained staff and can be repeated every week. But they require that the patient can read the language of the form, understands the items, is honest and has some insight. A severely depressed patient may be too slowed to complete the form, a patient seeking compensation or disability benefits may exaggerate, a patient who fears admission may minimise, and a patient with poor insight may not see his symptoms as symptoms at all. A clinician can rate what the patient cannot or will not report: slowed movements, agitation, a flat face, the hours spent washing that the patient calls "just being clean". A clinician-rated scale can also be used with patients who cannot read. Its costs are training, time (twenty minutes or more of interview) and the rater's own errors and biases, which Section 11.8.5 describes.

The two kinds of scale often disagree, and the disagreement is informative rather than a nuisance. Part of the reason is content: self-reports of depression such as Beck's give much of their weight to the cognitive and emotional symptoms (guilt, worthlessness, hopelessness), while Hamilton's clinician scale gives much of its weight to the bodily ones (sleep, appetite, agitation, physical anxiety). Part is the person: some patients feel far more than they show, and others, including many who express distress through the body rather than in words about mood, report less than the clinician sees. Table 11.34 sets out the three kinds side by side.

Table 11.34 Self-report, clinician-rated and informant-rated scales compared.
FeatureSelf-reportClinician-ratedInformant-rated
Who marks the scoreThe patientA trained interviewerParent, teacher, spouse, nurse
What it draws onInner experience: mood, guilt, worry, hopelessnessInterview, observation, notes, familyBehaviour seen in daily settings
StrengthsQuick, cheap, private, repeatableRates signs the patient cannot report; no literacy neededSees behaviour outside the clinic
WeaknessesNeeds literacy, insight, honestyNeeds training and time; rater errorsSees one setting; informant's own bias
Examples (this section)BDI-II, BAI, PHQ-9, GAD-7, Zung SDS, STAIHAM-D, HAM-A, MADRS, Y-BOCSChild scales of Section 11.9

Now work through the question that the papers set (RML 2022): "Which of the following is a clinician-rated questionnaire: (a) Beck Depression Inventory, (b) Beck Anxiety Inventory, (c) Hamilton Anxiety Rating Scale, (d) both b and c?" Take the options one at a time. The Beck Depression Inventory is a self-report inventory: the patient reads each group of statements and circles the one that fits. The Beck Anxiety Inventory, from the same group of authors, is also self-report: the patient rates how much each symptom has bothered her. The Hamilton Anxiety Rating Scale is completed by a trained clinician after an interview, so it is the clinician-rated one. Option (d) would be right only if both (b) and (c) were clinician-rated; since (b) is self-report, (d) is wrong. The answer is (c). A useful rule of thumb, with exceptions you will meet in Section 11.9, is that scales called "inventory" or "questionnaire" are self-reports and scales called "rating scale" (the Hamilton scales, the Montgomery-Åsberg) are clinician-rated; the word "questionnaire" in the question's stem is loose usage, since a clinician-rated scale is not really a questionnaire at all.

Exam trap

All the Beck inventories you meet here (depression, anxiety, hopelessness) are self-report; both Hamilton scales (anxiety 1959, depression 1960) are clinician-rated. So "both the Beck Anxiety Inventory and the Hamilton Anxiety Rating Scale are clinician-rated" is false. The one Beck instrument of this section that is clinician-rated is the 1979 Scale for Suicide Ideation, which is an interview; its 1991 successor, the Beck Scale for Suicide Ideation, is a self-report. The Yale-Brown Obsessive Compulsive Scale is clinician-rated too, although self-report versions have since been made.

How a scale is built: items, anchors, time frames, totals, bands and cut-offs

Open any rating scale and you will find the same parts. Each item covers one symptom (low mood, sleep, guilt, worry) or one aspect of a symptom (the time obsessions take, the distress they cause). Each item offers a set of response options, and the format of those options comes in a few standard kinds. In a Likert-type response format, named after Rensis Likert (1903 to 1981), whose 1932 method of attitude measurement summed people's ratings of agreement with a set of statements (Chapter 8 teaches the Likert scale as an attitude scale), each item is answered on an ordered set of five or so options, such as "not at all, several days, more than half the days, nearly every day" (the frequency format of the PHQ-9 and GAD-7) or "not at all, mildly, moderately, severely" (the intensity format of the Beck Anxiety Inventory). In a dichotomous format each item is simply yes or no, or true or false, as on the Geriatric Depression Scale and the Beck Hopelessness Scale; this is easier for elderly or ill patients but gives less information per item. In a graded-statement format, the one the Beck Depression Inventory made famous, each item is a group of four statements describing the same symptom at increasing severity, and the patient picks the statement that fits. And in a visual analogue scale the patient marks a point on a line, traditionally 100 millimetres long, whose ends are labelled with the extremes ("no anxiety at all", "the worst anxiety I can imagine"); the score is the distance from the left end in millimetres, from 0 to 100.

For a clinician-rated scale the response options are anchors (or anchor points): written descriptions attached to each score that tell the rater exactly what a 0, a 1 or a 3 looks like. A scale whose points carry only bare labels such as "mild" and "severe" leaves each rater to decide for himself what "severe" means, and two raters will disagree. A scale whose points carry concrete descriptions ("wakes most nights and takes more than an hour to fall asleep again") makes them agree. The quality of the anchors is the single biggest influence on inter-rater reliability, the agreement between two raters scoring the same interview, which Chapter 3, Section 3.6 teaches with its statistics (kappa and the intraclass correlation). Figure 11.16 shows an invented anchored item and how its rating becomes part of a total.

Every scale also states a time frame: the period the ratings refer to. The Beck Depression Inventory-II asks about the past two weeks, including today; the PHQ-9 and GAD-7 also ask about the past two weeks; the Hamilton scales and the Yale-Brown scale are usually rated for the past week; some mania ratings use the past 48 hours (Section 11.9). The time frame is not a detail. A rating of "how you feel today" swings with the weather of the day, while a two-week window matches the duration that the diagnostic criteria for a depressive episode require; a scale used to track weekly change in therapy cannot use a window longer than a week or two without each rating overlapping the last.

Next comes the total. On most scales it is a simple sum of the item scores, and the possible range follows from the number of items and the range of each item. This little piece of arithmetic is worth doing every time, because examiners love to ask for ranges.

Remember

Lowest possible total = number of items × lowest item score. Highest possible total = number of items × highest item score (when all items share one range).

So 21 items scored 0 to 3 give 0 to 63 (21 × 3 = 63); 10 items scored 0 to 4 give 0 to 40; 20 items scored 1 to 4 give 20 to 80, not 0 to 80, because the lowest score on each item is 1. When items have different ranges, add the item maxima one by one: Hamilton's 17-item depression scale has nine items scored 0 to 4 and eight scored 0 to 2, so its maximum is 9 × 4 + 8 × 2 = 36 + 16 = 52, not 17 × 4 = 68.

Some scales deliberately word half their items in the healthy direction ("I enjoy the things I used to do") and half in the ill direction ("I feel downhearted"). They do this to catch acquiescence, the tendency of some respondents to agree with whatever they are asked (Chapter 2 teaches it among the response biases). An item worded in the healthy direction is a reverse-keyed item: before adding up, its score is turned round, so that agreeing with "I enjoy things" counts as health, not illness. For an item scored from a lowest value to a highest value, the rule is:

Remember

Reversed score = (highest item score + lowest item score) − raw score

For an item scored 1 to 4, the reversed score is 5 − raw; for an item scored 0 to 3, it is 3 − raw.

Worked example. Kirti, a trainee clinical psychologist in a district hospital outpatient department, is scoring a self-rating depression form whose 20 items are each answered 1 ("a little of the time") to 4 ("most of the time"), with ten items worded positively; this is the design of William Zung's scale, met again in Section 11.8.7, but the patient's answers here are invented. On the ten negatively worded items the patient's answers add up to 27. On the ten positive items her raw answers are 1, 2, 1, 1, 2, 1, 1, 2, 1, 1. Step 1: reverse each one with 5 − raw: the answers 1 become 4 and the answers 2 become 3, giving 4, 3, 4, 4, 3, 4, 4, 3, 4, 4. Step 2: add them: seven 4s make 28 and three 3s make 9, so the reversed total is 28 + 9 = 37. Step 3: add the two halves: 27 + 37 = 64. The total of 64 lies in the possible range 20 to 80. Had Kirti forgotten to reverse, she would have added 13 instead of 37 and reported 40, a score that makes a fairly depressed woman look nearly well. Reversal mistakes are among the commonest scoring errors in practice, which is why scoring templates and computer scoring exist.

Finally, the total has to be interpreted. There are two ways of doing this. A severity band divides the range into labelled segments (minimal, mild, moderate, severe), so that a total can be described in words in a report. A cut-off score is a single point chosen so that people at or above it are treated as probable cases: on the PHQ-9 the usual cut-off is 10. Both are conventions set by the scale's authors or later researchers, usually by comparing scores with the diagnoses of clinicians who interviewed the same people, and choosing the point that best balances sensitivity (the proportion of true cases the cut-off catches) against specificity (the proportion of non-cases it correctly passes). When Kurt Kroenke and his colleagues checked the PHQ-9 against interviews by mental health professionals in 580 patients, a score of 10 or more caught 88 percent of the people with major depression and correctly passed 88 percent of those without it. Chapter 4 teaches sensitivity, specificity and how cut-offs are chosen; for now, notice that a different cut-off would have traded one kind of error for the other, and that bands are agreed conventions, not facts of nature. That is why different sources sometimes print different bands for the same scale, and why a careful report names the bands it used. Norms, the score distributions of reference groups that let you say "higher than 90 percent of outpatients", are a further aid, taught in Chapter 5.

Exam trap

Do the range arithmetic before you choose an option. A scale whose items start at 1 cannot have a minimum of 0 (the Zung scale runs 20 to 80; the State-Trait Anxiety Inventory's two halves each run 20 to 80). A scale with mixed item ranges cannot be "items × 4" (the 17-item Hamilton depression scale tops out at 52, not 68). And "Likert scale" names a response format and an attitude-scaling method (Rensis Likert, 1932), not a clinical test: many clinical scales use Likert-type options, but "a Likert scale" is never the name of a depression or neuropsychological instrument.

What makes a good scale: reliability, validity and sensitivity to change

A rating scale is judged by the same standards as any psychological measure, and Chapters 3 and 4 teach those standards in full. Here the question is what each standard means for a symptom scale in particular.

Reliability comes first. For a self-report scale the usual evidence is internal consistency, most often Cronbach's alpha (Chapter 3, Section 3.5): do the items hang together as measures of one thing? For a clinician-rated scale the crucial evidence is inter-rater reliability (Chapter 3, Section 3.6): if two trained clinicians watch the same interview, do they give the same total? When Wayne Goodman and his colleagues introduced the Yale-Brown Obsessive Compulsive Scale in 1989, their reliability study had four raters score 40 patients with OCD, and agreement on the total and on each of the ten items was excellent; that study, more than anything else, is why the scale became the standard. Test-retest reliability (Chapter 3, Section 3.2) is checked over a short interval of days, not months, because a symptom scale is supposed to change when the patient changes: a low correlation between a depression score in January and one in June says more about the course of depression than about the scale (Chapter 3, Section 3.2.4 makes this point about states and traits).

Validity asks whether the scale measures what it claims to (Chapter 4). Content validity asks whether the items cover the symptoms of the disorder: the Beck Depression Inventory was rewritten in 1996 partly so that its items matched the DSM-IV criteria for a depressive episode, and the PHQ-9's nine items simply are those nine criteria. Convergent validity asks whether the scale correlates with other measures of the same thing (a new depression scale should correlate with the Hamilton scale), and discriminant validity asks whether it correlates less with measures of different things: the Beck Anxiety Inventory was built specifically to overlap as little as possible with depression, and the Yale-Brown scale's total correlates only weakly with depression and anxiety ratings in OCD patients who are not markedly depressed. Criterion validity, for a screening scale, is the comparison with an interviewer's diagnosis that produced the PHQ-9's sensitivity and specificity figures above.

The third standard is the one that makes a clinical scale clinical. A scale's sensitivity to change (also called responsiveness) is its ability to move when the patient genuinely improves or worsens. A scale can be reliable and valid as a snapshot of severity and still be sluggish as a measure of change, if its items cover symptoms that change slowly or that a treatment does not touch. For a drug trial, a sluggish scale is expensive: it hides real effects, so the trial needs more patients to show them.

Key study: building a scale to be sensitive to change (Montgomery and Åsberg, 1979)

The question. By the 1970s the Hamilton depression scale was the standard outcome measure in antidepressant trials, but it was criticised as heavy with bodily symptoms, some of which the drugs' own side effects could produce, and as slow to show change. Could a scale be built on purpose to detect change? The method. Stuart Montgomery in England and Marie Åsberg in Sweden started from a long 65-item psychopathology scale (the Comprehensive Psychopathological Rating Scale) rated on 54 English and 52 Swedish patients, and picked out the 17 symptoms most common in depressive illness. They then followed 64 patients through trials of four different antidepressants and kept the 10 items that changed most with treatment and correlated best with overall change. The result. The new 10-item scale had high agreement between raters, correlated well with the Hamilton scale (so it measured the same thing), and separated responders from non-responders better than the Hamilton scale did. The conclusion. Selecting items for responsiveness produces a scale that needs fewer patients to show a drug's effect, which the authors pointed out had ethical as well as practical value. The criticism. A scale chosen for what changed with antidepressants may suit drug trials better than psychological treatments or very different patient groups.

How much change counts? Three yardsticks are used, and they answer different questions. The first is percentage change from the starting score:

Remember

Percentage reduction = (baseline score − follow-up score) ÷ baseline score × 100

Baseline is the score before treatment; follow-up is the score at the later rating.

Following the consensus definitions that Ellen Frank's task force proposed in 1991 (Book 3, Section 5.7.1), and the conventions of the trials since, a response in depression is usually a fall of at least 50 percent on a scale such as Hamilton's, and remission is reaching a score low enough to count as essentially well, commonly 7 or less on the 17-item Hamilton scale (the threshold the task force suggested). Worked example. Charvi, a 27-year-old bank clerk in Pune, starts treatment for a depressive episode; her clinician's Hamilton rating at the first visit is 26. At week 8 it is 12. Step 1: the fall is 26 − 12 = 14 points. Step 2: divide by the baseline: 14 ÷ 26 = 0.538. Step 3: multiply by 100: 53.8 percent, which rounds to 54 percent. Since 54 percent is more than 50 percent, Charvi has responded; but since 12 is well above 7, she has not remitted, and treatment should continue toward full recovery. A patient who starts at 40 and falls to 20 has also responded (50 percent) while remaining very ill, which is why trials report both response and remission.

The second yardstick asks whether a change is bigger than the scale's own measurement error. Neil Jacobson and Paula Truax's reliable change index, taught with a worked example in Chapter 3, Section 3.7.6, divides the change by the standard error of the difference; a value beyond 1.96 means the change is unlikely to be measurement error alone. The third asks whether the change matters to the patient. The minimal clinically important difference (MCID), a term introduced by Roman Jaeschke, Joel Singer and Gordon Guyatt in 1989, is the smallest change in score that patients perceive as beneficial and that would make a clinician change the management; in their studies it was about half a point per item on seven-point scales. A change can be statistically reliable yet too small to matter, or large enough to matter yet within the error of an unreliable scale, so the three yardsticks are used together.

Two last properties complete the list. A scale has a floor effect when many people score at or near its lowest possible value, so it cannot distinguish among the mildly ill, and a ceiling effect when many score at or near the top, so it cannot show that a very ill patient has become worse. A scale built for hospitalised melancholic patients may show a floor effect in a college counselling centre, and a brief screen may show a ceiling effect on an inpatient ward. And a total score assumes that the items measure one underlying thing. When factor analysis (taught in the Research Methodology book) shows that a scale's items fall into several separate clusters, as it does for Hamilton's depression scale, the total mixes different things, and researchers have proposed shorter "core" subscales that measure the central mood symptoms more cleanly.

Exam trap

The Montgomery-Åsberg scale (1979) is the depression scale designed for sensitivity to change, by keeping the 10 items that moved most with antidepressant treatment. Response (usually a fall of 50 percent or more) is not remission (a score low enough to be essentially well, for example 7 or less on the 17-item Hamilton scale): a patient can respond and still be ill. The minimal clinically important difference (Jaeschke, Singer and Guyatt, 1989) is about what matters to patients; the reliable change index (Jacobson and Truax, 1991) is about measurement error. Do not swap them.

Errors that raters make

Whenever a person, rather than a scoring key, decides the score, the person becomes part of the measuring instrument, and people bring their own systematic errors with them. Chapter 3, Section 3.6 explains how agreement between raters is measured and noted these errors in one line each. This is the place to understand them, because clinical ratings are where they do their damage.

Key study: Thorndike and the halo (1920)

Edward Lee Thorndike (1874 to 1949) examined ratings that had real consequences: official ratings of army officers and aviation cadets by their superior officers in the United States Army, used to decide salaries and promotions, and, in an earlier study of 1915, ratings of employees of two large industrial corporations. The officers had been rated separately on qualities such as physique, intelligence, leadership and character. These are different qualities, and in reality they are only modestly related, yet the ratings correlated with each other too highly and too evenly: even physique correlated about 0.3 to 0.4 with intelligence and leadership. Thorndike concluded that the raters could not treat each quality on its own; a general impression of the man suffused every separate judgement, like a halo. His paper, "A constant error in psychological ratings" in the Journal of Applied Psychology (1920), gave the error its name. The lesson for clinicians is uncomfortable: the more a rater likes, or dislikes, a patient overall, the more every item drifts in the same direction.

The halo effect, then, is the error in which an overall impression, or one striking attribute, colours the ratings of other, separate attributes. It can run either way: a negative halo (sometimes called the horns effect) drags every rating down. In symptom rating it has a particular form that an RML paper (2024) asked about: when a patient's anxiety improves markedly, the rater may also record an improvement in her depression, although her depression has not changed. The striking improvement in one symptom spreads, as a halo, to the rating of another, and the right answer to "the improvement in anxiety may sometimes spuriously improve depression; this is called..." is the halo effect, not a ceiling effect (a scale too short at the top to register change), a Hawthorne effect (people changing because they know they are being studied) or a therapeutic effect (a real improvement).

Several other errors were catalogued in the classic psychometrics textbooks, notably Joy Paul Guilford's (1897 to 1987) Psychometric Methods, first published in 1936. A leniency error (or generosity error) is a rater's constant tendency to rate too favourably; its mirror image, the severity error, is a constant tendency to rate too harshly. On a symptom scale "favourable" means "less ill", so a clinician who is fond of a patient, or who hopes his treatment is working, may rate symptoms as milder than they are. The reverse pressure exists too, and trial methodologists warn about it: in drug trials that admit patients only above a threshold score, raters may nudge entry scores upward (so-called baseline score inflation), so that the patients then appear to improve partly because their first scores were inflated. A central tendency error is the avoidance of the extremes of the scale: the rater, unsure, gives everyone a 1 or a 2 on a 0 to 4 item, so that differences between patients shrink. A logical error is rating two attributes alike because the rater believes, as a matter of his own theory, that they go together: he rates a patient who sleeps badly as anxious, because "poor sleepers are anxious people", without asking about anxiety. The difference from halo is the source: halo comes from a general impression of the person, a logical error from the rater's beliefs about which symptoms belong together. A contrast error is rating a person against an inappropriate standard, either the previous patient (a moderately depressed patient seen after a profoundly depressed one looks mild) or the rater himself. A proximity error is rating items alike because they sit next to each other on the form. Expectancy is a further, separate danger: a rater who knows which patients are receiving the active drug sees what he expects, which is why trials use blind raters who do not know the treatment. And rater drift is the slow wandering of a rater's standards away from the anchors over months of use, which repeated calibration corrects. Table 11.35 lists these errors with an example and a remedy for each.

Table 11.35 Rater errors on clinical rating scales.
ErrorWhat happensClinical exampleMain remedy
Halo (Thorndike 1920)A general impression or one striking feature colours other ratingsAnxiety improves, so depression is also rated betterRate each item from its own evidence; anchors
Leniency or severityConstant tendency to rate too mildly or too harshlyClinician hoping treatment works under-rates symptomsBlind raters; calibration against a standard
Central tendencyExtremes avoided; everyone rated in the middleEvery patient given 2 on a 0 to 4 itemConcrete anchors for the extreme points
Logical errorItems rated alike because the rater thinks they go togetherPoor sleep, so anxiety rated high without askingAsk about each symptom; structured interview guide
ContrastRated against the last patient or oneselfModerate case looks mild after a severe oneRate against the anchors, not other patients
ProximityNeighbouring items on the form rated alikeAdjacent sleep items given identical scoresClear item-by-item questioning
Rater driftStandards wander from the anchors over timeSame interview scored higher a year laterPeriodic retraining and reliability checks

The remedies follow from the causes. Concrete anchors leave less room for impressions. A semi-structured interview guide, which gives the rater suggested questions in a fixed order but lets him follow up, makes raters gather the same evidence before they rate: Janet Williams published such a guide for the Hamilton depression scale in 1988, and the Yale-Brown scale has always come with one. Training on videotaped interviews, with each trainee's ratings compared against expert ratings until they agree, deals with leniency, severity and central tendency. Blind raters deal with expectancy. Hamilton himself suggested that two clinicians should rate each interview independently and that their ratings be combined, which, as Chapter 3, Section 3.6 shows, makes the combined rating more reliable than either alone.

Exam trap

Halo (Edward Thorndike, 1920) is the error in which one global impression or one attribute colours the ratings of others; it is the answer to "the improvement in anxiety may spuriously improve depression" (RML 2024) and to "judging an individual on the basis of one single attribute" (IHBAS 2024). It is not a logical error (the rater's belief that two traits go together), a central tendency error (rating everyone average), a contrast error (rating relative to the last person or to oneself) or a similarity error (favouring people like the rater). Leniency and severity are constant errors: they shift everyone the same way.

The Beck Depression Inventory

In the late 1950s Aaron Temkin Beck (1921 to 2021), a psychiatrist at the University of Pennsylvania in Philadelphia, was trying to test a psychoanalytic idea about depression, and he needed a way of measuring how depressed his patients were that did not depend on one psychiatrist's impression. (The research led him away from psychoanalysis to cognitive therapy, whose story Book 2, Chapter 5 tells.) In 1961, with C. H. Ward, M. Mendelson, J. Mock and J. Erbaugh, he published "An inventory for measuring depression" in the Archives of General Psychiatry. The items were not taken from a theory or a textbook. Beck chose them from his clinical observations: the attitudes and symptoms that depressed patients often described and that other psychiatric patients rarely did. Each became a group of statements describing the same symptom at increasing severity, from its absence to its extreme, and in the first version an interviewer read each group aloud while the patient followed on her own copy and chose the statement that fitted. The Beck Depression Inventory (BDI) became the most widely used self-report measure of depression in the world.

It has had three main forms. The 1961 original was revised during the 1970s at Beck's Center for Cognitive Therapy as the BDI-IA (copyrighted in 1978 and published in 1979 in Beck's Cognitive Therapy of Depression), with simpler wording, fully self-administered, and asking about the past week, including today. In 1996 Beck, Robert Steer and Gregory Brown published the BDI-II, rewritten so that the items matched the DSM-IV criteria for a major depressive episode. Four items that dealt with weight loss, body image, bodily preoccupation and difficulty working were dropped; four on agitation, worthlessness, difficulty concentrating and loss of energy were added; the sleep and appetite items now allow for an increase as well as a decrease, because depression can make people sleep and eat more as well as less; and the time frame was lengthened to the past two weeks, including today, to match the two-week duration in the diagnostic criteria. The BDI-II is meant for ages 13 to 80, takes five to ten minutes, and is published, under copyright, by Pearson (formerly The Psychological Corporation). Like every copyrighted test in this book, its items are not reproduced here: in general terms, they cover the cognitive and emotional symptoms of depression (sadness, pessimism, a sense of failure, loss of pleasure, guilt, self-dislike and self-criticism, suicidal thoughts, crying, indecisiveness, worthlessness) and its bodily and vegetative symptoms (agitation, loss of energy, sleep, appetite, concentration, tiredness and loss of interest in sex). Factor analyses usually find two clusters, a cognitive-affective one and a somatic one.

Scoring is simple, which is part of the inventory's appeal. Each of the 21 items is scored 0, 1, 2 or 3 according to the statement chosen; if the patient finds that several statements in a group fit equally well, the instructions ask her to choose the highest. The total is the sum of the 21 item scores, so it runs from 0 (21 × 0) to 63 (21 × 3). The BDI-II manual divides that range into four bands, which Figure 11.17 draws to scale.

Remember: BDI-II

21 items, each 0 to 3, for the past two weeks including today; total 0 to 63.

0 to 13 minimal, 14 to 19 mild, 20 to 28 moderate, 29 to 63 severe (Beck, Steer and Brown, 1996). Self-report, ages 13 to 80.

Worked example. Charvi, the bank clerk whose Hamilton ratings you followed in Section 11.8.4, also completes the BDI-II at her first visit. Her item scores, in order from item 1 to item 21, are invented for this example (the item content is not shown): 2, 1, 1, 3, 1, 0, 2, 1, 0, 1, 1, 2, 1, 1, 2, 2, 1, 0, 1, 1, 0. Step 1: rather than adding 21 numbers in a row, where slips are easy, count how many times each score appears. There is one 3 (item 4). There are five 2s (items 1, 7, 12, 15 and 16). There are four 0s (items 6, 9, 18 and 21). That leaves 21 − 1 − 5 − 4 = 11 items scored 1. Step 2: multiply and add: 1 × 3 = 3; 5 × 2 = 10; 11 × 1 = 11; 4 × 0 = 0; and 3 + 10 + 11 + 0 = 24. Step 3: check the count of items: 1 + 5 + 11 + 4 = 21, so none has been missed. Step 4: place the total in a band. Since 24 is at least 20 and no more than 28, Charvi's score falls in the moderate band. Step 5: look at the single items as well as the total. Her item 9 (a 0 here, invented) would in a real record be checked first, because on the BDI-II that item concerns suicidal thoughts and any score above 0 calls for a risk assessment (Book 2, Section 2.7.2). Interpreted in plain words: by her own account, Charvi has a moderate level of depressive symptoms, with her highest ratings on a few items rather than mild complaints spread evenly, a pattern her therapist would explore.

Eight weeks later Charvi scores 12. The fall is 24 − 12 = 12 points, and 12 ÷ 24 = 0.50, a reduction of exactly 50 percent; her score has moved from the moderate band into the minimal band. Is the change larger than measurement error? Using the illustrative figures of Chapter 3, Section 3.7.6 (a depression inventory with a standard deviation of 8 and a reliability of 0.88), a fall of about 7.7 points or more is needed before chance can be set aside; Charvi's 12 points clears that bar. Her self-report and her clinician's Hamilton rating tell the same story here. When they do not, the clinician asks why: has the patient learned what the clinician hopes to hear, or does she feel better than she looks?

Four of this chapter's questions turn on the BDI, and each rewards the facts above. The first (NIMHANS practice mock) describes a 35-year-old with two months of persistent sadness, loss of pleasure, weight loss, insomnia, worthlessness and poor concentration, and asks which assessment would be most appropriate to evaluate her symptoms further. The Beck Depression Inventory is right: the picture is of a depressive episode, and the BDI quantifies its severity in ten minutes and gives a baseline against which treatment can be judged. The Rorschach Inkblot Test is a projective technique for personality and perceptual-cognitive functioning (Chapter 10); its Comprehensive System even has a Depression Index (Chapter 10, Section 10.5), but it is long, needs expert scoring and is not the instrument for measuring a depressive episode. The MMPI is a broad personality and psychopathology inventory of several hundred items (Chapter 9); its depression scale is one of ten clinical scales, so it is far less direct. The WAIS measures intelligence (Chapter 6), which is not the question here. Note what the BDI does not do in this case: it does not confirm the diagnosis (a clinical interview against the criteria does that), and it does not replace asking her directly about suicidal thoughts.

The second (MET mock) asks how the BDI-II measures severity. Option (a), "21 items rated 0 to 3 corresponding to symptoms over the past two weeks", is exactly right. Option (b), "31 items rated 0 to 4", matches no Beck scale. Option (c), "10 items rated 0 to 10", sounds like a short clinician scale, but the 10-item Montgomery-Åsberg scale rates each item 0 to 6, not 0 to 10. Option (d), "1 item rated 0 to 100", describes a single global rating, such as a visual analogue scale of mood or the old 0 to 100 Global Assessment of Functioning (Section 11.9), not an inventory.

The third and fourth (MET mocks) ask for the bands. The conventional BDI-II bands are 0 to 13 minimal, 14 to 19 mild, 20 to 28 moderate and 29 to 63 severe, so the band that flags "moderate" depression is 20 to 28; 14 to 19 is mild, 0 to 13 minimal and 29 to 63 severe. The distractor bands in the other options are worth a moment. "0 to 9 minimal" is the opening of the older BDI's bands: for the 1961 and 1978 versions the commonly cited bands, the Center for Cognitive Therapy guidelines reported in Beck, Steer and Garbin's 1988 review of the inventory, are 0 to 9 none or minimal, 10 to 18 mild to moderate, 19 to 29 moderate to severe and 30 to 63 severe (other sources print slightly different cut-offs), so a question about the BDI-II should not be answered with them. A set ending at 40 cannot belong to a scale whose maximum is 63; 40 is the Yale-Brown maximum. A three-band split of 0 to 20 minimal, 21 to 40 moderate and 41 to 60 severe is simply invented: it has no mild band and stops at 60, three points short of the BDI-II's maximum. And bands of five points such as 0 to 5, 6 to 10 and 11 to 15 belong to no Beck scale; the PHQ-9's thresholds, which step up by five (5, 10, 15, 20), are the likeliest source of the confusion.

Exam trap: the BDI keyed as a neuropsychological test (RML 2025)

RML 2025 asked "Which of the following tests is typically used for neuropsychological assessment?" with the options Beck Depression Inventory, Likert Scale, 16 Personality Factor Questionnaire and Peabody Individual Achievement Test, and keyed the Beck Depression Inventory. The BDI is not a neuropsychological test: it is a self-report inventory of depressive symptoms and tests no brain function. It earns its place in a neuropsychological assessment because neuropsychologists routinely add a mood measure: depression lowers effort, speed and memory on tests, and severe depression in an older adult can even mimic dementia (Book 3, Section 10.11.6). The other three options are further off. A Likert scale is a response format and an attitude-scaling method (Rensis Likert, 1932; Chapter 8), not a test at all. The 16PF is Raymond Cattell's personality inventory (Chapter 9). The Peabody Individual Achievement Test (Lloyd Dunn and Frederick Markwardt, 1970) is an individually given test of school achievement (Chapter 8); achievement tests can enter a neuropsychological work-up, for example when a learning disability is in question, but not as the typical example. In the examination, choose the BDI as keyed, knowing that the defensible reason is "the mood measure that a neuropsychological battery commonly includes", not "a test of brain function".

Clinical link: depression scales in neurological and medical patients

When a neuropsychologist gives the BDI-II to a patient after a head injury, a stroke or in multiple sclerosis, the somatic items need care: fatigue, poor concentration, poor sleep and loss of energy may come from the brain illness itself, not from depression, and inflate the total. Clinicians therefore look at the cognitive-affective items separately, or use a scale that leaves out bodily symptoms: Beck's seven-item BDI-FastScreen for medical patients, or the Hospital Anxiety and Depression Scale (Section 11.8.7), which was designed for this problem. The reverse error matters too: an apathetic patient with frontal damage may report little distress on any self-report, and a clinician's rating and the family's account are needed.

Indian context

A self-report inventory assumes that the patient can read the form in a language she thinks in. In many Indian clinics the clinician reads the items aloud and records the answers, which turns a self-report into an interviewer-administered scale (as the 1961 BDI originally was); the report should say so. A translation should be one that has been checked, not one improvised at the desk, because a word chosen for "guilt" or "worthless" can shift an item's meaning. Many patients in Indian primary care first describe depression in bodily terms (aches, weakness, poor sleep), so the somatic items carry a heavy load and a mainly cognitive scale can under-read their distress. Brief scales are also used to select patients for Indian trials: the PREMIUM trial of lay-counsellor treatment in Goa enrolled adults with PHQ-9 scores above 14 (Book 2, Section 5.11.5).

Exam trap

BDI-II: 21 items, 0 to 3, two weeks, 0 to 63, 1996, Beck, Steer and Brown, self-report; moderate is 20 to 28. The BDI and the Beck Anxiety Inventory both have 21 items and a 0 to 63 range, so questions swap them. And two different Becks appear in this book: Aaron T. Beck (depression inventory, cognitive therapy) and Samuel J. Beck, whose Rorschach scoring system is in Chapter 10, Section 10.5.

Other depression scales: Hamilton, Montgomery-Åsberg, Zung, PHQ-9 and the rest

Max Hamilton (1912 to 1988) was born in Offenbach in Germany, came to Britain as a small child, trained in medicine at University College Hospital in London, began his psychiatric training at the Maudsley Hospital and finished it at University College Hospital, and spent most of his career at the University of Leeds, where he became professor of psychiatry in 1963. He was unusual among the psychiatrists of his time for his command of statistics and measurement. His "A rating scale for depression", published in the Journal of Neurology, Neurosurgery and Psychiatry in 1960, set out what the Hamilton Rating Scale for Depression (HAM-D, also abbreviated HRSD or HDRS) was for, and its limits. It was meant for patients already diagnosed with a depressive illness, to quantify how severe it was; it was not a diagnostic or screening instrument. The ratings were to be based on a clinical interview, and Hamilton wrote that the scale's value depended entirely on the skill of the interviewer.

The 1960 scale had 21 items, but Hamilton advised that only the first 17 be added into the total: the last four (diurnal variation, depersonalisation and derealisation, paranoid symptoms and obsessional symptoms) describe the kind of depression rather than its severity. His 1967 revision kept the 17-item total, and the 17-item version (HAM-D17) is still the one meant when a trial reports "the Hamilton". Its items are a mixture of the psychological symptoms of depression (low mood, guilt, suicidal ideas, loss of interest in work and activities, psychic anxiety, slowing and agitation, insight) and a large share of bodily ones (three separate items for difficulty falling asleep, waking in the night and waking early, plus appetite, weight loss, bodily anxiety, general physical symptoms, sexual symptoms and hypochondriasis). Nine items are scored 0 to 4 and eight are scored 0 to 2, which is why the total runs from 0 to 52 (the arithmetic is in Section 11.8.3). The time frame is usually the past week. Bands vary between sources; one commonly used set is 0 to 7 normal, 8 to 13 mild, 14 to 18 moderate, 19 to 22 severe and 23 or more very severe, while an empirical study by Mark Zimmerman and colleagues (2013), which compared scores with clinicians' global severity ratings in 627 outpatients, placed the boundaries at 8 to 16 mild, 17 to 23 moderate and 24 or more severe. Whatever the bands, 7 or less is the usual definition of remission.

Why has a 1960 scale lasted so long? Because so many trials used it that every new trial had to use it too, so that results could be compared, and because a clinician's rating catches the slowing, agitation and bodily changes of severe depression that a self-report can miss. Its weaknesses are just as well known. The heavy bodily content reflects the hospitalised, melancholic patients of the 1950s; it means that side effects of a drug (or the sleep-inducing effect of a sedating antidepressant) can move the score; some items have poor agreement between raters; and the items do not measure one single thing. A 2004 review by Michael Bagby and colleagues in the American Journal of Psychiatry asked, in its title, whether the "gold standard" had become "a lead weight". Structured interview guides, such as Janet Williams's of 1988, improved its reliability. Book 2, Section 5.4.3 shows the Hamilton and Beck scales in use side by side in cognitive therapy research.

The Montgomery-Åsberg Depression Rating Scale (MADRS), whose construction was the key study of Section 11.8.4, was published by Stuart Montgomery and Marie Åsberg in the British Journal of Psychiatry in 1979. It is clinician-rated, with 10 items covering sadness as the patient reports it and as the rater sees it, inner tension, sleep, appetite, concentration, lassitude, inability to feel, pessimistic thoughts and suicidal thoughts. Each item is scored 0 to 6, with written anchors at 0, 2, 4 and 6 and the odd numbers for ratings in between, so the total runs from 0 to 10 × 6 = 60. Only one item, the sadness the rater observes, rests on observation alone. Because it has fewer bodily items than the Hamilton scale and was selected for change, it is favoured in antidepressant trials. Its bands also vary between sources.

Another early and widely used self-report scale for depression came from William W. K. Zung of Duke University, whose Zung Self-Rating Depression Scale (SDS) appeared in the Archives of General Psychiatry in 1965. Its 20 statements cover the emotional, physiological and psychological symptoms of depression, and the patient rates each for how much of the time it applies, from "a little of the time" (1) to "most of the time" (4). Half the statements are worded in the healthy direction to guard against acquiescence, so half are reverse-scored, exactly as in Kirti's worked example in Section 11.8.3. The raw total therefore runs from 20 to 80, and Zung converted it to an index out of 100:

Remember

SDS index = raw score ÷ 80 × 100

The raw score is the sum of the 20 items after reversing the positive ones; 80 is the highest possible raw score. The index runs from 25 (a raw score of 20) to 100.

For the patient in Kirti's example, whose raw total was 64, the index is 64 ÷ 80 = 0.80, and 0.80 × 100 = 80. A raw score of 20, the lowest possible, gives 20 ÷ 80 × 100 = 25, which is why the index can never be 0. Zung also built a matching Self-Rating Anxiety Scale (1971).

The scale you will most often see in a general hospital or a primary health centre today is the Patient Health Questionnaire-9 (PHQ-9) of Kurt Kroenke, Robert Spitzer (1932 to 2015, the psychiatrist who led the making of DSM-III) and Janet Williams, published in 2001. It is the depression module of the Patient Health Questionnaire, a self-report version of an earlier interview for primary care called PRIME-MD. Its nine items are simply the nine DSM-IV criteria for a major depressive episode, and the patient rates how often each has bothered her over the past two weeks: not at all (0), several days (1), more than half the days (2), nearly every day (3). The total runs from 0 to 27. In the validation study, 6,000 patients in primary-care and obstetrics clinics completed it; scores of 5, 10, 15 and 20 marked the thresholds of mild, moderate, moderately severe and severe depression; and a score of 10 or more, checked against interviews of 580 patients, had a sensitivity and a specificity of 88 percent each for major depression. The ninth item asks about thoughts of death or self-harm and, whatever the total, a positive answer must be followed by a direct risk assessment. Its first two items, low mood and loss of interest, form the even shorter PHQ-2 screen. Because it is short, free to use and tied to the diagnostic criteria, it is the measure that stepped-care services use session by session (Book 2, Section 5.11.5).

Remember: PHQ-9

9 items, each 0 to 3, past two weeks; total 0 to 27. 0 to 4 minimal, 5 to 9 mild, 10 to 14 moderate, 15 to 19 moderately severe, 20 to 27 severe; 10 or more is the usual screening cut-off.

Worked example. Gunjan, a 41-year-old schoolteacher, fills in the PHQ-9 at a primary health centre because she has been sleeping badly and feeling tense for three months. Her invented item ratings are 1, 2, 1, 2, 1, 1, 1, 0, 0. Adding: 1 + 2 = 3; 3 + 1 = 4; 4 + 2 = 6; 6 + 1 = 7; 7 + 1 = 8; 8 + 1 = 9; and the two zeros add nothing, so the total is 9. That lies in the mild band (5 to 9) and below the screening cut-off of 10, and her ninth item is 0. In plain words: she has some depressive symptoms, but not a probable major depressive episode. Section 11.8.8 shows what her anxiety scale says.

Several other depression scales are named in the papers, and each has one thing worth knowing. The Geriatric Depression Scale (GDS) of Jerome Yesavage and colleagues (in the Journal of Psychiatric Research, in a volume dated 1982 to 1983, so you will see it cited under both years, most often 1983) was built for older adults: the authors gave 100 candidate questions to normal and depressed elderly people and kept the 30 that correlated best with the total. Every item is answered yes or no, which suits frail or mildly impaired older people, and the final scale contains almost no bodily symptoms, an advantage because aches, poor sleep and low energy are common in old age from physical illness alone. A 15-item short form followed in 1986. For people with dementia, who may not be able to answer questions about their own mood reliably, the Cornell Scale for Depression in Dementia of George Alexopoulos and colleagues (1988) has a clinician rate the patient from interviews with a carer as well as the patient. The Hospital Anxiety and Depression Scale (HADS) of A. S. Zigmond and R. P. Snaith (1983) was designed for patients attending general hospital medical clinics: 14 self-report items, seven for anxiety and seven for depression, each 0 to 3, so each subscale runs 0 to 21, with 8 to 10 read as borderline and 11 or more as a probable case; like the GDS it leaves out bodily symptoms, so that physical illness does not inflate the scores. For children, Maria Kovacs adapted the BDI into the Children's Depression Inventory, for ages 7 to 17.

Two Beck scales concern suicide risk rather than depression as such, and Book 2, Section 2.7.2 places them in the assessment of risk. The Beck Hopelessness Scale (Aaron Beck, Arlene Weissman, David Lester and Larry Trexler, 1974) has 20 true-or-false statements about the future, scored 0 to 20; the follow-up studies in which hopelessness predicted eventual suicide better than depression did are told in Book 3, Section 5.9.3. The Scale for Suicide Ideation (Beck, Maria Kovacs and Arlene Weissman, 1979) is a clinician-rated interview of 19 items, each 0 to 2 (a total of 0 to 38), that measures how intense and specific a person's current suicidal thinking is; its self-report version, the Beck Scale for Suicide Ideation, followed in 1991.

Exam trap

HAM-D: Hamilton 1960, clinician-rated, 17 scored items (21 in the 1960 form), 0 to 52, for patients already diagnosed. MADRS: 1979, clinician-rated, 10 items 0 to 6, 0 to 60, built for sensitivity to change. Zung SDS: 1965, self-report, 20 items 1 to 4, raw 20 to 80 (never 0). PHQ-9: 2001, self-report, 9 DSM criteria, 0 to 27, cut-off 10. Two "GDS" scales exist: the Geriatric Depression Scale (Yesavage, 30 yes or no items) and the Global Deterioration Scale (Barry Reisberg, 1982, seven stages of dementia, Section 11.7). The Beck Hopelessness Scale is the Beck scale that is true or false, with 20 items, not 21.

Anxiety scales: Hamilton, Beck, GAD-7, Spielberger and the Fear Survey Schedule

Hamilton's first scale was for anxiety. "The assessment of anxiety states by rating", in the British Journal of Medical Psychology in 1959, introduced the Hamilton Anxiety Rating Scale (HAM-A, also HARS), and its logic is the same as the depression scale's a year later: a clinician, after an interview, rates the severity of anxiety in a patient already known to have an anxiety state; it is not a diagnostic or screening test. It has 14 items, each rated 0 (not present) to 4 (very severe), so the total runs from 0 to 14 × 4 = 56. The items fall into two halves. The psychic items cover the mental side of anxiety (anxious mood, tension, fears, poor sleep, poor concentration, low mood) and the patient's behaviour at the interview; the somatic items go through the body system by system (muscles, senses, heart, breathing, gut, urinary and sexual function, and other autonomic signs). That heavy bodily content is both its strength (it captures the physical misery of anxiety) and its weakness: it does not separate anxiety well from depression or from physical illness, and a patient with a thyroid or heart condition scores high for reasons that are not psychological. As with the depression scale, bands vary from source to source.

Nearly thirty years later Beck's group built the self-report partner. Aaron Beck, Norman Epstein and their colleagues described the Beck Anxiety Inventory (BAI) in the Journal of Consulting and Clinical Psychology in 1988. Their stated aim was an anxiety measure that overlapped as little as possible with depression, the known failing of the older anxiety scales. The patient rates how much each of 21 symptoms has bothered her during the past week, including today (the wording of the record form in the 1993 manual), from not at all (0) to severely (3); the total therefore runs from 0 to 63, the same range as the BDI-II. The 1993 manual's bands are 0 to 7 minimal, 8 to 15 mild, 16 to 25 moderate and 26 to 63 severe. Most of its items describe bodily arousal of the kind seen in a panic attack, involving the heart, breathing, balance and skin sensations, and a few describe frightening thoughts of catastrophe or loss of control. Its critics point out that this makes it a better measure of panic-like arousal than of the worry of generalised anxiety.

The GAD-7, the Generalized Anxiety Disorder seven-item scale, is the PHQ-9's anxiety partner, by Robert Spitzer, Kurt Kroenke, Janet Williams and Bernd Löwe (2006). It was tested on 2,740 adults in 15 primary-care clinics in the United States, 965 of whom were then interviewed by mental health professionals. Its seven items ask how often, over the past two weeks, the patient has been bothered by the core symptoms of generalised anxiety (nervousness, uncontrollable worry, restlessness, irritability and the like), each scored 0 to 3 on the same frequency options as the PHQ-9, so the total runs from 0 to 21. Scores of 5, 10 and 15 mark mild, moderate and severe anxiety; a cut-off of 10 gave a sensitivity of 89 percent and a specificity of 82 percent for generalised anxiety disorder, and later work showed that it also picks up panic disorder, social anxiety disorder and post-traumatic stress disorder reasonably well. Its first two items form the GAD-2.

Worked example. Gunjan, whose PHQ-9 total was 9, also completes the GAD-7. Her invented item ratings are 3, 2, 3, 2, 2, 2, 2. Adding: 3 + 2 + 3 = 8, and four 2s make 8 more, so the total is 8 + 8 = 16. Since 16 is 15 or more, she falls in the severe band, well above the cut-off of 10. Put the two scales together and the picture is clear: severe anxiety with mild depressive symptoms, a pattern that points the clinician towards a careful assessment for generalised anxiety disorder (Book 3, Chapter 6) rather than a depressive episode. Her clinician also rates her on the HAM-A at interview. If the clinician's rating is much lower than her self-report, it is worth asking why: some patients feel far more than they show.

The State-Trait Anxiety Inventory (STAI) of Charles Donald Spielberger (1927 to 2013), with Richard Gorsuch and Robert Lushene, rests on a distinction Spielberger made central to anxiety research. The first, state anxiety, is a temporary condition, the tension and apprehension a person feels right now, which rises before an examination or a surgical operation and falls afterwards. The second, trait anxiety, is a stable disposition, the person's general tendency to respond to many situations with anxiety. The STAI measures both with two separate 20-item halves, one asking how the person feels "right now" and the other how she "generally" feels, each item rated 1 to 4 and some reverse-keyed, so each half runs from 20 to 80. The first form (Form X) appeared in 1970 and the revised Form Y in 1983. It is a research workhorse in studies of stress, surgery and test anxiety more than a clinical severity scale. It should not be confused with the older Taylor Manifest Anxiety Scale (Janet Taylor, 1953), whose items were drawn from the MMPI.

The Fear Survey Schedule (FSS) of Joseph Wolpe (1915 to 1997) and Peter J. Lang (1964) is different in kind from all of these. It is not a severity scale for a disorder but a list of common feared objects and situations (animals, heights, injections, criticism, being alone and many more), each of which the patient rates on a five-point scale from "not at all" to "very much". Wolpe used it to find out what a client feared before building the anxiety hierarchy for systematic desensitisation; its best-known later edition, the FSS-III, lists 108 items. Within the hierarchy itself each situation is rated on Wolpe's 0 to 100 subjective units of distress scale (SUDS), which is not a standardised scale at all but the client's own moment-to-moment rating; Book 2, Section 4.2.3 teaches both in their therapeutic setting. Specific anxiety disorders also have scales of their own, which you need only recognise: the Liebowitz Social Anxiety Scale (Michael Liebowitz, 1987), the Panic Disorder Severity Scale (Katherine Shear and colleagues, 1997), the Penn State Worry Questionnaire (1990) for generalised worry, and, for post-traumatic stress disorder, the clinician-rated Clinician-Administered PTSD Scale (CAPS, 1995) and the self-report PTSD Checklist (now the PCL-5).

Exam trap

HAM-A: Hamilton 1959 (a year before the depression scale), clinician-rated, 14 items 0 to 4, total 0 to 56. BAI: Beck 1988, self-report, 21 items 0 to 3, total 0 to 63, mostly bodily symptoms, built to separate anxiety from depression. GAD-7: 2006, 0 to 21, cut-off 10. The STAI is Spielberger's (state versus trait), not Taylor's. The Fear Survey Schedule (Wolpe and Lang, 1964) lists feared stimuli; the 0 to 100 SUDS (Wolpe) rates hierarchy items. And the PCL-5 (PTSD Checklist) is not the PCL-R (Robert Hare's Psychopathy Checklist-Revised, Section 11.9).

The Yale-Brown Obsessive Compulsive Scale

Before 1989, the scales for obsessive-compulsive disorder mostly counted symptoms. The Leyton Obsessional Inventory (John Cooper, 1970) and the Maudsley Obsessional-Compulsive Inventory (Ray Hodgson and Stanley Rachman, 1977), for example, asked which of a long list of obsessional symptoms a person had. That works as a description, but as a measure of severity it fails in an obvious way. A man with one devastating obsession about harming his child, which fills his day and keeps him from going home, would score lower than a woman with a dozen mild habits of checking and tidying that cost her a few minutes a day. And OCD comes in so many forms (contamination and washing, doubt and checking, symmetry and ordering, forbidden aggressive, sexual or religious thoughts, hoarding) that a list of contents favours whichever forms the list happens to contain.

Wayne Goodman, with Lawrence Price, Steven Rasmussen, Carolyn Mazure and their colleagues at Yale University and Brown University, set out to remedy this. In two papers in the Archives of General Psychiatry in 1989 they described the Yale-Brown Obsessive Compulsive Scale (Y-BOCS), designed to measure the severity of OCD in a way that is not influenced by the type of obsessions or compulsions present. The trick is to separate two questions: what are your symptoms, and how bad are they? The Y-BOCS is a clinician-rated semi-structured interview in two parts.

The first part is the symptom checklist: the clinician goes through several dozen examples of obsessions and compulsions, grouped by theme (aggressive, contamination, sexual, hoarding, religious, symmetry, bodily and miscellaneous obsessions; cleaning, checking, repeating, counting, ordering, hoarding and miscellaneous compulsions), to identify the patient's own target symptoms, past and current. The checklist is not scored into the total. Its job is to make sure that the clinician and the patient know exactly which symptoms the severity ratings are about.

The second part is the 10-item severity scale. The same five questions are asked twice, first about the obsessions taken together and then about the compulsions taken together, for the past week. The five dimensions are:

  • Time occupied by the symptoms, anchored by how many hours of the day they take up.
  • Interference: how much the symptoms get in the way of work, study, social life and family life.
  • Distress: how much anxiety or upset the obsessions cause, or how anxious the patient becomes if prevented from carrying out the compulsions.
  • Resistance: how much effort the patient makes to resist the obsessions or compulsions.
  • Control: how much success the patient has in stopping or diverting them.

Each item is rated from 0 (no symptoms) to 4 (extreme), so the obsession subtotal (items 1 to 5) runs from 0 to 20, the compulsion subtotal (items 6 to 10) from 0 to 20, and the total from 0 to 40. Further items (insight, avoidance, indecisiveness and others) are rated for clinical description but are not added into the total. One item needs care. Resistance is scored for the effort made, not for its success, and effort counts as health: a patient who always tries to resist scores 0 on that item, and one who gives in completely without trying scores 4. Success is captured separately by the control item. So a high resistance score means little resistance, which is the opposite of what the word suggests.

Remember: Y-BOCS

Goodman and colleagues, 1989; clinician-rated semi-structured interview. Checklist (not scored), then 10 severity items: 5 for obsessions, 5 for compulsions, each rating time, interference, distress, resistance and control from 0 to 4. Subtotals 0 to 20; total 0 to 40.

Conventional bands: 0 to 7 subclinical, 8 to 15 mild, 16 to 23 moderate, 24 to 31 severe, 32 to 40 extreme.

Those bands are widely printed with the scale, but they do not come from the 1989 papers, and a large study published in 2022, drawing on data from more than 5,000 people, derived somewhat different benchmarks; treat the bands as a convention for describing a score. Drug trials have commonly required a total of 16 or more for entry. Change is judged by the definitions an international expert consensus led by David Mataix-Cols proposed in 2016: a response is a fall of at least 35 percent on the Y-BOCS together with a clinician's global rating of "much" or "very much" improved; a fall of 25 to 35 percent is a partial response; and remission is a score of 12 or less together with a clinician's global rating of no more than borderline illness, in each case lasting at least a week. (The clinician's global ratings, the Clinical Global Impression scales, are in Section 11.9.)

Worked example. Elina, a 29-year-old architect in Hyderabad, has feared contamination since her second year at college. She spends three to four hours a day washing, avoids public transport and has nearly stopped visiting friends. Her clinician's invented ratings at the first assessment are shown in Figure 11.18. Obsessions: time 3, interference 2, distress 3, resistance 1 (she still tries hard to push the thoughts away), control 3. Step 1, the obsession subtotal: 3 + 2 + 3 + 1 + 3 = 12. Compulsions: time 3, interference 3, distress 3 (she becomes very anxious if she is stopped from washing), resistance 3 (she rarely tries to resist the urge), control 3. Step 2, the compulsion subtotal: 3 + 3 + 3 + 3 + 3 = 15. Step 3, the total: 12 + 15 = 27. Step 4, the band: 27 lies between 24 and 31, the severe band. After twelve weeks of exposure and response prevention her total is 15. Step 5, the change: 27 − 15 = 12 points, and 12 ÷ 27 = 0.444, a reduction of about 44 percent (rounded to the nearest whole percent). Step 6, interpretation: 44 percent is more than 35 percent, so if her clinician also rates her much improved, she meets the consensus definition of a response; but 15 is above 12, so she is not in remission, and her score now sits at the top of the mild band. In plain words: Elina's OCD has gone from severe to mild, a real and worthwhile improvement, with work still to do.

Book 3, Section 6.9.7 gives a real example of the scale at work: in a long follow-up of Swedish patients with severe, treatment-resistant OCD who underwent capsulotomy, the mean Y-BOCS score fell from 34 to 18. You can now read those numbers: 34 is in the extreme band and 18 in the moderate band, and the fall of 16 points is 16 ÷ 34 = 0.47, a reduction of about 47 percent, which is large by any standard, although, as that section explains, it came at a price in adverse effects.

The scale has two important relatives. The Children's Yale-Brown Obsessive Compulsive Scale (CY-BOCS) of Lawrence Scahill and colleagues (1997), including Goodman, keeps the same checklist-then-ten-items design in simpler language and combines what the child and the parents report; it was validated on 65 children and adolescents aged 8 to 17. The Y-BOCS-II of Eric Storch, Goodman and colleagues (2010) rescored each item from 0 to 5 (so its total runs to 50), built avoidance into the ratings and replaced the resistance item. Self-report versions of the Y-BOCS also exist, but the classic scale, and the one examinations mean, is the 1989 clinician-rated interview. Its authors also showed in their second 1989 paper that its total fell when patients improved on the drug fluvoxamine in a placebo-controlled trial, evidence of the sensitivity to change that makes it the standard outcome measure in OCD trials.

Three of this chapter's questions ask simply what the Y-BOCS measures, and every option is worth a sentence because each wrong option has a scale of its own. Asked what the Y-BOCS assesses the severity of (MET mock), the options are schizophrenia symptoms, obsessive-compulsive disorder, depression and PTSD; the answer is OCD. Schizophrenia symptoms are rated with the PANSS or the BPRS (Section 11.9), depression with the BDI-II, Hamilton or Montgomery-Åsberg scales (Sections 11.8.6 and 11.8.7), and PTSD with the Clinician-Administered PTSD Scale or the PTSD Checklist (Section 11.8.8). Asked what the Y-BOCS rates (a testing paper), the options are severity of suicidal ideation, depression severity only, hallucination frequency, and the severity of OCD across 10 items, five for obsessions and five for compulsions, each 0 to 4, on time, interference, distress, resistance and control. The last is right, and is a complete description of the severity scale. Suicidal ideation is rated with Beck's Scale for Suicide Ideation (Section 11.8.7); hallucinations with the hallucination items of the positive-symptom scales (Section 11.9). And IHBAS 2025 asked "Y-BOCS is a scale for: depression, bipolar disorder, schizophrenia, OCD", and the answer is OCD; the mania of bipolar disorder is rated with the Young Mania Rating Scale (Section 11.9).

Exam trap

The Y-BOCS (Goodman, 1989, Yale and Brown universities) rates OCD severity, never depression, mania, schizophrenia, PTSD, hallucinations or suicidal ideation. It is clinician-rated; 10 scored items (5 + 5) on time, interference, distress, resistance and control, each 0 to 4, total 0 to 40; the symptom checklist is not scored. On resistance, more effort means a lower score. The children's version is the CY-BOCS (Scahill, 1997). A 35 percent fall is the consensus "response"; 12 or less is "remission".

The scales side by side

Table 11.36 brings together every scale in this section. Read across each row and you have the facts the papers ask for: who built it and when, how many items and how they are answered, who rates it, and what the scores mean. Read down the "Rater" column and you have the answer to every "which of these is clinician-rated?" question.
