Y-BOCSGoodman and colleagues 1989Checklist, then 10 items (5 + 5), 0 to 4; past weekClinician0 to 40; 0 to 7, 8 to 15, 16 to 23, 24 to 31, 32 to 40
CY-BOCSScahill and colleagues 1997Same design for children and adolescentsClinician (child and parent)0 to 40

Choosing among them follows from the purposes of Section 11.8.1. To screen many people in primary care, use a short self-report built for screening, such as the PHQ-9 and GAD-7. To grade and track severity in a patient whose diagnosis is already made, use a severity scale, self-report (the BDI-II for session-by-session monitoring in therapy) or clinician-rated (the Hamilton or Montgomery-Åsberg scale in a trial, where a blind rater can be used). Choose a scale built for the population: the Geriatric Depression Scale for older adults, the Hospital Anxiety and Depression Scale for the medically ill, the children's versions for children. For OCD, the Y-BOCS is the standard whatever the content of the symptoms. Section 11.9 extends this choice to psychosis, mania, substance use, children, disability and forensic questions, and closes the chapter with a table matching each assessment question to its first-choice tool.

Exam trap: numbers that get swapped

Ranges: 0 to 63 is the BDI-II and the BAI (21 items, 0 to 3); 0 to 60 is the MADRS; 0 to 52 the 17-item Hamilton depression scale; 0 to 56 the Hamilton anxiety scale; 0 to 40 the Y-BOCS; 0 to 27 the PHQ-9; 0 to 21 the GAD-7 and each HADS subscale; 20 to 80 the Zung scale and each STAI half. Formats: the BHS is 20 true or false items, the GDS 30 yes or no items. Years in order: HAM-A 1959, HAM-D 1960, BDI 1961, FSS 1964, Zung 1965, STAI 1970, BHS 1974, MADRS and SSI 1979, GDS and HADS 1983, BAI 1988, Y-BOCS 1989, BDI-II 1996, PHQ-9 2001, GAD-7 2006.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. A scale that a trained interviewer completes from an interview and observation of the patient is called a scale.
  2. The written descriptions attached to each score point of an item, which help two raters agree, are called .
  3. When a patient's marked improvement in anxiety leads the rater to record an improvement in her depression too, the error is the effect, named by Edward Thorndike in 1920.
  4. The BDI-II has 21 items, each scored 0 to 3, for the past weeks, so its total runs from 0 to .
  5. A BDI-II total of 22 falls in the band.
  6. The Montgomery-Åsberg scale (1979) was built to be sensitive to by keeping the 10 items that moved most with antidepressant treatment.
  7. The Hamilton Anxiety Rating Scale has 14 items, each scored 0 to 4, so its maximum total is .
  8. On the Zung Self-Rating Depression Scale, a raw score of 50 gives an SDS index of .
  9. The ten Y-BOCS severity items rate time, interference, distress, resistance and , once for obsessions and once for compulsions.
  10. A patient's Y-BOCS total falls from 30 to 18. The reduction is percent, which the consensus criterion for response.

Answers: (1) clinician-rated (or observer-rated); (2) anchors (anchor points); (3) halo; (4) two, 63 (21 × 3 = 63); (5) moderate (20 to 28); (6) change; (7) 56 (14 × 4); (8) 62.5 (50 ÷ 80 = 0.625, and 0.625 × 100 = 62.5); (9) control; (10) 40, meets (30 − 18 = 12, and 12 ÷ 30 = 0.40, which is more than the 35 percent required, provided the clinician also rates her much improved).

11.9 Clinical Rating Scales II: Psychosis, Mania, Substance Use, Children, Disability and Forensic UseLower yield · 2 Qs

Section 11.8 dealt with depression, anxiety and obsessions, conditions in which the patient is usually the best witness to his own suffering. Nobody knows better than you how hopeless you feel at three in the morning, and a self-report inventory simply asks you. This section deals with the situations in which that witness is unreliable, unavailable or interested in the outcome. A man with schizophrenia may not know that his beliefs are unusual, and he cannot see his own flattened face. A woman in a manic episode feels better than she has ever felt and sees nothing to report. A heavy drinker minimises. A seven-year-old cannot fill in a questionnaire about her own attention. A disability board needs a number that will stand up in a government office, and a court wants to know whether a prisoner is dangerous, while the prisoner has every reason to look harmless. Each of these problems has produced its own family of scales. Read them with one question held steadily in mind (who supplies the information, and what does the design do about that person's blind spots?) and a long list of acronyms turns into a handful of ideas.

The section takes the scales in that order: psychosis (Sections 11.9.1 to 11.9.3), mania (11.9.4), the broad screens for distress (11.9.5), substance use (11.9.6), global functioning and disability (11.9.7), children (11.9.8), and two special settings, the weeks after childbirth (11.9.9) and the prison (11.9.10). It ends by showing you how to choose a scale for a referral question (11.9.11), with two tables that pull the whole chapter together (11.9.12). The principles every rating scale shares, such as anchors, time frames, cut-offs, rater errors and the difference between self-report and clinician rating, were taught in Section 11.8 and are not repeated here; the reliability ideas behind them are in Chapter 3, especially inter-rater agreement in Section 3.6 and the standard error of measurement in Section 3.7.

Rating psychosis: from the BPRS to the PANSS

How do you put a number on a delusion? For most of the history of psychiatry nobody needed to. Case notes said that a patient was "improved" or "unchanged", and the doctor who wrote the note was the only judge. Then chlorpromazine arrived in the early 1950s (Book 3, Section 4.10), followed by a stream of new drugs that had to be compared with placebo and with each other. A drug trial cannot run on "improved". It needs a number that two raters will agree on, that moves when the patient changes, and that can be averaged across a hundred patients in a dozen hospitals. The psychosis rating scales are the answer to that need, and their history is a history of making the number more trustworthy.

The first answer that lasted was the Brief Psychiatric Rating Scale (BPRS) of John E. Overall and Donald R. Gorham (the Gorham of the Proverbs Test in Section 11.6.10), published in Psychological Reports in 1962. As the name says, its virtue was brevity. Overall and Gorham picked out, from factor analyses of longer inpatient rating scales, sixteen symptom areas that between them described most of what psychiatrists saw on the wards, and they asked the rater to judge each one after a short interview. Two more items, excitement and disorientation, were added in the 1960s, and the 18-item form is the one an exam means by "the BPRS". Notice how the items get their information. Some are rated from what the patient says during the interview (worry about his body, anxiety, guilt, low mood), and others from what the rater sees (emotional withdrawal, tension, odd mannerisms and postures, blunted affect, slowed movement). That split between reported and observed items runs through every psychosis scale, because a patient with psychosis can tell you about his fears but cannot tell you that his face no longer moves. Each item is rated on a seven-point scale, from 1 (not present) to 7 (extremely severe), so the 18-item total runs from 18 to 126. Some centres score the same seven levels from 0 to 6, which gives a range of 0 to 108; always check which convention a paper or a question is using.

The BPRS had one serious weakness, and it teaches a lesson about all rating scales. The original form gave each level only a word ("mild", "moderate", "severe") and no description of what that level looks like. Each rater therefore carried a private picture of "moderately severe suspiciousness", and two psychiatrists who had not trained together could rate the same patient two or three points apart. The remedy is the anchor, a written description of the behaviour that earns each score (Section 11.8 introduced anchored items). In 1986 David Lukoff, Keith H. Nuechterlein and Joseph Ventura published an expanded 24-item version, the BPRS-E, with a manual that anchors every level of every item, and inter-rater agreement rose accordingly. Chapter 3, Section 3.6 explains how such agreement is measured, with the intraclass correlation for ratings like these.

By the early 1980s the question in schizophrenia research had changed. Timothy Crow had proposed in 1980 that positive and negative symptoms reflected two different disease processes, and Nancy C. Andreasen had begun to measure them separately (Book 3, Section 4.3.6 tells that story; it is not repeated here). The results of positive-negative research were inconsistent, and part of the reason was measurement: different studies used different scales, and those scales gave positive and negative symptoms unequal weight. In 1987 Stanley R. Kay, Abraham Fiszbein and Lewis A. Opler published, in the Schizophrenia Bulletin, a scale designed to fix exactly that problem: the Positive and Negative Syndrome Scale (PANSS). They built it from two established rating systems, the 18 items of the BPRS and 12 items of the Psychopathology Rating Schedule, and they arranged the 30 items so that positive and negative symptoms had equal representation and could be compared with each other and with overall severity. They described it as an operationalised, drug-sensitive instrument.

The PANSS has three scales. The positive scale has 7 items covering delusional and suspicious thinking, hallucinations, disorganised thinking, grandiosity, excitement and hostility. The negative scale has 7 items covering blunted affect, emotional and social withdrawal, poor rapport with the interviewer, lack of spontaneity and flow in conversation, stereotyped thinking and difficulty in abstract thinking. The general psychopathology scale has 16 items covering everything else a person with schizophrenia may show: anxiety, guilt, depression, bodily concern, tension, odd movements and postures, disorientation, poor attention, lack of judgement and insight, poor impulse control and so on. That makes 7 + 7 + 16 = 30 items. A fourth score, the composite scale, is simply the positive total minus the negative total: a positive number means the positive syndrome predominates, a negative number the negative syndrome. It was meant for the "typological" use the authors had in mind, sorting patients by which syndrome dominates.

Every item is rated from 1 to 7, and this is the feature that matters most. Each of the seven levels has a written anchor: 1 is absent, 2 minimal, 3 mild, 4 moderate, 5 moderate severe, 6 severe and 7 extreme, and for each item the manual says what the patient must show to earn each level. The rating usually covers the past week. The information comes from three sources: a semi-structured interview of about 30 to 40 minutes (the Structured Clinical Interview for the PANSS, or SCI-PANSS), the rater's observation of the patient during that interview, and reports from family members or ward staff about behaviour the interviewer cannot see, such as how the patient spends the day or whether he talks to anyone on the ward. A clinician who rates the PANSS from a five-minute ward round, without the interview or the informants, is not using the PANSS.

Remember

PANSS ranges. Each item scores 1 (absent) to 7 (extreme), so the lowest possible score on any scale is the number of its items.

Positive scale: 7 items, range 7 to 49. Negative scale: 7 items, range 7 to 49. General psychopathology scale: 16 items, range 16 to 112. Total: 30 items, range 30 to 210.

Composite scale = Positive − Negative, range −42 (7 − 49) to +42 (49 − 7).

Kay, Fiszbein and Opler standardised the scale on 101 patients with schizophrenia and reported that the four scales were normally distributed and reliable, and later studies confirmed good inter-rater agreement among trained raters. The PANSS became the standard outcome measure in antipsychotic drug trials, which is why its scores fill the research literature. When researchers factor-analysed large sets of PANSS ratings, however, the items did not fall neatly into the three published scales. They fell better into five factors (positive, negative, disorganised, excited and depressed), a finding that Book 3, Section 4.3.6 describes. For exam purposes the published structure is still 7, 7 and 16.

Exam trap

BPRS: Overall and Gorham, 1962; 18 items in the standard form (16 in the original, 24 in Lukoff, Nuechterlein and Ventura's expanded BPRS-E); each 1 to 7, total 18 to 126 (or 0 to 108 when scored 0 to 6). PANSS: Kay, Fiszbein and Opler, 1987; built from the 18 BPRS items plus 12 from the Psychopathology Rating Schedule, so the PANSS contains the BPRS. Its 30 items are 7 positive, 7 negative and 16 general, each 1 to 7, so the total runs from 30 to 210 and can never be 0; the composite is positive minus negative. Two placements surprise people: "difficulty in abstract thinking" is a negative item, and "lack of judgement and insight" a general one.

Reading a PANSS score: a worked example, response and remission

Tanuj, 27, is admitted to a psychiatric ward in Lucknow during his second episode of schizophrenia. He believes that the neighbours are filming him through the ceiling fan, hears two voices commenting on what he does, speaks in loosely connected sentences and has threatened his brother. He has also become withdrawn over the past year, and his face moves little. A trained psychologist rates him on the PANSS after a SCI-PANSS interview and a talk with his mother. The invented ratings are as follows.

  • Positive items: 6, 5, 6, 4, 3, 6, 4. Sum: 6 + 5 = 11; 11 + 6 = 17; 17 + 4 = 21; 21 + 3 = 24; 24 + 6 = 30; 30 + 4 = 34. Positive total = 34 (out of a possible 7 to 49).
  • Negative items: 3, 3, 2, 3, 2, 3, 2. Sum: 3 + 3 + 2 = 8; 8 + 3 = 11; 11 + 2 = 13; 13 + 3 = 16; 16 + 2 = 18. Negative total = 18 (out of 7 to 49).
  • General psychopathology: the 16 ratings add up to 48 (an average of 48 ÷ 16 = 3, "mild", per item).

His total is 34 + 18 + 48 = 100. His composite score is 34 − 18 = +16, so the positive syndrome clearly predominates. What does a total of 100 mean? A number on a 30-to-210 scale tells a clinician very little until it is tied to something clinically familiar, and that tie was made by one of the most useful studies in this field.

Key study: What does the PANSS mean? (Leucht and colleagues, 2005)

Drug trials had long reported that a medicine produced, say, a 20 percent or a 50 percent fall in PANSS scores, but nobody could say what such numbers meant to a patient. Stefan Leucht of the Technical University of Munich, with John Kane and other colleagues, pooled the baseline and follow-up ratings of 4,091 patients with acute exacerbations of schizophrenia from seven trials of olanzapine or amisulpride. In those trials each patient had been rated on the PANSS and, at the same visit, on the Clinical Global Impression (CGI, Section 11.9.7), the clinician's one-line judgement of how ill the patient is and how much he has improved. The team used equipercentile linking: they lined up the two distributions and found the PANSS score that sat at the same percentile as each CGI category.

A PANSS total of about 58 corresponded to "mildly ill", about 75 to "moderately ill", about 95 to "markedly ill" and about 116 to "severely ill". A CGI rating of "minimally improved" went with a mean PANSS reduction of about 19 to 28 percent (depending on the week of treatment), and "much improved" with about 40 to 53 percent. The conclusion: the popular 20 percent response criterion captures only minimal improvement, while a 50 percent reduction marks clinically substantial change. The limitation is the sample: these were acutely ill patients in drug trials, and the links may differ for chronic outpatients.

By those anchors Tanuj, at 100, sits between "markedly" and "severely" ill. Six weeks after starting a new antipsychotic he is rated again: positive 15, negative 15 and general 34, a total of 15 + 15 + 34 = 64, close to "mildly ill". How much has he improved? The obvious calculation is the fall (100 − 64 = 36 points) divided by the starting score (100), which gives 36 percent. That calculation is wrong, and the reason is worth understanding, because it applies to every scale whose items start at 1.

A percentage change only makes sense when zero means "none of the thing being measured". On the PANSS, a patient with no symptoms at all scores 1 on every item, which is 30, not 0. The first 30 points of the scale can never be lost, so dividing by the raw score treats them as illness that could have improved. In 2010 Obermeier and colleagues, writing in the Schizophrenia Bulletin under the title "Should the PANSS be rescaled?", showed that this habit distorts response rates and can even change the significance of treatment effects, and that the correct method is to subtract 30 from every score before calculating a percentage. (Their suggested alternative was to score each item 0 to 6, which builds the subtraction in.)

Remember

Percentage improvement on the PANSS = (baseline total − follow-up total) ÷ (baseline total − 30) × 100

Here 30 is the score of a patient with no symptoms (30 items × the minimum rating of 1). On a scale that starts at 0, such as the Young Mania Rating Scale, nothing is subtracted.

For Tanuj: the fall is 100 − 64 = 36 points. The part of his baseline score that was real illness is 100 − 30 = 70 points. His improvement is 36 ÷ 70 = 0.514, or about 51 percent (rounded to the nearest whole number). Now look at what the two methods do to a decision. Many trials define response as a 50 percent reduction. By the naive method Tanuj improved by 36 percent and is a non-responder; by the correct method he improved by 51 percent and is a responder. Same patient, same ratings, opposite conclusions. Figure 11.19 shows the scale's structure and why the floor matters.

One more yardstick deserves a sentence. A large reduction is not the same thing as being well. In 2005 a working group led by Nancy Andreasen proposed standard criteria for symptomatic remission in schizophrenia that do not depend on change at all: eight core PANSS items (covering delusions, unusual thought content, hallucinations, disorganised thinking, mannerisms and posturing, blunted affect, social withdrawal and lack of spontaneity) must all be rated mild or better, that is 3 or less, and must stay there for at least six months. A response criterion asks "how far has he come?"; a remission criterion asks "where is he now, and has he stayed there?". Tanuj at week 6 may have responded, but he cannot yet be in remission, because six months have not passed.

Exam trap

Percentage change on the PANSS must subtract the floor: (baseline − follow-up) ÷ (baseline − 30). The same rule applies to the 18-item BPRS scored 1 to 7, whose floor is 18, not 30. Using the raw baseline understates improvement. By Leucht and colleagues' 2005 linking, a PANSS total of about 58 means mildly ill, 75 moderately, 95 markedly and 116 severely ill; a 20 percent reduction corresponds only to minimal improvement and a 50 percent reduction to much improvement. Response is a change criterion; remission (Andreasen and colleagues, 2005) is a state criterion: eight core items all at 3 (mild) or less, held for at least six months.

Andreasen's SANS and SAPS, and the scales that followed

While the PANSS packed both syndromes into one interview, Nancy Andreasen (born 1938), at the University of Iowa, had already built a separate, more detailed instrument for each. Her Scale for the Assessment of Negative Symptoms (SANS) was first reported in 1982, with a manual in 1983, and its companion, the Scale for the Assessment of Positive Symptoms (SAPS), followed in 1984. Book 3, Section 4.3.6 explains why she wanted them; here the interest is in how they measure. The SANS has 25 items grouped into five symptom complexes: affective flattening or blunting, alogia, avolition-apathy, anhedonia-asociality, and attention (20 symptom items plus one global rating for each complex). The SAPS has 34 items in four: hallucinations, delusions, bizarre behaviour and positive formal thought disorder (30 symptom items plus four global ratings). Both rate each item from 0 (none) to 5 (severe), so, unlike the PANSS, a zero really does mean absent. Their great strength is detail: the SAPS, for example, rates about a dozen kinds of delusion separately, where the PANSS has one delusions item, and the SANS breaks blunted affect into separate observations of facial expression, gesture, eye contact and vocal inflection.

Each symptom complex in both scales ends with a global rating, the rater's overall judgement of that complex, which is not simply the sum or average of the item ratings above it. This creates a choice that confuses readers of the research: some studies report the sum of all the item ratings, and others the sum of the five (or four) global ratings. The two are not interchangeable, so a SANS score of 15 means one thing in a paper that sums globals (a maximum of 25) and something quite different in one that sums items (a maximum of 100 for the 20 symptom items, or 125 if the globals are counted too).

The SANS also shows how a measure can carry a theory that later research rejects. Andreasen included attention among the negative symptoms. Factor analyses kept finding that inattention goes with disorganisation and cognitive impairment rather than with blunted affect and loss of drive, and a consensus conference on negative symptoms held by the United States National Institute of Mental Health and its MATRICS initiative (its statement was published in 2006) concluded that the negative symptoms proper are five: blunted affect, alogia, asociality, anhedonia and avolition. Two newer scales were built to that blueprint and dropped attention: the 13-item Brief Negative Symptom Scale of Brian Kirkpatrick and colleagues (2011) and the 13-item Clinical Assessment Interview for Negative Symptoms of Ann Kring and colleagues (2013), whose items fall into an expression scale and a motivation and pleasure scale. Both also separate what a patient shows in expression from what he experiences inside, such as the pleasure he anticipates or the drive he feels, because a person may look flat and yet feel a great deal.

Finally, two neighbouring needs have their own instruments, and they matter because exam options borrow them. Depression is common in schizophrenia but hard to rate, because negative symptoms and the slowing caused by antipsychotics look like depression. The Calgary Depression Scale for Schizophrenia of Donald Addington, Jean Addington and B. Schissel (1990) was built for exactly this problem: its authors selected, from the Hamilton Rating Scale for Depression and the Present State Examination, the items that best measured depression in 50 acutely ill patients with schizophrenia, leaving out items that overlap with negative symptoms and drug side effects. The first report had 11 items; the final scale has nine, each rated 0 to 3, so its total runs from 0 to 27. Cognitive impairment, the third great domain of schizophrenia, is measured not by any symptom rating but by neuropsychological tests, and for drug trials a standard set of them was agreed in 2008 as the MATRICS Consensus Cognitive Battery (MATRICS stands for Measurement and Treatment Research to Improve Cognition in Schizophrenia), which samples seven cognitive domains with ten tests of the kinds taught in Sections 11.4 and 11.6. Table 11.37 sets the psychosis scales side by side.

Table 11.37 The psychosis rating scales compared.

Exam trap

"The PANSS is used to assess..." The answer is symptom severity in schizophrenia, on three scales (positive, negative, general psychopathology) with 30 items, each 1 to 7. It does not assess personality disorders (those need a personality disorder interview such as the SCID-5-PD or IPDE, Book 2, Section 2.1.4). It is not a depression severity scale, even though one of its 16 general items is depression (use the BDI or HAM-D of Section 11.8, or the Calgary scale in schizophrenia). It does not measure cognitive impairment, even though it has items on attention and abstract thinking (use tests, such as the MoCA of Section 11.7 or the MATRICS battery).

SANS: 25 items in five complexes (affective flattening, alogia, avolition-apathy, anhedonia-asociality, attention); SAPS: 34 items in four (hallucinations, delusions, bizarre behaviour, positive formal thought disorder); both rated 0 to 5, both Nancy Andreasen (1982 to 1984), not Kay and not Crow (Crow proposed Types I and II, not a scale). "Attention" as a negative symptom domain is a favourite odd one out, and the newer negative symptom scales (BNSS, CAINS) dropped it.

Rating mania: the Young Mania Rating Scale

Mania poses the self-report problem in its purest form. A woman in a manic episode may not have slept for three nights, may have spent her family's savings and may be shouting down the ward corridor, and if you hand her a questionnaire she will tell you, quite sincerely, that she has never felt better. Loss of insight is part of the illness (Book 3, Chapter 5 describes it), so a scale that relies on her own account will report the least illness precisely when there is the most. Mania therefore had to be rated by an observer, and the scale that became the standard was built in deliberate imitation of the Hamilton depression scale of Section 11.8.

In 1978 Robert C. Young, with J. T. Biggs, V. E. Ziegler and D. A. Meyer, published "A rating scale for mania: reliability, validity and sensitivity" in the British Journal of Psychiatry. The Young Mania Rating Scale (YMRS) has 11 items, rated by a clinician after an interview, covering the core features of mania: elevated mood, increased activity and energy, sexual interest, sleep, irritability, the rate and amount of speech, disordered language and thought, the content of thought (grandiose or persecutory ideas), disruptive or aggressive behaviour, appearance, and insight. The rating draws on the patient's own account of the previous 48 hours together with what the rater observes during the interview. The short time frame is deliberate: mania changes from day to day, and a week-long window, like the PANSS's, would blur the change a drug trial needs to see.

The YMRS has one unusual design feature that you must know. Seven of the eleven items are rated from 0 to 4, but the other four (irritability, speech, thought content, and disruptive or aggressive behaviour) are rated from 0 to 8. These four carry double weight, and the authors explained why: they compensate for the poor cooperation of severely ill patients. A patient who is too irritable or distractible to answer questions about sleep or sexual interest can still be rated, from observation alone, on how irritable, pressured, grandiose and disruptive she is, and the double weighting makes sure that these observable items dominate the total in exactly those patients. The arithmetic follows directly.

Remember

YMRS maximum = (7 items × 4) + (4 items × 8) = 28 + 32 = 60. The minimum is 0, so the range is 0 to 60.

Young and his colleagues reported that two independent clinicians agreed closely, with a correlation of 0.93 for the total score and 0.66 to 0.92 for individual items. The YMRS correlated highly with an independent global rating and with two other mania scales, its score predicted the number of days the patient would go on to spend in hospital, and it distinguished patients before and after two weeks of treatment, which is the property a drug trial needs most. There are no official severity bands. By convention, many drug trials admit patients with a score of 20 or more and count a score of 12 or less as remission, while some use a stricter remission threshold of 8 or less.

Here is the scale at work. Laxmi, 34, a schoolteacher with bipolar I disorder, is admitted in a manic episode. The invented ratings on the seven ordinary items are: elevated mood 3, activity and energy 3, sexual interest 2, sleep 3, language and thought disorder 2, appearance 1 and insight 3, which add to 3 + 3 + 2 + 3 + 2 + 1 + 3 = 17. On the four double-weighted items she is rated irritability 6, speech 6, thought content 4 and disruptive behaviour 2, which add to 6 + 6 + 4 + 2 = 18. Her total is 17 + 18 = 35, well above the usual threshold for entering a trial. Ten days after starting lithium and an antipsychotic she is rated again: the seven ordinary items now add to 1 + 1 + 0 + 1 + 1 + 0 + 2 = 6, and the four double-weighted items to 2 + 2 + 2 + 0 = 6, for a total of 12. She has fallen 35 − 12 = 23 points. Because the YMRS starts at 0, the ordinary percentage is correct here, unlike the PANSS: 23 ÷ 35 = 0.657, a reduction of about 66 percent (rounded). She meets the standard remission threshold of 12 or less, but not the stricter one of 8 or less, so her psychiatrist would continue to watch closely before calling the episode over.

Two other tools complete the picture, and they do different jobs from the YMRS. Self-report mania scales exist, such as Edward Altman's five-item Altman Self-Rating Mania Scale (1997), and they are useful for patients with milder hypomania and good insight, but they share the blind spot described above. The bigger clinical problem runs the other way: many people with bipolar disorder first seek help when they are depressed, and if nobody asks about past highs, they may be treated with an antidepressant alone. Screening questionnaires for a history of bipolarity were built for that problem. The Mood Disorder Questionnaire of Robert Hirschfeld and colleagues (2000) asks 13 yes-or-no questions about past manic symptoms, followed by two questions on whether several of them happened during the same period and how much trouble they caused; in its validation study with 198 patients from mood-disorder clinics, a score of 7 or more gave a sensitivity of 0.73 and a specificity of 0.90 against a structured diagnostic interview (Chapter 4 explains sensitivity and specificity). The Hypomania Checklist (HCL-32) of Jules Angst and colleagues does the same job with 32 items. Neither rates current severity; both ask "has this person ever been high?".

Clinical link

In mania the family is often the best informant. A patient may deny that she has slept little or spent heavily, while her husband can describe the last two nights in detail. The YMRS is anchored in the patient's account and the interview, but raters commonly check that account against what relatives and nurses have seen, and on an Indian ward the relatives who stay with the patient are an unusually rich source. Record who told you what: if the YMRS is repeated later with a different informant, part of any change may be a change of witness, not of illness.

Exam trap

YMRS: Robert Young and colleagues, 1978, 11 items, clinician-rated, previous 48 hours, four items (irritability, speech, thought content, disruptive or aggressive behaviour) double-weighted 0 to 8, total 0 to 60. Do not confuse it with the MADRS of Section 11.8, which also runs from 0 to 60 but has 10 items rated 0 to 6 and measures depression. Do not confuse Robert Young with Jeffrey Young, whose Young Schema Questionnaire belongs to schema therapy. And the Mood Disorder Questionnaire (13 items, 7 or more positive) screens for a history of bipolar disorder; it does not rate how manic someone is today. Watch the names, too: the Altman Self-Rating Mania Scale is a "rating scale" that the patient completes himself, and the Psychopathy Checklist of Section 11.9.10 is a "checklist" scored by a trained clinician, two exceptions to the naming rule of thumb in Section 11.8.2.

Screening for distress: the General Health Questionnaire and the SCL-90-R

Every scale so far assumes that you already know what is wrong: you would not rate a PANSS on a man without psychosis. But most people with a mental disorder are never seen by a psychiatrist. They sit in the waiting rooms of general practitioners and primary health centres, complaining of headaches, poor sleep or tiredness, and their doctors miss a large share of them. In the late 1960s the British psychiatrist David P. Goldberg, working at the Institute of Psychiatry in London, set out to build a questionnaire that a patient could complete in a few minutes in such a waiting room and that would pick out the people most likely to have what he called non-psychotic psychiatric disorder: the common anxiety and depressive conditions. The result was the General Health Questionnaire (GHQ), published in 1972 in his monograph The Detection of Psychiatric Illness by Questionnaire.

Goldberg's central design decision explains both the GHQ's strength and its weakness. Each item asks whether the person has recently experienced a particular symptom or behaviour, and the four answers are graded relative to the person's usual state, from something like "better than usual" or "not at all", through "same as usual" or "no more than usual", to "less than usual" or "rather more than usual", and finally "much less than usual" or "much more than usual" (the wording depends on whether the item describes something healthy or a symptom). The GHQ is therefore a detector of change: it is designed to catch a recent break in a person's normal functioning, not to describe lifelong traits. That is exactly what a screen in primary care needs, because a recent onset is what brings the patient in. Its weakness is the mirror image. A woman who has been anxious and miserable for ten years may truthfully answer "no more than usual" to every question, and the questionnaire will call her well.

The GHQ comes in several lengths. The original had 60 items; shorter forms followed with 30, 28, 20 and 12 items. The 28-item form, the GHQ-28 of Goldberg and Valerie Hillier (1979), was derived by factor analysis of questionnaires completed in primary care and is the only common version with subscales: four of them, seven items each, called somatic symptoms, anxiety and insomnia, social dysfunction and severe depression. The 12-item GHQ-12 gives a single score and became the most widely used screen of its kind in the world. How much is lost by shortening it? In a World Health Organization study of psychological disorders in general health care, Goldberg and his colleagues (1997) compared the GHQ-12 with the GHQ-28 in 5,438 patients interviewed in 15 centres in many countries. Both versions worked well, with an average area under the receiver operating characteristic curve of 0.88 (ranging from 0.83 to 0.95 across centres); the GHQ had been translated into ten further languages and worked almost as well in them; it was no less efficient in developing countries; and age, sex and education made no significant difference to its validity. (The area under the curve is explained in Chapter 4: 0.5 means guessing and 1.0 perfect discrimination.)

The GHQ also illustrates something Chapter 1 says in general: the same answers can be scored in more than one way, and the scoring must suit the purpose. There are three common methods. In GHQ scoring, the two "healthy" answers score 0 and the two "unhealthy" answers score 1 (often written 0-0-1-1), so the GHQ-12 runs from 0 to 12; this is the method for deciding whether someone is a probable case, because it ignores the difference between a person who habitually ticks extreme answers and one who habitually ticks moderate ones. In Likert scoring, the four answers score 0, 1, 2 and 3, so the GHQ-12 runs from 0 to 36; this spreads people out more and suits research on severity. And in the C-GHQ (chronic) scoring proposed by M. E. Goodchild and P. Duncan-Jones in 1985, the answer "no more than usual" to an item describing a symptom is scored as 1 rather than 0, on the argument that a person who reports the symptom as "usual" may be chronically ill rather than healthy. They found that the revised scoring predicted caseness better and was more stable on repeated measurement.

Work through two invented respondents to see what the methods do. The GHQ-12 has six items worded in a positive direction (about coping or enjoying things) and six worded in a negative direction (about strain or unhappiness). Suppose a clinic in Mysuru has chosen, from a local validation study, a threshold of 2/3 on GHQ scoring, so that 3 or more marks a probable case. The first respondent gives four answers in the healthiest column, three in the second, three in the third and two in the most unhealthy column. GHQ scoring counts only the third and fourth columns: 3 + 2 = 5, a probable case. Likert scoring gives (4 × 0) + (3 × 1) + (3 × 2) + (2 × 3) = 0 + 3 + 6 + 6 = 15 out of 36. The second respondent ticks the second column, "same as usual" or "no more than usual", on all twelve items. GHQ scoring gives 0: not a case. Likert scoring gives 12 × 1 = 12. C-GHQ scoring gives 1 for each of the six negatively worded items answered "no more than usual" and 0 for the six positive ones, a total of 6, double the clinic's threshold of 3 (a service that adopts chronic scoring should validate its own, higher threshold, because every respondent scores more under it). If this second person has in fact been depressed for years, only the chronic scoring has noticed.

Indian context

The GHQ was among the first Western screens to be standardised in India. S. Gautam, M. Nijhawan and P. Kamal published a Hindi version in the Indian Journal of Psychiatry in 1987, translated into everyday language and checked by back-translation; its split-half reliability was tested on 500 psychiatric outpatients and 500 people from the general population, and it separated them sharply (mean scores of 30.64 in the patients against 4.9 in the comparison group). The GHQ-12 has since been used in many Indian community and primary-care surveys. Because thresholds depend on the setting and the population, an Indian study should report which scoring method and which threshold it used, and preferably justify the threshold against a clinical interview. A second screen built for such settings is the World Health Organization's Self-Reporting Questionnaire (SRQ-20), twenty yes-or-no questions about non-psychotic symptoms, which grew out of the questionnaire used in the WHO's study of mental disorders in primary health care in four developing countries (Colombia, India, the Philippines and Sudan). In that study (T. W. Harding and colleagues, 1980, with the Indian psychiatrists R. Srinivasa Murthy and N. N. Wig among the authors), 13.9 percent of 1,624 primary-care attenders had a psychiatric disorder, most presented with a bodily complaint such as headache or weakness, and the health workers recognised only about a third of them: the very gap that screening questionnaires exist to close.

The GHQ answers "is this person probably a case?" with a single number. Sometimes a clinician wants a profile instead: which kinds of distress, and how much of each? The most widely used self-report profile of current symptoms is the Symptom Checklist-90-Revised (SCL-90-R) of Leonard R. Derogatis. It grew out of the older Hopkins Symptom Checklist; Derogatis, R. S. Lipman and L. Covi described the 90-item SCL-90 in 1973 as "an outpatient psychiatric rating scale", and the revised form, the SCL-90-R, followed in the 1970s, with later editions of its manual. The respondent rates how much each of 90 problems has distressed or bothered him during the past seven days, including today, on a five-point scale from 0 (not at all) to 4 (extremely).

The 90 items are scored on nine primary symptom dimensions: somatisation, obsessive-compulsive, interpersonal sensitivity, depression, anxiety, hostility, phobic anxiety, paranoid ideation and psychoticism. Seven further items (about appetite, sleep, thoughts of death, guilt and the like) belong to no dimension but are counted in the totals. On top of the profile sit three global indices, and they are best learned through their formulas.

Remember

Global Severity Index: GSI = (sum of all 90 item ratings) ÷ 90

Positive Symptom Total: PST = the number of items rated above 0

Positive Symptom Distress Index: PSDI = (sum of all 90 item ratings) ÷ PST

The GSI is the average distress per item across the whole checklist; the PST counts how many different symptoms the person reports; the PSDI is the average intensity of the symptoms he does report.

Suppose an invented outpatient's 90 ratings add up to 117, and 45 of the items are rated above 0. Then GSI = 117 ÷ 90 = 1.30; PST = 45; PSDI = 117 ÷ 45 = 2.60. In words: he reports half the symptoms on the list, and the ones he reports bother him, on average, between "moderately" (2) and "quite a bit" (3). Now imagine a second person with exactly the same sum of 117 spread thinly over 90 endorsed items: her GSI is also 1.30, but her PST is 90 and her PSDI is 117 ÷ 90 = 1.30. Same overall severity, very different pattern: he has fewer, intense complaints; she ticks every item at a low level. Derogatis suggested that the PSDI also says something about response style, whether a person tends to amplify or play down distress, which is why it is worth looking at alongside the GSI.

Raw scores are converted to T-scores (mean 50, standard deviation 10; Chapter 5 explains them) against the norm group that fits the person: adult non-patients, psychiatric outpatients, psychiatric inpatients or adolescents. The manual's rule for "caseness" is a GSI T-score of 63 or more, or T-scores of 63 or more on any two of the primary dimensions. A T of 63 is 1.3 standard deviations above the mean, roughly the top tenth of the comparison group. One caution matters more than any other. Many factor-analytic studies have failed to recover the nine dimensions cleanly; the dimensions correlate highly with each other, and much of the variance reflects a single general distress factor. The GSI is therefore the most trustworthy SCL-90-R score, and a profile with "elevated paranoid ideation" should not be read as evidence of paranoia without an interview. A 53-item short form with the same nine dimensions, the Brief Symptom Inventory (Derogatis and N. Melisaratos, 1983), is often used when time is short.

Exam trap

The GHQ (David Goldberg, 1972) screens for non-psychotic psychiatric disorder, the common anxiety and depressive conditions, in community and primary-care settings; it does not diagnose, and it is not a psychosis screen. It asks about recent change from the usual state, so it can miss chronic illness (the C-GHQ scoring of Goodchild and Duncan-Jones, 1985, was devised for that). Versions: 60, 30, 28, 20, 12 items; only the GHQ-28 (Goldberg and Hillier, 1979) has the four subscales (somatic symptoms, anxiety and insomnia, social dysfunction, severe depression). "GHQ scoring" means 0-0-1-1, Likert scoring 0-1-2-3.

The SCL-90-R (Leonard Derogatis) has 90 items rated 0 to 4 for the past seven days, nine primary symptom dimensions and three global indices (GSI, PST, PSDI); its short form, the BSI, has 53 items. Do not confuse its nine dimensions with the MMPI's ten clinical scales (Chapter 9), and do not call it a projective or a personality test: it measures current symptomatic distress.

Substance use: screening, dependence and withdrawal

Mayank, 42, a clerk in a cooperative bank, sees his general practitioner about burning pain in his stomach. Over the next month he will meet three different alcohol instruments, and each will answer a different question. At the first visit the doctor needs to know whether his drinking is a problem at all. If it is, the de-addiction team needs to know how dependent he is, because that decides whether he can stop at home or needs admission. And if he is admitted, the nurses need to know, every few hours, how severe his withdrawal is right now. These are three different constructs, and each has its own time frame: lifetime or past year for a screen, a recent period of heavy drinking for dependence, and the present moment for withdrawal. The commonest error with substance scales is to use one built for one of these jobs to answer another. The clinical side of these tools is taught elsewhere: the AUDIT, its risk zones and brief interventions in Book 2, Section 9.4.5; the CAGE questions and the blood markers of heavy drinking in Book 3, Section 10.3.8; and the use of the CIWA-Ar in detoxification in Book 3, Sections 10.3.5 and 10.4.3. What follows is the measurement side.

Screening. The Alcohol Use Disorders Identification Test (AUDIT) of the World Health Organization (1989; validation study by John B. Saunders, Thomas F. Babor and colleagues, 1993) has ten items in three groups: consumption (items 1 to 3), dependence (items 4 to 6) and harm (items 7 to 10). Its scoring has a detail that students miss. Items 1 to 8 have five answer options scored 0 to 4, but items 9 and 10, about injury and about other people's concern, have only three options, scored 0, 2 and 4 (no; yes, but not in the past year; yes, during the past year). The maximum is still 10 × 4 = 40. The first three items alone form the AUDIT-C (consumption), scored 0 to 12, which K. Bush and colleagues (1998) showed to be at least as good as the full AUDIT at detecting heavy drinking in male primary-care patients (though slightly weaker at detecting abuse or dependence); in men a score of 4 or more is the usual positive screen, and lower thresholds are often used for women. The full AUDIT's usual threshold of 8 also varies by setting, and some guidelines lower it for women and older adults, who reach harmful levels at lower intakes.

Mayank's invented answers run: consumption items 4, 2 and 3; dependence items 2, 1 and 1; harm items 2, 1, 0 and 4. His consumption subtotal (which is also his AUDIT-C) is 4 + 2 + 3 = 9; his dependence subtotal 2 + 1 + 1 = 4; his harm subtotal 2 + 1 + 0 + 4 = 7. His total is 9 + 4 + 7 = 20, which falls in the highest WHO zone, possible dependence calling for specialist assessment (Book 2, Table 9.5). Notice that the subtotals carry information the total hides: a man with 20 points mostly from consumption is a heavy drinker who may not yet be dependent, while a man with the same total weighted towards the dependence items probably needs supervised withdrawal.

The four-item CAGE questionnaire is older and shorter, and its history is a small lesson in how names attach to tests. John A. Ewing devised it around 1968; it was first validated in print by D. Mayfield, G. McLeod and P. Hall in the American Journal of Psychiatry in 1974; and Ewing's own 1984 paper in the Journal of the American Medical Association made it famous. Two or more positive answers count as a positive screen. Its measurement limits follow from its wording: every question asks whether something has ever happened, so a man who stopped drinking ten years ago still screens positive and the score cannot fall with recovery; and its questions pick up dependence far better than they pick up the risky but not yet dependent drinking that the AUDIT was designed to catch. The Michigan Alcoholism Screening Test (MAST) of Melvin L. Selzer (1971) is a longer lifetime screen of 25 yes-or-no questions about the social, legal, occupational and medical consequences of drinking, with weighted items (some answers count more than others); in Selzer's original scoring a total of 5 or more indicated alcoholism. Shorter forms include the 13-item Short MAST. For drugs other than alcohol, Harvey Skinner's Drug Abuse Screening Test (DAST, 1982) asks 28 self-report questions about the consequences of drug use (internal consistency 0.92 in its first clinical sample of 256 clients), and a 10-item short form is common. The WHO's ASSIST (Alcohol, Smoking and Substance Involvement Screening Test, 2002) asks the same short set of questions about each of tobacco, alcohol and the main classes of other drugs, so that one interview screens for all of them; India was one of the countries in which it was first tested.

Severity of dependence. A screen says "probably a problem"; it does not say how dependent someone is. The Severity of Alcohol Dependence Questionnaire (SADQ) of Tim Stockwell, Ray Hodgson, Griffith Edwards, Colin Taylor and Howard Rankin (1979), from the Addiction Research Unit in London, was built on Griffith Edwards and Milton Gross's 1976 description of the alcohol dependence syndrome (Book 3, Chapter 10). The drinker thinks of a recent, typical period of heavy drinking and answers 20 questions in five groups of four: physical withdrawal symptoms, emotional withdrawal symptoms, drinking to relieve withdrawal, typical daily consumption, and how quickly withdrawal returns when drinking restarts after a period of abstinence. Each item is scored 0 to 3, so the total runs from 0 to 60, and the conventional bands are below 16 mild, 16 to 30 moderate and 31 or more severe dependence. The bands have a practical use: a person with severe dependence is at higher risk of complicated withdrawal, and many services use a high SADQ score as one reason to advise admission rather than withdrawal at home.

Withdrawal right now. Mayank's SADQ is 34, and he is admitted. Withdrawal is a state that changes by the hour, so it needs a measure that can be repeated quickly and that responds to every change. The Clinical Institute Withdrawal Assessment for Alcohol, revised (CIWA-Ar) of John T. Sullivan, Karen Sykora, Joseph Schneiderman, Claudio Naranjo and Edward Sellers (1989), from the Addiction Research Foundation in Toronto, shortened an earlier 15-item version to ten items that a nurse can rate in a few minutes. Nine items (nausea and vomiting, tremor, sweating, anxiety, agitation, and tactile, auditory and visual disturbances, and headache) are rated 0 to 7, and the tenth, orientation and clouding of consciousness, is rated 0 to 4.

Remember

CIWA-Ar maximum = (9 items × 7) + (1 item × 4) = 63 + 4 = 67.

At eight in the morning, a day after his last drink, the nurse rates Mayank: nausea 2, tremor 3, sweating 2, anxiety 3, agitation 2, tactile disturbances 1, auditory 0, visual 0, headache 2 and orientation 0. His score is 2 + 3 + 2 + 3 + 2 + 1 + 0 + 0 + 2 + 0 = 15. After a dose of a benzodiazepine the noon rating is 1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 0, 0, 0, 1 and 0, a total of 6. In typical hospital protocols a score below about 8 to 10 calls for observation only, a score in the teens for medication, and a score above about 15 to 20 marks severe withdrawal with a raised risk of seizures and delirium, though each unit sets its own thresholds. In a symptom-triggered regimen (Book 3, Section 10.4.3) the 8 a.m. score of 15 earns a dose and the noon score of 6 does not.

Three measurement points complete the picture. First, the CIWA-Ar depends on the patient being able to describe his nausea, itching or anxiety, so it fails exactly where withdrawal is most dangerous: in a patient who is already delirious, sedated or unable to communicate. Second, several items (anxiety, headache, sweating) are not specific to alcohol withdrawal; a patient anxious about losing his job, or one with a fever, will score points that are not withdrawal at all, which is why the scale is used only after the diagnosis of withdrawal has been made. Third, other substances have their own withdrawal scales built the same way, the best known being the 11-item Clinical Opiate Withdrawal Scale (COWS) of Donald Wesson and Walter Ling (2003), used, for example, to decide when an opioid-dependent patient is far enough into withdrawal to start buprenorphine safely.

Nicotine. In 1978 the Swedish researcher Karl-Olov Fagerström published an eight-item Fagerström Tolerance Questionnaire. When Todd Heatherton, Lynn Kozlowski, Richard Frecker and Fagerström examined it in 254 smokers (1991), two items, about the nicotine rating of the brand and about inhaling, were unrelated to biochemical measures of how heavily people smoked and were the main source of its psychometric weaknesses. They dropped them, rescored the items on time to the first cigarette of the day and number of cigarettes per day, and produced the six-item Fagerström Test for Nicotine Dependence (FTND), scored 0 to 10. The two strongest items alone, time to first cigarette and cigarettes per day, form the Heaviness of Smoking Index. This is a neat example of item analysis (Chapter 2) improving a test by removing items that measured nothing.

Indian context

Scales built for cigarettes fit India poorly. In the second Global Adult Tobacco Survey of India (2016 to 2017), 28.6 percent of adults used tobacco, but only 10.7 percent smoked, while 21.4 percent used smokeless tobacco such as khaini, gutkha or betel quid with tobacco. Questions about "cigarettes per day" mean little to such users, which is why adaptations of the Fagerström test for smokeless tobacco have been developed and why the WHO's ASSIST, which asks about tobacco in any form, is often preferred in Indian primary care.

Table 11.38 gathers the substance instruments and sorts them by the question each one answers, which is the order in which you should think of them.

Exam trap

Match the instrument to its job. Screens: AUDIT (WHO, 10 items, 0 to 40, cut-off 8, past year, catches hazardous drinking early), CAGE (4 items, 2 or more, lifetime, good for dependence, poor for risky drinking), MAST (Selzer 1971, 25 weighted items, lifetime), DAST (Skinner 1982, drugs). Severity of dependence: SADQ (Stockwell 1979, 20 items, 0 to 60). Withdrawal: CIWA-Ar (Sullivan 1989, 10 items, maximum 67, not 70, because orientation stops at 4; nurse-rated; repeated). Nicotine: FTND (1991, 6 items, 0 to 10), revised from Fagerström's 8-item questionnaire of 1978. A withdrawal scale cannot diagnose dependence, and a screen cannot tell you how much medication to give.

Global functioning and disability: the CGI, the GAF, WHODAS 2.0 and IDEAS

Every scale so far counts symptoms. But a patient and his family rarely care about the symptom count for its own sake. They care about two broader things: how ill is he, all things considered, and what can he still do? The first question is answered by global ratings, the second by measures of functioning and disability, and the two are easy to confuse because both often come out as a single number.

The simplest rating in psychiatry is the Clinical Global Impression (CGI), published by William Guy in 1976 in the ECDEU Assessment Manual for Psychopharmacology, the collection of standard scales prepared for the drug-evaluation programme (the Early Clinical Drug Evaluation Unit) of the United States National Institute of Mental Health. It has two main parts. The CGI-Severity (CGI-S) asks the clinician to rate how ill the patient is at this moment, judged against his total clinical experience with this particular kind of patient. The CGI-Improvement (CGI-I) asks how much the patient has changed since the start of treatment. Both are single items rated on seven points (Table 11.39); a score of 0 means "not assessed". A third part, the efficacy index, crosses four levels of therapeutic effect with four levels of side effects to judge whether a treatment's benefit outweighs its cost.

Table 11.39 The two Clinical Global Impression ratings (Guy, 1976).
ScoreCGI-Severity: how ill now?CGI-Improvement: how changed since baseline?
1Normal, not at all illVery much improved
2Borderline mentally illMuch improved
3Mildly illMinimally improved
4Moderately illNo change
5Markedly illMinimally worse
6Severely illMuch worse
7Among the most extremely ill patientsVery much worse

Look at how the two columns differ, because exam questions turn on it. On the severity scale a higher number is worse throughout. On the improvement scale 4 is the middle ("no change"), numbers below 4 are improvement and numbers above 4 are deterioration. The usual definition of a treatment responder in a drug trial is a CGI-I of 1 or 2. Why use such a crude instrument alongside a 30-item PANSS? Because the CGI is the clinician's bottom line in plain language, and that is exactly what made it useful to Leucht and colleagues in the key study of Section 11.9.2: a PANSS total means something to a clinician once it is linked to "markedly ill". For Tanuj, a baseline PANSS of 100 sits close to the PANSS value of about 95 that corresponds to "markedly ill", and his psychiatrist rates him CGI-S 5; at week 6, with a corrected PANSS reduction of 51 percent, she rates him CGI-I 2, much improved. The weakness of the CGI is the weakness of any single global judgement: it depends on the rater's frame of reference ("compared with the patients I usually see"), so a psychiatrist in a busy government hospital and one in a private clinic for mild anxiety may rate the same patient differently. It works best when the same rater makes all the ratings.

The Global Assessment of Functioning (GAF) scale, the 0 to 100 rating that formed Axis V of DSM-III-R and DSM-IV, is taught in Book 3, Section 1.7.5, with its bands and the rule that the rater takes the worse of symptoms and functioning; Book 3, Section 1.7.6 records that DSM-5 dropped it. The measurement reason is worth stating. DSM-5 (2013) gave as its reasons the scale's "conceptual lack of clarity", because its descriptions mixed symptoms, suicide risk and disability, and its "questionable psychometrics in routine practice". A single number that mixes two constructs cannot be interpreted: a GAF of 45 may mean a man who functions well but has serious suicidal thoughts, or a man with mild symptoms who cannot hold a job, and those two need different help. One answer, already in DSM-IV's appendix, was the Social and Occupational Functioning Assessment Scale (SOFAS), which rates functioning alone. For children the equivalent of the old global scale is the Children's Global Assessment Scale (CGAS) of David Shaffer and colleagues (1983), an adaptation of the adult Global Assessment Scale rated from 1 to 100, which they found reliable between raters and over time.

DSM-5's replacement was a measure built on an entirely different model. The World Health Organization Disability Assessment Schedule 2.0 (WHODAS 2.0), described by T. Bedirhan Üstün and colleagues in 2010, is based on the WHO's International Classification of Functioning, Disability and Health, so it measures disability in the same way whatever its cause, a stroke, arthritis or schizophrenia. It covers six domains of life: understanding and communicating (cognition), getting around (mobility), self-care, getting along with people, life activities (household and work or school), and participation in society. The full version has 36 items and the short version 12; each comes in self-administered, interviewer-administered and proxy forms (the proxy form is completed by a relative when the person cannot answer, for example in dementia). Every item asks how much difficulty the person has had in the past 30 days and is answered on five levels: none (1), mild (2), moderate (3), severe (4) and extreme or cannot do (5). In the development studies, which included more than 65,000 respondents in many countries, it showed high internal consistency (Cronbach's alpha 0.86), very high test-retest reliability (an intraclass correlation of 0.98), a stable factor structure and good sensitivity to change.

WHODAS 2.0 can be scored in three ways, and they illustrate a choice Chapter 2 discusses in general. In simple scoring the 36 item scores are added, giving 36 to 180; this needs no computer and suits a busy clinic. In item-response-theory scoring, a computer weights each item and each level of difficulty differently and converts the result to a metric from 0 (no disability) to 100 (full disability), which can be compared with population norms. And DSM-5-TR recommends a third, very practical method: average scores, in which each domain's raw total is divided by its number of items, so that every result reads directly on the 1-to-5 scale of none, mild, moderate, severe and extreme.

Remember

WHODAS 2.0 average domain score = raw domain score ÷ number of items in the domain

Average general disability score = raw total ÷ 36. Both read on the item scale: 1 none, 2 mild, 3 moderate, 4 severe, 5 extreme. DSM-5-TR warns that if 10 or more of the 36 items are unanswered, the overall scores may not be helpful.

Six months after her manic episode Laxmi, who has returned to teaching, completes the 36-item self-administered form. Her invented raw domain totals are: understanding and communicating 12 (6 items), getting around 5 (5 items), self-care 4 (4 items), getting along with people 15 (5 items), life activities 24 (8 items) and participation 20 (8 items). Her simple total is 12 + 5 + 4 + 15 + 24 + 20 = 80, out of a possible 36 to 180. Her average general disability score is 80 ÷ 36 = 2.22, which rounds to 2, mild. The domain averages tell a sharper story: understanding 12 ÷ 6 = 2.0 (mild), getting around 5 ÷ 5 = 1.0 (none), self-care 4 ÷ 4 = 1.0 (none), getting along 15 ÷ 5 = 3.0 (moderate), life activities 24 ÷ 8 = 3.0 (moderate) and participation 20 ÷ 8 = 2.5 (between mild and moderate). The overall "mild" hides two moderate problems, in relationships and at work, which are precisely where her rehabilitation should aim.

In India, however, the instrument that decides legal entitlement for disability caused by mental illness is not WHODAS but the Indian Disability Evaluation and Assessment Scale (IDEAS), developed by the Rehabilitation Committee of the Indian Psychiatric Society and adopted for certification by the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment in 2002. Book 2, Section 9.9.8 teaches its use in full: a psychiatrist, interviewing the person and the family, rates four areas (self-care, interpersonal activities, communication and understanding, and work) from 0 to 4 each, adds a score of 1 to 4 for the duration of illness, and converts the global total of 0 to 20 into a grade and a percentage (Book 2, Table 9.17). Here the interest is in IDEAS as a measurement, and three features stand out.

First, the global score is not purely a measure of disability. The duration score (1 for less than two years of illness up to 4 for more than ten) adds as much as 4 points that have nothing to do with how the person functions today, so two people with identical current functioning can receive different grades because one has been ill longer. That is a policy decision (it favours certifying chronic rather than temporary disability), not a psychometric one, and you should be able to say so in an interview. The duration rule just described is that of the version gazetted in 2002, which Book 2 teaches; the revised guidelines of March 2024 instead score the months of illness in the preceding two years, which ties the score to recent illness rather than to the whole history, although, as Indian commentators have noted, it still measures illness rather than functioning. Second, the scale's most important boundary sits between a global score of 6 (mild, below 40 percent) and 7 (moderate, 40 percent or more), because 40 percent is the "benchmark disability" that unlocks most legal benefits. At a legal cut-off, a one-point disagreement between two raters on any single area changes a person's entitlements, so the reliability of ratings near the cut-off matters far more than the reliability of the scale on average; Chapter 3, Section 3.7 shows how the standard error of measurement turns a single score into a band, and how that band can straddle a cut-off. Third, its validity has been studied against other measures: in a study at PGIMER, Chandigarh, of 178 patients with schizophrenia in remission (S. Sahoo, S. Grover and colleagues, 2017), all four IDEAS area scores and the total correlated significantly with the GAF and with a social and occupational functioning scale, and 84.8 percent of these patients had disability above 40 percent. Comparisons of IDEAS with WHODAS 2.0 have also been published, a natural question now that DSM-5 recommends the WHO's instrument.

Exam trap

CGI (Guy, 1976): CGI-S rates current severity from 1 (normal) to 7 (among the most extremely ill); CGI-I rates change from baseline from 1 (very much improved) through 4 (no change) to 7 (very much worse); a responder is usually CGI-I 1 or 2. GAF: 0 to 100, Axis V of DSM-III-R and DSM-IV, dropped by DSM-5 and replaced by WHODAS 2.0 (WHO; 36 or 12 items; six domains; past 30 days; items 1 to 5). In India, disability from mental illness is certified with IDEAS (Indian Psychiatric Society, 2002), not WHODAS.

A trap from the IHBAS 2024 paper: asked for "the Indian tool to assess the severity and disability of children with autism", the answer is ISAA, the Indian Scale for Assessment of Autism (Book 2, Section 9.9.8). The other options are not Indian disability tools for autism: the CGAS rates a child's overall functioning from 1 to 100, the CBCL rates general behaviour problems (Section 11.9.8), and the Childhood Autism Rating Scale (CARS) is the American scale of Eric Schopler and colleagues (1980). The paper keyed CARS; Book 2, Section 9.9.8 explains why ISAA is the correct fact and how to handle the IHBAS key.

Rating children: the CBCL, the Conners scales, the SDQ and the CPMS

Riddhi, 9, is referred by her school in Chandigarh because she "does not concentrate" and has fallen behind in mathematics. Who should you ask about her? A nine-year-old can tell you some things about how she feels, but she cannot rate her own attention against that of other girls her age. Her mother sees her at home, in the mornings and evenings; her class teacher sees her for six hours a day among thirty other children. Book 2, Section 10.1.4 showed how poorly such informants agree with each other, and why: children behave differently in different settings, so each informant sees a different slice of the child. Child rating scales are built around that fact. They come in parallel forms for parents, teachers and older children, and each form is normed separately, so that a mother's rating is compared with other mothers' ratings and a teacher's with other teachers'.

Two further distinctions organise the field. A broad-band scale surveys the whole range of emotional and behavioural problems, while a narrow-band scale concentrates on one area, such as ADHD. And a scale can be empirically based, with its scales derived by factor analysis of how problems actually cluster in children (a "bottom-up" approach), or built "top-down" from the diagnostic criteria of DSM or ICD.

The best-known broad-band, empirically based system is that of Thomas M. Achenbach at the University of Vermont. In factor analyses of children's psychiatric problems in the 1960s, Achenbach found that most problems fall into two broad groupings, which he called internalising (problems directed inward, such as anxiety, sadness, withdrawal and bodily complaints) and externalising (problems directed outward, such as aggression and rule-breaking). The distinction is now used throughout child psychology. His Child Behavior Checklist (CBCL), completed by a parent, was published with a manual co-written with Craig Edelbrock in 1983 and became the core of the Achenbach System of Empirically Based Assessment (ASEBA). The school-age forms in use today (Achenbach and Leslie Rescorla, 2001) are the CBCL/6-18 for parents, the Teacher's Report Form (TRF) and the Youth Self-Report (YSR) for ages 11 to 18; preschool forms cover ages 1½ to 5. On the school-age parent form, more than a hundred problem items (118 specific problems and two open-ended items, printed as 113 numbered questions because one question has eight parts) are each rated 0 (not true), 1 (somewhat or sometimes true) or 2 (very true or often true) for the past six months, and separate competence items cover activities, social life and school.

The problem items are scored on eight syndrome scales: anxious/depressed, withdrawn/depressed, somatic complaints, social problems, thought problems, attention problems, rule-breaking behaviour and aggressive behaviour. The first three add up to the Internalising score and the last two to the Externalising score; social, thought and attention problems belong to neither but count in the Total Problems score. A set of DSM-oriented scales, regrouping the same items by diagnostic criteria, was added later, so the CBCL now offers both bottom-up and top-down views of one set of answers. Raw scores are converted to T-scores using norms for the child's sex, age range and informant. On the syndrome scales, T-scores of 65 to 69 (roughly the 93rd to 97th percentiles) are in the borderline clinical range and 70 or above in the clinical range; the broad Internalising, Externalising and Total Problems scores use lower cut-offs, with 60 to 63 (roughly the 84th to 90th percentiles) counting as borderline and 64 and above as clinical. Why percentile cut-offs rather than "two standard deviations"? Because problem scores are strongly skewed: most children have few problems, so a symmetric bell curve does not describe them, and the manual's T-scores are anchored to percentiles of the actual distribution (Chapter 5 explains normalised standard scores).

Riddhi's mother completes the CBCL and her class teacher the TRF. Their invented T-scores are plotted in Figure 11.20. Her mother's ratings reach the borderline range for anxious/depressed (68) and somatic complaints (66): she describes stomach aches on school mornings and worry about tests. Her teacher's ratings are unremarkable except for attention problems, at 74, in the clinical range: Riddhi stares out of the window, leaves work unfinished and loses track of instructions. Her mother rates attention problems at 63, raised but below the borderline range. Neither informant is wrong. Attention problems show most where sustained attention is demanded, in the classroom; anxiety and stomach aches show most where the child is about to face school, at home in the morning. The two profiles together generate the clinical questions: is this ADHD, predominantly inattentive, with secondary anxiety; is it anxiety that disrupts concentration; or is a specific learning disorder in mathematics producing both? A rating scale cannot settle that, but it tells you where to look next: a narrow-band ADHD scale from both informants, cognitive and attention tests (Sections 11.4 and 11.6), an educational assessment and an interview with Riddhi herself.

Where the CBCL is broad, the scales of C. Keith Conners (1933 to 2017) are the classic narrow-band instrument for ADHD and its neighbours. Conners published a teacher rating scale in 1969, "for use in drug studies with children", because the stimulant trials of the time needed a way to measure change in classroom behaviour, and a parent scale followed. The scales were revised several times (the Conners' Rating Scales-Revised in 1997, the Conners 3 in 2008 and the Conners 4 in 2022) and now come in parent, teacher and self-report forms, the last for older children and adolescents. They yield T-scores, against age and sex norms, for inattention, hyperactivity and impulsivity, and related areas such as learning problems, executive functioning, defiance and aggression, and peer relations, together with scales that follow the DSM criteria for ADHD, oppositional defiant disorder and conduct disorder. A T-score of 70, two standard deviations above the mean of the norm group, marks a very elevated rating. Do not confuse these rating scales with the Conners Continuous Performance Test, a computerised attention task by the same author (Section 11.6). Other ADHD rating scales, such as the Vanderbilt scales of Mark Wolraich, are listed in Book 3, Section 3.1.5.

A shorter broad-band screen, now used worldwide, is the Strengths and Difficulties Questionnaire (SDQ) of the British child psychiatrist Robert Goodman (1997). Its 25 items fall into five scales of five items each: emotional symptoms, conduct problems, hyperactivity-inattention, peer relationship problems and prosocial behaviour. Each item is scored 0, 1 or 2, so each scale runs from 0 to 10, and the first four scales add up to a total difficulties score of 0 to 40; the prosocial scale is kept separate, because it measures a strength. Goodman designed a single form that parents and teachers both complete, which he hoped would raise their agreement, and in his first study of 403 children from dental and psychiatric clinics the SDQ discriminated between the two groups as well as the longer Rutter questionnaires it was meant to replace. A self-report version exists for adolescents, and because the questionnaire is free to use, translations exist in many languages, including Indian ones.

India has its own broad-band instrument, and it is a standard exam answer. The Childhood Psychopathology Measurement Schedule (CPMS) was developed at the Postgraduate Institute of Medical Education and Research (PGIMER), Chandigarh, by Savita Malhotra, V. K. Varma, S. K. Verma and Anil Malhotra, from Savita Malhotra's doctoral work of 1984, and published in the Indian Journal of Psychiatry in 1988. It is an Indian adaptation of the Child Behavior Checklist, standardised on Indian children of both sexes aged 4 to 14, and used in Hindi and English. It has 75 items, each marked yes or no by a parent or other informant, and it yields a total score for overall psychopathology and scores on eight factorially derived syndromes: low intelligence with behaviour problems, conduct disorder, anxiety, depression, psychotic symptoms, special symptoms, physical illness with emotional problems, and somatisation. A total above about 10 is commonly taken to mark significant psychopathology. Its authors proposed it as a screening instrument for population surveys and as a research tool, and Indian studies of children with chronic physical illness and surveys of schoolchildren have used it in exactly those ways. The same team built a companion temperament schedule. Instruments for autism (CARS, ISAA, the M-CHAT) and for adaptive behaviour (the Vineland scales) are taught in Chapter 7, with the Indian certification rules in Book 2, Section 9.9.8. Table 11.40 summarises the child scales of this section.
