Exam trap

CBCL = Thomas Achenbach (Vermont), parent-completed, items rated 0, 1 or 2, internalising versus externalising, with the TRF for teachers and the YSR for youths of 11 to 18. Conners scales = ADHD (teacher scale 1969). SDQ = Robert Goodman (1997), 25 items, five scales including a prosocial strength scale. CPMS = Savita Malhotra and colleagues at PGIMER, Chandigarh (1988), 75 items, 8 factors, ages 4 to 14, adapted from the CBCL: it is not a NIMHANS tool, and it is not an autism scale. The CGAS rates global functioning, not autism; ISAA is India's autism certification tool.

After childbirth: the Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale

Why would the weeks after childbirth need their own depression scale, when Section 11.8 already offers the BDI and the PHQ-9? Think about what a general depression scale asks. Several of its items concern the body: tiredness, poor sleep, changes in appetite and weight, loss of sexual interest. Almost every mother of a six-week-old baby is exhausted, sleeps in fragments and eats at odd hours, depressed or not. A general scale therefore gives many healthy new mothers points for symptoms that are simply part of caring for a baby, and the result is a flood of false positives. The obvious remedy is a scale that leaves those items out, and that is what a team in Edinburgh built.

Key study: Building a postnatal screen (Cox, Holden and Sagovsky, 1987)

The psychiatrist John L. Cox, with Jeni M. Holden and Ruth Sagovsky, set out to give health visitors and community health workers a short questionnaire that mothers would accept and that would pick out those with postnatal depression. After extensive pilot interviews with mothers, they wrote ten items about mood, enjoyment, self-blame, anxiety, panic, feeling overwhelmed, unhappiness, tearfulness and thoughts of self-harm, and deliberately avoided items about bodily symptoms that are normal after delivery. They then validated the scale on 84 mothers, comparing their scores with diagnoses made by the Research Diagnostic Criteria from Goldberg's Standardised Psychiatric Interview. The scale showed satisfactory sensitivity and specificity, could be completed in about five minutes, was simple to score, and was sensitive to change in the severity of depression over time. They published the Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale (EPDS) in the British Journal of Psychiatry in 1987.

Each EPDS item has four answers scored 0 to 3, with some items reverse-scored so that the mother cannot simply tick the same column throughout, and she answers for the past seven days. The total therefore runs from 0 to 30. Cox and his colleagues proposed two thresholds, and the reason for having two is a lesson in screening. A threshold of 12/13 (13 or more) marks probable depression and keeps false positives low; a lower threshold of 9/10 (10 or more) catches more of the depressed mothers at the price of more false alarms, which suits a first-stage screen in primary care where a positive result only means "see the doctor". Chapter 4 explains this trade between sensitivity and specificity. A large meta-analysis of individual participants' data pooled from many countries (Brooke Levis and colleagues, 2020) later found that a threshold of 11 or more balanced sensitivity and specificity best, while 13 or more was less sensitive but more specific. The scale is now also used during pregnancy, and it has been validated in fathers and in many languages, including Indian ones; each validation tends to find its own best threshold, which is why a clinic should use one established for its own population.

One item is treated differently from all the others. The last question asks about thoughts of harming oneself, and any score above 0 on it calls for a same-day assessment of risk, whatever the total. A total score is an average judgement; a single answer about self-harm is a fact that must be acted on.

Nupura, 26, is seen at a primary health centre near Pune six weeks after the birth of her first child. She completes a translated EPDS. Her invented item scores are 2, 1, 2, 2, 1, 2, 1, 2, 1 and 0. Adding them: 2 + 1 = 3; 3 + 2 = 5; 5 + 2 = 7; 7 + 1 = 8; 8 + 2 = 10; 10 + 1 = 11; 11 + 2 = 13; 13 + 1 = 14; 14 + 0 = 14. Her total of 14 is above both thresholds, so she is a probable case and is referred for a clinical interview, which is what will decide whether she has a depressive episode. Now change one detail. Suppose instead that her total had been 8, below both thresholds, but that she had scored 1 on the self-harm item. The total says "probably not depressed"; the single item says "assess today". The item wins.

Exam trap

EPDS: Cox, Holden and Sagovsky, 1987; 10 items, each 0 to 3, total 0 to 30; past seven days; self-report; a screen, not a diagnosis. Its defining design feature is that it avoids the bodily symptoms that are normal after childbirth. Thresholds: 12/13 for probable depression, 9/10 for a more sensitive first screen. A positive answer on the self-harm item needs immediate assessment regardless of the total. Do not confuse the EPDS with the Geriatric Depression Scale (Section 11.8), which solves a similar problem for older adults by using simple yes-or-no items and few bodily symptoms.

In the forensic setting: the PCL-R as a measurement

Forensic assessment is the setting in which the person being assessed has the strongest reasons to shape the result. A man facing a parole board does not report his lack of remorse. That single fact explains most of the design of the instrument that dominates forensic psychology, the Psychopathy Checklist-Revised (PCL-R) of Robert D. Hare (born 1934). Book 3, Section 11.5.6 tells its history, from Hervey Cleckley's (1903 to 1984) portrait of the psychopath in The Mask of Sanity (1941) to Hare's 22-item Psychopathy Checklist of 1980, the 20-item PCL-R of 1991 and its second edition of 2003, and it sets out the two factors and four facets with their content. This subsection takes the PCL-R as a piece of measurement.

How it is scored. A trained rater scores each of the 20 items 0 (does not apply), 1 (applies to some extent) or 2 (definitely applies), so totals run from 0 to 40. The information comes from two sources: a semi-structured interview, and a review of the person's files (police records, court papers, prison and hospital notes) and, where possible, interviews with people who know him. The file review is not optional. Because many of the traits being rated (pathological lying, conning, lack of remorse, shallow affect) are exactly the traits that distort a self-account, the PCL-R should not be scored from an interview alone; when the interview and the files conflict, the documented record weighs heavily. Research ratings are sometimes made from files alone when the files are rich, but never from the interview alone.

Missing information and prorating. Files are often incomplete, so the manual allows a rater to omit an item for which there is not enough information, up to a maximum of five items, and to prorate the total, that is, to scale it up to what it would be on 20 items.

Remember

Prorated PCL-R total = (sum of the scored items ÷ number of items scored) × 20

No more than five items may be omitted; with more missing information the total cannot be trusted.

Take an invented prisoner whose files say nothing about two items, so 18 items are scored, and suppose those 18 items add up to 25. His prorated total is (25 ÷ 18) × 20 = 1.389 × 20 = 27.8, which rounds to 28. Is he "a psychopath" by the usual North American cut-off of 30? Here the reliability ideas of Chapter 3 earn their keep. Every score carries error, and Chapter 3, Section 3.7 shows how the standard error of measurement (SEM) turns a single score into a band. Suppose, for the sake of the example, that the SEM of a single rater's PCL-R total is 3 points (an assumed figure, chosen to make the arithmetic clear). A 95 percent band is the score plus or minus 1.96 SEM: 28 ± (1.96 × 3) = 28 ± 5.9, which runs from about 22 to about 34. The band straddles 30. The honest conclusion is not "he is not a psychopath" but "his true score could lie on either side of the cut-off", and a court should hear exactly that.

Key study: Are forensic experts biased by the side that retained them? (Murrie and colleagues, 2013)

In research studies trained PCL-R raters agree closely. Yet in real trials, experts called by the prosecution and by the defence often report strikingly different scores for the same offender. Is that because they see different files, or because the side that pays them shapes their judgement? Daniel C. Murrie, Marcus Boccaccini and their colleagues designed an experiment to find out. They recruited more than a hundred experienced forensic psychologists and psychiatrists through a two-day workshop, and 108 of them were then paid to review and score the same set of offender case files as a consultation. The experts were deceived about one thing: some were told that they had been retained by the prosecution and others by the defence. They scored each offender on two widely used instruments, the PCL-R and the Static-99R, a risk scale for sexual offenders.

Experts who believed they were working for the prosecution gave higher risk scores to the same offenders than those who believed they were working for the defence, with effect sizes (Cohen's d) as large as 0.85. The gap was larger on the PCL-R, whose items demand judgement, than on the more mechanical Static-99R, which is scored largely from documented facts. The authors, writing in Psychological Science, called this an allegiance effect, and they noted that it appeared in some experts, not all. The lesson for measurement is that inter-rater reliability measured in a research team does not guarantee reliability in an adversarial setting: the rater's situation is a source of error in its own right.

The family of instruments. Because the full PCL-R takes several hours of interview and file review, a 12-item Psychopathy Checklist: Screening Version (PCL:SV), scored 0 to 24, was published in 1995 by Stephen Hart, David Cox and Hare for use in civil psychiatric and other settings, and a 20-item youth version (PCL:YV) by Adelle Forth, David Kosson and Hare (2003) adapts the items for adolescents, whose personalities are still forming, which makes the label especially risky (Book 3, Section 11.5.6). Self-report questionnaires of psychopathic traits also exist, such as the Psychopathic Personality Inventory of Scott Lilienfeld and Brian Andrews (1996); they are cheap and useful in research with ordinary adults, but in forensic settings they face the obvious problem that the people they are meant to detect are skilled at managing impressions. Note, finally, what the PCL-R is not. It measures a personality construct; it is not in itself a violence risk assessment, although its scores predict reoffending (Book 3, Section 11.5.6 describes the evidence) and it appears as one item in structured risk instruments.

Exam trap

"The PCL-R was developed by..." Robert Hare (PCL 1980; PCL-R 1991; second edition 2003). Not Hervey Cleckley alone in 1941: Cleckley described the psychopath in The Mask of Sanity and listed his features, but he built no rating scale. Not Aaron Beck (cognitive therapy, the BDI of Section 11.8) and not Hans Eysenck (the Eysenck Personality Questionnaire and the PEN model, Chapter 9). Measurement facts: 20 items, each 0, 1 or 2, total 0 to 40, rated by a trained clinician from a semi-structured interview plus file review, with at most five items omitted and the total prorated; cut-off 30 in North America (25 is often used in Britain and Europe). PCL:SV has 12 items (0 to 24); PCL:YV is the youth version.

Choosing a scale: three referral questions

You have now met several dozen instruments in this chapter, and a referral letter never says "please administer the PANSS". It asks a question: is he getting better, does she have ADHD, is he fit to be released, can she get a disability certificate? Choosing the instrument is a skill in its own right, and it rests on a short sequence of questions that the rest of this book has prepared you to ask. Figure 11.21 sets them out.

Two kinds of instrument mentioned in the figure are not rating scales at all, and they mark the edges of this chapter. If the question is "which disorder does he have?", the tool is a structured diagnostic interview, such as the Structured Clinical Interview for DSM (SCID-5), the Schedules for Clinical Assessment in Neuropsychiatry with its Present State Examination, or the Mini International Neuropsychiatric Interview (MINI); Book 2, Section 2.1.4 teaches them. A rating scale presupposes the diagnosis and measures how much. And if the question is how much stress a person has been exposed to, the tool is a life-events scale, such as Thomas Holmes and Richard Rahe's Social Readjustment Rating Scale (1967), taught in Book 1, Section 11.9.1; it measures events, not symptoms. With those boundaries in place, here are three invented referrals worked through the figure.

Referral 1: "Is Tanuj responding to clozapine?" The decision is about severity and change in a known diagnosis of schizophrenia, so the family is the clinician-rated psychosis scales. The informant problem points to a scale that combines interview, observation and family report, because Tanuj's own account of his voices is incomplete and he cannot report his blunted affect. The time frame is the past week, and the rating must be repeated at fixed intervals. The PANSS fits, with a CGI alongside to translate the number into clinical language. Two further details follow from Sections 11.9.1 and 11.9.2: the same trained rater should make every rating if possible (rater differences would otherwise masquerade as change), and the percentage improvement must be calculated after subtracting 30. A GHQ-12 would be useless (it screens for non-psychotic disorder), and so would a BDI-II, unless the specific question were whether he has become depressed, in which case the Calgary scale is the better choice.

Referral 2: "Does Riddhi have ADHD?" This is a diagnostic question, and no rating scale can answer it alone. But scales are an essential part of the answer, because the diagnosis requires symptoms in two or more settings, and only informants from those settings can supply them. The path therefore runs: a broad-band scale first, the CBCL from her mother and the TRF from her teacher, to see the whole range of problems (as Figure 11.20 showed, anxiety may be part of the picture); then a narrow-band ADHD scale such as the Conners from both; then tests of attention and executive function (Section 11.6) and an educational assessment for a possible learning disorder, and finally a clinical interview that brings it all together. For a Hindi-speaking family in north India, the CPMS offers Indian norms. Throughout, the scores are read with the expectation that the informants will disagree, and the disagreement itself is information.

Referral 3: "Can Laxmi get a disability certificate?" Two years after the episode described in Section 11.9.4, and after two further episodes, Laxmi has lost her teaching post. She has now had bipolar I disorder for nine years, with poor recovery between episodes, and her family asks about a disability certificate. The decision is legal, in India, for a mental illness, so the instrument is fixed by law: IDEAS, rated by the certifying psychiatrist (Book 2, Section 9.9.8 gives the procedure). Suppose the invented ratings are self-care 0, interpersonal activities 2, communication and understanding 1 and work 2, which add to 0 + 2 + 1 + 2 = 5, and that her nine years of illness earn a duration score of 3 (six to ten years, by the 2002 rule of Book 2, Section 9.9.8; under the 2024 guidelines the duration score would instead count her months of illness in the past two years). Her global score is 5 + 3 = 8, in the moderate band of 40 to 70 percent, above the 40 percent benchmark. Now apply the fourth check of Figure 11.21. If a second rater scored her interpersonal activities 1 instead of 2, her global score would be 7, still moderate; but if that rater also scored work 1, it would be 6, mild, below the benchmark. A disagreement of one point on each of two areas separates entitlement from no entitlement. That is why the rating must rest on a careful interview with Laxmi and her family, with the evidence for each rating written down. Her earlier WHODAS 2.0 profile from Section 11.9.7, or a new one, is useful clinical information about where she needs rehabilitation, but it is not the instrument the certifying authority uses.

Remember

To choose a scale, name the decision first (screen, diagnose, rate severity and change, measure disability, assess forensic risk), then check the informant, the time frame, the norms and language, and the reliability at the cut-off that matters. Rating scales measure how much; diagnostic interviews decide which disorder.

Exam trap

"Which of these is a diagnostic instrument?" The SCID-5, the MINI and the SCAN (with the Present State Examination) are structured diagnostic interviews; the PANSS, YMRS, HAM-D, BDI-II and Y-BOCS are severity scales; the GHQ, AUDIT, EPDS and MoCA are screens. A positive screen means "assess further", never "diagnosis made". The Social Readjustment Rating Scale of Holmes and Rahe (1967) measures life events, not symptoms. And when an item asks for "the Indian tool", check the purpose: IDEAS certifies disability in mental illness, ISAA in autism, and the CPMS rates childhood psychopathology.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. The PANSS has 30 items: positive, negative and 16 general psychopathology, each rated from 1 to 7.
  2. A patient's PANSS total falls from 90 to 60. Correctly calculated, his percentage improvement is percent.
  3. Nancy Andreasen's scale with five symptom complexes, including attention, is the .
  4. The YMRS has 11 items; four of them are rated 0 to 8, so its maximum score is .
  5. In GHQ scoring the four answers are scored ; the chronic scoring that counts "no more than usual" on symptom items was proposed by Goodchild and Duncan-Jones in 1985.
  6. On the SCL-90-R, a person whose 90 ratings add up to 108, with 36 items rated above zero, has a GSI of and a PSDI of .
  7. The CIWA-Ar measures the severity of alcohol , and its maximum score is .
  8. On the CGI-Improvement scale, a rating of means "no change".
  9. The Indian adaptation of the Child Behavior Checklist, with 75 items, developed at PGIMER by Savita Malhotra and colleagues, is the .
  10. A PCL-R rater can score only 16 items, which add up to 24. The prorated total is .

Answers: (1) 7, 7; (2) 50 (the fall is 90 − 60 = 30 points; the part of the baseline that was illness is 90 − 30 = 60 points; 30 ÷ 60 = 0.50; the naive answer of 30 ÷ 90 = 33 percent is wrong); (3) Scale for the Assessment of Negative Symptoms (SANS); (4) 60 (7 × 4 = 28, plus 4 × 8 = 32); (5) 0-0-1-1; (6) GSI = 108 ÷ 90 = 1.20, PSDI = 108 ÷ 36 = 3.00; (7) withdrawal, 67; (8) 4; (9) Childhood Psychopathology Measurement Schedule (CPMS); (10) 30 ((24 ÷ 16) × 20 = 1.5 × 20 = 30; only 4 items are omitted, within the limit of five).

Halstead's laboratory, revisited

Go back to Chicago and Indianapolis with what this chapter has taught. Halstead's conviction that the intelligence tests of his day missed what brain-injured people had lost is the reason a neuropsychological assessment samples separate domains rather than one general ability (Section 11.1.2), and his tasks survive in the battery of Section 11.2: the Category Test for abstract reasoning and concept formation, the blindfolded Tactual Performance Test, the Seashore Rhythm and Speech-Sounds Perception Tests and Finger Tapping, joined by Reitan's Trail Making Test, aphasia screening test and sensory-perceptual examination. Reitan's 1955 study made its case with the Impairment Index, the proportion of the seven core scores in the impaired range, and the index's two kinds of mistake, 4 per cent of the patients and 14 per cent of the controls misclassified, are what Section 11.7.1 calls false negatives and false positives. By matching his two groups for age and education, Reitan respected a lesson that Heaton and his colleagues later built into every score with their demographically corrected norms (1991), and the same lesson, that schooling, language and test familiarity change the scores of healthy people, is why India built its own tools: the NIMHANS Neuropsychology Battery, the PGI Battery of Brain Dysfunction and the PGI Memory Scale (Section 11.3). His blind reports are the four methods of inference of Section 11.1.5 at work. For the 42-year-old man his strongest clue was the difficulty with language, the kind of sign, dysphasia, that in his blind reports took precedence over every other indicator in placing a lesion in the left hemisphere; and the printed miss, a slow-growing tumour that the scores made look like the static after-effect of an old injury, shows what a profile read from scores alone cannot settle.

What changed after 1959 was the question, not the logic. Once CT and MRI could show a lesion directly, localisation lost its central place, and the hypothesis-testing approach that Lezak taught and Edith Kaplan's Boston process approach (Sections 11.1.3 and 11.1.4) asked instead what a person can still remember, plan, say or do, and how he fails: the memory tests of Section 11.4, the Bender and Benton tests of Section 11.5 and the Wisconsin Card Sorting Test, Trail Making Test and Stroop of Section 11.6 are chosen to answer that question. Reitan's battery took one to one and a half days; the MMSE and MoCA of Section 11.7 take ten minutes or so, and a positive result means only "assess further". The rating scales of Sections 11.8 and 11.9 answer the other question the opening promised, not "where is the damage?" but "how ill is this person, and are they getting better?", and they carry Reitan's lesson into a new setting: a number means something only against a stated standard, and only when the rater who produces it is trained, anchored and checked. Halstead began by watching brain-injured people go about the ordinary business of their lives; the patient of Section 11.6.8 who passes the tests and fails at life shows that the field is still learning the lesson he started with.

Chapter summary

11.1 What Neuropsychological Assessment Is: Questions, Domains, Approaches and Premorbid Ability. Neuropsychology studies the relationship between brain and behaviour, and every assessment answers a referral question: detecting impairment, differential diagnosis, localisation, a baseline for change, rehabilitation, capacity or medico-legal work. It tests abilities grouped into domains (orientation, attention, processing speed, memory, language, visuospatial and constructional skill, executive functions, motor and sensory function), never opinions. Broca and Wernicke founded localisation, Goldstein described the abstract attitude, single tests of organicity failed, Luria analysed functional systems, and Lezak (1976) and Kaplan's process approach shaped modern practice beside the fixed and flexible batteries. Reitan read profiles by level, pattern, pathognomonic signs and right-left comparisons. Scores are judged against norms (nomothetic) or against the person's premorbid level (idiographic; RML's "ideometric"), estimated from demographics, best performance, Wechsler's hold and don't-hold tests (the deterioration quotient) and Nelson's NART. Performance validity tests check effort.

11.2 The Fixed Batteries: Halstead-Reitan and Luria-Nebraska. Reitan turned Halstead's tests into the Halstead-Reitan Neuropsychological Battery, with the WAIS and MMPI as allied procedures, not parts of it. At its core are the Category Test (abstract reasoning and concept formation), the blindfolded Tactual Performance Test (time, memory and localisation scores), the Seashore Rhythm and Speech-Sounds Perception Tests and Finger Tapping; Reitan added the Trail Making Test, the Reitan-Indiana Aphasia Screening Test, the Reitan-Klove Sensory-Perceptual Examination, grip strength and lateral dominance. The Halstead Impairment Index is the proportion of seven core scores impaired, 0.5 or more suggesting impairment; Heaton's demographically corrected T scores (1991) replaced fixed cut-offs. It serves comprehensive neuropsychological assessment, never personality, projective or vocational testing, and excludes the Rorschach. The Luria-Nebraska ran from Luria through Christensen (1975) to Golden, Hammeke and Purisch (1978, 1980): Form I has 269 items in 11 clinical scales. Critics attacked it, but the two batteries proved about equally accurate.

11.3 Indian Neuropsychological Tools: The NIMHANS Battery, the PGI Battery and the PGI Memory Scale. Indian norms are needed because education, literacy, language and test familiarity change the scores of healthy people, so borrowed cut-offs misclassify them. The NIMHANS Neuropsychology Battery (Rao, Subbakrishna and Gopukumar, 2004) adapts 19 Western tests for adults aged 16 to 65, with percentile norms by age, sex and education and no single index; it uses the Colour Trails Test, the WCST, the Stroop, the Tower of London, fluency, verbal learning and a complex figure. Kar and colleagues built the children's battery (5 to 15), and the NNB-E serves older adults. At PGIMER, Pershad and Verma's PGI Battery of Brain Dysfunction combines the PGI Memory Scale, Bhatia's short scale, the Verbal Adult Intelligence Scale, the Nahor-Benson Test and the Bender-Gestalt into 19 ratings totalling 0 to 57. Pershad and Wig's PGI Memory Scale (1977) has ten subtests. The AIIMS battery is a Hindi Luria-Nebraska.

11.4 Memory Tests: The Wechsler Memory Scale, Digit Span and the Learning Tests. A memory examination separates encoding, storage and retrieval, immediate from delayed recall, recall from recognition and verbal from visual material. Digit span forward measures immediate attention span; backward span measures working memory, not long-term, sensory or procedural memory. Wechsler's Memory Scale (1945) gave a single memory quotient; the WMS-IV (2009) gives five indices: Auditory Memory, Visual Memory, Visual Working Memory, Immediate Memory and Delayed Memory. André Rey's Auditory Verbal Learning Test reads 15 unrelated words over five trials, then an interference list, a delayed recall and a recognition trial, separating proactive from retroactive interference and storage from retrieval deficits; the California Verbal Learning Test (Delis and colleagues, 1987) adds categories and so semantic clustering. Rey's complex figure, standardised by Osterrieth (1944), is copied and then recalled, Benton's Visual Retention Test (1946) has ten designs, and Wilson's Rivermead (1985) samples everyday memory.

11.5 Visuomotor and Visuospatial Tests: The Bender-Gestalt, the Benton Tests and Constructional Tasks. Visuoperception, visuospatial judgement, construction and visual-motor integration are separate skills, and schooling affects them all. Lauretta Bender's Visual Motor Gestalt Test (1938) asks for copies of nine designs, Figure A and 1 to 8, adapted from Max Wertheimer's Gestalt patterns. Pascal and Suttell (1951) scored adults, Hutt read copies projectively, Lacks (1984) built a twelve-error screen, and Koppitz (1963) scored children for distortion, rotation, integration and perseveration. Brannigan and Decker's Bender-Gestalt II (2003) has 16 designs. The Bender assesses visual-motor integration and screens for organic impairment and developmental immaturity, not personality, depression, schizophrenia, ADHD, reading or premorbid intelligence. Benton's Finger Localization Test scores each hand separately, so consistently more errors on one side point to the opposite hemisphere; his line orientation, facial recognition and right-left tests isolate single functions. Line bisection and cancellation reveal neglect, and the Beery VMI serves children.

11.6 Executive Function, Attention, Concept Formation and Language Tests. Executive functions set goals, plan, inhibit, monitor and shift strategy, and depend heavily on the prefrontal cortex. Grant and Berg's Wisconsin Card Sorting Test (1948), standardised by Heaton, has four key cards and 128 response cards sorted by colour, form or number under unannounced rule changes; perseverative errors are its signature score. Milner (1963) tied it to dorsolateral prefrontal damage, though other damage can lower it too. The Trail Making Test (1944) contrasts Part A (attention and speed) with Part B (set-shifting) and helps assess inattention in ADHD; the Colour Trails Test avoids the alphabet. Stroop interference tests inhibition, Shallice's Tower of London (1982) planning, and fluency generation. Patient EVR showed why ecological validity matters. The Continuous Performance Test (1956) and the Test of Everyday Attention (1994) test attention, Goldstein and Scheerer's Object Sorting Test (1941) thought disorder, and the Boston Naming Test, Token Test, BDAE and WAB language.

11.7 Bedside Screening and Staging: The MMSE, MoCA, Glasgow Coma Scale and Dementia Ratings. A screen picks out who needs full assessment; sensitivity, specificity and predictive values describe its accuracy, and a positive result means "assess further". The MMSE (Folstein, Folstein and McHugh, 1975) is scored out of 30 with a 23/24 cut-off and screens for and tracks global cognitive impairment and dementia, but it has a ceiling, depends on schooling and has no executive tasks. Ganguli's Hindi Mental State Examination (1995) adapted it for rural elders without schooling. Nasreddine's MoCA (2005), also out of 30 with 26 and above normal, adds executive tasks and clock drawing and is more sensitive to mild cognitive impairment. Addenbrooke's examination has 100 points; the clock and Mini-Cog are briefer. Teasdale and Jennett's Glasgow Coma Scale (1974) rates consciousness from 3 to 15, 8 or below severe. The CAM detects delirium, and the CDR, Reisberg's Global Deterioration Scale and the Hachinski score stage and type dementia.

11.8 Clinical Rating Scales I: Principles, Depression, Anxiety and OCD. A rating scale measures how much, where a diagnostic interview decides whether. Scales are self-report, clinician-rated or informant-rated: the Beck inventories are self-report and the Hamilton scales clinician-rated. Anchors, time frames and item ranges fix the totals, bands and cut-offs, and a good scale is reliable, valid and sensitive to change. Raters make halo (Thorndike, 1920), leniency, central tendency, logical and contrast errors. Beck's Depression Inventory (1961; BDI-II 1996) has 21 self-report items rated 0 to 3 for two weeks: 0 to 13 minimal, 14 to 19 mild, 20 to 28 moderate, 29 to 63 severe; neuropsychologists add it as a mood measure, but it is not a neuropsychological test. Other depression scales are the HAM-D (1960), MADRS (1979), Zung (1965) and PHQ-9 (2001); anxiety scales include the HAM-A (1959), BAI (1988), GAD-7 and STAI. Goodman's Y-BOCS (1989) rates OCD severity on 10 items, 0 to 40.

11.9 Clinical Rating Scales II: Psychosis, Mania, Substance Use, Children, Disability and Forensic Use. Psychosis is rated by clinicians: Overall and Gorham's BPRS (1962) and Kay, Fiszbein and Opler's PANSS (1987), whose 30 items (7 positive, 7 negative, 16 general), each 1 to 7, measure symptom severity in schizophrenia; percentage change must subtract its floor of 30. Andreasen's SANS and SAPS rate the two syndromes in detail, and Young's scale (1978) rates mania. Goldberg's GHQ (1972) screens for non-psychotic disorder and Derogatis's SCL-90-R profiles distress. AUDIT and CAGE screen drinking, the SADQ rates dependence and the CIWA-Ar withdrawal. The CGI (Guy, 1976), the GAF, WHODAS 2.0 and India's IDEAS rate global functioning and disability. Achenbach's CBCL, the Conners scales, Goodman's SDQ and Malhotra's CPMS rate children, and the EPDS (Cox and colleagues, 1987) screens mothers after childbirth. Robert Hare's PCL-R has 20 items scored 0 to 2, building on Cleckley's The Mask of Sanity (1941).

From the past papers

  1. Which of these is NOT typically assessed by neuropsychological tests? (RML 2025)

    (a) Opinion (b) Language (c) Memory (d) Executive functioning

    Answer: (a) Opinion. Neuropsychological tests measure abilities that depend on particular brain systems, grouped into domains: orientation and consciousness, attention, processing speed, memory, language, visuospatial and constructional skill, executive functions, and motor and sensory function (Section 11.1.2 and Table 11.1). An opinion is an attitude with no better or worse level of performance, so it is the outsider; language, memory and executive functioning are three of the core domains.

  2. Which domains are assessed by neuropsychological tests? (RML 2025)

    (a) Language, Memory, Executive Functioning (b) Opinion, Language, Reaction (c) Memory, Reaction, Opinion (d) Executive Functioning, Opinion, Reaction

    Answer: (a) Language, Memory, Executive Functioning. All three are domains of cognition (Section 11.1.2). Every other option contains "opinion" or "reaction", and neither is a domain. Do not over-learn the item, though: reaction time is measured, as an index of processing speed and attention, so an option that says "reaction time" or "processing speed" in a future paper may be right.

  3. In neuropsychological assessments, the Ideometric Approach primarily focuses on: (RML 2025)

    (a) Evaluating patient's premorbid functioning and current status (b) Quantitative analysis of standardized test scores (c) Comparison with normative population data (d) Assessment of emotional and psychological well-being

    Answer: (a), the keyed option, but "ideometric" is not a standard term. It appears in no major textbook and is probably a garbling of idiographic; the item uses it for Lezak's individual comparison standard, which compares the person's present scores with his or her own estimated premorbid level (Section 11.1.6). Option (c) is the opposite standard, the normative (nomothetic) comparison; (b) is psychometric scoring, which every approach uses and which is not a standard of comparison; (d) is mood assessment (Section 11.8).

  4. Impact of psychiatric illness on one's cognitive and intellectual abilities can be estimated on the basis of: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Deterioration quotient (b) Mediation index (c) Sense of coherence index (d) Theory of Mind index

    Answer: (a) Deterioration quotient. David Wechsler compared the hold tests (Vocabulary, Information, Object Assembly, Picture Completion), which resist brain disease and prolonged mental illness, with the don't-hold tests (Digit Span, Similarities, Digit Symbol, Block Design), which give way; the gap estimates the loss (Section 11.1.7). A mediation index is a statistic from mediation analysis, the sense of coherence is Aaron Antonovsky's predictor of health, and a theory of mind index would concern social cognition; none estimates intellectual decline.

  5. The Halstead-Reitan Neuropsychological Battery is primarily designed to: (RML Practice 1, practice mock)

    (a) Screen for depression using self-report items (b) Assess a broad range of cognitive functions to detect and localize brain dysfunction (c) Measure vocational aptitude (d) Assess attachment style

    Answer: (b). The battery samples a broad range of cognitive, sensory and motor functions to detect brain dysfunction, estimate its severity and, in Reitan's hands, suggest its side and site (Section 11.2.5). A self-report depression screen is a questionnaire such as the BDI (Section 11.8), vocational aptitude is measured by aptitude batteries such as the Differential Aptitude Tests (Chapter 8), and attachment style by the Strange Situation and the Adult Attachment Interview.

  6. The Halstead-Reitan Neuropsychological Battery includes all of the following EXCEPT: (MET Mock 3, practice mock)

    (a) Category Test (b) Tactual Performance Test (c) Trail Making Test (d) Rorschach Inkblot Test

    Answer: (d) Rorschach Inkblot Test. The Rorschach is a projective personality technique (Chapter 10) and is never part of the battery. The Category Test (abstract reasoning and concept formation) and the blindfolded Tactual Performance Test are Halstead's own tests, and the Trail Making Test, taken from the Army Individual Test Battery of 1944, was added by Reitan (Section 11.2.2).

  7. The backward digit span test, where participants must repeat a sequence of numbers in reverse order, is primarily used to assess which type of memory system? (Real PYQ 2025)

    (a) Long-term memory (b) Working memory (c) Sensory memory (d) Procedural memory

    Answer: (b) Working memory. Reversing the string means holding the digits and manipulating them at once, which is what Baddeley and Hitch meant by working memory; forward span measures immediate attention span (Section 11.4.2). Long-term memory is tested by delayed recall after a filled delay, sensory memory by laboratory methods such as Sperling's partial report, and procedural memory by learning a skill such as mirror drawing; amnesic patients can have a normal span.

  8. The Wechsler Memory Scale-IV (WMS-IV) yields which primary indices? (MET Mock 4, practice mock)

    (a) Verbal IQ and Performance IQ (b) Crystallised + Fluid Intelligence only (c) Auditory Memory, Visual Memory, Visual Working Memory, Immediate Memory, Delayed Memory (d) Attention, Concentration, and Vigilance only

    Answer: (c). These are the five WMS-IV indices (2009), kept in the WMS-5 (Section 11.4.3). Verbal and Performance IQ were the summary scores of the old Wechsler intelligence scales, crystallised and fluid intelligence are Cattell and Horn's broad abilities (Chapter 6), and "attention, concentration and vigilance" borrows the WMS-R's Attention/Concentration index; vigilance belongs to continuous performance tests.

  9. The Bender Visual-Motor Gestalt Test is primarily used to assess: (RML Practice 1, practice mock)

    (a) Verbal intelligence (b) Visual-motor integration and possible neurological impairment (c) Unconscious sexual conflicts (d) Vocational interests

    Answer: (b). Lauretta Bender's test (1938) asks the person to copy nine designs adapted from Max Wertheimer's Gestalt patterns; it measures visual-motor integration and screens for organic impairment in adults and for developmental immaturity in children (Section 11.5.5). Copying needs no words, so it says nothing about verbal intelligence; unconscious conflicts are the business of projective techniques (Chapter 10), and Hutt's projective reading of the Bender is secondary; vocational interests are measured by interest inventories (Chapter 8).

  10. Which test can diagnose if an examinee consistently makes more errors on one side of the body than the other? (Entrance Mock 1, practice mock)

    (a) Finger Localization Test (b) Continuous Performance Test (c) Sustained Attention Test (d) The Test of Everyday Attention

    Answer: (a) Finger Localization Test. Arthur Benton's test scores each hand separately, and touch from each hand is analysed mainly in the parietal lobe of the opposite hemisphere, so consistently more errors on one hand point to damage on the other side of the brain (Section 11.5.6). The other three measure attention, which is not tested side by side: the Continuous Performance Test (Rosvold and colleagues, 1956) sustained attention, "sustained attention tests" vigilance in general, and the Test of Everyday Attention (Robertson and colleagues, 1994) several kinds of attention through everyday tasks.

  11. One of the most widely used tests to assess executive functioning: (Amity 2023)

    (a) Wisconsin Card Sorting Test (b) Minnesota Number Ordering Test (c) Manhattan Card Sorting Test (d) None of the above

    Answer: (a) Wisconsin Card Sorting Test. David Grant and Esta Berg's card-sorting task (1948), standardised clinically by Robert Heaton, is the most widely used test of executive function, measuring set-shifting and cognitive flexibility (Sections 11.6.2 and 11.6.4). The "Minnesota Number Ordering Test" and the "Manhattan Card Sorting Test" do not exist, so "none of the above" is wrong too; do not confuse them with real look-alikes such as the Minnesota Rate of Manipulation Test (manual dexterity) or Nelson's Modified Card Sorting Test.

  12. The Wisconsin Card Sorting Test (WCST) is most sensitive to dysfunction of which region? (Biopsych + Testing paper, practice mock)

    (a) Dorsolateral prefrontal cortex (set-shifting, response to changing rules) (b) Primary motor cortex (c) Hippocampus (d) Cerebellum

    Answer: (a) Dorsolateral prefrontal cortex. Brenda Milner (1963) found card sorting impaired after dorsolateral frontal excisions but not after orbitofrontal or temporal ones, and Demakis's meta-analysis (2003) found the frontal deficit largest after dorsolateral damage, although damage outside the frontal lobes can also lower the score (Section 11.6.4). The hippocampus serves memory, the primary motor cortex movement (tested by finger tapping) and the cerebellum coordination.

  13. Trail Making Test helps to assess ______ in children with ADHD. (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Inattention (b) Social Skills (c) Impulsivity (d) Over-activity

    Answer: (a) Inattention. A child with ADHD loses the place, skips circles, makes sequencing errors and takes longer, especially on Part B, where attention must switch between numbers and letters (Section 11.6.5). Social skills are judged from observation and informants, over-activity is a classroom behaviour recorded on rating scales such as the Conners scales, and impulsivity, for which the test has no score, is better shown by the commission errors of a continuous performance test.

  14. Disorder in thinking can be effectively assessed by: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Rorschach Psychodiagnostics (b) Thematic Apperception Test (c) Bender Gestalt Test (d) Object Sorting Test

    Answer: (d) Object Sorting Test. Goldstein and Scheerer's test (1941), brought into clinical psychology by David Rapaport, asks the person to group everyday objects and explain why, and so examines concept formation directly: peculiar, fabulated and overinclusive sorts are the signs of disordered thinking (Section 11.6.10). The Rorschach shows thought disorder only indirectly, through its special scores and the Perceptual-Thinking Index; the TAT taps needs and themes in stories (Chapter 10); the Bender measures visual-motor integration.

  15. The Mini-Mental State Examination (MMSE) is primarily used to: (MET Mock 5, practice mock)

    (a) Diagnose specific personality disorders (b) Measure depression severity (c) Assess intelligence quotient (d) Screen for and track global cognitive impairment / dementia (out of 30)

    Answer: (d). The MMSE of Folstein, Folstein and McHugh (1975) is a 30-point bedside screen, with a cut-off of 23/24, for detecting and following global cognitive impairment, especially dementia (Section 11.7.2). Personality disorders are assessed by structured interviews and inventories, depression severity by scales such as the BDI and the Hamilton scale (although depression can lower an MMSE score), and an IQ needs a full intelligence test such as the Wechsler scales.

  16. The MoCA (Montreal Cognitive Assessment) differs from the MMSE in that MoCA: (Biopsych + Testing paper, practice mock)

    (a) Tests only language, not memory (b) Has greater sensitivity for mild cognitive impairment, includes executive function and clock-drawing, and is freely scored out of 30 with ≤25 as a screening cut-off (c) Is administered only by neurologists (d) Is much shorter (5 min versus 30 min)

    Answer: (b), with one correction. Nasreddine's MoCA (2005) adds executive and visuospatial tasks, clock drawing and a harder five-word recall, and in its validation study it caught 90 percent of people with mild cognitive impairment against 18 percent for the MMSE; 26 and above is normal, so "25 or below" is the same cut-off (Section 11.7.4). "Freely" describes only its early years: since 2019 its use has been tied to the publisher's training and certification. Delayed recall carries 5 of its 30 points, it was designed for first-line doctors, and both tests take about ten minutes.

  17. Which aspect of cognitive function is assessed by the Glasgow Coma Scale? (Entrance Mock 1, practice mock)

    (a) Consciousness (b) Attention (c) Memory (d) Intelligence

    Answer: (a) Consciousness. Graham Teasdale and Bryan Jennett's scale (1974) rates the level of consciousness from eye opening (1 to 4), verbal response (1 to 5) and motor response (1 to 6), a total of 3 to 15, with 8 or below meaning severe injury or coma (Section 11.7.6). Attention is tested with forward digit span, continuous performance and cancellation tasks, memory with the Wechsler Memory Scale and learning tests, and intelligence with the Wechsler intelligence scales.

  18. Which of the following is a clinician-rated questionnaire? (RML 2022)

    (a) Beck Depression Inventory (b) Beck Anxiety Inventory (c) Hamilton Anxiety Rating Scale (d) Both b and c

    Answer: (c) Hamilton Anxiety Rating Scale. Max Hamilton's scale (1959) is completed by a trained clinician after an interview (Section 11.8.2). The Beck Depression Inventory and the Beck Anxiety Inventory are both self-report, so (a) is wrong and (d), which needs both (b) and (c) to be clinician-rated, is wrong too. A useful rule: "inventory" and "questionnaire" usually mean self-report, "rating scale" usually means clinician-rated.

  19. Which of the following tests is typically used for neuropsychological assessment? (RML 2025)

    (a) Beck Depression Inventory (b) Likert Scale (c) 16 Personality Factor Questionnaire (d) Peabody Individual Achievement Test

    Answer: keyed (a) Beck Depression Inventory, but the key needs a caution: the BDI is not a neuropsychological test. It is Aaron Beck's self-report inventory of depressive symptoms and tests no brain function; it earns a place in a neuropsychological assessment only because neuropsychologists routinely add a mood measure, since depression lowers effort, speed and memory and can even mimic dementia (Section 11.8.6). A Likert scale is a response format and attitude-scaling method (Chapter 8), the 16PF is Cattell's personality inventory (Chapter 9), and the Peabody Individual Achievement Test measures school achievement (Chapter 8). Choose (a) as keyed, for the reason given.

  20. A 35-year-old presents with persistent sadness, anhedonia, weight loss, insomnia, worthlessness, and concentration difficulties for 2 months. Which assessment would be MOST appropriate to further evaluate her symptoms? (NIMHANS Mock 1, practice mock)

    (a) Beck Depression Inventory (BDI) (b) Rorschach Inkblot Test (c) Minnesota Multiphasic Personality Inventory (MMPI) (d) Wechsler Adult Intelligence Scale (WAIS)

    Answer: (a) Beck Depression Inventory. The picture is a depressive episode, and the BDI quantifies its severity in about ten minutes and gives a baseline for judging treatment, though it does not confirm the diagnosis (Section 11.8.6). The Rorschach is a long projective technique needing expert scoring (Chapter 10), the MMPI a broad inventory whose depression scale is one of ten (Chapter 9), and the WAIS measures intelligence (Chapter 6).

  21. BDI-II severity bands are conventionally: (MET Mock 4, practice mock)

    (a) 0-13 minimal, 14-19 mild, 20-28 moderate, 29-63 severe (b) 0-5 minimal, 6-10 mild, 11-15 moderate, 16+ severe (c) 0-9 minimal, 10-15 mild, 16-23 moderate, 24-40 severe (d) 0-20 minimal, 21-40 moderate, 41-60 severe

    Answer: (a). The BDI-II (Beck, Steer and Brown, 1996) has 21 items scored 0 to 3 for the past two weeks, a total of 0 to 63, with the manual's bands 0 to 13 minimal, 14 to 19 mild, 20 to 28 moderate and 29 to 63 severe (Section 11.8.6). Option (c) opens with the older BDI's "0 to 9 minimal" and ends at 40, the Yale-Brown maximum, which a scale running to 63 cannot do; (d) has no mild band and stops at 60; and the five-point steps of (b) belong to no Beck scale.

  22. Y-BOCS is a scale for : (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Depression (b) Bipolar disorder (c) Schizophrenia (d) OCD

    Answer: (d) OCD. Wayne Goodman and colleagues' Yale-Brown Obsessive Compulsive Scale (1989) is a clinician-rated measure of OCD severity: 10 items, five for obsessions and five for compulsions, rating time, interference, distress, resistance and control from 0 to 4, a total of 0 to 40 (Section 11.8.9). Depression is rated with the BDI-II, Hamilton or Montgomery-Åsberg scales, the mania of bipolar disorder with the Young Mania Rating Scale, and schizophrenia with the PANSS or the BPRS.

  23. The PANSS (Positive and Negative Syndrome Scale) is used to assess: (Biopsych + Testing paper, practice mock)

    (a) Personality disorders (b) Depression severity (c) Cognitive impairment (d) Symptom severity in schizophrenia, with separate positive, negative, and general psychopathology subscales (30 items)

    Answer: (d). Kay, Fiszbein and Opler's PANSS (1987) has 7 positive, 7 negative and 16 general psychopathology items, each rated 1 to 7, so its total runs from 30 to 210 (Section 11.9.1). Personality disorders need a personality disorder interview; one general item is depression, but depression severity is rated with the BDI or the Hamilton scale (or the Calgary scale in schizophrenia); and cognitive impairment needs tests such as the MoCA, even though the PANSS has items on attention and abstract thinking (Section 11.9.3).

  24. The PCL-R (Psychopathy Checklist-Revised) was developed by: (Biopsych + Testing paper, practice mock)

    (a) Hervey Cleckley alone, 1941 (b) Aaron Beck (c) Robert Hare (d) Hans Eysenck

    Answer: (c) Robert Hare. Hare built the Psychopathy Checklist (1980) and the 20-item PCL-R (1991; second edition 2003), each item scored 0, 1 or 2 by a trained rater from interview and file review, a total of 0 to 40 (Section 11.9.10). Cleckley described the psychopath in The Mask of Sanity (1941) but built no rating scale; Beck built the BDI and cognitive therapy; Eysenck built the Eysenck Personality Questionnaire.

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. The Bender Visual Motor Gestalt Test (Lauretta Bender, 1938; nine designs, Figure A and 1 to 8, adapted from Max Wertheimer's Gestalt patterns) assesses visual-motor integration and screens for organic (neurological) impairment and developmental immaturity; not personality, depression, schizophrenia, ADHD, reading, verbal intelligence or premorbid intelligence. The Bender-Gestalt II (Brannigan and Decker, 2003) has 16 designs. asked 5 times
  2. The Wisconsin Card Sorting Test (David Grant and Esta Berg, 1948; Heaton's clinical manual, 1981) is the most widely used test of executive function: set-shifting, cognitive flexibility and abstract reasoning, with perseverative errors its signature score; not memory, language, reading or mood. The "Minnesota Number Ordering Test" and "Manhattan Card Sorting Test" do not exist. asked 4 times
  3. The Y-BOCS (Wayne Goodman and colleagues, 1989) is a clinician-rated scale of OCD severity, not depression, bipolar disorder, schizophrenia, PTSD, hallucinations or suicidal ideation: 10 items (5 for obsessions, 5 for compulsions) rating time, interference, distress, resistance and control, each 0 to 4, total 0 to 40. asked 3 times
  4. Neuropsychological tests assess brain-dependent abilities: language, memory and executive functioning, with orientation, attention, processing speed, visuospatial, motor and sensory function. Opinion and "reaction" are not domains (though reaction time is measured as an index of processing speed). asked twice
  5. The Halstead-Reitan Neuropsychological Battery (Ward Halstead, 1947; Ralph Reitan) is used for comprehensive neuropsychological assessment, sampling a broad range of functions to detect and localise brain dysfunction; not personality, vocational, projective or attachment assessment, and not a self-report depression screen. asked 3 times
  6. BDI-II (Beck, Steer and Brown, 1996): 21 self-report items, each 0 to 3, for the past two weeks, total 0 to 63; 0 to 13 minimal, 14 to 19 mild, 20 to 28 moderate, 29 to 63 severe. asked 3 times
  7. The WCST is most sensitive to dorsolateral prefrontal cortex dysfunction (Brenda Milner, 1963), not the hippocampus, primary motor cortex or cerebellum, although damage outside the frontal lobes can also lower the score. asked 3 times
  8. The Halstead-Reitan tests are the Category Test, the Tactual Performance Test, the Seashore Rhythm Test, the Speech-Sounds Perception Test, Finger Tapping and the Trail Making Test, with the Reitan-Indiana Aphasia Screening Test and the Reitan-Klove Sensory-Perceptual Examination; the Rorschach, the BDI, the HAM-D and the Bender are not in it, and the WAIS and MMPI are allied procedures. A Halstead Impairment Index of 0.5 or more suggests impairment. asked twice
  9. WMS-IV (2009): five indices, Auditory Memory, Visual Memory, Visual Working Memory, Immediate Memory and Delayed Memory; not Verbal and Performance IQ, crystallised and fluid intelligence, or Mechanical Reasoning (a Differential Aptitude Tests subtest). Only the 1945 scale gave a memory quotient. asked twice
  10. Backward digit span measures working memory, because the digits must be held and manipulated at once; forward span measures immediate attention span; neither measures long-term, sensory or procedural memory. asked once
  11. The "ideometric approach" (RML 2025; not a standard term, probably a garbling of idiographic) is the individual comparison standard: evaluating the patient's premorbid functioning against current status, as opposed to the normative (nomothetic) comparison with population data. asked once
  12. Wechsler's deterioration quotient (deterioration index) estimates the impact of brain or psychiatric illness on cognitive and intellectual abilities from hold tests (Vocabulary, Information, Object Assembly, Picture Completion) against don't-hold tests (Digit Span, Similarities, Digit Symbol, Block Design); not a mediation index, sense of coherence (Antonovsky) or theory of mind index. asked once
  13. Thought disorder is assessed most effectively by the Object Sorting Test (Kurt Goldstein and Martin Scheerer, 1941; brought into clinical use by David Rapaport), which examines concept formation directly; not the Rorschach, the TAT or the Bender. asked once
  14. The Trail Making Test (Army Individual Test Battery, 1944; adopted by Reitan) helps assess inattention in children with ADHD; Part A measures attention and speed, Part B set-shifting; it does not measure social skills, over-activity or, as such, impulsivity. asked once
  15. Clinician-rated versus self-report: the Hamilton Anxiety Rating Scale (1959) and the Hamilton depression scale (1960) are clinician-rated; the Beck Depression and Beck Anxiety Inventories are self-report, so "both the BAI and the HAM-A" is wrong. asked once
  16. The BDI is not a neuropsychological test: RML 2025 keyed it as "typically used for neuropsychological assessment" because neuropsychologists add a mood measure; choose it as keyed, knowing that a Likert scale is a response format, the 16PF a personality inventory and the Peabody Individual Achievement Test an achievement test. asked once
  17. Glasgow Coma Scale (Graham Teasdale and Bryan Jennett, 1974) assesses the level of consciousness: eye opening 1 to 4, verbal 1 to 5, motor 1 to 6, total 3 to 15, 8 or below severe; not attention, memory or intelligence. asked once
  18. Benton's Finger Localization Test shows whether an examinee consistently makes more errors on one side of the body than the other, pointing to the opposite hemisphere; the Continuous Performance Test, sustained attention tests and the Test of Everyday Attention measure attention, not side differences. asked once
  19. MMSE (Marshal Folstein, Susan Folstein and Paul McHugh, 1975): out of 30, cut-off 23/24; screens for and tracks global cognitive impairment and dementia; not personality disorders, depression severity or IQ, and it has no clock drawing. asked once
  20. MoCA (Ziad Nasreddine and colleagues, 2005): out of 30, 26 and above normal, one point added for 12 or fewer years of education; adds executive tasks and clock drawing and is more sensitive than the MMSE to mild cognitive impairment; it takes about as long as the MMSE and is not reserved for neurologists. asked once
  21. PANSS (Stanley Kay, Abraham Fiszbein and Lewis Opler, 1987): 30 items (7 positive, 7 negative, 16 general psychopathology), each 1 to 7, total 30 to 210; rates symptom severity in schizophrenia, not personality disorders, depression severity or cognitive impairment. asked once
  22. The PCL-R was developed by Robert Hare (PCL 1980; PCL-R 1991): 20 items scored 0 to 2, total 0 to 40, building on Hervey Cleckley's The Mask of Sanity (1941); not Cleckley alone, Beck or Eysenck. asked once
  23. For a depressive presentation (two months of sadness, anhedonia, weight loss, insomnia, worthlessness and poor concentration), the instrument to evaluate it further is the Beck Depression Inventory, not the Rorschach, the MMPI or the WAIS. asked once
